Question 1  [ID: 0000a9f4c86741bca5e8439680e89965]
A 24-year-old man presents with 5 days of pain and swelling of the right knee and left ankle. Three weeks ago, he experienced dysuria and scant urethral discharge that resolved without treatment. He has no history of trauma. Physical examination shows right knee effusion, swelling of the left ankle, tenderness at the left Achilles tendon insertion, and bilateral conjunctival injection. His temperature is 37.2°C (99.0°F). Erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein levels are elevated. Arthrocentesis of the right knee yields cloudy fluid with 18,000 leukocytes/mm3, predominantly neutrophils; Gram stain, culture, and crystal analysis are negative. Radiographs show soft-tissue swelling without erosions. Which of the following diagnostic studies is most appropriate to identify the likely precipitating infection?

A. First-catch urine nucleic acid amplification testing for Chlamydia trachomatis
B. Serum antistreptolysin O antibody titer
C. Blood cultures for Neisseria gonorrhoeae
D. Stool culture for Salmonella species
E. Serum HLA-B27 testing

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. The combination of acute asymmetric lower-extremity joint swelling, enthesitis, conjunctival inflammation, and recent urethritis strongly suggests a sterile inflammatory condition triggered by a genitourinary infection. Chlamydia trachomatis is a common precipitating pathogen, and first-catch urine nucleic acid amplification testing is the preferred study because of its high sensitivity and specificity, even when urethral symptoms have resolved. The inflammatory but culture-negative synovial fluid further supports a sterile process rather than direct infection of the joint. B is incorrect because an elevated antistreptolysin O titer supports recent group A streptococcal infection, which is associated with poststreptococcal syndromes but does not fit the preceding urethritis and conjunctival findings. C is incorrect because disseminated gonococcal infection may cause migratory pain, tenosynovitis, dermatitis, or purulent joint infection, but blood cultures have limited sensitivity and the sterile synovial fluid plus enthesitis and conjunctival findings favor a postinfectious process. D is incorrect because enteric pathogens such as Salmonella can precipitate a similar clinical syndrome, but this patient reports genitourinary rather than gastrointestinal symptoms; stool culture would have low yield without recent diarrhea. E is incorrect because HLA-B27 is associated with more severe or persistent disease but is neither required nor sufficiently specific for diagnosis and does not identify the precipitating infection.

Question 2  [ID: 001ce92584f6424c91341428748d2cba]
A 62-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe left-sided thoracic pain and difficulty breathing after his vehicle struck a tree. He was wearing a seat belt, and the side airbag deployed. His temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), blood pressure is 104/68 mm Hg, pulse is 112/min, respirations are 30/min, and oxygen saturation is 89% on room air. Examination reveals extensive ecchymosis and marked tenderness over the left lateral thorax. During inspiration, a portion of the left thoracic wall moves inward while the surrounding wall expands; during expiration, this segment moves outward. Breath sounds are diminished at the left base. Hemoglobin is 13.1 g/dL. Radiography demonstrates cortical discontinuities involving the lateral and posterior aspects of the left fourth through seventh ribs at two separate sites per rib, with patchy underlying airspace opacities. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Flail chest
B. Isolated simple rib fracture
C. Traumatic pneumothorax
D. Traumatic hemothorax
E. Sternal fracture

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Flail chest occurs when at least three adjacent ribs are each fractured in two or more places, creating a free-floating segment. The hallmark examination finding is paradoxical movement: the unstable segment moves inward during inspiration and outward during expiration. The described cortical disruptions of ribs 4 through 7 at two sites each confirm the structural pattern. The underlying patchy airspace opacities suggest an associated pulmonary contusion, which commonly contributes to hypoxemia and respiratory distress. An isolated simple rib fracture causes localized pain and splinting but does not produce a free-floating segment or paradoxical motion. A traumatic pneumothorax may cause dyspnea and decreased breath sounds but would typically demonstrate pleural air and does not explain paradoxical wall movement. A traumatic hemothorax may produce diminished breath sounds and dependent pleural fluid, often with blood loss, but it also does not cause paradoxical movement. A sternal fracture causes anterior midline tenderness and may be associated with cardiac injury, but the injury pattern and lateral wall instability described here are not consistent with an isolated sternal injury.

Question 3  [ID: 00fc3915d9d54a0d870bccfaa5114da4]
A 15-year-old boy is evaluated for progressive exertional dyspnea, reduced exercise tolerance, and intermittent palpitations. His parents first noticed an inward depression of his lower sternum during early childhood, but it has become substantially more prominent during his recent growth spurt. He has no history of trauma, asthma, or congenital heart disease. Physical examination shows a thin adolescent with a deep, symmetric depression of the lower sternum that becomes more pronounced with inspiration. Cardiac auscultation reveals a systolic flow murmur along the left sternal border. Pulmonary function testing demonstrates a mild restrictive pattern. Echocardiography shows right ventricular compression without valvular abnormalities. Computed tomography demonstrates a transverse thoracic diameter of 25 cm and an anteroposterior diameter of 6 cm. Complete blood count and comprehensive metabolic panel are normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate definitive intervention for this patient?

A. Observation with annual cardiopulmonary assessment
B. External bracing worn for 12 to 18 hours daily
C. Minimally invasive placement of a substernal corrective bar
D. Needle decompression followed by tube thoracostomy
E. Systemic glucocorticoid therapy and pulmonary rehabilitation

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: This adolescent has a severe pectus excavatum deformity with clinically significant cardiopulmonary compromise. The calculated Haller index is approximately 4.2 (25 cm divided by 6 cm); an index greater than 3.25 generally indicates a severe deformity and may support operative correction when accompanied by symptoms or objective cardiopulmonary impairment. His exertional dyspnea, restrictive physiology, and right ventricular compression are indications for definitive surgical management. Minimally invasive placement of a curved substernal bar, known as the Nuss procedure, elevates and remodels the depressed sternum and is commonly preferred in adolescents with a symmetric, flexible deformity. Observation (A) may be appropriate for mild, asymptomatic cases without cardiopulmonary impairment but is inadequate here. External bracing (B) is primarily used for an outward-projecting, flexible sternal deformity and does not reliably correct a severe inward depression. Needle decompression and tube thoracostomy (D) are emergency treatments for a tension pneumothorax, which is not supported by the chronic presentation or imaging findings. Systemic glucocorticoids and pulmonary rehabilitation (E) do not correct the structural abnormality or relieve cardiac compression.

Question 4  [ID: 01be296499fb4305a700b5842d01c143]
A 29-year-old woman presents with 4 months of intermittent aching in the right shoulder and paresthesias along the medial forearm and fourth and fifth digits. Her symptoms worsen when she stocks overhead shelves at work and improve when she lowers her arm. She denies trauma, fever, neck pain, or nocturnal symptoms. Examination reveals normal upper-extremity strength and reflexes, intact peripheral pulses, and no swelling or discoloration. Holding both arms abducted and externally rotated while repeatedly opening and closing the hands reproduces her symptoms within 1 minute. Spurling testing and Tinel testing at the elbow and wrist are negative. Plain radiographs show no fracture or cervical rib, and electrodiagnostic testing shows no focal neuropathy. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial intervention?

A. Activity modification and physical therapy emphasizing postural correction, shoulder-girdle strengthening, and stretching
B. Immediate surgical decompression with first-rib resection
C. Therapeutic anticoagulation for 3 months
D. Cervical epidural corticosteroid injection
E. Endoscopic release of the transverse carpal ligament

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is most consistent with neurogenic thoracic outlet syndrome. Compression of the lower brachial plexus produces pain and paresthesias in the medial forearm and fourth and fifth digits, particularly during overhead activity. Reproduction of symptoms with the elevated arm stress maneuver supports the diagnosis. In the absence of progressive weakness, muscle atrophy, vascular compromise, or a structural lesion requiring correction, initial management is conservative. Activity modification and targeted physical therapy improve posture, strengthen the shoulder girdle, stretch tight scalene and pectoral muscles, and increase the available space for the neurovascular structures. B is incorrect because surgical decompression is reserved for persistent, disabling symptoms despite an adequate trial of conservative therapy or for significant neurologic or vascular compromise. C is incorrect because anticoagulation is used when venous thrombosis is present; this patient has no swelling, discoloration, or imaging evidence of thrombosis. D is incorrect because the negative Spurling test, absence of neck pain, and symptom pattern make cervical radiculopathy less likely. E is incorrect because carpal tunnel release treats median nerve compression, which typically causes paresthesias in the thumb, index, middle, and radial half of the ring finger rather than the medial forearm and fifth digit.

Question 5  [ID: 01cd09f10e69479cb1023ca8a1bda8cc]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 9 months of diffuse aching involving both sides of her body above and below the waist. She also reports persistent fatigue, nonrestorative sleep, difficulty concentrating, and intermittent headaches. She denies joint swelling, prolonged morning stiffness, fever, weight loss, rash, oral ulcers, or focal weakness. Examination shows no synovitis, deformity, or limitation of joint motion. Strength and sensation are intact. Palpation produces tenderness in multiple regions, including the neck, shoulders, upper back, lower back, hips, and extremities. Complete blood count, comprehensive metabolic panel, thyroid-stimulating hormone, creatine kinase, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein are within reference ranges. Which of the following is the most appropriate next diagnostic step?

A. Order an antinuclear antibody panel to confirm the suspected condition
B. Obtain radiographs of the most painful regions
C. Perform electromyography and nerve conduction studies
D. Make a clinical diagnosis without additional confirmatory testing
E. Obtain a biopsy from a tender region

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: The patient's chronic widespread pain, fatigue, nonrestorative sleep, cognitive symptoms, multifocal tenderness, absence of objective inflammation or weakness, and normal screening laboratory results are characteristic of fibromyalgia. Fibromyalgia is diagnosed clinically using the pattern and duration of symptoms, often supported by criteria incorporating widespread pain and symptom severity. No laboratory, imaging, electrodiagnostic, or histopathologic study confirms the diagnosis. Limited testing is used primarily to evaluate reasonable alternative causes of symptoms; the normal CBC, metabolic panel, TSH, creatine kinase, ESR, and CRP make anemia, major metabolic disease, hypothyroidism, inflammatory disease, and inflammatory or destructive myopathy less likely. Therefore, no additional confirmatory testing is indicated. Choice A is incorrect because antinuclear antibodies do not confirm fibromyalgia and may be positive in healthy individuals; testing without clinical evidence of systemic autoimmune disease can produce misleading results. Choice B is incorrect because radiographs are generally normal and are not indicated without focal pain, trauma, deformity, or other evidence of structural disease. Choice C is incorrect because electrodiagnostic studies are reserved for suspected neuropathy, radiculopathy, neuromuscular junction disease, or myopathy; this patient has normal strength, sensation, and creatine kinase. Choice E is incorrect because biopsy has no role in confirming fibromyalgia and would be appropriate only if another condition, such as inflammatory myopathy or vasculitis, were suspected.

Question 6  [ID: 02362c8bb9ab4852ae8d23ed7dcfee81]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 8 months of diffuse, bilateral pain involving her neck, shoulders, lower back, arms, and legs. She also reports persistent fatigue, difficulty concentrating, and awakening feeling unrefreshed despite sleeping 8 hours nightly. Her symptoms began during a period of significant occupational stress. She denies joint swelling, fever, rash, oral ulcers, focal weakness, and weight loss. Examination shows widespread tenderness to palpation over multiple regions without joint erythema, warmth, swelling, or restricted range of motion. Strength and sensation are intact. Complete blood count, comprehensive metabolic panel, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, C-reactive protein, thyroid-stimulating hormone, and creatine kinase levels are within normal limits. Antinuclear antibody and rheumatoid factor testing are negative. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Fibromyalgia
B. Polymyalgia rheumatica
C. Rheumatoid arthritis
D. Systemic lupus erythematosus
E. Polymyositis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Fibromyalgia is characterized by chronic, widespread pain accompanied by fatigue, nonrestorative sleep, and cognitive symptoms. Examination commonly demonstrates diffuse tenderness without objective inflammation, synovitis, neurologic deficits, or true weakness. Routine laboratory studies are typically normal, and testing is primarily used to exclude alternative causes. Polymyalgia rheumatica generally occurs in adults older than 50 years and causes prominent shoulder and hip girdle pain with morning stiffness and an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate or C-reactive protein level. Rheumatoid arthritis causes persistent inflammatory synovitis, typically involving the small joints symmetrically, and may be associated with elevated inflammatory markers or positive serologic testing. Systemic lupus erythematosus may cause arthralgias and fatigue but usually has additional manifestations, such as rash, oral ulcers, cytopenias, renal abnormalities, or positive antinuclear antibodies. Polymyositis causes progressive, symmetric proximal muscle weakness rather than diffuse tenderness and is typically associated with an elevated creatine kinase level.

Question 7  [ID: 026ece2d66bb4d69b3ebbb4c39fea25a]
A 16-year-old boy is evaluated for progressive exertional dyspnea, reduced exercise tolerance, and intermittent palpitations. His parents report that an abnormal contour of his anterior torso has become more pronounced during his adolescent growth spurt. Physical examination shows a symmetric inward depression of the lower sternum that becomes more apparent with inspiration. Cardiac auscultation reveals a midsystolic click. Pulmonary function testing demonstrates a mild restrictive pattern. CT shows a transverse internal diameter of 25 cm and an anteroposterior distance of 6.5 cm at the point of maximal depression. Echocardiography demonstrates mild right ventricular compression. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Pectus carinatum
B. Pectus excavatum
C. Sternal cleft
D. Poland syndrome
E. Flail chest

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Pectus excavatum is characterized by posterior displacement of the sternum, producing a sunken anterior contour that often becomes more prominent during adolescent growth. Severe cases may cause restrictive pulmonary physiology, exercise intolerance, palpitations, and right ventricular compression. The CT measurements yield a Haller index of approximately 3.85 (25 divided by 6.5); an index greater than 3.25 indicates a severe deformity and may support surgical evaluation in a symptomatic patient. A midsystolic click may occur because mitral valve prolapse is associated with this condition. Pectus carinatum causes anterior protrusion rather than depression of the sternum. A sternal cleft is a congenital failure of sternal fusion and generally presents as a midline gap rather than a funnel-shaped depression. Poland syndrome involves unilateral absence or hypoplasia of the pectoralis major muscle, often with ipsilateral hand abnormalities. Flail chest is an acute traumatic condition caused by multiple contiguous rib fractures and produces paradoxical motion of a free segment.

Question 8  [ID: 03f1d348eb594830aa7769562bd67553]
A 13-year-old boy is brought by his parents because his breastbone has become increasingly prominent over the past 2 years. He denies pain, dyspnea, palpitations, or exercise intolerance but has stopped swimming because he is embarrassed by his appearance. He has no history of trauma or prior surgery. Vital signs are normal. Examination shows a symmetric anterior protrusion of the lower sternum and adjacent costal cartilages. The prominence is flexible and can be substantially corrected with gentle manual pressure. Cardiac and pulmonary examinations are normal. Radiographs show anterior displacement without cardiopulmonary compression, and spirometry is normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate counseling and management recommendation for this patient?

A. Provide reassurance only because treatment is ineffective until skeletal maturity
B. Recommend a customized external compression brace with close follow-up and emphasize that consistent daily use improves the likelihood of correction
C. Recommend immediate operative reconstruction to prevent progressive restrictive lung disease
D. Advise avoidance of resistance training and contact sports to prevent worsening of the prominence
E. Prescribe calcium and vitamin D supplementation to promote remodeling of the affected area

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This adolescent has a flexible anterior protrusion of the sternum and costal cartilages consistent with pectus carinatum. In a skeletally immature patient with a flexible deformity, customized external compression bracing is the preferred initial management. Successful correction depends heavily on adherence, so patient education should emphasize regular daily brace use, proper fit, skin care, and scheduled follow-up for pressure adjustment and monitoring. Psychosocial concerns, including embarrassment and activity avoidance, should also be addressed. A is incorrect because observation may be reasonable for a mild deformity that does not concern the patient, but this patient is experiencing psychosocial impairment, and bracing is most effective before skeletal maturity. C is incorrect because surgery is generally reserved for severe, rigid, asymmetric, or brace-refractory cases; prophylactic surgery is not indicated when cardiopulmonary function is normal. D is incorrect because routine physical activity does not cause progression, and unnecessary activity restriction may worsen psychosocial distress. E is incorrect because calcium and vitamin D do not correct this structural contour abnormality unless a separate nutritional deficiency is present.

Question 9  [ID: 040fd52753424b12a169a69f07f9d199]
A 54-year-old man presents after being struck on his left side by a motor vehicle. He reports severe pain with inspiration and increasing shortness of breath. His blood pressure is 118/74 mm Hg, pulse is 112/min, respirations are 30/min, and oxygen saturation is 89% on room air. Examination shows extensive ecchymosis over the left lateral thorax. A portion of the injured area retracts during inspiration and bulges outward during expiration. Breath sounds are present bilaterally but diminished at the left base. CT demonstrates cortical disruptions of the left fourth through seventh ribs at two separate locations along each rib, with an adjacent pulmonary contusion. Which of the following physiologic mechanisms most directly contributes to this patient's impaired ventilation?

A. Loss of structural continuity causes paradoxical movement that reduces effective tidal volume
B. Increased intrapleural pressure from air accumulation causes complete ipsilateral lung collapse
C. Disruption of pulmonary capillaries causes increased hydrostatic pressure and cardiogenic edema
D. Reflex bronchial smooth muscle contraction increases expiratory airway resistance
E. Loss of surfactant production increases compliance and promotes alveolar overdistention

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Multiple adjacent ribs disrupted in two locations create a free-floating segment. During inspiration, negative intrathoracic pressure pulls this unstable segment inward rather than expanding it outward; during expiration, the segment moves outward. This paradoxical movement reduces effective chest expansion and tidal volume, increases the work of breathing, and contributes to hypoxemia. Pain-related splinting and the associated pulmonary contusion further impair ventilation and produce ventilation-perfusion mismatch.

B is incorrect because air under pressure in the pleural space can collapse the ipsilateral lung and cause obstructive shock, but the vignette does not describe absent unilateral breath sounds, hypotension, or other findings suggesting this as the primary mechanism.

C is incorrect because traumatic pulmonary contusion causes noncardiogenic alveolar hemorrhage and edema due to increased alveolar-capillary permeability, not increased hydrostatic pressure from cardiac dysfunction.

D is incorrect because bronchial smooth muscle contraction is characteristic of obstructive airway disorders and does not explain the paradoxical motion observed after blunt trauma.

E is incorrect because reduced surfactant would decrease, not increase, pulmonary compliance and would promote alveolar collapse rather than overdistention.

Question 10  [ID: 049191e0b0e74eb1934b9c620dcfda44]
A 13-year-old boy is evaluated for 3 weeks of progressive left groin and medial knee pain. He denies trauma, fever, or recent illness. His BMI is at the 98th percentile for age. He walks with an antalgic gait, and the left lower extremity rests in external rotation. Examination demonstrates decreased internal rotation, flexion, and abduction at the left hip. When the hip is passively flexed, the thigh involuntarily rotates externally. Laboratory studies, including a leukocyte count and C-reactive protein level, are normal. An anteroposterior radiograph shows widening and irregularity of the proximal femoral growth plate; a line drawn along the superior femoral neck does not intersect the femoral head. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Legg-Calvé-Perthes disease
B. Septic arthritis of the hip
C. Slipped capital femoral epiphysis
D. Transient synovitis of the hip
E. Femoral neck stress fracture

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The correct answer is C. Slipped capital femoral epiphysis typically occurs in overweight adolescents during periods of rapid growth. Patients often report insidious hip, groin, thigh, or referred knee pain and may walk with an antalgic gait. Characteristic findings include loss of hip internal rotation, obligate external rotation during hip flexion, and resting external rotation of the affected extremity. Radiographs may demonstrate physeal widening and displacement of the femoral head relative to the femoral neck. Failure of a line drawn along the superior femoral neck to intersect the femoral head is an abnormal Klein line and strongly supports the diagnosis.

A is incorrect because Legg-Calvé-Perthes disease is avascular necrosis of the femoral head that usually affects younger children, most commonly between ages 4 and 8 years. Imaging typically shows femoral head sclerosis, flattening, or fragmentation rather than physeal widening and an abnormal Klein line.

B is incorrect because septic arthritis generally presents acutely with severe pain, markedly limited range of motion, inability to bear weight, and often fever or elevated inflammatory markers. The gradual course and normal laboratory results make infection unlikely.

D is incorrect because transient synovitis usually occurs in younger children, often after a viral illness, and typically causes acute, self-limited hip pain. It does not produce proximal femoral physeal widening or displacement.

E is incorrect because a femoral neck stress fracture may cause activity-related groin pain, particularly in endurance athletes, but it does not characteristically cause obligate external rotation with flexion or an abnormal Klein line. Imaging would more likely show a fracture line or stress reaction in the femoral neck.

Question 11  [ID: 0503d055d57f451ba3ee5e710917a9f8]
A 42-year-old warehouse employee presents with 10 days of lower back pain that began after lifting a heavy box. The pain radiates down the posterolateral aspect of his left leg to the dorsum of the foot and worsens with coughing. He denies fever, weight loss, urinary retention, fecal incontinence, and saddle anesthesia. Examination shows decreased sensation over the dorsum of the left foot, 4+/5 strength with left great-toe extension, and a positive straight-leg raise test on the left. Patellar and Achilles reflexes are symmetric. MRI demonstrates left paracentral soft-tissue material at L4-L5 that narrows the lateral recess and contacts the descending L5 nerve root. After discussing conservative management, which of the following counseling recommendations is most appropriate to promote recovery and reduce the risk of future episodes?

A. Remain on strict bed rest until all pain and sensory symptoms have resolved
B. Continue normal activities as tolerated, begin a progressive exercise program emphasizing trunk stabilization, and use proper lifting mechanics
C. Avoid all lifting, bending, and structured exercise permanently
D. Wear a rigid lumbar brace during all waking hours for at least 6 months
E. Seek urgent surgical treatment if radiating pain persists for more than 7 days

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has findings consistent with lumbar herniated disk disease causing L5 radiculopathy without cauda equina syndrome or another indication for emergency intervention. Patients should be encouraged to remain active within tolerable limits rather than undergo prolonged bed rest. Once acute pain permits, a progressive exercise program that emphasizes trunk stabilization, flexibility, conditioning, and proper body mechanics can improve function and help reduce recurrent episodes. Education should also address safe lifting techniques, weight management when applicable, and smoking cessation. Choice A is incorrect because prolonged bed rest can cause deconditioning and may delay functional recovery. Choice C is incorrect because permanent activity restriction is unnecessary and may worsen disability; gradual return to activity is preferred. Choice D is incorrect because routine prolonged use of a rigid brace has not been shown to prevent recurrence and can contribute to muscle deconditioning. Choice E is incorrect because persistent pain for 7 days alone is not an indication for urgent surgery. Surgical evaluation is generally reserved for progressive or severe neurologic deficits, cauda equina syndrome, or disabling symptoms that persist despite an adequate trial of conservative treatment.

Question 12  [ID: 05358bc6194649729ba4bca663ca5c2c]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 5 months of progressive pain and swelling in both hands and wrists. She reports fatigue and morning stiffness lasting approximately 2 hours that improves with activity. She denies recent infection, psoriasis, oral ulcers, photosensitivity, or urethral symptoms. Physical examination demonstrates symmetric, boggy swelling and tenderness of the wrists and the second through fifth metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints; the distal interphalangeal joints are spared. Laboratory studies show an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, normocytic anemia, and positive rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies. Radiographs of the hands reveal periarticular osteopenia, uniform joint-space narrowing, and small marginal erosions. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Osteoarthritis
B. Psoriatic arthritis
C. Rheumatoid arthritis
D. Systemic lupus erythematosus
E. Parvovirus B19 infection

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The correct answer is C. Rheumatoid arthritis classically presents with chronic, symmetric involvement of the wrists, metacarpophalangeal joints, and proximal interphalangeal joints, with relative sparing of the distal interphalangeal joints. Prolonged morning stiffness that improves with activity, boggy synovial swelling, elevated inflammatory markers, and normocytic anemia further support the diagnosis. Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies are highly specific, and the radiographic findings of periarticular osteopenia, uniform joint-space narrowing, and marginal erosions are characteristic of established disease. A is incorrect because osteoarthritis typically causes brief morning stiffness, pain worsened by use, and bony enlargement of the distal and proximal interphalangeal joints; radiographs more often show osteophytes, subchondral sclerosis, and nonuniform joint-space narrowing. B is incorrect because psoriatic arthritis commonly occurs with psoriasis or nail changes and may produce asymmetric involvement, distal interphalangeal disease, dactylitis, or pencil-in-cup deformities. D is incorrect because joint manifestations associated with systemic lupus erythematosus are usually nonerosive and are often accompanied by findings such as photosensitivity, oral ulcers, nephritis, cytopenias, or serositis. E is incorrect because parvovirus B19 can cause an acute symmetric small-joint presentation in adults, but symptoms are generally self-limited and would not typically cause marginal erosions after a chronic 5-month course.

Question 13  [ID: 05a85adf1b39495b9fe9765f7582c7c8]
A 2-week-old girl is brought to the clinic for a routine well-child visit. She was delivered by cesarean section at 39 weeks because of persistent frank breech presentation. Her older sister required treatment with a brace during infancy. The newborn is feeding and growing appropriately. Physical examination shows symmetric gluteal folds, equal leg lengths, full bilateral abduction, and no palpable instability with provocative maneuvers. Prenatal ultrasonography showed no fetal abnormalities other than breech positioning. Which of the following is the most appropriate preventive management for this infant?

A. Reassure the parents and perform no additional screening unless symptoms develop
B. Obtain targeted ultrasonography at approximately 4 to 6 weeks of age
C. Obtain an anteroposterior pelvic radiograph immediately
D. Begin prophylactic abduction bracing without additional evaluation
E. Order computed tomography at 6 months of age

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This infant has major risk factors for abnormal acetabular and femoral head development, including female sex, breech presentation, and a positive family history. A normal newborn examination does not exclude an underlying abnormality because physical examination maneuvers have limited sensitivity. Selective imaging with ultrasonography at approximately 4 to 6 weeks is recommended for high-risk infants with a normal examination. Delaying ultrasonography until after the immediate newborn period reduces false-positive findings caused by transient physiologic laxity. A is incorrect because high-risk infants require selective imaging even when the examination is normal. C is incorrect because plain radiographs are less useful in young infants before sufficient ossification of the femoral head; radiography becomes more appropriate after approximately 4 to 6 months of age. D is incorrect because bracing should not be initiated prophylactically without confirmation and appropriate specialist evaluation. E is incorrect because computed tomography exposes the infant to unnecessary radiation and is not used for routine screening.

Question 14  [ID: 0612c502b7a843f5ac4ad0844723bad1]
A 72-year-old man presents with 8 months of progressively worsening aching and heaviness in both buttocks and thighs. The symptoms begin after he stands for approximately 10 minutes or walks two blocks and resolve within several minutes of sitting. He can ride a stationary bicycle without discomfort and reports that leaning forward over a shopping cart allows him to walk farther. He denies fever, weight loss, recent trauma, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and saddle anesthesia. Examination shows full strength in both lower extremities, symmetric patellar reflexes, mildly diminished Achilles reflexes, and intact sensation at rest. Dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses are 2+ bilaterally. Straight-leg raising is negative. Plain radiographs demonstrate multilevel disc-space narrowing and facet joint hypertrophy. Which of the following additional physical examination findings is most likely in this patient?

A. Reproduction of bilateral leg symptoms with sustained lumbar extension
B. Reproduction of unilateral radicular pain with passive straight-leg raising
C. Absent pedal pulses after walking until symptoms resolve
D. Groin pain with passive internal rotation of the hip
E. Focal vertebral tenderness accompanied by fever

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's exertional bilateral buttock and thigh discomfort, relief with sitting or forward flexion, preserved ability to cycle, normal peripheral pulses, and degenerative radiographic findings are characteristic of lumbar spinal stenosis causing neurogenic claudication. Lumbar extension decreases the available space within the central canal and neural foramina, so sustained extension commonly reproduces the patient's leg symptoms. Flexion increases the available space and typically provides relief.

B is incorrect because a positive straight-leg raise suggests lumbosacral nerve-root irritation, often from an acute disc herniation, and is more likely to produce unilateral radicular pain.

C is incorrect because absent pedal pulses during symptoms would favor arterial insufficiency with vascular claudication. This patient has normal pulses, and his symptoms are posture dependent.

D is incorrect because pain with passive hip internal rotation is more consistent with intra-articular hip disease, which commonly causes groin pain and reduced hip range of motion rather than posture-dependent bilateral thigh heaviness.

E is incorrect because focal vertebral tenderness with fever raises concern for vertebral osteomyelitis or another infectious process. The chronic exertional pattern, absence of systemic symptoms, and relief with flexion do not support infection.

Question 15  [ID: 066d520593a247bda9e5838df799a3e7]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 6 months of progressive pain and swelling involving both hands and wrists. She reports morning stiffness lasting approximately 2 hours that improves with activity. Examination shows symmetric tenderness and swelling of the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints, with sparing of the distal interphalangeal joints. Laboratory studies reveal an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, a positive anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody test, and normocytic anemia. Radiographs demonstrate periarticular osteopenia and marginal erosions. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most directly accounts for the progressive erosive changes in this patient?

A. Deposition of monosodium urate crystals that activates the NLRP3 inflammasome
B. Formation of an invasive inflammatory pannus with cytokine-mediated osteoclast activation
C. Degeneration of articular cartilage due to age-related loss of proteoglycans
D. Deposition of calcium pyrophosphate crystals caused by increased extracellular pyrophosphate
E. Antibody-mediated destruction of acetylcholine receptors at the postsynaptic membrane

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The symmetric inflammatory polyarticular presentation, prolonged morning stiffness, positive anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies, periarticular osteopenia, and marginal erosions are characteristic of rheumatoid arthritis. Activated CD4-positive T cells, macrophages, B cells, and synovial fibroblasts produce inflammatory mediators, particularly tumor necrosis factor-alpha, interleukin-1, and interleukin-6. The resulting synovial hyperplasia forms an invasive pannus that extends over articular cartilage. Expression of receptor activator of nuclear factor kappa-B ligand promotes osteoclast differentiation and activation, causing marginal bone erosions. A is incorrect because monosodium urate deposition causes gout, which typically presents with episodic, intensely painful monoarthritis and negatively birefringent needle-shaped crystals. C is incorrect because proteoglycan loss and mechanical cartilage degeneration characterize osteoarthritis, which causes brief morning stiffness, pain worsened by activity, and noninflammatory bony enlargement. D is incorrect because calcium pyrophosphate deposition causes pseudogout, often affecting the knees or wrists and producing positively birefringent rhomboid-shaped crystals and chondrocalcinosis. E is incorrect because antibodies against postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors cause fatigable skeletal muscle weakness rather than inflammatory synovitis or bone erosions.

Question 16  [ID: 06733fb8dc534889a40bbafbb673e2c4]
A 6-week-old firstborn girl is evaluated because her parents noticed asymmetric thigh folds during diaper changes. She was delivered by cesarean section after a frank breech presentation. She has no fever, irritability, or feeding difficulty. Examination demonstrates decreased abduction of the left proximal lower extremity and a palpable clunk when the flexed left thigh is abducted while anterior pressure is applied. Ultrasonography shows a shallow left acetabular roof, decreased femoral head coverage, and displacement of the femoral head with stress. Which of the following mechanisms most directly contributes to progression of this infant's anatomic abnormality?

A. Loss of concentric femoral head contact impairs normal stimulation of acetabular growth and deepening
B. Disruption of epiphyseal blood flow causes ischemic necrosis and collapse of the femoral head
C. A physeal shear injury causes posteroinferior displacement of the femoral epiphysis
D. Hematogenous bacterial seeding destroys the articular cartilage and widens the joint space
E. Defective type I collagen synthesis causes generalized bone fragility and progressive deformity

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The findings are characteristic of developmental dysplasia of the hip. Normal acetabular development depends on stable, concentric positioning of the femoral head within the acetabulum. When the femoral head is subluxated or dislocated, the acetabulum does not receive the mechanical stimulation required for normal growth and deepening. This leads to progressive acetabular shallowness and further instability, creating a self-perpetuating cycle. Breech presentation, female sex, and firstborn status are important risk factors. The palpable clunk with abduction and anterior pressure describes a positive Ortolani maneuver. Choice B describes avascular necrosis, such as Legg-Calvé-Perthes disease, which typically presents in older children and does not explain dynamic neonatal instability. Choice C describes slipped capital femoral epiphysis, an adolescent disorder involving physeal failure. Choice D describes septic arthritis, which would usually cause fever, pain, irritability, restricted movement, and inflammatory findings. Choice E describes osteogenesis imperfecta, which causes recurrent fractures, bone fragility, and possible blue sclerae rather than isolated instability with a shallow acetabulum.

Question 17  [ID: 06ffd46cd8df4144b25742133c307756]
A 46-year-old woman presents with progressive tightening of the skin and difficulty swallowing for 8 months. She reports that her fingers become pale and then blue when exposed to cold. Physical examination reveals shiny, thickened skin of the fingers extending proximal to the metacarpophalangeal joints, decreased oral aperture, scattered facial telangiectasias, and bibasilar inspiratory crackles. Pulmonary function testing demonstrates a restrictive pattern with a decreased diffusing capacity for carbon monoxide, and high-resolution CT of the chest shows bilateral basilar ground-glass opacities and reticular changes. Antinuclear antibody testing is positive. Which of the following additional serologic findings is most likely in this patient?

A. Anti–double-stranded DNA antibodies
B. Anti–topoisomerase I antibodies
C. Anti–cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies
D. Anticentromere antibodies
E. Anti–Jo-1 antibodies

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Anti–topoisomerase I antibodies, also called anti–Scl-70 antibodies, are associated with diffuse cutaneous involvement and an increased risk of interstitial lung disease. This patient has skin thickening extending proximal to the metacarpophalangeal joints, cold-induced digital color changes, esophageal dysfunction, telangiectasias, restrictive pulmonary physiology, and characteristic interstitial abnormalities on high-resolution CT. Anti–double-stranded DNA antibodies are strongly associated with systemic lupus erythematosus, particularly lupus nephritis. Anti–cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies are highly specific for rheumatoid arthritis and are associated with erosive joint disease. Anticentromere antibodies are more commonly associated with limited cutaneous involvement and a later risk of pulmonary arterial hypertension rather than prominent early interstitial lung disease. Anti–Jo-1 antibodies are associated with an inflammatory myopathy and the antisynthetase syndrome, which typically features proximal muscle weakness, elevated muscle enzymes, mechanic's hands, inflammatory arthritis, and interstitial lung disease.

Question 18  [ID: 0725057c8f9a441e85c476ef11a25011]
A 24-year-old man presents to the emergency department 4 hours after his right lower leg was pinned between two vehicles. He reports rapidly worsening pain despite intravenous opioid administration and describes tingling over the dorsum of his foot. Radiographs demonstrate a comminuted midshaft tibial fracture. On examination, the anterior aspect of the lower leg is swollen, firm, and exquisitely tender. Passive plantar flexion of the ankle produces severe pain. Dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses are palpable, and capillary refill is 2 seconds. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis based on this history and physical examination?

A. Acute compartment syndrome
B. Acute arterial occlusion
C. Common fibular nerve injury
D. Deep venous thrombosis
E. Complex regional pain syndrome

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Acute compartment syndrome should be suspected after a high-energy tibial fracture when the patient develops rapidly progressive pain that is out of proportion to the apparent injury, pain with passive stretching of muscles within the affected space, tense swelling, and early sensory symptoms such as paresthesia. Palpable distal pulses do not exclude the diagnosis because arterial flow is generally preserved until late in the course. The diagnosis is primarily clinical, and urgent surgical evaluation is required; pressure measurement can help when the examination is unreliable or equivocal. B is incorrect because acute arterial occlusion more commonly causes a cool, pale extremity with absent pulses, delayed capillary refill, and distal ischemic findings. C is incorrect because an isolated common fibular nerve injury may cause sensory loss over the dorsum of the foot and weakness of ankle dorsiflexion, but it does not explain escalating pain, tense swelling, or severe pain with passive movement. D is incorrect because deep venous thrombosis typically causes swelling, warmth, and discomfort but is unlikely to develop within hours of trauma or produce pain out of proportion and marked pain with passive stretch. E is incorrect because complex regional pain syndrome generally develops weeks to months after an injury and is associated with persistent neuropathic pain, allodynia, edema, and autonomic or trophic changes rather than an acute tense extremity following trauma.

Question 19  [ID: 089db263579244359ec8d72f2ef6e5b2]
A 32-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe left shoulder pain and inability to move his arm after a witnessed generalized tonic-clonic seizure. He has no prior history of shoulder injury. The affected arm is held tightly against the torso and internally rotated. Examination demonstrates posterior fullness of the shoulder, prominence of the coracoid process, and marked restriction of external rotation. Distal pulses, sensation, and motor function are intact. An anteroposterior radiograph shows a rounded, symmetrically appearing humeral head with loss of the normal half-moon overlap between the humeral head and glenoid. An axillary view demonstrates the humeral head positioned posterior to the glenoid. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Anterior shoulder dislocation
B. Posterior shoulder dislocation
C. Acromioclavicular joint separation
D. Proximal humeral fracture
E. Rotator cuff tear

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. Posterior shoulder dislocation classically occurs after a seizure, electrical injury, or high-energy trauma because forceful contraction of the internal rotator muscles drives the humeral head posteriorly. Patients typically hold the arm adducted and internally rotated and have a mechanical block to external rotation. The rounded 'lightbulb' appearance on the anteroposterior view and posterior displacement of the humeral head on the axillary view confirm the diagnosis. Anterior shoulder dislocation usually produces an abducted, externally rotated arm with an anteriorly displaced humeral head. Acromioclavicular separation causes focal superior shoulder pain, tenderness, and a step-off deformity at the acromioclavicular joint without glenohumeral malalignment. A proximal humeral fracture would demonstrate a cortical break or displaced fracture fragments on radiographs and commonly causes substantial swelling and ecchymosis. A rotator cuff tear may cause weakness and impaired active abduction, but it does not produce fixed internal rotation or posterior displacement of the humeral head relative to the glenoid.

Question 20  [ID: 0942b30409f8493eaeb90d697c9fd9a0]
A 13-year-old girl is brought to the clinic after her mother notices that the patient's right shoulder is higher than the left and that her clothing hangs unevenly. She has no back pain, weakness, sensory changes, bowel or bladder dysfunction, or history of trauma. Menarche occurred 6 months ago. Examination shows asymmetric shoulder height and a prominent right posterior rib contour during forward bending. The asymmetry persists with sitting and does not correct with lateral bending. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes are normal. Standing radiographs demonstrate a right-convex thoracic curvature with vertebral rotation measuring 28 degrees by the Cobb method. No congenital vertebral abnormalities are identified. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Adolescent idiopathic scoliosis
B. Postural kyphosis
C. Congenital scoliosis
D. Functional scoliosis due to leg-length discrepancy
E. Scheuermann kyphosis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Adolescent idiopathic scoliosis typically presents in otherwise healthy patients age 10 years or older, most commonly girls, with asymmetric shoulder height, rib prominence on the forward-bend test, and a structural lateral spinal curvature with vertebral rotation. A Cobb angle of at least 10 degrees establishes the radiographic diagnosis. The absence of neurologic abnormalities, congenital vertebral defects, trauma, or another identifiable cause supports the idiopathic classification.

B is incorrect because postural kyphosis produces a flexible increase in the normal posterior thoracic curvature rather than a fixed lateral curvature with vertebral rotation and rib prominence.

C is incorrect because congenital scoliosis results from vertebral formation or segmentation defects, such as hemivertebrae or unsegmented bars, which would be visible on imaging and often present earlier in childhood.

D is incorrect because functional scoliosis is a nonstructural curvature caused by factors such as leg-length discrepancy or muscle spasm. It usually corrects with sitting, lateral bending, or correction of the underlying cause and does not typically produce vertebral rotation or a persistent rib prominence.

E is incorrect because Scheuermann kyphosis causes a rigid sagittal-plane deformity associated with anterior wedging of at least three consecutive vertebral bodies. It does not primarily cause the rotational, lateral thoracic curvature described here.

Question 21  [ID: 0ac0b5635ff7486da7250b82fc52c6ef]
A 46-year-old warehouse worker presents with 3 weeks of sharp lower back pain that began while lifting a heavy box. The pain radiates down the posterior aspect of his left thigh and calf to the lateral foot and worsens with coughing or prolonged sitting. Examination shows decreased sensation over the lateral left foot, diminished left Achilles reflex, and weakness with plantar flexion. Straight-leg raise reproduces his symptoms at 40 degrees. He is afebrile, and complete blood count and inflammatory markers are normal. MRI demonstrates focal left posterolateral displacement of tissue at the L5-S1 level with compression of the traversing nerve root. Which of the following age-related biochemical changes most likely predisposed this patient to the underlying structural abnormality?

A. Increased type I collagen synthesis within the nucleus pulposus, causing excessive tensile strength
B. Loss of proteoglycans with reduced water-binding capacity of the nucleus pulposus
C. Increased hyaluronic acid production causing expansion of the annulus fibrosus
D. Deposition of monosodium urate crystals within the posterior longitudinal ligament
E. Autoantibody-mediated destruction of acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. With aging and repetitive mechanical stress, the nucleus pulposus loses proteoglycans, particularly aggrecan, reducing its ability to bind water and resist compressive forces. The resulting dehydration and loss of elasticity increase stress on the surrounding annulus fibrosus, promoting annular fissures and posterolateral displacement of nuclear material. At L5-S1, this commonly compresses the traversing S1 nerve root, producing posterior leg pain, lateral foot sensory loss, diminished Achilles reflex, and plantar-flexion weakness. A is incorrect because increased type I collagen would not explain reduced hydration or weakening of the central tissue; degeneration instead involves altered collagen composition and loss of proteoglycans. C is incorrect because increased hyaluronic acid and expansion of the annulus are not characteristic degenerative changes. D is incorrect because urate deposition causes gouty inflammation and does not account for this radicular pattern or the MRI finding. E is incorrect because acetylcholine receptor autoantibodies cause fatigable weakness without dermatomal sensory loss, radicular pain, or reduced deep tendon reflexes.

Question 22  [ID: 0b6c249d0c9b408cb52ac8057b963734]
A 42-year-old man presents with 6 weeks of lower back pain that began after lifting a heavy box. The pain radiates from his right buttock down the posterior thigh to the lateral aspect of the foot and worsens with coughing and prolonged sitting. Treatment with activity modification, naproxen, and physical therapy has provided minimal relief. He denies fever, weight loss, urinary retention, fecal incontinence, and perineal numbness. Examination shows reduced right ankle plantar-flexion strength, a diminished right Achilles reflex, and decreased sensation along the lateral foot. Raising the extended right leg reproduces his symptoms at 40 degrees. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are normal. Plain radiographs show no fracture, destructive lesion, or vertebral instability. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic study to identify the structural cause of this patient's persistent symptoms?

A. Computed tomography of the lumbar spine without contrast
B. Magnetic resonance imaging of the lumbar spine without contrast
C. Magnetic resonance imaging of the lumbar spine with and without gadolinium
D. Technetium-99m bone scintigraphy
E. Electromyography and nerve conduction studies

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has persistent radiating pain with objective S1 nerve root findings, including reduced plantar-flexion strength, a diminished Achilles reflex, lateral foot sensory loss, and reproduction of symptoms with straight-leg raising. MRI of the lumbar spine without contrast is the preferred diagnostic study for evaluating suspected disk herniation and nerve root compression when symptoms persist despite conservative therapy or when a neurologic deficit is present. It provides excellent visualization of disks, nerve roots, the spinal canal, and surrounding soft tissues without ionizing radiation.

A is incorrect because CT provides less soft-tissue and nerve root detail than MRI. It is generally reserved for patients who cannot undergo MRI or may be combined with myelography when MRI is contraindicated or nondiagnostic.

C is incorrect because gadolinium is not routinely required for an initial evaluation. Contrast-enhanced MRI is more appropriate when infection, malignancy, inflammatory disease, or recurrent symptoms after prior spinal surgery are suspected.

D is incorrect because bone scintigraphy is useful for detecting metabolically active osseous abnormalities, such as occult fracture, osteomyelitis, or metastatic disease. The patient's normal inflammatory markers, absence of systemic symptoms, and focal neurologic pattern do not suggest these conditions.

E is incorrect because electrodiagnostic testing can help confirm nerve root dysfunction or distinguish it from peripheral neuropathy when the diagnosis is uncertain. It does not directly visualize the structural lesion and is not the preferred initial study when MRI is available and intervention may be considered.

Question 23  [ID: 0be8c6e2006d43c3b25f3d6276c660dc]
An 8-year-old girl is evaluated for 8 weeks of progressive pain and swelling involving both wrists, both knees, and several proximal interphalangeal joints. Her symptoms are most severe in the morning, when she requires approximately 90 minutes before she can move comfortably. Her parents report decreased participation in school activities but deny recent trauma, travel, tick exposure, fever, rash, weight loss, or preceding pharyngitis. Examination shows symmetric joint swelling, warmth, limited range of motion, and a small right knee effusion. There is no hepatosplenomegaly or lymphadenopathy. Laboratory studies show a hemoglobin level of 10.8 g/dL, platelet count of 480,000/mm3, erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 58 mm/hr, negative antinuclear antibody testing, and a positive rheumatoid factor on two tests obtained 3 months apart. Plain radiographs show periarticular soft-tissue swelling without erosions. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Polyarticular juvenile idiopathic arthritis
B. Acute rheumatic fever
C. Systemic lupus erythematosus
D. Lyme disease
E. Acute lymphoblastic leukemia

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Polyarticular juvenile idiopathic arthritis, historically called juvenile rheumatoid arthritis, is characterized by involvement of five or more joints during the first 6 months of illness in a patient younger than 16 years, with symptoms persisting for at least 6 weeks after alternative causes have been excluded. This patient has chronic, symmetric involvement of multiple small and large joints, prolonged morning stiffness, objective swelling, elevated inflammatory markers, anemia, and thrombocytosis. Rheumatoid factor positivity on two occasions at least 3 months apart supports the rheumatoid factor-positive polyarticular subtype, which often resembles adult rheumatoid arthritis and carries a greater risk of erosive disease. Acute rheumatic fever typically causes a transient, migratory process affecting large joints after group A streptococcal infection and may be accompanied by carditis, chorea, subcutaneous nodules, or erythema marginatum. Systemic lupus erythematosus can cause symmetric joint symptoms but would be more likely to feature positive antinuclear antibodies and systemic findings such as photosensitive rash, oral ulcers, nephritis, cytopenias, or serositis. Lyme disease most often causes intermittent or persistent swelling of one or a few large joints, especially the knee, and requires compatible exposure or serologic evidence. Acute lymphoblastic leukemia can present with limb or joint pain, but constitutional symptoms, bruising, lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, and blood count abnormalities such as thrombocytopenia or circulating blasts would be more typical.

Question 24  [ID: 0d99782dfb6948e3ab495eb9e1d6d023]
A 13-year-old boy is evaluated for 6 weeks of progressively worsening left knee pain and a limp. He denies trauma, fever, or recent illness. His BMI is at the 98th percentile for age. Temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F). Examination of the left knee shows no swelling, tenderness, or instability. Examination of the left hip reveals decreased internal rotation, flexion, and abduction. When the hip is flexed, the thigh involuntarily rotates outward. The patient can bear weight with discomfort. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are within normal limits. Anteroposterior and lateral radiographs of the left knee are normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic study to obtain next?

A. Anteroposterior pelvis and frog-leg lateral radiographs of both hips
B. Arthrocentesis of the left knee with synovial fluid analysis
C. MRI of the left knee without contrast
D. Ultrasonography of the left hip to assess for an effusion
E. CT angiography of the pelvis and lower extremities

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This obese adolescent has insidious knee pain, a limp, restricted hip internal rotation, and obligatory external rotation of the thigh during hip flexion. These findings strongly suggest slipped capital femoral epiphysis (SCFE), which frequently presents with referred thigh or knee pain despite a normal knee examination. The appropriate initial diagnostic evaluation in a patient who can bear weight and is therefore clinically stable is anteroposterior pelvis and frog-leg lateral radiography of both hips. Bilateral imaging is recommended because the disorder may be bilateral, even when symptoms are unilateral. Radiographs may demonstrate displacement of the femoral metaphysis relative to the epiphysis, widening or irregularity of the physis, and failure of Klein's line to intersect the femoral epiphysis. B is incorrect because the knee examination, inflammatory markers, and absence of systemic findings do not suggest septic arthritis or another inflammatory knee effusion. C is incorrect because the pain is referred from the hip, and further imaging of a normal-appearing knee would delay diagnosis. D is incorrect because ultrasonography may identify a hip effusion but does not adequately characterize physeal displacement and is not the preferred confirmatory study. E is incorrect because there are no findings suggesting vascular occlusion or traumatic vascular injury. If the patient were unable to bear weight, a cross-table lateral view rather than a frog-leg lateral view would be preferred to avoid worsening an unstable displacement.

Question 25  [ID: 0f838ffa8db044d693e99d424badce9a]
A 72-year-old woman presents with 6 weeks of symmetric aching and stiffness involving both shoulders, her neck, and both hips. The stiffness lasts approximately 90 minutes each morning and makes it difficult for her to dress and rise from a chair. She reports fatigue and decreased appetite but denies headache, jaw pain with chewing, vision changes, rash, or recent infection. Examination shows painful limitation of active shoulder and hip movement, but passive range of motion is largely preserved. Strength is 5/5 when pain is overcome, and there is no joint swelling or muscle tenderness. Laboratory studies show hemoglobin of 10.8 g/dL with a normal mean corpuscular volume, normal thyroid-stimulating hormone, and normal hepatic and renal function. Which additional laboratory finding would most strongly support the most likely explanation for this patient's symptoms?

A. Elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein with a normal creatine kinase level
B. Markedly elevated creatine kinase and aldolase levels with myoglobinuria
C. High-titer anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies with an elevated rheumatoid factor
D. Positive antinuclear antibodies with low serum complement levels
E. Positive HLA-B27 testing with a normal erythrocyte sedimentation rate

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is most consistent with polymyalgia rheumatica, which typically affects adults older than 50 years and causes bilateral shoulder and hip girdle pain with prolonged morning stiffness. Patients may have systemic symptoms and mild normocytic anemia. ESR and CRP are usually elevated, reflecting systemic inflammation, whereas creatine kinase remains normal because this condition does not cause primary muscle fiber injury. Choice B is incorrect because markedly elevated muscle enzymes and myoglobinuria suggest an inflammatory or necrotizing myopathy, which typically produces objective proximal muscle weakness rather than predominantly pain and stiffness. Choice C is incorrect because anti-CCP antibodies and rheumatoid factor support rheumatoid arthritis, which more commonly causes persistent synovitis, particularly in the small joints of the hands and feet. Choice D is incorrect because positive antinuclear antibodies and hypocomplementemia suggest a systemic autoimmune connective tissue disorder, such as systemic lupus erythematosus, which would generally have additional clinical manifestations. Choice E is incorrect because HLA-B27 is associated with axial spondyloarthritis and related conditions, which usually begin in younger patients and are not characterized by this laboratory profile. The absence of headache, jaw claudication, and visual symptoms lowers concern for concurrent giant cell arteritis, although patients should continue to be monitored for these manifestations.

Question 26  [ID: 0fb2435461be46c8828aba8d50a97ffb]
A 22-year-old man presents with severe right lower-leg pain after colliding with another player during a soccer game. Radiographs show a closed, displaced midshaft tibial fracture. The injury is reduced, and a well-padded long-leg splint is applied. On repeat examination, his toes are warm, capillary refill is less than 2 seconds, and dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses are palpable. Sensation and active toe movement are intact. He is being discharged with orthopedic follow-up. Which of the following patient instructions is most important for preventing permanent functional impairment from an evolving postinjury complication?

A. Return immediately for rapidly worsening pain despite medication, pain when the toes are passively moved, or new numbness or weakness
B. Expect increasing pain and numbness during the first 48 hours, as these are normal consequences of swelling
C. Apply a heating pad over the splint for 20 minutes every 2 hours to improve blood flow
D. Remove the splint at home if swelling develops and reapply it tightly after the swelling subsides
E. Wait until the scheduled orthopedic appointment unless the toes become pale and pedal pulses are absent

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. A tibial shaft fracture places the patient at substantial risk for acute compartment syndrome. The most important preventive education is early recognition of escalating pain out of proportion to the injury, pain with passive stretching of the involved muscles, paresthesia, and weakness. These findings require immediate emergency evaluation and, when confirmed, urgent fasciotomy to prevent irreversible muscle necrosis, nerve injury, contracture, renal complications, and limb loss. Normal pulses and capillary refill do not exclude an evolving compartment syndrome because arterial flow may persist until late in the course.

B is incorrect because worsening pain and numbness should not be normalized after splint application; they may indicate increasing intracompartmental pressure and require urgent reassessment.

C is incorrect because heat can increase swelling and may cause a thermal injury, particularly when applied over a splint where skin temperature cannot be adequately monitored.

D is incorrect because patients should not independently remove and tightly reapply immobilization devices. A constrictive dressing or splint may worsen tissue pressure; concerning swelling or symptoms require professional evaluation.

E is incorrect because pallor and absent pulses are late findings. Waiting for these signs can delay treatment until irreversible tissue damage has occurred.

Question 27  [ID: 1036d165171a4acb8d408b6ce152a7c3]
A 13-year-old boy presents with 3 months of gradually worsening pain at the front of his right knee. He plays competitive basketball and recently increased the frequency of practices. The pain is most pronounced during running, jumping, squatting, and climbing stairs and improves with rest. He denies fever, nighttime pain, acute trauma, locking, or giving way. Examination shows a mildly antalgic gait and a prominent area just below the patella without erythema or warmth. Knee range of motion is intact, and there is no joint effusion or ligamentous instability. Plain radiographs show mild irregularity and fragmentation at the site where the patellar tendon attaches to the proximal tibia. Which additional examination finding would most strongly support the leading clinical impression?

A. Focal tenderness over the tibial tubercle with pain reproduced by resisted knee extension
B. Tenderness along the medial joint line with pain during rotational loading of the flexed knee
C. Increased anterior translation of the tibia with the knee flexed to 20–30 degrees
D. Pain and a palpable click during compression of the patella against the femoral groove
E. Tenderness at the inferior pole of the patella without tenderness over the proximal tibia

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is characteristic of Osgood-Schlatter disease, a traction apophysitis involving the tibial tubercle in physically active adolescents during periods of rapid growth. Repetitive force transmitted through the quadriceps and patellar tendon produces focal pain, swelling, and prominence at the tibial tubercle. Symptoms are typically reproduced by resisted knee extension, jumping, running, or squatting. Radiographs may show fragmentation or irregularity of the tibial tubercle apophysis.

A is correct because focal tibial tubercle tenderness and pain with resisted knee extension are the most characteristic examination findings.

B is incorrect because medial joint-line tenderness and pain with rotational loading suggest a medial meniscal injury, which more commonly follows trauma and may cause locking or catching.

C is incorrect because increased anterior tibial translation suggests anterior cruciate ligament insufficiency, usually associated with an acute twisting injury, instability, and often an effusion.

D is incorrect because pain or crepitus with patellar compression is more consistent with patellofemoral pain or chondral irritation rather than a traction injury at the tibial tubercle.

E is incorrect because isolated tenderness at the inferior patellar pole is more consistent with Sinding-Larsen-Johansson syndrome, which involves traction-related irritation at the inferior pole of the patella rather than at the tibial tubercle.

Question 28  [ID: 1320a81801b84212847c62f44eddb241]
A 24-year-old man presents with 5 days of pain and swelling in the right knee and left ankle. Two weeks ago, he developed dysuria and scant urethral discharge after having unprotected intercourse with a new partner. He also reports redness and irritation of both eyes. Examination shows bilateral conjunctival injection, a small painless ulcer on the glans penis, and warmth with limited range of motion of the affected joints. Nucleic acid amplification testing of a first-catch urine specimen is positive for Chlamydia trachomatis. Synovial fluid analysis shows 18,000 leukocytes/mm3 with negative Gram stain and culture. In addition to treating the patient's current symptoms and infection, which of the following counseling recommendations is most appropriate to reduce the risk of reinfection and future episodes?

A. Recommend HLA-B27 screening for all first-degree relatives
B. Continue suppressive doxycycline therapy indefinitely after completing the initial regimen
C. Ensure recent sexual partners are evaluated and treated, abstain from intercourse until treatment is completed by all parties, and recommend consistent condom use
D. Avoid weight-bearing exercise permanently to prevent recurrence of joint symptoms
E. Begin daily prophylactic nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory therapy after symptoms resolve

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The presentation is consistent with reactive arthritis triggered by Chlamydia trachomatis infection. Prevention focuses on reducing the risk of recurrent or new sexually transmitted infection. Recent sexual partners should be notified, evaluated, and presumptively treated as appropriate. The patient should abstain from sexual activity until the recommended treatment period has been completed, symptoms have resolved, and partners have been treated. Consistent condom use and repeat chlamydia testing approximately 3 months after treatment should also be encouraged. A is incorrect because HLA-B27 may be associated with more severe or persistent disease, but routine screening of relatives does not prevent illness. B is incorrect because indefinite suppressive antibiotics are not routinely recommended; antibiotics treat an active triggering infection but do not reliably eliminate established joint manifestations. D is incorrect because permanent activity restriction is unnecessary and may worsen deconditioning; graded activity and physical therapy are generally beneficial as acute symptoms improve. E is incorrect because nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs are used to treat active symptoms, not as prophylaxis after resolution.

Question 29  [ID: 13b9831324fe4198af046dd8ae247d7f]
A 62-year-old man with poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus and peripheral neuropathy presents with 6 weeks of worsening drainage from an ulcer over his right heel. His temperature is 37.8°C (100.0°F), blood pressure is 132/78 mm Hg, and pulse is 88/min. Examination shows a 2.5-cm plantar heel ulcer with surrounding erythema; a sterile metal probe passed through the ulcer contacts the calcaneus. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 12,400/mm3, erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 92 mm/hr, and C-reactive protein level of 11 mg/dL. MRI demonstrates confluent marrow signal abnormality and cortical disruption of the calcaneus. The patient undergoes surgical debridement, and an intraoperative specimen grows methicillin-sensitive Staphylococcus aureus. Blood cultures are negative, and he has no medication allergies. Which of the following is the most appropriate definitive antimicrobial therapy?

A. Intravenous cefazolin for approximately 6 weeks
B. Intravenous vancomycin for approximately 6 weeks
C. Oral azithromycin for 5 days
D. Intravenous piperacillin-tazobactam for 48 hours
E. Oral nitrofurantoin for approximately 6 weeks

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The ulcer that probes to the calcaneus, markedly elevated inflammatory markers, characteristic MRI findings, and positive intraoperative culture establish osteomyelitis caused by methicillin-sensitive Staphylococcus aureus. After culture results are available, therapy should be narrowed to an antistaphylococcal beta-lactam. Cefazolin has reliable activity against methicillin-sensitive S aureus and favorable tissue penetration and is typically administered for approximately 6 weeks when infected bone remains after debridement. Vancomycin is less effective than beta-lactams for methicillin-sensitive S aureus and should generally be reserved for methicillin-resistant isolates or severe beta-lactam allergy. Azithromycin does not provide dependable therapy for this deep-seated infection, and a 5-day course is inadequate. Piperacillin-tazobactam may be reasonable as broad empiric therapy for a severe polymicrobial diabetic foot infection, but it should be narrowed once cultures identify a susceptible organism; 48 hours is also insufficient. Nitrofurantoin achieves therapeutic concentrations primarily in the urine and is inappropriate for this infection.

Question 30  [ID: 1593be42144848149a60727da706d76d]
A 16-year-old male competitive gymnast presents with 3 weeks of gradually worsening low back pain. The pain began after increasing the intensity of his training and is exacerbated by back extension, tumbling, and prolonged standing. He denies pain radiating into the legs, weakness, paresthesias, bowel or bladder dysfunction, fever, and recent trauma. Examination shows focal tenderness over the lower lumbar region and pain reproduced with single-leg hyperextension. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, reflexes, and straight-leg raising are normal. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein are within normal limits. Lumbar radiographs demonstrate a linear lucency through the pars interarticularis of L5 without vertebral displacement. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial intervention?

A. Immediate posterior lumbar fusion
B. Activity restriction with physical therapy emphasizing core stabilization and hamstring flexibility
C. Lumbar epidural glucocorticoid injection
D. Strict bed rest for 6 weeks
E. Immediate return to training while taking opioid analgesics before activity

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The imaging finding of a pars interarticularis defect without vertebral displacement, together with extension-related pain in an adolescent athlete, supports the target condition. Initial treatment is conservative and includes temporary cessation of pain-provoking activities, particularly repetitive lumbar extension, followed by physical therapy focused on core stabilization, controlled flexibility, and gradual return to activity. NSAIDs or acetaminophen may be used for short-term pain control, and bracing can be considered if symptoms persist despite initial conservative measures. A is incorrect because surgical fusion is reserved for persistent, disabling symptoms despite prolonged nonoperative management or for selected cases with instability or progression. C is incorrect because epidural glucocorticoid injections are generally used for radicular pain from nerve-root inflammation; this patient has no radicular symptoms or neurologic deficits. D is incorrect because prolonged strict bed rest can cause deconditioning and is not recommended; relative activity restriction with rehabilitation is preferred. E is incorrect because continued participation in pain-provoking activity may delay healing or worsen the defect, and opioids are not appropriate first-line treatment for this presentation.

Question 31  [ID: 160a782b89754c97ad3f4356d2f03f0e]
A 29-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a high-speed motorcycle collision. He reports severe pain and inability to bear weight on his right leg. Examination reveals a 12-cm laceration over the anteromedial lower leg with extensive soft-tissue damage, gross contamination, and exposed bone. The extremity is visibly deformed, but dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses are palpable. Sensation is intact distally. Radiographs demonstrate marked cortical disruption and comminution of the midshaft tibia with an associated fibular injury. His serum creatinine is 0.9 mg/dL, and he has no medication allergies. In addition to urgent irrigation, debridement, stabilization, and tetanus prophylaxis, which of the following is the most appropriate initial antimicrobial regimen?

A. Intravenous cefazolin alone
B. Intravenous cefazolin plus gentamicin
C. Oral amoxicillin-clavulanate
D. Intravenous vancomycin alone
E. Oral ciprofloxacin

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The extensive wound, exposed bone, gross contamination, comminution, and high-energy mechanism indicate a severe Gustilo type III open tibial shaft fracture. Antimicrobial prophylaxis should be administered as soon as possible. Traditional PANCE-oriented management includes a first-generation cephalosporin, such as cefazolin, for gram-positive coverage plus an aminoglycoside, such as gentamicin, for additional gram-negative coverage in type III injuries. Cefazolin alone is generally used for less severe type I or II open injuries and does not provide the broader coverage recommended here. Oral amoxicillin-clavulanate is not appropriate initial therapy because immediate parenteral antibiotics are required. Vancomycin alone lacks adequate gram-negative coverage and is generally reserved for significant beta-lactam allergy or specific concern for methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus. Oral ciprofloxacin alone does not provide reliable gram-positive coverage and is not preferred as monotherapy. If the wound had farm, fecal, or heavy soil contamination, additional anaerobic coverage, historically with penicillin, would be indicated.
