Question 32  [ID: 164a06aec79342fb96c378b635c9fc67]
A 15-year-old boy presents with progressive shortness of breath and reduced exercise tolerance during soccer practice. His parents report that an inward depression of his anterior thorax has become more prominent during his recent growth spurt. He denies cough, wheezing, fever, or nocturnal symptoms. Examination shows a symmetric, deep depression of the lower sternum that becomes more pronounced with inspiration. Cardiac auscultation reveals a mild systolic ejection murmur; breath sounds are clear. Pulmonary function testing demonstrates a mild restrictive pattern. Echocardiography shows external compression of the right ventricle without an intrinsic structural abnormality. Computed tomography demonstrates a transverse thoracic diameter of 25 cm and an anteroposterior diameter of 6 cm at the point of maximal depression. Which of the following is the most appropriate intervention for this patient?

A. Observation with annual clinical follow-up
B. External bracing worn for 12 to 18 months
C. Minimally invasive placement of a substernal corrective bar
D. Inhaled short-acting beta-2 agonist therapy before exercise
E. Needle aspiration of the depressed area followed by compression dressing

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The correct answer is C. The calculated Haller index is approximately 4.2 (25 cm divided by 6 cm), indicating a severe deformity; values greater than 3.25 generally support consideration of operative correction. This adolescent also has objective physiologic compromise, including reduced exercise tolerance, a restrictive pulmonary pattern, and right ventricular compression. Minimally invasive placement of a substernal corrective bar, commonly performed using the Nuss technique, is an appropriate intervention for a symptomatic adolescent with a severe, relatively symmetric deformity. The bar elevates and remodels the depressed sternum and is typically removed after several years. A is incorrect because observation is appropriate for mild, asymptomatic cases without cardiopulmonary compromise. B is incorrect because external bracing is primarily used for an outwardly protruding, flexible anterior thoracic deformity and is not effective for a deep inward depression. D is incorrect because there is no clinical or pulmonary function evidence of exercise-induced bronchoconstriction, and bronchodilator therapy would not relieve the mechanical cardiopulmonary compression. E is incorrect because there is no fluid collection to aspirate, and this intervention has no role in correcting a structural thoracic deformity.

Question 33  [ID: 1788cceecdb14cbab5cbbf5ff5131c04]
A 16-year-old boy presents for follow-up 18 months after completing treatment for a rapidly enlarging, painful mass near the left knee. Initial examination showed localized swelling, tenderness, and decreased range of motion. Radiographs demonstrated a mixed lytic and sclerotic lesion of the distal femoral metaphysis with periosteal elevation and radiating mineralization. Pathologic examination revealed pleomorphic cells producing irregular osteoid. He underwent multiagent chemotherapy followed by limb-sparing resection with negative margins. Chest imaging at diagnosis showed no abnormalities. He currently feels well, has a normal physical examination, and has normal alkaline phosphatase, lactate dehydrogenase, renal function, and hepatic function tests. Which of the following is the most appropriate education regarding surveillance during the first several years after treatment?

A. He should undergo regular clinical evaluation with imaging of the treated site and periodic chest imaging because asymptomatic recurrence may occur locally or in the lungs.
B. No scheduled imaging is needed after complete resection with negative margins unless new symptoms develop.
C. Monthly whole-body positron emission tomography is required indefinitely to identify recurrence at the earliest possible stage.
D. Routine brain magnetic resonance imaging should be performed every 3 months because the brain is the most common site of distant spread.
E. Annual serum alkaline phosphatase measurement alone provides adequate surveillance when the initial chest imaging is normal.

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical presentation is characteristic of osteosarcoma. During the first several years after treatment, patients require scheduled follow-up that includes a history and physical examination, imaging of the primary site, and chest imaging. The lungs are the most common site of distant metastasis, and pulmonary recurrence may initially be asymptomatic. Detecting limited pulmonary disease can be clinically important because surgical resection may be possible. Local imaging is also necessary to evaluate for recurrence at the treated site. Option B is incorrect because negative surgical margins do not eliminate the risk of local or distant recurrence. Option C is incorrect because routine monthly whole-body PET is not recommended and would cause excessive radiation exposure, cost, and false-positive findings. Option D is incorrect because the brain is not the most common site of distant spread, so routine brain MRI is not indicated without neurologic symptoms. Option E is incorrect because alkaline phosphatase lacks sufficient sensitivity and specificity to serve as the sole surveillance method; normal laboratory values do not exclude recurrence.

Question 34  [ID: 1793b5b45dc847979e7854b54218be14]
A 19-year-old man presents after being tackled while playing football. He reports severe pain and inability to move his right arm. Examination shows loss of the normal rounded contour near the proximal arm, and the patient holds the arm slightly abducted and externally rotated. Distal pulses, sensation, and motor function are intact. Radiographs demonstrate the humeral head positioned anteroinferior to the glenoid without associated bony injury. After procedural sedation, the joint surfaces are restored to normal alignment, and repeat neurovascular examination is normal. Following a brief period of immobilization, which of the following recommendations is most appropriate to reduce this patient's risk of recurrence?

A. Begin supervised rehabilitation emphasizing rotator cuff and scapular stabilizer strengthening
B. Maintain immobilization continuously for 8 weeks before beginning range-of-motion exercises
C. Resume contact sports as soon as pain resolves, without additional rehabilitation
D. Perform repetitive overhead stretching to increase external rotation during the first week
E. Avoid all future upper-body exercise and athletic participation

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical presentation and radiographic findings indicate an anterior shoulder dislocation. Young male athletes have a particularly high risk of recurrent instability. After reduction and a short period of immobilization, supervised rehabilitation should restore range of motion and strengthen the rotator cuff, deltoid, and scapular stabilizers. These muscles provide dynamic stabilization and help reduce recurrence risk. Patients should delay return to contact sports until they have painless full range of motion, near-normal strength, and no evidence of instability. Choice B is incorrect because prolonged immobilization increases stiffness, muscle atrophy, and functional impairment without reliably preventing recurrence. Choice C is incorrect because returning to contact sports without rehabilitation increases the risk of recurrent instability and additional structural damage. Choice D is incorrect because early forced abduction, external rotation, or overhead stretching can stress healing anterior capsulolabral structures. Choice E is incorrect because permanent avoidance of exercise is unnecessary; a gradual, criterion-based return to activity is appropriate after rehabilitation.

Question 35  [ID: 1933121715394d6f8817ab3fd22b01f1]
A 29-year-old woman presents with 3 months of aching pain in the right shoulder and intermittent numbness involving the medial forearm and fourth and fifth fingers. Her symptoms worsen when she stocks overhead shelves at work and improve when she lowers her arm. She denies trauma, fever, limb swelling, skin discoloration, or persistent weakness. Examination shows full shoulder range of motion and normal grip strength. Holding both arms abducted and externally rotated for 3 minutes reproduces her pain and paresthesias. Radial pulses are symmetric, and there is no edema or cyanosis. Complete blood count and metabolic panel are normal. Cervical spine radiographs show no acute abnormality, and duplex ultrasonography demonstrates normal arterial and venous flow without thrombosis. She is referred for posture modification and a supervised stretching and strengthening program. Which of the following medications is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic adjunct to her treatment?

A. Naproxen
B. Apixaban
C. Alteplase
D. Prednisone
E. Oxycodone

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has findings consistent with neurogenic thoracic outlet syndrome: activity-related shoulder and upper-extremity pain, paresthesias in a lower brachial plexus distribution, symptom reproduction with sustained arm elevation, and no evidence of arterial or venous obstruction. Initial treatment is conservative and includes activity modification, postural correction, and physical therapy. A nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as naproxen is an appropriate initial pharmacologic adjunct for pain and local inflammation. Apixaban is used when venous thrombosis is present, which is excluded by the normal duplex study and absence of swelling or cyanosis. Alteplase may be considered for selected patients with acute, severe thrombotic venous obstruction but is inappropriate without thrombosis. Systemic prednisone is not routinely recommended because its risks outweigh its limited benefit in this chronic mechanical presentation. Oxycodone is not appropriate first-line therapy for nonmalignant, nonacute pain when safer nonopioid options are available.

Question 36  [ID: 19353046f3bd42799ae81a3c28e99d07]
A 15-year-old boy is evaluated for decreased exercise tolerance and intermittent shortness of breath during soccer practice. His parents report that the center of his anterior thorax has appeared increasingly sunken since his recent growth spurt. He denies trauma, fever, cough, or pain. Vital signs are normal. Examination shows a symmetric posterior depression of the lower sternum, flaring of the inferior costal margins, rounded shoulders, and mild thoracic scoliosis. The point of maximal impulse is displaced slightly to the left. Complete blood count and metabolic studies are normal. A chest radiograph demonstrates posterior displacement of the lower sternum and mild leftward displacement of the cardiac silhouette without pulmonary infiltrates. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Pectus excavatum
B. Pectus carinatum
C. Poland syndrome
D. Scheuermann kyphosis
E. Flail chest

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Pectus excavatum is characterized by posterior depression of the sternum and adjacent costal cartilages, often becoming more prominent during adolescent growth. Associated findings include flared costal margins, rounded shoulders, scoliosis, leftward cardiac displacement, and exercise intolerance in more pronounced cases due to reduced cardiopulmonary reserve or cardiac compression. Pectus carinatum causes anterior protrusion rather than depression of the sternum. Poland syndrome typically presents with unilateral absence or hypoplasia of the pectoralis major muscle and may include ipsilateral upper-extremity abnormalities. Scheuermann kyphosis produces rigid excessive thoracic curvature rather than a focal sternal depression. Flail chest occurs after significant trauma and is characterized by paradoxical movement of a segment of the thoracic wall due to multiple contiguous rib fractures.

Question 37  [ID: 19ef7be101444c6cb8f4d9f2b68e8ee7]
A 58-year-old man with poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus and peripheral neuropathy presents with 3 weeks of drainage from an ulcer beneath the right first metatarsal head. He does not recall an injury and reports minimal pain. Examination shows a 1.5-cm plantar ulcer that probes deeply, surrounding erythema, diminished monofilament sensation, and palpable pedal pulses. His temperature is 37.8°C (100.0°F), leukocyte count is 12,100/mm3, erythrocyte sedimentation rate is 78 mm/hr, and hemoglobin A1c is 9.4%. MRI demonstrates marrow edema, cortical disruption, and an adjacent fluid collection. He undergoes surgical debridement and completes culture-directed antimicrobial therapy. At a follow-up visit, which of the following patient education recommendations is most important for reducing the risk of recurrence?

A. Inspect both feet daily, use properly fitted pressure-offloading footwear, and seek prompt care for any skin breakdown
B. Soak the feet in warm water each evening to soften calluses and prevent ulcer formation
C. Walk barefoot while indoors to minimize friction from shoes
D. Take prophylactic oral antibiotics whenever blood glucose exceeds 200 mg/dL
E. Apply a heating pad to areas of decreased sensation to improve local circulation

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has diabetes-associated osteomyelitis arising from a neuropathic plantar ulcer. Recurrence prevention requires meticulous daily foot inspection, pressure redistribution with properly fitted or custom offloading footwear, prompt evaluation of new skin breakdown, regular podiatric care, and improved glycemic control. Peripheral neuropathy may prevent the patient from noticing repetitive trauma, so early visual detection is essential. Choice B is incorrect because soaking can cause skin maceration and increase the risk of breakdown; patients should instead wash briefly and dry carefully, especially between the toes. Choice C is incorrect because walking barefoot increases exposure to unnoticed puncture wounds and pressure-related injury. Choice D is incorrect because episodic hyperglycemia is not an indication for prophylactic antibiotics; unnecessary antimicrobial use promotes adverse effects and resistance. Choice E is incorrect because decreased sensation increases the risk of unrecognized thermal burns from heating pads, which can create new portals of entry.

Question 38  [ID: 1c3d210ee7464f4c8580548e9c496a4d]
A 24-year-old woman presents with pain and swelling after her right foot rolled inward while she was playing volleyball 6 hours ago. She was able to walk immediately afterward and can currently bear weight with a limp. Examination shows mild swelling and tenderness anterior and inferior to the lateral malleolus. There is no tenderness over the posterior edge of either malleolus, the base of the fifth metatarsal, or the navicular. Active range of motion is limited by pain, but strength, sensation, and distal pulses are intact. The skin is intact. Plain radiographs show no acute bony abnormality. She has no chronic medical conditions and takes no medications. Which of the following is the best initial pharmacologic therapy for this patient's pain?

A. Topical diclofenac gel
B. Oral prednisone
C. Oral oxycodone
D. Intra-articular triamcinolone injection
E. Oral colchicine

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Topical diclofenac gel is the best initial pharmacologic therapy for acute, localized pain from a lateral ankle sprain. Topical nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs provide effective short-term analgesia with less systemic exposure and fewer gastrointestinal, renal, and cardiovascular adverse effects than oral NSAIDs. Pharmacologic treatment should be combined with protection, early weight bearing as tolerated, compression, elevation, and progressive range-of-motion exercises. Oral prednisone is not routinely indicated and may impair tissue healing while causing systemic adverse effects. Oxycodone is inappropriate because opioids offer no important advantage over nonopioid therapy for this uncomplicated injury and introduce risks of sedation, constipation, misuse, and dependence. An intra-articular corticosteroid injection is not indicated for an acute ligamentous injury and may weaken local tissue. Colchicine is used primarily for crystal-induced inflammation and has no role in this traumatic presentation.

Question 39  [ID: 1c75077c23e642849d951c46c64fef1b]
A 13-year-old boy presents with 3 months of gradually worsening pain just below the right knee. He recently began participating in competitive basketball, and the pain is most pronounced during running, jumping, and climbing stairs. He denies acute trauma, fever, morning stiffness, or pain at rest. Examination reveals a prominent, tender tibial tubercle with discomfort reproduced by resisted knee extension. There is no joint effusion, instability, or limitation of passive range of motion. Plain radiographs demonstrate mild fragmentation and soft-tissue swelling at the tibial tubercle without an acute fracture. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's findings?

A. Repetitive traction by the patellar tendon on an incompletely ossified tibial tubercle apophysis
B. Avascular necrosis of subchondral bone involving the lateral femoral condyle
C. Autoimmune destruction of articular cartilage mediated by circulating immune complexes
D. Acute rupture of the patellar tendon due to eccentric overload
E. Hematogenous bacterial seeding of the proximal tibial metaphysis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. This adolescent has activity-related pain and focal tenderness over the tibial tubercle, with symptoms reproduced by resisted knee extension and radiographic fragmentation at the tubercle. These findings are characteristic of Osgood-Schlatter disease, a traction apophysitis caused by repetitive contraction of the quadriceps transmitting force through the patellar tendon to the incompletely ossified tibial tubercle apophysis. It commonly occurs during growth spurts in adolescents participating in running and jumping sports. B is incorrect because osteochondritis dissecans involves focal injury and possible avascular necrosis of subchondral bone, typically at the lateral aspect of the medial femoral condyle, and may cause swelling, catching, or locking. C is incorrect because autoimmune articular disease typically causes joint swelling, prolonged morning stiffness, and potentially systemic manifestations rather than isolated tibial tubercle tenderness. D is incorrect because patellar tendon rupture usually follows an acute injury and causes impaired active knee extension, a palpable tendon defect, and patella alta. E is incorrect because osteomyelitis generally presents with more persistent pain and may be accompanied by fever, elevated inflammatory markers, and metaphyseal abnormalities rather than activity-dependent apophyseal pain.

Question 40  [ID: 1d41da435db9496098156cbc439f45bd]
A 10-year-old boy is evaluated for 3 weeks of progressive left knee and groin pain without preceding trauma. His parents also report fatigue, constipation, weight gain, and declining school performance. He is at the 8th percentile for height and the 92nd percentile for weight. Examination shows an antalgic gait and obligatory external rotation of the left leg during hip flexion, with limited internal rotation. Radiographs demonstrate widening and irregularity of the proximal femoral growth plate, and a line drawn along the superior femoral neck does not intersect the femoral head. Laboratory testing shows a thyroid-stimulating hormone level of 48 mIU/L and a free thyroxine level of 0.3 ng/dL. In addition to immediate non–weight-bearing status and urgent operative management, which of the following pharmacologic therapies should be initiated?

A. Levothyroxine
B. Methimazole
C. Somatropin
D. Calcitriol
E. Prednisone

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical presentation and radiographic findings are consistent with slipped capital femoral epiphysis (SCFE). The patient's young age, short stature, fatigue, constipation, weight gain, markedly elevated thyroid-stimulating hormone level, and low free thyroxine level indicate primary hypothyroidism, an important endocrine risk factor for SCFE. Levothyroxine should be initiated to replace deficient thyroid hormone while the patient undergoes urgent orthopedic treatment, typically in situ screw fixation. Pharmacotherapy does not replace operative stabilization. Methimazole inhibits thyroid hormone synthesis and is used for hyperthyroidism, so it would worsen this patient's deficiency. Somatropin is used for selected causes of growth hormone deficiency but is not appropriate before correcting hypothyroidism and may increase the risk of epiphyseal complications. Calcitriol is used for disorders such as hypocalcemia, hypoparathyroidism, or chronic kidney disease and is not indicated by the laboratory findings. Prednisone has no therapeutic role in this condition and may adversely affect bone health with prolonged use.

Question 41  [ID: 1ee1ef7a2b96420f8b34e24f011beaeb]
A 72-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe neck pain and weakness after falling forward and striking his forehead on a countertop. He did not lose consciousness. His medical history is significant for hypertension and longstanding degenerative joint disease. On examination, he is alert and hemodynamically stable. There is midline tenderness in the lower cervical region without a palpable step-off. Motor strength is 2/5 in both hands, 3/5 at the elbows, and 4/5 in both lower extremities. Sensation to pinprick is diminished in the upper extremities but relatively preserved in the lower extremities and perianal region. Rectal tone is intact. CT demonstrates multilevel degenerative narrowing without acute fracture or dislocation. MRI reveals central T2 hyperintensity from C4 through C6 with surrounding degenerative changes. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Anterior cord syndrome
B. Brown-Séquard syndrome
C. Central cord syndrome
D. Conus medullaris syndrome
E. Cauda equina syndrome

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: Central cord syndrome is characterized by disproportionately greater motor impairment in the upper extremities than in the lower extremities, often with variable sensory loss and relative sacral sparing. It commonly occurs in older adults with preexisting cervical degenerative narrowing after a hyperextension injury, even when CT shows no acute fracture or dislocation. The MRI finding of central intramedullary T2 hyperintensity supports this diagnosis. Anterior cord syndrome causes bilateral motor paralysis and loss of pain and temperature sensation below the lesion, with preservation of vibration and proprioception; it does not typically produce upper-extremity-predominant weakness. Brown-Séquard syndrome results from hemisection and causes ipsilateral motor and proprioceptive loss with contralateral pain and temperature loss. Conus medullaris syndrome typically causes early bowel and bladder dysfunction, saddle anesthesia, and relatively symmetric lower-extremity findings. Cauda equina syndrome produces lower motor neuron weakness, asymmetric radicular symptoms, saddle anesthesia, and bowel or bladder dysfunction rather than upper-extremity-predominant deficits.

Question 42  [ID: 1ee435fe4565498ba9fb4549ab125965]
A 16-year-old boy presents with a painless, slowly enlarging lump near his right knee that he first noticed 2 years ago. He denies trauma, fever, night sweats, weight loss, or nocturnal pain. Examination shows a hard, immobile prominence over the distal medial thigh without warmth or tenderness. The knee has full range of motion, and distal neurovascular findings are normal. Complete blood count, serum calcium, alkaline phosphatase, and inflammatory markers are within normal limits. Plain radiographs demonstrate a pedunculated projection arising from the metaphysis of the distal femur and extending away from the adjacent joint. The cortex and medullary cavity of the projection are continuous with those of the femur. Which of the following best describes the biologic origin of this finding?

A. Displacement of growth plate cartilage that undergoes endochondral ossification
B. Proliferation of osteoblasts producing disorganized woven bone within a fibrovascular stroma
C. Somatic activation of GNAS causing replacement of normal tissue by fibro-osseous matrix
D. Malignant transformation of primitive mesenchymal cells producing osteoid
E. Accumulation of histiocytes containing grooved nuclei and eosinophilic cytoplasmic granules

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The radiographic finding of a metaphyseal, pedunculated projection with cortical and medullary continuity with the parent structure is characteristic of an osteochondroma. It develops from displaced or herniated growth plate cartilage. The cartilage cap proliferates and undergoes endochondral ossification, causing the projection to enlarge while the patient is growing. Growth typically stops at skeletal maturity. Choice B describes the histologic mechanism of osteoid osteoma or osteoblastoma, which typically appears as a radiolucent nidus surrounded by reactive sclerosis rather than a metaphyseal projection with medullary continuity. Choice C describes fibrous dysplasia, in which activating GNAS mutations produce fibro-osseous replacement and a ground-glass radiographic appearance. Choice D describes osteosarcoma, an aggressive malignant process associated with malignant osteoid production, pain, cortical destruction, and often a soft-tissue mass. Choice E describes Langerhans cell histiocytosis, which commonly produces lytic defects and is associated histologically with grooved histiocytes and Birbeck granules.

Question 43  [ID: 1ee6364250924b27807745a2b7a9413f]
A 34-year-old man presents with severe left shoulder pain and inability to move his arm after a witnessed generalized tonic-clonic seizure. He did not fall or sustain direct trauma. Examination shows that the left arm is held adducted and internally rotated. The anterior shoulder contour appears flattened, and attempts at passive external rotation cause marked pain and resistance. Distal pulses, sensation, and motor function are intact. An anteroposterior radiograph demonstrates a rounded, symmetric appearance of the humeral head with loss of the normal overlap between the humeral head and glenoid. Which of the following additional radiographic projections would best confirm the suspected alignment abnormality?

A. Axillary lateral view
B. Anteroposterior view with internal rotation
C. Clavicular serendipity view
D. Contralateral anteroposterior comparison view
E. Dedicated acromioclavicular joint view with weights

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. This presentation is classic for a posterior shoulder dislocation, which may occur after a seizure because powerful involuntary contractions of the internal rotators overpower the external rotators. The arm is typically held adducted and internally rotated, with an inability to externally rotate. On an anteroposterior radiograph, the internally rotated humeral head may have a rounded 'lightbulb' appearance, but this projection can miss the diagnosis. An axillary lateral view directly demonstrates the relationship between the humeral head and glenoid and is the best projection for confirming posterior displacement. If the patient cannot abduct the arm sufficiently for a standard axillary view, a modified axillary or Velpeau view can be obtained. B is incorrect because an anteroposterior view with internal rotation may accentuate the lightbulb appearance but does not reliably confirm the direction of displacement. C is used primarily to evaluate sternoclavicular joint alignment. D does not provide the orthogonal projection needed to define the humeral head's position relative to the glenoid. E is intended to assess acromioclavicular joint separation and is not appropriate for this suspected abnormality.

Question 44  [ID: 1f2f1cfe3f1b45628d43c76cab927bfe]
A 46-year-old right-handed carpenter presents with 6 weeks of gradually worsening pain over the outer aspect of his right elbow. The pain began after he started a project requiring repetitive use of a screwdriver and is exacerbated by gripping tools, lifting objects with the palm facing downward, and extending the wrist. He denies trauma, fever, joint swelling, numbness, or weakness. Examination shows focal tenderness just distal to the lateral epicondyle and reproduction of pain with resisted wrist extension while the elbow is extended. Strength, sensation, and reflexes are intact. There is no erythema, warmth, or limitation of passive range of motion. Complete blood count and C-reactive protein are normal, and radiographs show no fracture or degenerative changes. He has no history of kidney disease, peptic ulcer disease, cardiovascular disease, or medication allergies. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy?

A. A short course of oral naproxen
B. Oral prednisone for 14 days
C. Immediate local triamcinolone injection
D. Oxycodone-acetaminophen as needed
E. Oral cephalexin for 7 days

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is characteristic of lateral epicondylitis, an overuse-related tendinopathy involving the common wrist extensor origin. Initial treatment includes activity modification, relative rest, therapeutic exercises, and short-term analgesia with a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug. Naproxen is appropriate because this patient has no renal, gastrointestinal, cardiovascular, or allergic contraindications to NSAID therapy. NSAIDs can reduce pain sufficiently to permit participation in rehabilitation, although they do not directly reverse tendon degeneration.

B is incorrect because systemic glucocorticoids are not recommended for this localized condition and expose the patient to unnecessary adverse effects, including hyperglycemia, mood changes, hypertension, and immunosuppression.

C is incorrect because local glucocorticoid injection may provide short-term pain relief but is not preferred as initial therapy. It is associated with high recurrence rates and potentially worse intermediate- and long-term outcomes compared with conservative care.

D is incorrect because opioid therapy is not appropriate for a nontraumatic, localized overuse condition that can be managed with nonopioid analgesics and rehabilitation. Opioids carry risks of sedation, constipation, dependence, and misuse.

E is incorrect because there are no findings suggesting bacterial infection. The patient is afebrile, has no erythema or warmth, and has normal inflammatory markers; therefore, antibiotic therapy is not indicated.

Question 45  [ID: 1fbf40e298fd498ea1cf36c4abd04379]
A 27-year-old man presents with rapidly worsening right lower-leg pain 6 hours after sustaining a closed, comminuted tibial shaft fracture in a motorcycle collision. The fracture was reduced and immobilized in a splint, but his pain has continued to intensify despite intravenous opioid therapy. His blood pressure is 128/72 mm Hg. Examination reveals a markedly tense and swollen anterior lower leg, severe pain with passive extension of the great toe, and decreased sensation over the first dorsal web space. The dorsalis pedis pulse is palpable, and capillary refill is 2 seconds. Radiographs confirm appropriate fracture alignment. Laboratory studies show a creatine kinase level of 620 U/L and a serum creatinine level of 1.0 mg/dL. Which of the following diagnostic findings would most strongly support the need for immediate operative intervention?

A. An ankle-brachial index of 0.95
B. Absence of venous flow on compression ultrasonography
C. An intracompartmental pressure of 50 mm Hg
D. A serum creatine kinase level greater than 1,000 U/L
E. Loss of the dorsalis pedis arterial waveform on Doppler ultrasonography

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The patient has clinical findings of acute compartment syndrome following a tibial fracture, including escalating pain out of proportion to the injury, pain with passive stretch, a tense swollen compartment, and sensory changes. Intracompartmental pressure measurement is useful when the diagnosis requires objective confirmation. A delta pressure, calculated as diastolic blood pressure minus intracompartmental pressure, of 30 mm Hg or less supports immediate fasciotomy. Here, the delta pressure is 72 − 50 = 22 mm Hg, indicating critically impaired tissue perfusion and the need for emergent operative decompression. A is incorrect because an ankle-brachial index of 0.95 indicates preserved large-vessel arterial flow and does not exclude elevated compartment pressure. B is incorrect because absent venous compressibility would suggest deep venous thrombosis, which does not explain the acute post-traumatic findings or mandate fasciotomy. D is incorrect because creatine kinase may rise with muscle ischemia, but it is nonspecific, may be normal early, and should not delay treatment. E is incorrect because distal pulses and Doppler waveforms are often preserved until late in the disease process; their loss indicates advanced vascular compromise and is not required to establish the need for intervention.

Question 46  [ID: 1fea54b0d04b435cb1c9f903da4ef648]
A 48-year-old man presents with 6 weeks of progressive deep aching pain in both calves, migratory knee and ankle pain, fatigue, and a 6-kg (13-lb) unintentional weight loss. He also reports episodic postprandial abdominal pain and new numbness of the right foot. His blood pressure is 168/102 mm Hg. Examination shows tender calf muscles, a mottled violaceous pattern over both legs, and weakness of right foot dorsiflexion. Laboratory studies reveal an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 82 mm/hr, serum creatinine of 1.8 mg/dL, and positive hepatitis B surface antigen. Urinalysis shows mild proteinuria without red blood cell casts. Serum antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies are negative. Angiography of the abdominal circulation demonstrates multifocal stenoses and small aneurysmal dilations involving several medium-sized arterial branches. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis
B. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis
C. Microscopic polyangiitis
D. Polyarteritis nodosa
E. Systemic lupus erythematosus

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: Polyarteritis nodosa is the most likely diagnosis. The characteristic findings include constitutional symptoms, severe myalgias and arthralgias, livedo reticularis, mononeuritis multiplex manifested by foot drop, abdominal pain from mesenteric ischemia, hypertension and renal dysfunction from renal arterial involvement, and angiographic microaneurysms and stenoses of medium-sized arteries. Hepatitis B infection is a classic association. The absence of red blood cell casts is important because the renal injury results from arterial ischemia rather than glomerulonephritis. ANCA is typically negative, and pulmonary involvement is generally absent. Eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis is associated with asthma, eosinophilia, and upper airway disease, which are not present. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis usually causes upper and lower respiratory tract disease and rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis, often with PR3-ANCA positivity. Microscopic polyangiitis commonly produces pulmonary capillaritis and pauci-immune glomerulonephritis with red blood cell casts and is often MPO-ANCA positive; medium-artery microaneurysms are not typical. Systemic lupus erythematosus may cause arthralgias and renal disease but would more commonly feature immune-mediated glomerulonephritis, positive antinuclear antibodies, and other characteristic mucocutaneous or hematologic findings.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Question 47  [ID: 2117396ae9364e2c8a062285e930f9b8]
A 67-year-old man presents after being struck along the left side of his torso by a falling tree branch. He reports severe focal pain that worsens with inspiration and coughing. His respiratory rate is 30/min, pulse is 108/min, and oxygen saturation is 89% on room air. Examination reveals ecchymosis and marked tenderness over the left lateral thorax. During inspiration, a portion of the injured area moves inward while the surrounding thoracic wall expands; during expiration, the same area moves outward. Breath sounds are diminished at the left base. Imaging demonstrates displaced cortical disruptions of the left fourth through seventh arcuate thoracic bones at two separate locations in each bone, with adjacent pulmonary opacities. Which of the following mechanisms most directly explains this patient's impaired ventilation?

A. Loss of structural continuity creates a free segment that moves opposite the remainder of the thoracic wall, reducing effective tidal volume
B. Interruption of sympathetic innervation causes unilateral bronchial smooth-muscle constriction and increased airway resistance
C. Disruption of the phrenic nerve produces bilateral diaphragmatic paralysis and loss of inspiratory capacity
D. Increased surfactant production raises alveolar surface tension and promotes diffuse alveolar collapse
E. Reflex relaxation of the external intercostal muscles causes persistent elevation of intrathoracic pressure during inspiration

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has multiple adjacent osseous injuries occurring at two points in each involved structure, creating a free-floating segment. Negative intrathoracic pressure during inspiration pulls this unstable segment inward while the remainder of the thoracic wall expands. During expiration, positive intrathoracic pressure pushes the segment outward. This paradoxical motion decreases effective tidal volume and increases the work of breathing. Pain-related splinting and the adjacent pulmonary injury further impair gas exchange and can cause hypoxemia. Choice B is incorrect because sympathetic interruption does not produce this characteristic localized paradoxical motion; bronchial tone is primarily regulated by parasympathetic pathways and circulating catecholamines. Choice C is incorrect because phrenic nerve injury would cause ipsilateral diaphragmatic weakness or paralysis, not a focal segment moving opposite the surrounding thoracic wall; bilateral paralysis is not supported by the unilateral findings. Choice D is incorrect because increased surfactant lowers, rather than raises, alveolar surface tension and helps prevent alveolar collapse. Choice E is incorrect because external intercostal muscle relaxation alone does not create paradoxical movement, and normal inspiration lowers intrathoracic pressure rather than persistently elevating it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Question 48  [ID: 21fa231cc1f345098bba2ad212a6dff0]
A 46-year-old woman presents for follow-up of recurrent episodes of finger pallor and cyanosis triggered by cold exposure. During the past 3 years, she has developed progressive tightening of the skin over her fingers, intermittent dysphagia, and worsening heartburn. Physical examination shows facial telangiectasias, reduced oral aperture, sclerodactyly, and several digital pitting scars. Antinuclear antibody testing is positive at a high titer with a centromere pattern. Complete blood count, serum creatinine, urinalysis, and chest radiography are normal. She denies cough, exertional dyspnea, chest pain, or syncope. Which of the following is the most appropriate ongoing surveillance strategy for this patient?

A. Annual transthoracic echocardiography and pulmonary function testing with diffusing capacity
B. Annual coronary angiography and exercise stress testing
C. Chest radiography every 5 years unless respiratory symptoms develop
D. Monthly serum creatine kinase measurement and electromyography
E. Routine renal biopsy every 2 years despite normal serum creatinine and urinalysis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical findings indicate systemic sclerosis with a limited cutaneous pattern. These patients are at increased risk for pulmonary arterial hypertension, which may initially be asymptomatic, as well as interstitial lung involvement. Annual transthoracic echocardiography and pulmonary function testing, including diffusing capacity for carbon monoxide, are appropriate surveillance measures. A falling diffusing capacity or elevated estimated pulmonary artery pressure should prompt further evaluation; right-heart catheterization is required to confirm pulmonary arterial hypertension. Option B is incorrect because routine invasive coronary angiography and annual stress testing are not recommended without symptoms or other indications. Option C is incorrect because chest radiography is insensitive for early pulmonary vascular or interstitial disease, and waiting for symptoms may delay recognition. Option D is incorrect because creatine kinase testing and electromyography are used when inflammatory muscle involvement is suspected, not as routine monthly surveillance. Option E is incorrect because renal biopsy is not recommended in an asymptomatic patient with normal renal studies. Blood pressure and renal function should instead be monitored regularly to detect renal complications promptly.

Question 49  [ID: 233f12d36afc496098a3c8d045c0d464]
A 68-year-old man with type 2 diabetes mellitus presents with a 2-day history of rapidly worsening right knee pain, swelling, and inability to bear weight. He denies recent trauma. His temperature is 38.7°C (101.7°F), blood pressure is 108/66 mm Hg, and pulse is 106/min. Examination shows a warm, erythematous, markedly swollen right knee held in slight flexion. Both active and passive range of motion cause severe pain. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 15,800/mm3 and an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 78 mm/hr. Arthrocentesis yields cloudy fluid with 92,000 leukocytes/mm3, 94% neutrophils, low glucose, and no crystals. Gram stain demonstrates gram-positive cocci in clusters. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Acute gout
B. Septic arthritis
C. Reactive arthritis
D. Calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease
E. Osteoarthritis exacerbation

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Septic arthritis is the most likely diagnosis. The rapid onset of severe monoarticular pain, fever, inability to bear weight, marked pain with passive movement, and inflammatory synovial fluid containing more than 50,000 leukocytes/mm3 with neutrophil predominance strongly indicate an infected joint. Gram-positive cocci in clusters suggest Staphylococcus aureus, the most common causative organism in adults. Diabetes mellitus is an additional risk factor. Acute gout can produce intense monoarthritis and a high synovial leukocyte count, but monosodium urate crystals would typically be present, and the Gram stain would not show organisms. Reactive arthritis usually follows a gastrointestinal or genitourinary infection and commonly presents as an asymmetric oligoarthritis with possible urethritis or conjunctivitis. Calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease often affects the knee in older adults, but rhomboid-shaped, positively birefringent crystals would be expected. Osteoarthritis may cause activity-related pain and a noninflammatory effusion but does not cause high fever, severe pain with passive motion, or markedly purulent, neutrophil-predominant fluid.

Question 50  [ID: 235a07af66064d069f2ac1343541577e]
An 18-month-old boy is brought to the clinic because of delayed walking and progressive prominence along the front of his torso. He was exclusively breastfed until age 12 months, has not received vitamin supplementation, and consumes little dairy. Physical examination shows frontal bossing, widened wrists, symmetric bowing of the lower extremities, and bead-like enlargement of the costochondral junctions. Laboratory studies reveal a serum calcium level of 8.1 mg/dL, phosphorus level of 2.4 mg/dL, elevated alkaline phosphatase level, elevated parathyroid hormone level, and markedly decreased 25-hydroxyvitamin D level. Radiographs demonstrate metaphyseal widening, cupping, and fraying. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. Oral cholecalciferol plus adequate elemental calcium supplementation
B. Intranasal calcitonin monotherapy
C. Oral alendronate plus weight-bearing exercise
D. Recombinant human growth hormone therapy
E. Prednisone followed by a gradual taper

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This child has nutritional vitamin D deficiency rickets, which can produce characteristic chest and rib deformities, including enlargement of the costochondral junctions. The dietary history, low 25-hydroxyvitamin D and phosphorus levels, elevated alkaline phosphatase and parathyroid hormone levels, and metaphyseal cupping and fraying support the diagnosis. Initial pharmacologic management consists of vitamin D replacement, typically oral cholecalciferol, together with adequate elemental calcium to support remineralization and prevent worsening hypocalcemia. A common regimen for a child older than 1 year is cholecalciferol 2,000 IU daily for at least 6 weeks, along with approximately 500 mg/day of elemental calcium, followed by maintenance vitamin D. Calcitonin is used in selected hypercalcemic states and disorders of excessive resorption, not vitamin D deficiency. Alendronate is used for conditions such as osteoporosis and would not correct the underlying mineralization defect. Growth hormone is indicated for specific causes of growth failure but does not treat deficient vitamin D or calcium availability. Prednisone can impair calcium absorption and mineralization and may worsen the patient's condition.

Question 51  [ID: 235efdb750054a77aad290dd17c2a216]
A 24-year-old man presents to the emergency department with rapidly worsening pain in his right lower leg. Six hours earlier, he sustained a closed, comminuted midshaft tibial fracture in a motorcycle collision and was placed in a circumferential cast. Intravenous opioid medication has provided minimal relief. His temperature is 37.1°C (98.8°F), blood pressure is 132/78 mm Hg, pulse is 108/min, and respirations are 18/min. Examination shows a tense, swollen lower leg with severe tenderness. Passive extension of the great toe causes marked pain. Sensation is decreased over the first dorsal web space, but the dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses remain palpable. Radiographs confirm the previously identified tibial fracture without new displacement. Laboratory studies show a creatine kinase concentration of 1,200 U/L and a serum creatinine concentration of 1.0 mg/dL. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Acute arterial occlusion
B. Acute compartment syndrome
C. Complex regional pain syndrome
D. Deep venous thrombosis
E. Necrotizing soft tissue infection

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. Acute compartment syndrome is most likely because the patient has rapidly progressive pain out of proportion to the injury, inadequate relief with opioids, a tense swollen extremity, severe pain with passive stretching of the involved muscles, and an evolving sensory deficit after a tibial fracture and circumferential casting. Palpable distal pulses do not exclude this diagnosis because arterial pressure may remain sufficient to produce pulses until late in the course. The elevated creatine kinase supports ongoing muscle injury. This condition requires immediate removal of constrictive dressings or casts, urgent pressure assessment when the diagnosis is uncertain, and prompt surgical fasciotomy when clinically indicated. A is incorrect because acute arterial occlusion more typically causes a cool, pale extremity with diminished or absent distal pulses and marked perfusion deficits. C is incorrect because complex regional pain syndrome generally develops weeks to months after an injury and is associated with persistent neuropathic pain, allodynia, and autonomic or trophic changes rather than an acutely tense extremity. D is incorrect because deep venous thrombosis can cause swelling and tenderness but does not typically produce severe pain with passive muscle stretch, a tense extremity, or an acute sensory deficit shortly after fracture immobilization. E is incorrect because necrotizing soft tissue infection can cause severe pain and swelling, but it is more commonly associated with systemic toxicity, fever, rapidly progressive skin changes, bullae, crepitus, or laboratory evidence of infection, which are absent here.

Question 52  [ID: 263eedcf140841d4a31a9849b0ad584f]
A 47-year-old woman presents with 4 years of progressively worsening pain and swelling involving both hands and wrists. She reports stiffness lasting approximately 2 hours after awakening that improves with activity. She also notes fatigue and difficulty opening jars. Examination reveals symmetric, boggy swelling and tenderness of the second through fifth metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints, with sparing of the distal interphalangeal joints. Laboratory studies show an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate and antibodies against cyclic citrullinated peptides. Radiographs demonstrate periarticular osteopenia, uniform joint-space narrowing, and marginal erosions. Which of the following additional physical examination findings is most consistent with this patient's presentation?

A. Bony enlargement of the distal interphalangeal joints
B. Flexion of the proximal interphalangeal joint with hyperextension of the distal interphalangeal joint
C. Hyperextension of the proximal interphalangeal joint with flexion of the distal interphalangeal joint
D. Diffuse swelling of an entire digit with adjacent nail pitting
E. Firm subcutaneous deposits on the helix of the ear

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The presentation is characteristic of rheumatoid arthritis: prolonged morning stiffness, symmetric inflammatory involvement of the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints, distal interphalangeal joint sparing, elevated inflammatory markers, anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies, periarticular osteopenia, and marginal erosions. Hyperextension of the proximal interphalangeal joint with flexion of the distal interphalangeal joint describes a swan-neck deformity, which may develop from chronic synovitis and ligamentous or tendon imbalance. Choice A describes Heberden nodes, which are associated with osteoarthritis. Choice B describes a boutonnière deformity; although this can also occur in rheumatoid arthritis, the classic deformity produced by proximal interphalangeal hyperextension and distal interphalangeal flexion is the swan-neck deformity described in choice C. Choice D describes dactylitis with nail changes, findings commonly associated with psoriatic arthritis. Choice E describes tophi, which are deposits of monosodium urate associated with chronic gout.

Question 53  [ID: 26475382cb6e43e3ad77d0199d52abfe]
A 16-year-old female competitive gymnast presents with 4 months of progressive low back pain that worsens with tumbling, prolonged standing, and lumbar extension. She denies fever, weight loss, nocturnal pain, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and lower-extremity weakness. Examination reveals increased lumbar lordosis, tight hamstrings, and a palpable step-off at the lumbosacral junction. Pain is reproduced with single-leg hyperextension. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, reflexes, and distal pulses are normal. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein are within normal limits. Standing anteroposterior and lateral radiographs are obtained. Which of the following radiographic findings would best confirm the suspected diagnosis?

A. Anterior translation of the L5 vertebral body relative to S1
B. A defect in the pars interarticularis without vertebral displacement
C. Symmetric narrowing of both sacroiliac joints with marginal erosions
D. Loss of vertebral body height with anterior wedging at L1
E. Posterolateral herniation of the L5-S1 intervertebral disc

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Anterior translation of one vertebral body relative to the vertebra below it on a standing lateral radiograph confirms spondylolisthesis. In adolescent athletes, repetitive lumbar hyperextension can produce a pars interarticularis stress injury, allowing L5 to slip anteriorly over S1. The degree of displacement can be classified using the Meyerding grading system. B describes spondylolysis, an isolated pars interarticularis defect without vertebral translation; it can precede or accompany the tested condition but does not by itself confirm vertebral slippage. C suggests inflammatory sacroiliitis, which would be more consistent with an axial inflammatory disorder and may be accompanied by prolonged morning stiffness or elevated inflammatory markers. D indicates a vertebral compression fracture, which is less likely in this young athlete without significant trauma or risk factors for reduced bone density. E may cause radicular symptoms, but disc herniation is best demonstrated with MRI rather than plain radiographs and does not account for the palpable step-off or characteristic extension-related pain.

Question 54  [ID: 26e17f7de928490ab49a32e7a248119a]
A 32-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe left-sided pain and limited arm movement after a witnessed generalized tonic-clonic seizure. He holds the affected arm adducted and internally rotated. Examination reveals flattening of the anterior contour near the proximal arm and a palpable prominence posteriorly. He is unable to externally rotate the arm. Sensation over the lateral upper arm is intact, and the radial pulse is 2+. An anteroposterior radiograph demonstrates a symmetrically rounded appearance of the humeral head with loss of the normal overlap between the humeral head and glenoid. An axillary view shows the humeral head positioned posterior to the glenoid. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Anterior shoulder dislocation
B. Posterior shoulder dislocation
C. Acromioclavicular joint separation
D. Proximal humerus fracture
E. Rotator cuff tear

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. Posterior shoulder dislocation classically occurs after a generalized seizure, electrical injury, or high-energy trauma because forceful internal rotator muscle contraction drives the humeral head posteriorly. Patients typically hold the arm adducted and internally rotated and cannot externally rotate it. The rounded 'light-bulb' appearance on an anteroposterior radiograph and posterior displacement of the humeral head on an axillary view confirm the diagnosis. Anterior shoulder dislocation usually produces an abducted, externally rotated posture with the humeral head displaced anteroinferiorly. Acromioclavicular separation causes superior displacement of the distal clavicle and focal tenderness over the acromioclavicular joint rather than fixed internal rotation. A proximal humerus fracture would demonstrate a fracture line, cortical disruption, or displacement of the humeral segments on imaging. A rotator cuff tear may cause weakness with abduction or rotation but does not produce fixed deformity or posterior displacement of the humeral head.

Question 55  [ID: 2706cb369ba5492395da3559b5d7887e]
A 38-year-old woman presents with 4 months of progressively worsening deep right groin pain. The pain is exacerbated by weight-bearing and occasionally radiates to the anterior thigh. She has systemic lupus erythematosus and has taken prednisone 20 to 40 mg daily for several years. Examination reveals an antalgic gait and marked pain with passive internal rotation of the right hip. There is no erythema, warmth, or focal tenderness. Her leukocyte count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are within normal limits. Plain radiographs show subtle patchy sclerosis of the superior femoral head without joint-space narrowing. MRI demonstrates a well-demarcated, serpiginous subchondral lesion with surrounding bone marrow edema. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely produced these findings?

A. Loss of blood supply to subchondral bone causing osteocyte death and subsequent structural collapse
B. Autoantibody-mediated synovial inflammation causing marginal erosion of articular surfaces
C. Degeneration of articular cartilage causing reactive subchondral sclerosis and osteophyte formation
D. Hematogenous bacterial seeding causing suppurative destruction of the joint space
E. Defective mineralization of osteoid due to inadequate vitamin D activity

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has avascular necrosis of the femoral head, strongly associated with prolonged systemic glucocorticoid exposure. Glucocorticoids promote marrow adipocyte hypertrophy, alterations in lipid metabolism, vascular dysfunction, and increased intraosseous pressure, all of which can compromise the terminal blood supply to the femoral head. Ischemia causes death of osteocytes and marrow cells. Repetitive loading of the weakened subchondral bone can then produce microfractures and eventual collapse of the articular surface. The characteristic MRI appearance is a serpiginous subchondral lesion; MRI is more sensitive than plain radiography in early disease. Choice B describes an inflammatory arthritis such as rheumatoid arthritis, which typically causes symmetric synovitis, marginal erosions, and joint-space narrowing. Choice C describes osteoarthritis, which is characterized by progressive cartilage loss, asymmetric joint-space narrowing, osteophytes, and subchondral sclerosis rather than a serpiginous lesion. Choice D describes septic arthritis, which usually presents acutely with severe pain, restricted motion, inflammatory marker elevation, and often systemic findings. Choice E describes osteomalacia, which causes diffuse bone pain, muscle weakness, reduced bone mineralization, and characteristic biochemical abnormalities rather than focal subchondral injury of the femoral head.

Question 56  [ID: 286f678dee114fcea4c9a18f3abe0244]
A 72-year-old man presents with a 1-year history of progressive aching in his pelvis and right thigh. He reports that his hat size has increased and that he has gradually worsening hearing in his left ear. He denies recent trauma, fever, weight loss, or night sweats. Examination shows frontal prominence, reduced hearing on the left, and mild anterior bowing of the right tibia. Laboratory studies reveal an alkaline phosphatase level of 480 U/L, with normal calcium, phosphorus, parathyroid hormone, and aminotransferase levels. A skull radiograph demonstrates areas of patchy sclerosis and cortical thickening. Which of the following diagnostic studies is most appropriate to determine the full extent of active involvement in this patient?

A. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry
B. Whole-body radionuclide scintigraphy using technetium-99m
C. Magnetic resonance imaging of the brain with contrast
D. Serum protein electrophoresis
E. Percutaneous biopsy of the right tibia

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The clinical findings are characteristic of Paget disease of bone: older age, localized pain, enlargement of the skull, hearing impairment, long-bone deformity, isolated elevation of alkaline phosphatase, and patchy sclerosis with cortical thickening on radiography. Whole-body radionuclide bone scintigraphy using technetium-99m is highly sensitive for identifying areas of increased osteoblastic activity and is the preferred study for determining the distribution and extent of active disease. Plain radiographs can then be obtained for specific scintigraphically active sites when additional anatomic characterization is needed.

A is incorrect because dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry measures bone mineral density and is primarily used to diagnose osteoporosis; it does not reliably map the extent of this disorder. C is incorrect because brain MRI does not evaluate the distribution of active skeletal lesions. It may be considered if neurologic complications or compression are suspected. D is incorrect because serum protein electrophoresis is used to evaluate monoclonal gammopathies such as multiple myeloma, which more commonly produce lytic lesions and may be associated with anemia, renal dysfunction, or hypercalcemia. E is incorrect because biopsy is not routinely required when the clinical, biochemical, and radiographic findings are typical. Biopsy is reserved for atypical lesions or concern for malignant transformation, such as rapidly worsening pain, a soft-tissue mass, or destructive radiographic changes.

Question 57  [ID: 295b630324a141a1870dd8ea6b5e5bfa]
A 68-year-old man presents with sudden right knee pain and inability to walk after feeling a pop while stepping off a curb. He began levofloxacin 5 days ago for community-acquired pneumonia and takes prednisone 10 mg daily for chronic obstructive pulmonary disease. Examination shows swelling above the patella, a palpable suprapatellar defect, and inability to perform a straight-leg raise. Passive range of motion is preserved. Plain radiographs show no fracture but demonstrate a low-riding patella. Ultrasonography reveals discontinuity of the structure attaching the superior patella to the anterior thigh. Serum creatinine is 1.8 mg/dL, compared with a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL. In addition to immobilization and urgent orthopedic referral, which of the following is the most appropriate medication-related intervention?

A. Continue levofloxacin and add scheduled ibuprofen
B. Discontinue levofloxacin immediately and use a non-fluoroquinolone antibiotic if further antimicrobial therapy is required
C. Increase the prednisone dosage to reduce local inflammation
D. Administer an intra-articular glucocorticoid injection
E. Begin therapeutic enoxaparin to prevent extension of the injury

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The patient has disruption of the knee's active extension apparatus, supported by the sudden pop, suprapatellar defect, inability to perform a straight-leg raise, low-riding patella, and ultrasonographic discontinuity. Fluoroquinolones can cause tendinopathy and tendon rupture through adverse effects on collagen synthesis and degradation. The risk is increased in adults older than 60 years, patients receiving systemic glucocorticoids, and those with renal impairment. Levofloxacin should therefore be discontinued immediately, and a non-fluoroquinolone agent should be selected if antimicrobial treatment remains necessary. Immobilization in extension and urgent orthopedic evaluation are also indicated. A is incorrect because continued fluoroquinolone exposure may worsen tendon injury; ibuprofen also requires caution given the acute rise in creatinine. C is incorrect because systemic glucocorticoids increase the risk of tendon degeneration and rupture. D is incorrect because a glucocorticoid injection does not restore structural continuity and may further weaken periarticular tissues. E is incorrect because anticoagulation does not treat the structural injury and could increase bleeding or hematoma formation; venous thromboembolism prophylaxis should be based on the patient's overall immobilization and perioperative risk rather than used to prevent progression of this injury.

Question 58  [ID: 299c129c6b2b457997b3a850b7d56a23]
A 2-month-old infant is brought to the clinic because the parents have noticed that the child's head persistently tilts toward the right while the chin points toward the left. The infant was delivered vaginally after a prolonged breech presentation. Feeding and development have been normal. There is no history of fever, trauma, irritability, or vomiting. On examination, the infant is alert and afebrile. A firm, nontender, 1.5-cm fusiform mass is palpable along the right side of the neck. Neurologic examination is normal, and there is no midline tenderness. Which of the following additional physical examination findings is most likely in this patient?

A. Limited passive rotation of the head to the right and limited lateral flexion to the left
B. Pain with axial loading accompanied by bilateral upper-extremity paresthesias
C. Resistance to passive knee extension when the hip is flexed
D. Weak shoulder abduction with a diminished ipsilateral biceps reflex
E. Midline posterior tenderness with increased separation between adjacent spinous processes

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This infant has congenital muscular torticollis, most commonly caused by fibrosis and shortening of one sternocleidomastoid muscle following intrauterine positioning or birth-related injury. Contraction of the right sternocleidomastoid produces ipsilateral lateral flexion and contralateral rotation, causing the head to tilt right while the chin rotates left. Passive movement in the opposite directions—rotation to the right and lateral flexion to the left—will therefore be restricted. A firm, nontender mass within the affected muscle may be palpable during early infancy. Breech presentation and difficult delivery are recognized risk factors.

B is incorrect because pain with axial loading and upper-extremity paresthesias suggests cervical nerve root or spinal cord compression, which is not expected in an otherwise well infant with a normal neurologic examination.

C is incorrect because resistance to knee extension with the hip flexed describes the Kernig sign, which suggests meningeal irritation. This infant is afebrile and lacks systemic or neurologic findings of meningitis.

D is incorrect because weak shoulder abduction and a diminished biceps reflex suggest an upper brachial plexus injury involving C5-C6. Although such injuries may occur after difficult delivery, they do not explain the characteristic fixed head position and palpable neck mass.

E is incorrect because midline tenderness and increased interspinous separation can indicate traumatic ligamentous instability. There is no history of trauma, and the characteristic positional deformity with a nontender intramuscular mass points to unilateral sternocleidomastoid shortening.

Question 59  [ID: 2a77a0b428c4436e80516b6f83554378]
A 58-year-old man with poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus and peripheral neuropathy presents with 3 weeks of increasing drainage from an ulcer on the plantar surface of his right foot. He reports minimal pain but has noticed progressive swelling and a foul odor. His temperature is 38.1°C (100.6°F). Examination shows a 2-cm ulcer over the first metatarsal head with surrounding erythema, warmth, and purulent discharge. Pedal pulses are palpable, but sensation to monofilament testing is diminished. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 13,200/mm3, erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 92 mm/hour, and elevated C-reactive protein. Plain radiographs demonstrate focal cortical irregularity beneath the ulcer. Which additional physical examination finding would most strongly support the leading diagnosis?

A. A palpable click with compression of the forefoot
B. Reproduction of heel pain with passive dorsiflexion of the toes
C. Contact with a hard, gritty surface when the ulcer is gently explored with a sterile blunt probe
D. Increased pain with resisted flexion of the great toe
E. Pallor of the foot with elevation followed by dependent rubor

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The patient has a chronic diabetic foot ulcer with systemic inflammation, markedly elevated inflammatory markers, and cortical irregularity, making osteomyelitis highly likely. A positive probe-to-bone test, in which a sterile blunt probe passed through the ulcer contacts a hard or gritty surface, strongly supports underlying bone involvement, particularly in a high-risk patient with a deep infected diabetic foot ulcer. Choice A describes a finding associated with a Morton neuroma. Choice B is consistent with plantar fasciitis due to tension on the plantar fascia. Choice D suggests pathology involving the flexor hallucis longus tendon rather than infection extending to bone. Choice E indicates peripheral arterial insufficiency; although vascular disease may impair wound healing, this finding does not specifically support osteomyelitis.

Question 60  [ID: 2a8ccf2bfcac46a5aca132d80285c937]
A 16-year-old female competitive gymnast presents with 3 months of gradually worsening low back pain. The pain is exacerbated by tumbling and lumbar extension and improves with rest. She denies pain radiating into the legs, bowel or bladder dysfunction, saddle anesthesia, fever, or weight loss. Examination reveals increased lumbar lordosis, tight hamstrings, and tenderness over the lower lumbar region. Pain is reproduced with single-leg lumbar hyperextension. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, reflexes, and straight-leg raise testing are normal. Standing lateral radiographs demonstrate bilateral defects of the L5 pars interarticularis with 20% anterior translation of L5 relative to S1. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial intervention?

A. Immediate posterior decompression and instrumented fusion
B. Activity modification, a short course of an NSAID, and physical therapy emphasizing core stabilization and hamstring flexibility
C. Strict bed rest for 6 weeks followed by unrestricted athletic participation
D. Lumbar epidural corticosteroid injection
E. Chronic opioid therapy with continued participation in gymnastics

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This adolescent has a low-grade anterior vertebral translation associated with bilateral pars interarticularis defects, extension-related pain, and no neurologic deficits. Initial management is conservative and includes temporary cessation of aggravating activities, particularly repetitive lumbar hyperextension; short-term use of an NSAID or other nonopioid analgesic; and physical therapy focused on core stabilization, neutral-spine mechanics, and hamstring flexibility. A lumbosacral brace may be considered if symptoms do not improve with initial conservative measures. Most patients with low-grade displacement and an intact neurologic examination improve without surgery. Choice A is incorrect because decompression and fusion are generally reserved for progressive high-grade displacement, persistent disabling pain despite prolonged conservative therapy, progressive deformity, or neurologic compromise. Choice C is incorrect because prolonged strict bed rest causes deconditioning and is not recommended; return to sports should be gradual and based on resolution of pain and restoration of function. Choice D is incorrect because epidural corticosteroid injections are primarily used for radicular pain caused by nerve root inflammation or compression, which is absent here. Choice E is incorrect because chronic opioid therapy is inappropriate for this presentation, and continued participation in an activity that repeatedly provokes lumbar extension may worsen symptoms and impede healing.

Question 61  [ID: 2d08c97f4bad4b9a8a89c1b09e62a418]
A 49-year-old woman presents with 8 months of progressive pain and morning stiffness involving both hands and wrists. She also reports a persistent gritty sensation in her eyes, difficulty swallowing dry foods without water, and several recent dental caries. Examination shows decreased salivary pooling, bilateral nontender parotid enlargement, and tenderness of the metacarpophalangeal joints without synovial thickening or deformity. Schirmer testing demonstrates markedly reduced tear production. Laboratory studies reveal a positive antinuclear antibody, antibodies directed against Ro/SSA and La/SSB antigens, and an elevated rheumatoid factor. Radiographs of the hands show no erosions. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's clinical findings?

A. Immune-mediated lymphocytic infiltration and destruction of exocrine glands
B. Deposition of monosodium urate crystals with activation of the NLRP3 inflammasome
C. Antibody-mediated stimulation of thyroid-stimulating hormone receptors
D. Formation of antibodies against citrullinated proteins causing erosive synovitis
E. Defective type I collagen synthesis causing reduced tensile strength of connective tissue

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. This patient has Sjögren syndrome, an autoimmune condition characterized by T- and B-lymphocyte infiltration of exocrine glands, particularly the lacrimal and salivary glands. Progressive glandular destruction causes reduced tear production, xerostomia, dental caries, and parotid enlargement. Nonerosive inflammatory arthralgia or arthritis is a common extraglandular manifestation. Anti-Ro/SSA and anti-La/SSB antibodies strongly support the diagnosis, and rheumatoid factor may also be elevated. B is incorrect because monosodium urate crystals cause gout, which typically presents with episodic acute monoarthritis and negatively birefringent crystals rather than chronic ocular and oral dryness. C is incorrect because thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulins cause Graves disease, which produces hyperthyroidism and may cause ophthalmopathy but does not explain reduced tear and saliva production. D is incorrect because antibodies against citrullinated proteins are associated with rheumatoid arthritis, which typically causes persistent synovitis and may produce marginal erosions; it does not account for the prominent exocrine gland dysfunction in this vignette. E is incorrect because abnormal type I collagen synthesis is associated with conditions such as osteogenesis imperfecta and would cause tissue fragility rather than autoimmune glandular destruction.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Question 62  [ID: 2e761238f1694e20a1f972d20d937c74]
A 26-year-old woman presents with 3 months of fatigue, intermittent low-grade fever, and symmetric pain and swelling of the wrists and metacarpophalangeal joints. Morning stiffness lasts approximately 45 minutes. She also reports a facial rash that worsens after sun exposure and painless oral ulcers. Examination shows tenderness and mild swelling of both wrists and several metacarpophalangeal joints without deformity. Laboratory studies reveal leukopenia, a positive antinuclear antibody test, elevated anti-double-stranded DNA antibody levels, and decreased serum C3 and C4 concentrations. Radiographs of the hands show no erosions. Which of the following mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's clinical findings?

A. Deposition of circulating antigen-antibody complexes with complement activation and inflammatory tissue injury
B. Antibody-mediated stimulation of cell-surface hormone receptors
C. Cytotoxic T-lymphocyte destruction of synovial cells containing intracellular pathogens
D. Deposition of monosodium urate crystals causing activation of the NLRP3 inflammasome
E. Progressive loss of articular cartilage due to mechanical stress and chondrocyte dysfunction

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. The combination of symmetric nonerosive inflammatory polyarthritis, photosensitive facial eruption, painless oral ulcers, leukopenia, anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies, and hypocomplementemia reflects a systemic autoimmune process driven largely by type III hypersensitivity. Impaired clearance of apoptotic nuclear material promotes autoantibody production. Circulating antigen-antibody complexes deposit in tissues, activate complement, and recruit inflammatory cells, producing tissue injury. Consumption of complement accounts for the decreased C3 and C4 levels. Joint involvement is typically inflammatory but nonerosive. B is incorrect because antibody-mediated receptor stimulation is a type II hypersensitivity mechanism classically associated with conditions such as Graves disease; it does not explain immune-complex consumption of complement or this pattern of multisystem findings. C is incorrect because cytotoxic T-cell activity represents a type IV hypersensitivity mechanism and is not the primary cause of this patient's nonerosive inflammatory joint disease and hypocomplementemia. D is incorrect because monosodium urate crystal deposition typically causes acute, intensely painful monoarthritis and is identified by negatively birefringent needle-shaped crystals; it would not account for photosensitivity, oral ulcers, leukopenia, or autoantibodies. E is incorrect because cartilage degeneration from mechanical stress causes noninflammatory degenerative joint disease, which typically produces pain worsened by use, brief morning stiffness, and characteristic radiographic joint-space narrowing and osteophytes rather than systemic inflammatory findings.
