Question 63  [ID: 2eadef4173f34abf963323594476bce0]
A 14-year-old boy presents with his mother for a routine sports physical. His mother is concerned that the center of his anterior torso has become increasingly sunken during his recent growth spurt. The patient denies exertional dyspnea, palpitations, syncope, pain, or exercise intolerance. He participates in competitive swimming without difficulty. Physical examination shows a symmetric inward depression of the lower sternum without spinal curvature or features of a connective tissue disorder. Cardiac and pulmonary examinations are normal. Spirometry and electrocardiography show no abnormalities. Cross-sectional imaging obtained previously demonstrates a Haller index of 2.7. Which of the following is the most appropriate health maintenance and counseling recommendation for this patient?

A. Recommend immediate operative correction to prevent future cardiopulmonary complications
B. Restrict participation in competitive athletics until skeletal maturity
C. Prescribe full-time external bracing until the contour normalizes
D. Obtain annual cross-sectional imaging to monitor the Haller index
E. Provide reassurance, encourage unrestricted activity and posture exercises, and monitor clinically during growth for new symptoms or progression

Correct Answer: E
Explanation: This patient has a mild, symmetric pectus excavatum deformity without cardiopulmonary symptoms or functional impairment. A Haller index below approximately 3.25 generally does not indicate a severe deformity. Appropriate management consists of reassurance, unrestricted physical activity, attention to posture and conditioning, and periodic clinical reassessment during growth. The patient and family should be advised to return if exertional dyspnea, chest pain, palpitations, syncope, reduced exercise tolerance, rapid progression, or significant psychosocial distress develops. Posture and breathing exercises may improve posture and body image, although they do not eliminate the underlying structural contour.

A is incorrect because operative correction is generally reserved for patients with severe anatomical deformity, cardiopulmonary impairment, substantial symptoms, or significant psychosocial morbidity. This patient has none of these indications.

B is incorrect because asymptomatic patients with normal cardiopulmonary function do not require athletic restriction. Continued exercise may improve conditioning, posture, and self-confidence.

C is incorrect because external bracing is primarily used for a flexible outward sternal protrusion, not an inward depression. It is not appropriate for this patient's findings.

D is incorrect because routine annual cross-sectional imaging exposes the patient to unnecessary radiation and is not indicated in a stable, asymptomatic patient. Clinical monitoring is preferred, with additional imaging or physiologic testing reserved for progression, new symptoms, or preoperative assessment.

Question 64  [ID: 30a0093824a5441cbfc4a6da8570f37a]
A 24-year-old man presents with right wrist pain after falling onto an outstretched hand while skateboarding 2 hours ago. He reports immediate pain that worsens with gripping or moving his thumb. Examination shows swelling and focal tenderness in the anatomic snuffbox, with pain during axial loading of the thumb. Sensation, capillary refill, and radial pulse are normal. Radiographs demonstrate a thin radiolucent line traversing the central portion of the small carpal bone located on the radial side of the proximal carpal row. Which of the following anatomic features most directly increases the risk of ischemic necrosis of the proximal osseous fragment?

A. Arterial branches enter primarily at the distal pole and supply the proximal pole through retrograde flow
B. The proximal pole receives its blood supply exclusively from branches of the ulnar artery
C. A nutrient artery enters centrally and is routinely disrupted by injury to the surrounding flexor tendons
D. The proximal pole lacks vascular anastomoses because it lies outside the joint capsule
E. Venous drainage from the proximal pole is obstructed by the transverse carpal ligament

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The imaging and examination findings indicate a scaphoid fracture through the waist. Most of the scaphoid's arterial supply arises from branches of the radial artery that enter the bone distally, particularly along the dorsal ridge. Blood then travels retrogradely to supply the proximal pole. A disruption through the waist can interrupt this intraosseous circulation, placing the proximal fragment at risk for avascular necrosis and nonunion. B is incorrect because the scaphoid is supplied predominantly by branches of the radial artery, not exclusively by the ulnar artery. C is incorrect because the clinically important vascular pattern is distal entry with retrograde intraosseous flow; flexor tendon injury is not the usual cause of compromised perfusion. D is incorrect because the proximal pole is largely covered by articular cartilage and lies within the joint capsule; its vulnerability is due to limited vascular entry sites rather than an extracapsular location. E is incorrect because impaired arterial inflow, not venous compression by the transverse carpal ligament, accounts for the risk of ischemic necrosis.

Question 65  [ID: 310369ec9ec146cfa0cbd0a3c53f16b9]
A 16-year-old male gymnast presents with 4 months of gradually worsening low back pain. The pain is exacerbated by back extension, tumbling, and prolonged standing and improves with rest. He denies fever, weight loss, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and lower-extremity weakness or numbness. Examination reveals increased lumbar lordosis, tight hamstrings, and tenderness over the lower lumbar region. Pain is reproduced when he stands on one leg and extends his back. Strength, sensation, and deep tendon reflexes are normal in both lower extremities. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein levels are within normal limits. Standing lateral radiography demonstrates approximately 20% anterior displacement of the L5 vertebral body relative to S1, and oblique views show bilateral defects of the L5 pars interarticularis. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Isthmic spondylolisthesis
B. Lumbar spinal stenosis
C. Vertebral osteomyelitis
D. Lumbar disc herniation
E. Ankylosing spondylitis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Isthmic spondylolisthesis is anterior displacement of one vertebral body relative to the vertebra below due to a defect in the pars interarticularis. It commonly affects adolescent athletes who participate in activities involving repetitive lumbar hyperextension, such as gymnastics. Characteristic findings include extension-related low back pain, increased lumbar lordosis, hamstring tightness, a positive single-leg hyperextension test, bilateral pars defects, and anterior translation of L5 on S1. The approximately 20% displacement is consistent with grade I slippage. B is incorrect because lumbar spinal stenosis typically occurs in older adults and causes neurogenic claudication relieved by sitting or lumbar flexion. C is incorrect because vertebral osteomyelitis is usually associated with constant pain, systemic risk factors, and elevated inflammatory markers; it does not cause bilateral pars defects. D is incorrect because lumbar disc herniation more commonly causes radicular pain, sensory changes, weakness, altered reflexes, or a positive straight-leg raise test. E is incorrect because ankylosing spondylitis typically presents with inflammatory back pain, prolonged morning stiffness, improvement with activity, and sacroiliac joint abnormalities rather than focal pars defects and vertebral translation.

Question 66  [ID: 314650ccbb024bcba743e3530e30d5de]
A 46-year-old woman presents for follow-up of 10 months of pain, swelling, and morning stiffness lasting approximately 2 hours. Symptoms involve both wrists and several metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints. Examination shows symmetric tenderness, boggy swelling, and decreased grip strength. Laboratory studies demonstrate an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, positive anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies, and negative hepatitis B surface antigen. Radiographs show periarticular osteopenia and small marginal erosions. Her symptoms remain inadequately controlled after 6 months of adherent methotrexate therapy, and treatment with adalimumab is planned. She was born in India, received bacille Calmette-Guérin vaccination as a child, and has no cough, fever, night sweats, or weight loss. Which of the following is the best preventive strategy to reduce the risk of a serious infectious complication before starting the planned medication?

A. Administer a bacille Calmette-Guérin booster before initiating therapy
B. Obtain an interferon-gamma release assay and, if positive, exclude active infection and treat latent infection before initiating therapy
C. Begin adalimumab without screening because she has no constitutional or respiratory symptoms
D. Obtain sputum acid-fast bacillus cultures without first performing latent infection testing
E. Perform annual chest radiography after therapy begins instead of baseline screening

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Adalimumab inhibits tumor necrosis factor-alpha, a cytokine essential for maintaining granulomas that contain Mycobacterium tuberculosis. TNF-alpha inhibition can therefore cause reactivation of latent tuberculosis, often resulting in severe or disseminated infection. All patients should be screened for latent tuberculosis before beginning a TNF inhibitor. An interferon-gamma release assay is preferred in this patient because prior bacille Calmette-Guérin vaccination can produce a false-positive tuberculin skin test. If testing is positive, active tuberculosis should be excluded with symptom assessment and chest imaging, with sputum testing when indicated. Appropriate treatment of latent infection should then be initiated before adalimumab. A is incorrect because bacille Calmette-Guérin boosters are not recommended for this purpose and live vaccines are generally avoided during significant immunosuppression. C is incorrect because latent infection is typically asymptomatic, so the absence of cough, fever, night sweats, or weight loss does not eliminate the need for screening. D is incorrect because sputum acid-fast bacillus testing is primarily used when active pulmonary infection is suspected; it is not the preferred initial screening test in an asymptomatic patient. E is incorrect because annual chest radiography does not replace baseline testing for latent infection and would not reliably prevent reactivation after TNF-alpha inhibition.

Question 67  [ID: 3214658317884aa5bfa41b76170ccec0]
A 4-week-old boy is brought to the clinic because his parents have noticed that he persistently holds his head tilted toward the right with his chin rotated toward the left. He was delivered vaginally at 39 weeks after a pregnancy complicated by breech positioning. He is feeding well and has had no fever, vomiting, irritability, or recent trauma. Physical examination reveals a firm, nontender 1.5-cm mass within the right sternocleidomastoid muscle and restricted passive rotation of the head toward the right. The remainder of the neurologic examination is normal. Ultrasonography demonstrates focal fusiform thickening of the affected muscle without a cystic or vascular lesion. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Congenital muscular torticollis
B. Atlantoaxial rotatory subluxation
C. Cervical lymphadenitis
D. Klippel-Feil syndrome
E. Spasmodic torticollis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Congenital muscular torticollis is caused by unilateral shortening and fibrosis of the sternocleidomastoid muscle, often associated with intrauterine malposition or birth-related injury. Affected infants characteristically tilt the head toward the involved side and rotate the chin away from it. A firm, painless mass within the sternocleidomastoid muscle may appear during the first several weeks of life, and ultrasonography can demonstrate fusiform muscular thickening. Atlantoaxial rotatory subluxation generally presents acutely with painful limitation of motion, often after trauma, surgery, or an upper respiratory infection, and would not produce an intramuscular mass. Cervical lymphadenitis causes tender lymph node enlargement and may be accompanied by fever or overlying inflammation; the lesion would not be centered within the sternocleidomastoid muscle. Klippel-Feil syndrome results from congenital fusion of cervical vertebrae and classically causes a short neck, low posterior hairline, and restricted neck motion rather than a unilateral sternocleidomastoid mass. Spasmodic torticollis is a focal dystonia that typically develops in adults and causes involuntary, often painful contractions rather than a fixed postural abnormality in a neonate.

Question 68  [ID: 324a2976ed724bba951b040709b520b9]
A 15-year-old boy is evaluated for a progressively rounded upper back first noticed 2 years ago. He reports intermittent aching between the shoulder blades after prolonged sitting but denies trauma, morning stiffness, weakness, numbness, or bowel or bladder dysfunction. His mother states that the deformity has not improved despite repeated reminders to stand upright. Examination shows a fixed, sharply angulated posterior prominence of the upper back that becomes more apparent with forward flexion and does not correct with active extension. Shoulder and pelvic heights are symmetric, and the neurologic examination is normal. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein levels are within reference ranges. Standing lateral radiographs demonstrate a Cobb angle of 62 degrees and anterior wedging greater than 5 degrees in four consecutive thoracic vertebral bodies. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Scheuermann kyphosis
B. Adolescent idiopathic scoliosis
C. Postural kyphosis
D. Lumbar hyperlordosis
E. Ankylosing spondylitis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Scheuermann kyphosis is the most likely diagnosis. It typically presents during adolescence with a rigid, angular increase in posterior thoracic curvature that does not correct with voluntary extension. The characteristic radiographic finding is anterior vertebral body wedging of at least 5 degrees in three or more consecutive vertebrae; the increased Cobb angle further supports the diagnosis. Adolescent idiopathic scoliosis produces lateral curvature with vertebral rotation, often accompanied by asymmetric shoulder or pelvic height, rather than a fixed sagittal-plane deformity. Postural kyphosis is generally flexible, smoothly curved, and correctable with active extension; vertebral wedging is absent. Lumbar hyperlordosis is an exaggerated inward lumbar curvature and does not explain the thoracic vertebral wedging. Ankylosing spondylitis can eventually produce a stooped posture, but it is typically associated with inflammatory back pain, prolonged morning stiffness, sacroiliitis, and reduced spinal mobility rather than adolescent-onset multilevel anterior vertebral wedging.

Question 69  [ID: 32f4648aba604cc0887bb0269d156257]
A 16-year-old boy presents with a 2-year history of progressive rounding of his upper back and intermittent aching between the shoulder blades. He denies trauma, fever, weight loss, weakness, or sensory changes. Examination shows a fixed, sharply angulated posterior prominence that does not correct with active posture adjustment or hyperextension. Strength, sensation, reflexes, and gait are normal. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, calcium, and alkaline phosphatase levels are within reference ranges. A lateral chest radiograph obtained at an outside facility demonstrates exaggerated anterior concavity of the mid-thoracic vertebral column without focal vertebral destruction. Which of the following diagnostic studies is most appropriate to quantify the severity of this patient's deformity?

A. Standing lateral full-spine radiography with measurement of the Cobb angle
B. Supine lumbar radiography with measurement of the Ferguson angle
C. Computed tomography of the thorax with measurement of vertebral canal diameter
D. Magnetic resonance imaging with assessment of spinal cord signal intensity
E. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry with measurement of bone mineral density

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Standing lateral full-spine radiography with Cobb angle measurement is the standard method for quantifying excessive sagittal curvature. The angle is measured between lines drawn along the superior end plate of the uppermost involved vertebra and the inferior end plate of the lowermost involved vertebra. This patient has a fixed thoracic kyphotic deformity; in an adolescent, anterior wedging of at least 5 degrees across three consecutive vertebral bodies would further support a structural etiology such as Scheuermann disease. Choice B is incorrect because the Ferguson angle is measured on an anteroposterior lumbosacral radiograph and evaluates lumbosacral alignment rather than thoracic sagittal curvature. Choice C is incorrect because CT provides detailed osseous anatomy but is not the standard initial study for quantifying the overall deformity and adds unnecessary radiation exposure. Choice D is incorrect because MRI is primarily indicated when neurologic deficits, spinal cord compression, infection, tumor, or an atypical deformity is suspected; this patient's neurologic examination and inflammatory markers are normal. Choice E is incorrect because dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry evaluates bone mineral density and may be useful when osteoporosis is suspected, but it does not measure the magnitude of the spinal curvature.

Question 70  [ID: 32fea3f10d2d4e3898f0e8658f698ae3]
A 47-year-old right-handed house painter presents with 3 months of gradually worsening right shoulder pain. The pain is localized to the lateral aspect of the shoulder, is exacerbated by overhead work, and frequently awakens him when he lies on the affected side. He denies neck pain, numbness, or a preceding traumatic event. Examination shows no erythema, swelling, or deformity. Active abduction is painful between 60 and 120 degrees, whereas passive range of motion is preserved. Strength is mildly reduced with abduction, and distal sensation and pulses are normal. Plain radiographs show no fracture or significant degenerative changes. Which of the following examination maneuvers is most likely to reproduce this patient's symptoms?

A. Resisted abduction with the arm elevated in the scapular plane and the thumb directed downward
B. Resisted flexion of the supinated forearm with the elbow extended
C. Resisted extension of the wrist with the elbow fully extended
D. Passive flexion of the wrist while the elbow remains extended
E. Percussion over the volar aspect of the wrist

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's lateral shoulder pain with overhead activity, nocturnal symptoms, painful arc during active abduction, preserved passive range of motion, and weakness with abduction are characteristic of supraspinatus tendinopathy. The maneuver in choice A describes the empty-can, or Jobe, test. Pain or weakness during resisted abduction in the scapular plane assesses the supraspinatus and supports the suspected diagnosis.

Choice B describes resisted forearm supination or shoulder flexion, which may provoke pain arising from the long head of the biceps and is not the most specific maneuver for this presentation.

Choice C stresses the common wrist extensor origin and is used when evaluating lateral elbow pain, particularly symptoms aggravated by gripping or resisted wrist extension.

Choice D places tension on the wrist extensor structures and may also reproduce pain associated with an overuse injury at the lateral elbow rather than lateral shoulder pain.

Choice E describes a Tinel-type maneuver at the wrist, which is used to assess median nerve irritation and would be relevant in a patient with paresthesias involving the thumb, index finger, and middle finger.

Question 71  [ID: 334984467a3441faae0e92065f1d7b59]
A 16-year-old boy presents with 2 months of progressive pain near his right knee. He plays varsity basketball but recalls no specific injury. The pain was initially intermittent but has become constant and increasingly severe. Examination reveals a firm, tender, fixed swelling over the distal thigh, mild warmth, and decreased knee flexion. There is no erythema or fluctuance. Distal pulses, sensation, and motor function are intact. Plain radiographs demonstrate a poorly defined mixed-density lesion in the distal femoral metaphysis with cortical disruption, periosteal elevation, and radiating mineralized projections. Which additional historical finding would most strongly support the suspected cause of this patient's presentation?

A. Pain that improves after warming up and returns after prolonged activity
B. Morning stiffness lasting 15 minutes and improving with movement
C. Pain that is prominent at night and persists despite rest
D. A recent increase in running distance and training intensity
E. Intermittent joint locking associated with twisting movements

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The presentation is characteristic of osteosarcoma, which most commonly occurs in adolescents and frequently involves the metaphysis of a long bone, especially near the knee. Progressive pain that occurs at night and persists despite rest is an important historical warning sign for a malignant bone lesion. A firm, fixed mass and radiographic findings such as cortical destruction, periosteal elevation producing a Codman triangle, and radiating mineralized projections producing a sunburst pattern further support this diagnosis. Choice A is more consistent with an overuse tendinopathy or other mechanical condition. Choice B suggests a mild inflammatory or degenerative pattern but is not typical of an aggressive bone lesion. Choice D supports a stress injury, which generally has activity-related pain and a different radiographic appearance. Choice E suggests an internal derangement of the knee, such as a meniscal injury, rather than a lesion arising from the distal femur.

Question 72  [ID: 33b19097de5a4285aa04ee6a8e416431]
A 49-year-old woman presents with 8 months of pain and morning stiffness involving both hands and wrists. She also reports a persistent gritty sensation in her eyes, difficulty swallowing dry foods without water, and several new dental caries. Examination shows mild bilateral parotid enlargement and tenderness of the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints without synovial thickening or deformity. Schirmer testing demonstrates 3 mm of wetting after 5 minutes. Laboratory studies reveal a positive antinuclear antibody, elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and positive rheumatoid factor. Radiographs of the hands show no erosions. Which of the following additional laboratory findings would most strongly support the suspected unifying condition?

A. Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies
B. Anti-centromere antibodies
C. Anti-Ro/SSA antibodies
D. Anti-Jo-1 antibodies
E. HLA-B27 positivity

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The combination of keratoconjunctivitis sicca, xerostomia, recurrent dental caries, parotid enlargement, and nonerosive symmetric joint symptoms is characteristic of Sjögren syndrome. Anti-Ro/SSA antibodies are strongly associated with this condition and are included in current classification criteria. Anti-La/SSB antibodies may also be present, but isolated anti-La/SSB positivity is less diagnostically useful. A positive rheumatoid factor is common and does not necessarily indicate rheumatoid arthritis.

A is incorrect because anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies are highly specific for rheumatoid arthritis, which more commonly causes persistent inflammatory synovitis and marginal erosions. Although secondary Sjögren syndrome may occur in patients with rheumatoid arthritis, the vignette does not demonstrate erosive inflammatory arthritis.

B is incorrect because anti-centromere antibodies are most strongly associated with limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis, which typically presents with Raynaud phenomenon, sclerodactyly, telangiectasias, and esophageal dysmotility.

D is incorrect because anti-Jo-1 antibodies are associated with antisynthetase syndrome, characterized by inflammatory myopathy, interstitial lung disease, mechanic's hands, Raynaud phenomenon, and inflammatory arthritis.

E is incorrect because HLA-B27 is associated with axial spondyloarthritis and related seronegative conditions, which typically cause inflammatory back pain, sacroiliitis, enthesitis, or acute anterior uveitis rather than prominent ocular and oral dryness.

Question 73  [ID: 33c32d4feb634ea797a2b61e9b006b90]
A 13-year-old boy is evaluated for 3 months of gradually worsening pain just below the right knee. He plays competitive basketball, and the pain increases with running, jumping, kneeling, and climbing stairs. He denies a specific injury, fever, morning stiffness, or pain at rest. Examination shows a prominent, tender tibial tubercle and pain at that site with resisted knee extension. There is no joint effusion, erythema, ligamentous instability, or limitation of passive range of motion. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are normal. Which of the following findings is most likely to be seen on a lateral radiograph of the affected area?

A. Fragmentation and irregularity of the tibial tubercle apophysis with adjacent soft-tissue swelling
B. Fragmentation of the inferior pole of the patella
C. A subchondral radiolucent lesion involving the lateral aspect of the medial femoral condyle
D. A destructive metaphyseal lesion with periosteal elevation and an associated soft-tissue mass
E. Uniform narrowing of the tibiofemoral joint space with marginal osteophyte formation

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical presentation is classic for Osgood-Schlatter disease, a traction apophysitis caused by repetitive stress from the patellar tendon at the tibial tubercle in a skeletally immature athlete. The diagnosis is usually clinical, but radiographs may be obtained when symptoms are atypical, severe, unilateral, or persistent. A lateral radiograph may demonstrate fragmentation, irregularity, or increased density of the tibial tubercle apophysis, sometimes with overlying soft-tissue swelling or thickening of the distal patellar tendon. Normal inflammatory markers support a noninfectious, noninflammatory process. Choice B describes Sinding-Larsen-Johansson syndrome, which affects the inferior pole of the patella and causes tenderness at that location rather than at the tibial tubercle. Choice C is characteristic of osteochondritis dissecans, which commonly involves the lateral aspect of the medial femoral condyle and may cause poorly localized pain, swelling, or mechanical symptoms. Choice D suggests an aggressive bone tumor such as osteosarcoma; this would more often present with progressive pain, possible rest or nighttime symptoms, and aggressive radiographic features. Choice E describes degenerative osteoarthritis, which is unlikely in an adolescent and would not produce isolated tibial tubercle tenderness.

Question 74  [ID: 33fa6c495182463d913e1a53a599ee93]
A 72-year-old man presents with 8 months of progressive aching pain and heaviness in both buttocks and thighs. The symptoms begin after he walks approximately two blocks or stands for several minutes and resolve when he sits or leans forward over a shopping cart. He denies pain at rest, recent trauma, fever, weight loss, and bowel or bladder dysfunction. His medical history includes hypertension and osteoarthritis. On examination, lower-extremity strength is 5/5 bilaterally, patellar and Achilles reflexes are mildly diminished, and sensation to light touch is intact. Dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses are 2+ bilaterally. Straight-leg raise testing is negative. MRI demonstrates degenerative facet hypertrophy, thickening of the ligamentum flavum, and marked reduction of the central canal diameter at L3-L4 and L4-L5. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Lumbar spinal stenosis
B. Peripheral arterial disease
C. Lumbar disk herniation
D. Degenerative hip osteoarthritis
E. Polymyalgia rheumatica

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Lumbar spinal stenosis classically causes neurogenic claudication, characterized by bilateral buttock or leg pain, heaviness, or weakness that occurs with standing or walking and improves with sitting or forward flexion. Flexion increases the available canal area, explaining the characteristic relief obtained by leaning over a shopping cart. Advanced age, degenerative facet hypertrophy, ligamentum flavum thickening, and reduced central canal diameter further support this diagnosis. Neurologic examination may be normal or show mildly reduced reflexes. B is incorrect because peripheral arterial disease causes vascular claudication that is primarily related to exertion and improves with rest regardless of posture; diminished pulses, cool extremities, bruits, or other signs of impaired perfusion would also be expected. C is incorrect because a lumbar disk herniation more often produces acute or subacute unilateral radicular pain, sensory or motor deficits in a specific nerve-root distribution, and a positive straight-leg raise test. D is incorrect because hip osteoarthritis typically causes groin or anterior thigh pain, restricted hip range of motion, and pain with weight-bearing rather than posture-dependent bilateral leg symptoms. E is incorrect because polymyalgia rheumatica causes bilateral shoulder and hip girdle pain with prolonged morning stiffness and elevated inflammatory markers, not exertional leg symptoms relieved by spinal flexion.

Question 75  [ID: 3642131acafe4ec5ab34ae023cc6a60c]
A 13-year-old boy with obesity is brought to the clinic for 6 weeks of progressively worsening left knee and groin pain. He denies trauma, fever, or recent illness. His mother reports that he has developed a limp and has increasingly avoided bearing weight on the left leg. On examination, he is afebrile, and the left knee has no swelling, tenderness, or instability. The left foot rests in an externally rotated position. Radiographs demonstrate widening and irregularity of the proximal femoral growth plate with posterior-inferior displacement of the femoral head relative to the neck. Which of the following additional physical examination findings is most likely in this patient?

A. Obligatory external rotation of the affected hip during passive flexion
B. Increased internal rotation of the affected hip compared with the contralateral side
C. Pain and increased laxity with valgus stress testing of the knee
D. A palpable click during knee extension from a flexed position
E. Localized tenderness at the tibial tubercle with resisted knee extension

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. This adolescent has the classic history and imaging features of slipped capital femoral epiphysis. A characteristic examination finding is obligatory external rotation and abduction of the affected hip during passive flexion, known as the Drehmann sign. Internal rotation, flexion, and abduction are typically restricted, and the affected leg may rest in external rotation. Pain may be referred to the thigh or knee, so a normal knee examination in an adolescent with atraumatic knee pain should prompt careful evaluation of the hip.

B is incorrect because internal rotation is typically decreased and often painful rather than increased.

C is incorrect because pain and laxity with valgus stress suggest injury to the medial collateral ligament, which is generally associated with knee trauma and would not explain the proximal femoral radiographic abnormalities.

D is incorrect because a palpable click during knee movement is more suggestive of meniscal pathology or patellofemoral dysfunction. The patient's knee examination is normal, and the primary abnormality is proximal to the knee.

E is incorrect because tibial tubercle tenderness with resisted extension is characteristic of traction apophysitis at the tibial tubercle. That condition causes localized anterior knee pain and does not cause restricted hip motion, resting external rotation, or displacement at the proximal femoral growth plate.

Question 76  [ID: 36d5ec58fe3b4d61a4af713312df85ae]
A 16-year-old male competitive gymnast presents with 6 weeks of focal low back pain that worsens during back handsprings and other activities involving repeated lumbar extension. He denies radiation of pain, weakness, numbness, bowel or bladder dysfunction, fever, and recent trauma. Examination reveals focal tenderness over the lower lumbar region and pain with single-leg hyperextension. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, reflexes, and straight-leg raise testing are normal. Plain radiographs demonstrate a linear lucency through the L5 pars interarticularis without vertebral displacement. In addition to temporary activity restriction and a supervised rehabilitation program, which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy?

A. A short course of oral naproxen
B. Long-term oral oxycodone
C. Oral prednisone taper
D. Subcutaneous teriparatide
E. Oral gabapentin

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical presentation and pars interarticularis defect are consistent with spondylolysis, an overuse injury commonly seen in adolescent athletes who participate in activities requiring repetitive lumbar extension. Initial management is conservative and includes cessation of provoking activities, physical therapy emphasizing core stabilization and flexibility, and short-term analgesic therapy. A nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as naproxen is appropriate for short-term pain control when no contraindications are present. Long-term oxycodone is inappropriate because opioids are not indicated for this self-limited mechanical pain condition and carry substantial risks of dependence, sedation, and respiratory depression. Systemic prednisone has no established role and may impair bone health with unnecessary exposure. Teriparatide is used primarily for selected patients with severe osteoporosis and is not standard therapy for an adolescent stress injury. Gabapentin is most useful for neuropathic pain; this patient has no radicular symptoms or neurologic abnormalities.

Question 77  [ID: 37e3c8af520c4318a722002cab9c7ee7]
A 16-year-old boy presents with 3 months of progressive pain and swelling near his left knee. The pain initially occurred with activity but is now present at rest and frequently awakens him at night. He denies recent trauma, fever, or weight loss. Examination shows a firm, tender swelling over the distal thigh with reduced knee flexion. Laboratory studies reveal a mildly elevated alkaline phosphatase level; the complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and serum calcium level are normal. Plain radiographs demonstrate a mixed lytic and sclerotic lesion in the distal femoral metaphysis with cortical disruption, a sunburst periosteal reaction, and elevation of the periosteum. MRI shows extension into adjacent soft tissue without involvement of the neurovascular bundle. Chest CT shows no pulmonary lesions. Biopsy demonstrates pleomorphic malignant cells producing an eosinophilic extracellular matrix. Which of the following is the most appropriate neoadjuvant pharmaceutical regimen before definitive limb-sparing resection?

A. High-dose methotrexate, doxorubicin, and cisplatin
B. Vincristine, dactinomycin, and cyclophosphamide
C. Doxorubicin and ifosfamide alone
D. Imatinib monotherapy
E. Prednisone and rituximab

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical and radiographic findings indicate conventional high-grade osteosarcoma: an adolescent with progressive metaphyseal pain and swelling, a mixed destructive and sclerotic lesion, sunburst periosteal reaction, Codman-type periosteal elevation, and malignant cells producing osteoid. Standard treatment for localized disease consists of neoadjuvant multiagent chemotherapy followed by wide surgical resection and postoperative chemotherapy. The commonly used MAP regimen includes high-dose methotrexate, doxorubicin, and cisplatin. Histologic response to neoadjuvant therapy also provides important prognostic information. Choice B is a regimen commonly associated with treatment of Ewing sarcoma when combined with alternating ifosfamide and etoposide; it is not the preferred regimen for conventional osteosarcoma. Choice C includes agents with activity against some soft-tissue sarcomas, but doxorubicin and ifosfamide alone are not the standard first-line neoadjuvant regimen for this presentation. Choice D targets KIT or PDGFRA-driven gastrointestinal stromal tumors and selected other molecularly defined neoplasms. Choice E is used for certain CD20-positive lymphoid malignancies and has no standard role in treating this tumor.

Question 78  [ID: 3885fd1b13a240468a907ab693f1023a]
A 16-year-old competitive gymnast presents with 4 months of gradually worsening lower back pain. The pain is exacerbated by tumbling, prolonged standing, and extension of the trunk but improves with rest. She denies fever, nocturnal pain, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and lower-extremity weakness or numbness. Examination reveals increased lumbar lordosis, tight hamstrings, and a palpable step-off in the lower lumbar region. Pain is reproduced when she stands on one leg and extends her trunk. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes are normal. CBC, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein levels are within normal limits. Standing lateral radiography demonstrates bilateral defects of the L5 pars interarticularis with approximately 20% anterior translation of L5 relative to S1. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Lumbar disk herniation
B. Spondylolysis
C. Spondylolisthesis
D. Scheuermann kyphosis
E. Vertebral osteomyelitis

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: Spondylolisthesis is anterior displacement of one vertebral body relative to the vertebra below it. This adolescent athlete has characteristic extension-related pain, increased lumbar lordosis, hamstring tightness, a palpable step-off, and pain with single-leg hyperextension. Bilateral defects of the L5 pars interarticularis permit anterior translation of L5 over S1. A 20% translation is classified as grade I displacement. Lumbar disk herniation usually produces radicular pain, sensory changes, weakness, reflex abnormalities, or a positive straight-leg raise test. Spondylolysis is a defect or stress fracture of the pars interarticularis without vertebral displacement; the documented anterior translation establishes the more specific diagnosis. Scheuermann kyphosis causes rigid thoracic hyperkyphosis rather than lower lumbar pain with a step-off deformity. Vertebral osteomyelitis typically causes persistent pain and may be associated with fever, systemic risk factors, elevated inflammatory markers, and destructive imaging findings rather than pars defects and anterior translation.

Question 79  [ID: 38c5d02596c04f1ca774bc89a34cf0de]
A 42-year-old man presents with 4 months of gradually worsening right groin pain that increases with walking and climbing stairs. He has systemic lupus erythematosus treated with prednisone, 20 mg daily, for the past 2 years. He also consumes 4 to 5 alcoholic drinks nightly and smokes one pack of cigarettes per day. Examination shows an antalgic gait and pain with internal rotation of the right hip. Laboratory studies show a normal leukocyte count and erythrocyte sedimentation rate. Plain radiographs show no fracture or joint-space narrowing. MRI demonstrates a well-demarcated, serpiginous subchondral lesion of the right femoral head with preserved articular contour. In addition to arranging appropriate specialty follow-up, which of the following is the most important patient education intervention to reduce the risk of progression and involvement of the opposite side?

A. Begin daily high-impact weight-bearing exercise to increase bone remodeling
B. Coordinate a supervised reduction to the lowest effective glucocorticoid dose and strongly recommend alcohol and tobacco cessation
C. Increase dietary purine intake and discontinue dairy products
D. Avoid all physical activity indefinitely and remain on strict bed rest
E. Continue the current glucocorticoid dose and begin daily calcium supplementation as the sole preventive measure

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The clinical presentation and serpiginous subchondral MRI lesion are characteristic of avascular necrosis of the femoral head at a stage before articular collapse. Prolonged systemic glucocorticoid exposure and heavy alcohol use are major modifiable risk factors, and smoking may further impair osseous blood flow and healing. Patient education should emphasize coordination with the prescribing specialist to use the lowest effective glucocorticoid dose or a steroid-sparing regimen; glucocorticoids should not be stopped abruptly. Alcohol and tobacco cessation should also be strongly encouraged to reduce additional vascular and metabolic injury. A is incorrect because high-impact activity increases mechanical stress and may accelerate subchondral collapse; protected or reduced weight bearing may be advised depending on disease stage. C is incorrect because purine and dairy intake do not prevent progression. D is incorrect because indefinite bed rest causes deconditioning and is not recommended; activity modification is preferable. E is incorrect because calcium supplementation may support general bone health but does not mitigate the principal risk factors, and continuing unnecessary high-dose glucocorticoid therapy may promote progression or contralateral involvement.

Question 80  [ID: 38e861a39f5644fdaeef0a02ea8764f2]
A 6-year-old girl is evaluated for 3 months of persistent swelling of the left knee and right ankle. Her parent reports that she walks with a limp for approximately 1 hour after awakening but becomes more active later in the day. There is no history of trauma, fever, rash, weight loss, or recent infection. Examination shows warm, mildly tender effusions with limited range of motion at the affected sites. Laboratory studies reveal an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 48 mm/hr, negative rheumatoid factor, negative anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies, and an antinuclear antibody titer of 1:320. Lyme serology is negative. Radiographs show soft-tissue swelling without erosions. Based on these findings, which of the following diagnostic evaluations is most important to obtain next?

A. Arthrocentesis with crystal analysis
B. HLA-B27 testing
C. Slit-lamp ophthalmologic examination
D. Serum creatine kinase measurement
E. Magnetic resonance imaging of the lumbar spine

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: This child has oligoarticular juvenile rheumatoid arthritis, now more commonly called oligoarticular juvenile idiopathic arthritis. Persistent swelling in four or fewer joints, prolonged morning stiffness, elevated inflammatory markers, negative rheumatoid factor, and a positive antinuclear antibody are characteristic. Young age, female sex, oligoarticular involvement, and ANA positivity confer a particularly high risk of chronic anterior uveitis. Because ocular inflammation may be asymptomatic until irreversible visual damage occurs, routine slit-lamp examination is essential even when the child has no eye complaints.

A is incorrect because crystal-induced disease is exceedingly uncommon at this age, and the chronic presentation without acute severe pain is not suggestive of crystal deposition. Arthrocentesis would be appropriate if septic involvement were suspected.

B is incorrect because HLA-B27 testing is more relevant in older boys with enthesitis, hip involvement, sacroiliac symptoms, or acute symptomatic anterior uveitis. This patient's age, sex, peripheral oligoarticular pattern, and ANA positivity are more closely associated with chronic asymptomatic uveitis.

D is incorrect because creatine kinase is used to evaluate muscle injury or inflammatory myopathy. The primary findings here are persistent joint effusions and morning stiffness rather than proximal weakness.

E is incorrect because lumbar spine MRI is not indicated without back pain, neurologic deficits, or features suggesting axial involvement. The priority diagnostic study is ophthalmologic screening for clinically silent anterior uveitis.

Question 81  [ID: 394ba32c7b8f4b2d979ac2babcc3048b]
A 72-year-old man presents with several months of progressive, deep aching pain in the right hip and lower back. He denies recent trauma, fever, weight loss, or morning stiffness. Examination shows anterior bowing of the right tibia and increased warmth over the affected area without erythema or joint swelling. Serum alkaline phosphatase is markedly elevated, while calcium, phosphorus, and parathyroid hormone levels are within normal limits. Plain radiographs demonstrate cortical thickening, coarsened trabeculae, and enlargement of the pelvis and proximal femur. Which additional historical or physical examination finding is most likely in this patient?

A. Bilateral sensorineural hearing loss
B. Painful oral ulcers and genital lesions
C. Symmetric swelling of the metacarpophalangeal joints
D. Proximal muscle weakness that improves with repeated contraction
E. Hyperextensible skin with generalized joint hypermobility

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. The patient's advanced age, deep localized pain, tibial bowing, increased warmth, isolated elevation of alkaline phosphatase, and characteristic radiographic findings indicate Paget disease of bone. Cranial involvement can compress the vestibulocochlear nerve or alter the structures of the inner ear, making sensorineural hearing loss an important associated historical and examination finding. Patients may also report increasing hat size, headache, or facial deformity. B is associated with Behçet disease and is not explained by the laboratory or radiographic pattern. C suggests rheumatoid arthritis, which typically causes inflammatory, symmetric small-joint synovitis and prolonged morning stiffness. D describes a neuromuscular junction disorder such as Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome and does not account for the focal structural changes or elevated alkaline phosphatase. E is characteristic of a hereditary connective tissue disorder such as Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, which generally presents earlier in life and causes tissue laxity rather than cortical thickening and enlargement.

Question 82  [ID: 3b4edd39ad9c4ee4a6b98429321f62b4]
A 46-year-old woman presents with 4 months of progressive deep right groin pain that worsens with weight-bearing and occasionally occurs at night. She has systemic lupus erythematosus treated with prednisone for the past 3 years. Examination reveals an antalgic gait and painful limitation of internal rotation and abduction of the right hip. There is no erythema, warmth, or focal tenderness. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, C-reactive protein, and serum uric acid level are normal. Plain radiographs show no acute abnormality. MRI demonstrates a serpiginous subchondral low-signal band within the superior femoral head with surrounding marrow changes but no articular surface collapse. Her prednisone regimen is being reassessed, and she is referred for orthopedic evaluation. In addition to activity modification and analgesia, which of the following medications may be considered to inhibit osteoclast-mediated bone resorption and delay femoral head collapse in this patient?

A. Alendronate
B. Allopurinol
C. Colchicine
D. Methotrexate
E. Teriparatide

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has early, precollapse avascular necrosis of the femoral head associated with prolonged glucocorticoid exposure. Alendronate is a bisphosphonate that inhibits osteoclast-mediated bone resorption. It may be considered as an adjunctive treatment in selected patients with early-stage disease to help preserve femoral head architecture and delay collapse, although evidence is variable and orthopedic monitoring remains essential. Management also includes reducing modifiable risk factors, limiting weight-bearing when appropriate, providing analgesia, and considering joint-preserving procedures such as core decompression. Allopurinol decreases uric acid production and is used for chronic gout management; the presentation and normal uric acid level do not support gout. Colchicine treats and prevents crystal-induced inflammatory attacks but does not prevent structural collapse of the femoral head. Methotrexate is a disease-modifying antirheumatic drug used for inflammatory disorders and would not treat this glucocorticoid-associated complication. Teriparatide stimulates bone formation and is used primarily for patients with severe osteoporosis or high fracture risk; it is not the standard pharmacologic option for delaying femoral head collapse in this setting.

Question 83  [ID: 3c62291798f442828403c5bdbd7dc937]
A 48-year-old man presents with sudden pain near his right elbow after attempting to catch a falling refrigerator. He felt a distinct "pop" while his elbow was flexed against resistance. Over the next several hours, he developed swelling and bruising over the front of the elbow and proximal forearm. Examination shows a palpable defect in the antecubital region and proximal retraction of the muscle belly. Elbow flexion strength is mildly reduced, but forearm supination strength is markedly reduced. The examiner is unable to pass a finger beneath a cordlike structure in the antecubital fossa during the hook test. Plain radiographs show no fracture or dislocation. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Distal biceps tendon rupture
B. Lateral epicondylitis
C. Triceps tendon rupture
D. Brachialis muscle strain
E. Radial head fracture

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. A sudden eccentric load applied to a flexed elbow, followed by a pop, antecubital pain, bruising, a palpable defect, proximal muscle retraction, and pronounced weakness of forearm supination is characteristic of a distal biceps tendon rupture. An abnormal hook test, in which the examiner cannot hook a finger beneath the intact distal tendon, strongly supports the diagnosis. Elbow flexion may be only mildly impaired because the brachialis and brachioradialis remain functional. B is incorrect because lateral epicondylitis causes gradual-onset lateral elbow pain worsened by resisted wrist extension or gripping; it does not cause acute bruising, deformity, or supination weakness. C is incorrect because a triceps tendon rupture produces posterior elbow pain, swelling, and impaired active elbow extension rather than anterior bruising and supination weakness. D is incorrect because a brachialis strain may cause anterior elbow pain and painful flexion, but it does not typically produce proximal retraction, an abnormal hook test, or marked loss of supination strength. E is incorrect because a radial head fracture usually follows a fall on an outstretched hand and causes lateral elbow tenderness, restricted pronation and supination, and often an effusion or fracture finding on imaging; it does not explain the palpable antecubital defect or proximal muscle retraction.

Question 84  [ID: 3e70468a18ed4863a41e3ff1b9cff2ba]
A 46-year-old man presents with sudden onset of severe right knee pain and swelling that began overnight. He denies trauma, recent travel, or preceding gastrointestinal or genitourinary symptoms. Temperature is 37.4°C (99.3°F). Examination shows a warm, erythematous, markedly swollen right knee with a moderate effusion and severely limited range of motion due to pain. There are no skin plaques, nail changes, or palpable subcutaneous nodules. Synovial fluid contains 28,000 leukocytes/mm3 with 82% neutrophils; Gram stain is negative. Polarized light microscopy reveals rhomboid-shaped intracellular crystals with weakly positive birefringence. Radiographs demonstrate linear calcification within the articular cartilage. Given the patient's relatively young age for this presentation, which additional aspect of his history is most important to elicit?

A. A prior diagnosis or family history of hereditary iron overload
B. Recent ingestion of large quantities of shellfish and beer
C. A family history of HLA-B27-associated disease
D. A recent episode of urethritis or infectious diarrhea
E. Childhood exposure to untreated group A streptococcal infection

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The synovial fluid findings of rhomboid-shaped, weakly positively birefringent crystals and the radiographic finding of chondrocalcinosis indicate calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease, commonly called pseudogout. Although this condition usually occurs in older adults, presentation before age 60 should prompt evaluation for associated metabolic or hereditary disorders. Hemochromatosis is an important association, so the clinician should ask about a personal or family history of iron overload, liver disease, diabetes mellitus, skin hyperpigmentation, cardiomyopathy, or early arthropathy. Option B is more strongly associated with hyperuricemia and monosodium urate crystal deposition, or gout, which produces needle-shaped, strongly negatively birefringent crystals. Option C may support a seronegative spondyloarthropathy but does not explain the crystal morphology or chondrocalcinosis. Option D suggests reactive arthritis, which classically follows gastrointestinal or genitourinary infection and usually produces sterile inflammatory fluid without crystals. Option E is not associated with this acute presentation or the observed synovial fluid and radiographic findings.

Question 85  [ID: 4029427c121842ffa4f2460600368a4c]
A 24-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after diving headfirst into a shallow swimming pool. He reports severe posterior neck pain and inability to move his legs. Blood pressure is 88/54 mm Hg, pulse is 52/min, respirations are 18/min, and oxygen saturation is 96% on room air. He is alert and oriented. Examination shows midline tenderness at the base of the neck, flaccid paralysis of both lower extremities, marked weakness of both upper extremities, decreased sensation below the level of the clavicles, and absent rectal tone. His skin is warm and dry. Hemoglobin, serum electrolyte, and glucose levels are within normal limits. After airway protection, intravenous fluid resuscitation, vasopressor support, and rigid immobilization, CT demonstrates a C5 burst fracture with retropulsion of bony fragments and marked canal compromise. Which of the following is the most appropriate definitive intervention?

A. Administer high-dose intravenous methylprednisolone for 48 hours
B. Arrange urgent operative decompression and stabilization
C. Remove immobilization and begin passive range-of-motion exercises
D. Perform lumbar puncture to evaluate for occult hemorrhage
E. Discharge with a rigid collar and outpatient follow-up

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has an unstable cervical vertebral injury with retropulsed fragments, severe canal compromise, and acute neurologic deficits. After initial stabilization of the airway, breathing, circulation, blood pressure, and immobilization, urgent consultation for operative decompression and stabilization is indicated. Decompression relieves ongoing neural compression, while internal stabilization limits further displacement and neurologic deterioration. The hypotension, bradycardia, and warm skin are also consistent with loss of sympathetic tone, requiring fluids and vasopressors while definitive treatment is arranged. Choice A is incorrect because routine high-dose methylprednisolone is not recommended; evidence of meaningful benefit is limited, and treatment increases the risk of infection, gastrointestinal bleeding, and other complications. Choice C is incorrect because removing immobilization or initiating movement before stabilization could worsen displacement and neurologic injury. Choice D is incorrect because lumbar puncture has no therapeutic role and is unnecessary when CT has already demonstrated the structural cause of the deficits. Choice E is incorrect because outpatient management is inappropriate for an unstable injury with canal compromise, hemodynamic instability, and major neurologic deficits.

Question 86  [ID: 40b22c245edf4f01a1598ae66d477072]
A 24-year-old man presents with severe right shoulder pain and inability to move his arm after falling onto an outstretched hand during a basketball game. He holds the affected arm slightly abducted and externally rotated. Examination reveals a prominent acromion, loss of the normal rounded shoulder contour, and a palpable fullness inferior to the coracoid process. The radial pulse is intact. Radiographs show the humeral head positioned anteroinferior to the glenoid. Based on the anatomic relationship of the displaced structures, which additional neurologic finding is most likely?

A. Loss of sensation over the lateral shoulder with weakness of arm abduction from 15 to 90 degrees
B. Loss of sensation over the fifth digit with weakness of finger abduction and adduction
C. Loss of sensation over the palmar index finger with weakness of thumb opposition
D. Loss of sensation over the dorsal first web space with weakness of wrist extension
E. Loss of sensation over the medial forearm with preserved motor function of the hand

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The humeral head is displaced anteroinferiorly relative to the glenoid, placing the axillary nerve at risk because it passes through the quadrangular space and winds around the surgical neck of the humerus with the posterior circumflex humeral artery. Axillary nerve dysfunction produces decreased sensation over the lateral shoulder in the superior lateral cutaneous distribution and weakness of the deltoid, impairing arm abduction primarily from 15 to 90 degrees. Choice B describes an ulnar nerve deficit. Choice C describes a median nerve deficit. Choice D describes a radial nerve deficit. Choice E describes injury to the medial antebrachial cutaneous nerve, which is sensory and supplies the medial forearm but is not the nerve most closely associated with the displaced humeral head in this case.

Question 87  [ID: 40b8fbb9aba8477e8af215dd9427eb4b]
A 67-year-old woman presents with gradually progressive right knee pain for the past 4 years. The pain worsens with prolonged standing and walking and improves with rest. She reports stiffness upon awakening that lasts approximately 10 minutes. Examination shows crepitus with passive range of motion, mild bony enlargement, and tenderness along the joint line without warmth or erythema. Laboratory studies, including erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein, are within normal limits. Plain radiographs demonstrate asymmetric joint-space narrowing, subchondral sclerosis, and marginal osteophytes. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's findings?

A. Autoantibody-mediated synovial inflammation causing pannus formation and marginal erosions
B. Deposition of monosodium urate crystals triggering neutrophil-mediated inflammation
C. Imbalance between cartilage matrix synthesis and degradation leading to chondrocyte dysfunction and loss of articular cartilage
D. Immune-mediated inflammation at ligamentous and tendinous insertions causing new bone formation
E. Bacterial invasion of the synovial space causing rapid enzymatic destruction of cartilage

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The patient has osteoarthritis, characterized by activity-related pain, brief morning stiffness, crepitus, bony enlargement, normal inflammatory markers, asymmetric joint-space narrowing, subchondral sclerosis, and osteophyte formation. The underlying process involves chondrocyte dysfunction and an imbalance favoring degradation over synthesis of the extracellular cartilage matrix. Loss of proteoglycans and type II collagen causes progressive articular cartilage deterioration. Subchondral bone remodeling subsequently produces sclerosis, cysts, and marginal osteophytes. A is incorrect because autoantibody-mediated synovitis with pannus formation and marginal erosions describes rheumatoid arthritis, which typically causes prolonged morning stiffness and symmetric inflammatory polyarthritis. B is incorrect because monosodium urate deposition causes gout, which usually presents with acute, intensely inflammatory attacks and may produce punched-out erosions with overhanging edges. D is incorrect because immune-mediated enthesitis is characteristic of the seronegative spondyloarthropathies and is associated with inflammatory back pain, sacroiliitis, and syndesmophyte formation. E is incorrect because bacterial invasion causes septic arthritis, which typically presents acutely with severe pain, warmth, restricted motion, systemic symptoms, and inflammatory synovial fluid.

Question 88  [ID: 41559cb3231b4d05a631e085ed2633cb]
A 64-year-old man with poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus and peripheral neuropathy presents with 6 weeks of drainage from an ulcer beneath the right first metatarsal head. He reports increasing swelling and a dull ache but denies fever or chills. Temperature is 37.4°C (99.3°F), blood pressure is 132/78 mm Hg, and pulse is 88/min. Examination shows a 2.5-cm plantar ulcer with surrounding erythema and purulent drainage. A sterile metal probe passed through the ulcer contacts a firm underlying surface. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 10,800/mm3, erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 92 mm/hr, and C-reactive protein level of 8.6 mg/dL. Plain radiographs demonstrate focal cortical irregularity and adjacent lucency of the first metatarsal. The patient is hemodynamically stable, and antimicrobial therapy has not yet been initiated. Which of the following diagnostic studies would provide the most definitive confirmation of the suspected condition while also identifying the causative pathogen?

A. Culture of a superficial swab from the ulcer base
B. Serum procalcitonin measurement
C. Magnetic resonance imaging with intravenous contrast
D. Percutaneous bone biopsy for histopathologic examination and culture
E. Three-phase technetium-99m bone scintigraphy

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: The clinical findings strongly suggest contiguous osteomyelitis complicating a diabetic foot ulcer. A percutaneous bone biopsy submitted for histopathologic examination and culture is the diagnostic gold standard because it directly demonstrates inflammation or necrosis within bone and identifies the causative organism, allowing targeted antimicrobial therapy. Because the patient is clinically stable and has not yet received antibiotics, biopsy should ideally be obtained before antimicrobial treatment to maximize culture yield. A superficial swab (A) often reflects colonizing organisms and correlates poorly with pathogens present in bone. Serum procalcitonin (B) may support the presence of a systemic bacterial infection but cannot confirm the anatomic site or identify the pathogen. MRI with contrast (C) is the most sensitive imaging study for detecting early marrow involvement and defining the extent of disease, but imaging does not provide definitive microbiologic identification. Three-phase bone scintigraphy (E) is sensitive but has limited specificity, particularly in patients with neuropathic changes, prior trauma, surgery, or other causes of increased bone turnover.

Question 89  [ID: 41a580f4308641f5944985354d9a90f7]
A 49-year-old woman presents with 8 months of bilateral hand stiffness and aching that are most pronounced in the morning and improve with activity. She also reports difficulty swallowing dry foods, frequently drinks water while speaking, and describes a persistent gritty sensation in both eyes. Examination shows reduced salivary pooling, bilateral nontender parotid enlargement, and tenderness of several metacarpophalangeal joints without synovial swelling or deformity. A Schirmer test demonstrates 3 mm of moisture after 5 minutes. Laboratory studies show a positive antinuclear antibody, elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and positive rheumatoid factor. Hand radiographs show no erosions. Which of the following additional serologic findings would most strongly support the suspected unifying diagnosis?

A. Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies
B. Anti-centromere antibodies
C. Anti-Ro/SSA and anti-La/SSB antibodies
D. Anti-Jo-1 antibodies
E. Anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The combination of persistent ocular and oral dryness, reduced tear production on Schirmer testing, parotid enlargement, nonerosive joint symptoms, positive antinuclear antibody, and positive rheumatoid factor is most strongly supported by anti-Ro/SSA and anti-La/SSB antibodies. Anti-Ro/SSA is the more sensitive antibody, whereas anti-La/SSB is more specific when present with anti-Ro/SSA. These antibodies also identify patients at increased risk for systemic manifestations and, during pregnancy, fetal congenital heart block. Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies are highly specific for rheumatoid arthritis, which more commonly causes persistent synovitis and marginal erosions. Anti-centromere antibodies are associated with limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis. Anti-Jo-1 antibodies are associated with antisynthetase syndrome, which typically presents with inflammatory myopathy, interstitial lung disease, mechanic's hands, and arthritis. Anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies are specific for systemic lupus erythematosus and often correlate with lupus nephritis, but they do not best fit this patient's prominent glandular dryness and abnormal Schirmer test.

Question 90  [ID: 42f8a0e436204040877f2c0d5f12a887]
A 13-year-old boy with obesity presents with 6 weeks of progressive left groin pain that radiates to the medial thigh and knee. He denies trauma, fever, or recent illness. His temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F). He walks with an antalgic gait, and the affected lower extremity is held in external rotation. Passive flexion of the left hip produces obligatory external rotation, and internal rotation is markedly limited. Laboratory studies, including a complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level, are within normal limits. An anteroposterior radiograph shows that a line drawn along the superior border of the femoral neck does not intersect the femoral head. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's findings?

A. Ischemic necrosis of the femoral head caused by disruption of its retinacular blood supply
B. Mechanical failure through the hypertrophic zone of the proximal femoral physis, allowing posteroinferior displacement of the epiphysis relative to the femoral neck
C. Autoimmune inflammation of the synovium with pannus formation and progressive articular erosion
D. Bacterial seeding of the joint space followed by neutrophilic destruction of articular cartilage
E. Traction-induced fragmentation of the tibial tubercle apophysis during rapid growth

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. The patient's adolescent age, obesity, progressive groin pain referred to the knee, externally rotated gait, loss of internal rotation, obligatory external rotation with hip flexion, and abnormal line along the superior femoral neck are characteristic of mechanical failure at the proximal femoral growth plate. The failure occurs through the hypertrophic zone, where physeal cartilage is relatively vulnerable to shear stress during rapid growth. The femoral epiphysis remains seated in the acetabulum while the femoral neck and metaphysis move anterosuperiorly, which is conventionally described as posteroinferior displacement of the epiphysis relative to the neck.

A is incorrect because ischemic necrosis of the femoral head can cause hip pain and limited motion, but it is caused by impaired blood flow rather than primary failure through the physis. It may occur as a complication after the underlying condition is treated or manipulated.

C is incorrect because autoimmune synovitis would more commonly produce inflammatory symptoms such as prolonged morning stiffness, joint swelling, and possible involvement of multiple joints. The normal inflammatory markers and characteristic rotational findings do not support this mechanism.

D is incorrect because bacterial infection of a joint generally presents acutely with severe pain, fever, inability to bear weight, restricted motion in all directions, and elevated inflammatory markers.

E is incorrect because tibial tubercle apophyseal injury causes localized anterior knee pain and tenderness, usually worsened by running, jumping, or resisted knee extension. It does not explain restricted hip rotation or the proximal femoral radiographic abnormality.

Question 91  [ID: 437ed346122c4ff98e149caaf26b9008]
A 48-year-old woman presents with 8 months of bilateral hand and wrist pain accompanied by morning stiffness lasting approximately 45 minutes. She also reports persistent fatigue, a gritty sensation in both eyes, and difficulty swallowing dry foods unless she drinks water. Examination shows mild, symmetric tenderness of the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints without synovial thickening or deformity. There is bilateral, nontender enlargement near the angles of the mandible and multiple dental caries. Schirmer testing demonstrates 3 mm of wetting in 5 minutes. Laboratory studies reveal an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, positive antinuclear antibodies, positive rheumatoid factor, and antibodies to Ro/SSA and La/SSB. Radiographs of the hands show no erosions. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Rheumatoid arthritis
B. Systemic lupus erythematosus
C. Sjögren syndrome
D. Systemic sclerosis
E. Sarcoidosis

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: Sjögren syndrome is the most likely diagnosis. The characteristic findings are keratoconjunctivitis sicca, xerostomia, recurrent dental caries, salivary gland enlargement, and symmetric nonerosive joint symptoms. Reduced wetting on Schirmer testing provides objective evidence of decreased tear production, while anti-Ro/SSA and anti-La/SSB antibodies strongly support the diagnosis. Rheumatoid factor can be positive in Sjögren syndrome and does not establish rheumatoid arthritis. Rheumatoid arthritis typically produces persistent inflammatory synovitis and may cause marginal erosions; prominent ocular and oral dryness with anti-SSA and anti-SSB antibodies favors Sjögren syndrome. Systemic lupus erythematosus can cause nonerosive joint symptoms and anti-SSA positivity, but patients typically have additional findings such as photosensitive rash, cytopenias, nephritis, low complement levels, or anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies. Systemic sclerosis is associated with Raynaud phenomenon, skin thickening, telangiectasias, and esophageal dysmotility rather than this combination of glandular dryness and antibody findings. Sarcoidosis can cause salivary gland enlargement and ocular disease, but it is more commonly associated with pulmonary findings, granulomatous inflammation, and hilar lymphadenopathy; it does not typically cause this serologic pattern.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Question 92  [ID: 44223558e14a405bb972942155942f2a]
A 72-year-old woman presents with severe right-sided pain after falling against the edge of a bathtub 2 days ago. The pain is sharply localized over the right lateral torso and worsens with deep inspiration, coughing, and rotation. She denies fever, substernal pressure, hemoptysis, or loss of consciousness. Her temperature is 36.8°C (98.2°F), blood pressure is 138/82 mm Hg, pulse is 88/min, respirations are 20/min, and oxygen saturation is 97% on room air. Breath sounds are equal bilaterally, and there is no respiratory distress. A plain radiograph shows no pleural air, fluid collection, or focal pulmonary opacity. Which of the following physical examination findings would most strongly support the suspected injury?

A. Diffuse tenderness that decreases with firm palpation
B. Focal bony tenderness with palpable crepitus at the site of impact
C. A pleural friction rub that persists when the patient holds her breath
D. Pain reproduced primarily by resisted shoulder abduction
E. A unilateral reduction in tactile fremitus with hyperresonance to percussion

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Focal bony tenderness directly over the site of trauma, particularly when accompanied by palpable crepitus or a step-off, is the examination finding most supportive of a traumatic rib fracture. Pain commonly worsens with deep inspiration, coughing, and torso movement. Plain radiographs may fail to identify nondisplaced fractures, so the history and focused physical examination are important when no intrathoracic complication is evident. Choice A is nonspecific and is not characteristic of a focal osseous injury. Choice C is internally inconsistent because a true pleural friction rub generally disappears when breathing stops; a sound that persists may originate from the pericardium or superficial tissues. Choice D suggests an injury involving the shoulder girdle or associated musculature rather than a rib. Choice E suggests pneumothorax, which would typically produce decreased breath sounds, reduced fremitus, and hyperresonance; these findings are absent in this patient, and imaging shows no pleural air.

Question 93  [ID: 447978ccadd64c0cb500b9b016db7159]
A 76-year-old woman is brought to the emergency department after falling from standing height while walking to the bathroom. She reports severe left groin pain and cannot bear weight. Her left leg appears shortened and externally rotated. Radiographs demonstrate a displaced linear lucency through the proximal femur immediately distal to the femoral head. She undergoes operative treatment and is preparing for discharge. Her medical history includes hypertension and gastroesophageal reflux disease. She has never undergone bone-density testing. Laboratory studies show a serum creatinine of 0.8 mg/dL and a serum calcium concentration of 9.1 mg/dL. In addition to physical rehabilitation and a home fall-hazard assessment, which of the following is the most appropriate measure to reduce this patient's risk of a future similar event?

A. Begin estrogen-progestin therapy for long-term skeletal protection
B. Recommend calcium supplementation alone and repeat radiographs in 1 year
C. Initiate antiresorptive therapy after ensuring adequate calcium and vitamin D status
D. Defer preventive pharmacotherapy unless dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry shows a T-score below −2.5
E. Recommend prolonged activity restriction to minimize the risk of another fall

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: This older adult sustained a low-energy injury involving the proximal femur, which is sufficient clinical evidence of skeletal fragility and places her at high risk for another major injury. Pharmacologic therapy should not be delayed while awaiting bone-density results. After confirming adequate calcium and vitamin D status and evaluating renal function, antiresorptive treatment—such as a bisphosphonate—is appropriate. Intravenous zoledronic acid is often useful after operative treatment of this type of injury, particularly when adherence or gastrointestinal tolerance is a concern. Rehabilitation, weight-bearing as directed, vision and medication review, and correction of home fall hazards are also important preventive measures.

A is incorrect because systemic estrogen-progestin therapy is not first-line treatment for an older patient with established fragility and carries thromboembolic, cardiovascular, and malignancy risks. B is incorrect because calcium alone is inadequate secondary prevention in a patient at very high risk; follow-up radiographs do not assess overall skeletal fragility. D is incorrect because a low-energy injury at this site is an indication for treatment regardless of the dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry result, although testing may still be useful for baseline assessment and monitoring. E is incorrect because prolonged inactivity promotes deconditioning, worsens balance, and increases thromboembolic and future fall risk; supervised rehabilitation and progressive activity are preferred.

Question 94  [ID: 44a4e23dbc5e4e3a8fee7b775e5d5696]
A 62-year-old man with type 2 diabetes mellitus presents with 18 hours of rapidly worsening right knee pain, swelling, and inability to bear weight. He reports fever and chills but denies recent trauma. His temperature is 39.1°C (102.4°F), blood pressure is 128/76 mm Hg, and pulse is 108/min. The right knee is warm, erythematous, and markedly swollen, with severe pain during both active and passive range of motion. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 15,800/mm3 and an elevated C-reactive protein level. Arthrocentesis yields cloudy, purulent fluid with 92,000 leukocytes/mm3, 94% neutrophils, and no crystals. Blood and synovial fluid cultures are obtained. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate management?

A. Begin oral indomethacin and reassess in 48 hours
B. Administer an intra-articular glucocorticoid injection
C. Initiate intravenous vancomycin plus ceftriaxone and arrange urgent drainage of the joint
D. Begin oral doxycycline while awaiting culture results
E. Apply a compression wrap and initiate physical therapy

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The patient's acute febrile monoarticular presentation, severe pain with passive motion, purulent synovial fluid, and markedly elevated synovial leukocyte count with neutrophil predominance require immediate intravenous antimicrobial therapy and prompt drainage. Treatment should not be delayed while awaiting culture results. Empiric therapy should cover methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus with vancomycin and gram-negative organisms with a third-generation cephalosporin such as ceftriaxone. Drainage may be accomplished by repeated aspiration, arthroscopic irrigation, or open drainage depending on the joint involved and clinical response. Choice A is incorrect because an NSAID alone does not address the underlying process and delays definitive treatment. Choice B is incorrect because intra-articular glucocorticoids can worsen an active microbial process and are contraindicated in this setting. Choice D is incorrect because oral doxycycline does not provide sufficiently broad or reliable empiric coverage for a severely ill patient with purulent joint fluid. Choice E is incorrect because supportive care and physical therapy are not appropriate until urgent antimicrobial treatment and drainage have been performed.
