Question 95  [ID: 461d4f3f448545b3bd7a424df3a79200]
A 57-year-old right-hand-dominant construction worker presents with severe right shoulder pain and weakness 3 days after falling from a ladder and grabbing a railing with his right hand. He felt a sudden tearing sensation and has since been unable to raise the affected arm overhead. Examination shows no deformity or ecchymosis. Passive range of motion is nearly full, but active abduction is markedly limited. Strength is 3/5 with abduction and external rotation. The drop-arm and external rotation lag tests are positive. Sensation and distal pulses are intact. Plain radiographs show no fracture, dislocation, or significant degenerative changes. Which of the following diagnostic studies is most appropriate to confirm and characterize the suspected injury?

A. Computed tomography of the shoulder without contrast
B. Magnetic resonance imaging of the shoulder without contrast
C. Radionuclide bone scan
D. Electromyography and nerve conduction studies
E. Repeat plain radiographs in 2 weeks

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Magnetic resonance imaging without contrast is the preferred study for confirming and characterizing a suspected traumatic full-thickness rotator cuff tear. This patient has acute pain after a traction injury, preserved passive motion, profound weakness of active abduction and external rotation, and positive drop-arm and external rotation lag tests. MRI can identify the involved tendon or tendons, determine whether a tear is partial or full thickness, measure tendon retraction, assess muscle atrophy and fatty infiltration, and evaluate associated pathology. These findings are important for treatment planning, particularly in an active patient with an acute traumatic injury who may require surgical repair.

A is incorrect because computed tomography is most useful for evaluating complex fractures and bony architecture. It provides limited soft-tissue contrast and is not the preferred test for this suspected injury.

C is incorrect because a radionuclide bone scan detects areas of increased bone turnover and may help identify occult fractures, infection, or metastatic disease. It does not adequately define tendon integrity.

D is incorrect because electromyography and nerve conduction studies evaluate peripheral nerve and neuromuscular disorders. They may be considered if weakness is suspected to result from suprascapular neuropathy, cervical radiculopathy, or another neurologic process, but the acute traumatic mechanism and examination findings favor structural tendon disruption.

E is incorrect because repeat radiographs may reveal an initially occult fracture but cannot confirm or characterize a soft-tissue tear. The normal initial radiographs do not exclude the suspected injury.

Question 96  [ID: 4625032930e04f9c9a1c099c8a9c8b69]
A 16-year-old boy presents with a painless, firm bump near his right knee that he first noticed 1 year ago. He reports that it has enlarged slowly as he has grown. He denies trauma, night pain, fever, weight loss, weakness, or numbness. Examination shows a hard, immobile, nontender prominence over the medial distal thigh without overlying skin changes. He has full range of motion, normal strength, intact sensation, and normal distal pulses. Complete blood count, alkaline phosphatase, and inflammatory markers are within reference ranges. Plain radiographs demonstrate a pedunculated metaphyseal outgrowth whose cortex and medullary canal are continuous with those of the adjacent distal femur. There is no cortical destruction or associated soft-tissue mass. Which of the following is the most appropriate counseling and follow-up plan for this patient?

A. Provide clinical observation and advise prompt reevaluation for new pain, rapid enlargement, neurologic symptoms, vascular symptoms, or continued growth after skeletal maturity
B. Recommend immediate excision because these lesions routinely progress to malignancy during adolescence
C. Begin prophylactic systemic chemotherapy to prevent local invasion and metastatic spread
D. Arrange urgent radiation therapy because surgical intervention may stimulate aggressive transformation
E. Reassure the patient that no future evaluation is necessary, even if the lesion becomes painful or enlarges after skeletal maturity

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The radiographic continuity of the lesion's cortex and medullary canal with the adjacent structure, combined with slow growth during adolescence and the absence of pain or destructive features, is characteristic of an osteochondroma. An asymptomatic solitary lesion is generally managed with observation rather than immediate intervention. Patient education should emphasize warning signs that warrant reevaluation, including new or increasing pain, rapid enlargement, growth after skeletal maturity, restricted motion, or neurologic or vascular symptoms. These changes may indicate mechanical complications or, rarely, malignant transformation.

B is incorrect because solitary lesions have a low risk of malignant transformation and do not require routine excision when asymptomatic. Excision is considered for pain, functional impairment, neurovascular compression, suspicious growth, or concerning imaging changes.

C is incorrect because chemotherapy has no role in managing an uncomplicated benign lesion of this type.

D is incorrect because radiation therapy is not indicated and may itself increase the long-term risk of secondary malignancy.

E is incorrect because reassurance should include clear return precautions. New pain or enlargement after skeletal maturity requires reassessment for complications or malignant change.

Question 97  [ID: 46f12e86daf549f895bf1c518c5153db]
A 72-year-old man presents with gradually worsening deep pain in his pelvis and lower back. He reports that his hat size has increased over the past several years and that he has progressive difficulty hearing conversations. Examination shows frontal bossing and warmth over the tibia. Laboratory studies reveal an alkaline phosphatase level of 680 U/L, with normal serum calcium, phosphorus, and parathyroid hormone levels. Radiographs of the skull demonstrate areas of patchy sclerosis with cortical thickening, and pelvic imaging shows coarse trabeculae and enlargement of the affected structures. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for these findings?

A. Autoantibody-mediated stimulation of osteoclast precursors causing diffuse loss of mineral density
B. Excessive parathyroid hormone secretion causing subperiosteal resorption and replacement by fibrous tissue
C. Disorganized remodeling initiated by excessive osteoclastic resorption followed by increased, structurally unsound osteoblastic formation
D. Defective type I collagen synthesis causing impaired tensile strength and recurrent fractures beginning in childhood
E. Failure of osteoclast-mediated resorption due to impaired acidification of the resorption lacuna

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The patient has Paget disease of bone, characterized by focal areas of accelerated and disorganized remodeling. The process typically begins with excessive osteoclast-mediated resorption, followed by compensatory increases in osteoblast activity. The newly deposited matrix is arranged in a disorganized mosaic pattern and is enlarged, hypervascular, and mechanically weak. Increased osteoblast activity produces the markedly elevated alkaline phosphatase level, while serum calcium and phosphorus usually remain normal. Skull involvement can cause increased hat size, frontal bossing, and hearing loss; radiographs may show patchy sclerosis described as a cotton-wool appearance. C is correct because it describes the characteristic coupling of excessive resorption with subsequent disorganized formation. A is incorrect because autoantibody-mediated osteoclast activation is not the established mechanism and diffuse mineral loss would be more consistent with osteoporosis. B is incorrect because hyperparathyroidism typically causes hypercalcemia, hypophosphatemia, subperiosteal resorption, and osteitis fibrosa cystica. D is incorrect because defective type I collagen causes osteogenesis imperfecta, which usually presents earlier with recurrent fractures, blue sclerae, and dentinogenesis imperfecta. E is incorrect because defective osteoclast acidification causes osteopetrosis, producing diffusely dense but brittle structures due to failure of resorption rather than accelerated remodeling.

Question 98  [ID: 47ef8a1127e247f1ad66e908db4506c0]
A 6-week-old infant is brought to the clinic because the parents have noticed that the infant consistently holds the head tilted toward the right with the chin rotated toward the left. The infant was delivered at 39 weeks after a pregnancy complicated by breech positioning. Examination reveals a firm, nontender 1.5-cm enlargement along the right sternocleidomastoid muscle and limited passive rotation of the head toward the right. The neurologic examination is normal. Hip examination is stable, and ultrasonography shows no abnormalities. The posterior right side of the skull is mildly flattened. Which of the following parental counseling recommendations is most appropriate to improve the infant's range of motion and prevent progression of cranial asymmetry?

A. Place the infant prone for sleep and alternate the direction of the head each night
B. Use a soft cervical collar continuously until full passive range of motion returns
C. Perform gentle passive neck-stretching exercises, encourage supervised prone play while awake, and vary the infant's position during feeding and play
D. Avoid movement of the infant's neck until the sternocleidomastoid enlargement resolves spontaneously
E. Use a cranial molding helmet immediately without initiating positioning or stretching measures

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: This infant's persistent head tilt toward the affected side, rotation of the chin toward the opposite side, limited passive range of motion, and sternocleidomastoid enlargement are characteristic of congenital muscular torticollis. Early caregiver-directed passive stretching and positional therapy are first-line measures. Supervised prone play while awake, alternating the infant's orientation in the crib, changing feeding positions, and placing toys or visual stimuli on the side that encourages active rotation can improve range of motion and reduce persistent external pressure on the flattened occiput. These measures help prevent progression of positional plagiocephaly. Option A is incorrect because infants should always be placed supine for sleep to reduce the risk of sudden infant death syndrome; prone positioning is appropriate only while the infant is awake and directly supervised. Option B is incorrect because continuous soft-collar use is not routine first-line management and may limit active movement or cause skin complications. Option D is incorrect because delaying movement can worsen contracture, restricted range of motion, and craniofacial asymmetry. Option E is incorrect because helmet therapy is generally reserved for persistent moderate-to-severe cranial asymmetry that does not improve with repositioning and physical therapy, typically after an adequate trial of conservative measures.

Question 99  [ID: 48949012b0c74b40986f188dc58b0630]
A 16-year-old competitive gymnast presents with 6 weeks of progressively worsening focal low back pain. The pain increases during back walkovers and other activities involving repetitive extension and rotation and improves with rest. She denies fever, weight loss, morning stiffness, radiation of pain, weakness, numbness, and bowel or bladder dysfunction. Examination demonstrates focal tenderness near the lumbosacral junction and reproduction of pain with single-leg hyperextension. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, reflexes, and straight-leg raise testing are normal. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein are within normal limits. Anteroposterior and lateral radiographs show no vertebral displacement, fracture, or destructive lesion. MRI demonstrates focal marrow edema involving the posterior elements of L5 without disc abnormalities or neural compression. Which of the following additional imaging findings would most specifically confirm the suspected structural lesion?

A. A focal cortical lucency through the pars interarticularis of L5 on thin-slice CT
B. A posterolateral nucleus pulposus extrusion compressing the S1 nerve root on MRI
C. Bilateral sacroiliac joint erosions and subchondral sclerosis on pelvic radiography
D. Vertebral endplate destruction with adjacent disc enhancement on contrast-enhanced MRI
E. Anterior displacement of L5 relative to S1 exceeding 25% on lateral radiography

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is characteristic of spondylolysis, a stress injury or defect of the pars interarticularis that commonly occurs in adolescent athletes exposed to repetitive lumbar extension and rotation. Early radiographs may be normal. MRI can detect an early stress reaction by demonstrating marrow edema before a cortical defect becomes radiographically apparent. Thin-slice CT provides the most specific depiction of the bony anatomy and confirms the lesion by showing a cortical lucency or defect through the pars interarticularis. Choice B describes disc herniation with radiculopathy, which would more commonly produce radiating pain, sensory changes, weakness, reflex abnormalities, or a positive straight-leg raise test. Choice C suggests inflammatory sacroiliitis, which is associated with inflammatory back pain, prolonged morning stiffness, and possible elevation of inflammatory markers. Choice D suggests vertebral osteomyelitis with discitis; this is unlikely in an afebrile patient with normal inflammatory markers and no systemic symptoms. Choice E describes grade II spondylolisthesis. Vertebral displacement can occur as a complication of bilateral pars defects, but displacement is not required to confirm the underlying lesion and is absent on this patient's initial radiographs.

Question 100  [ID: 48b5ae6f7f974b6d9f8f66570cab3e15]
A 24-year-old man presents with 5 days of worsening pain and swelling of the right knee and left ankle. Three weeks ago, he experienced several days of dysuria and scant urethral discharge that resolved without treatment. He now also reports redness of both eyes and pain at the back of his left heel. His temperature is 37.4°C (99.3°F). Examination shows conjunctival injection without purulent drainage, a moderate right knee effusion, swelling and tenderness of the left ankle, and tenderness at the insertion of the Achilles tendon. Several shallow, painless ulcers are present on the oral mucosa. Arthrocentesis of the right knee yields cloudy fluid with a leukocyte count of 18,000/mm3, predominantly neutrophils; Gram stain and culture are negative, and no crystals are identified. A nucleic acid amplification test of the urine is positive for Chlamydia trachomatis. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Disseminated gonococcal infection
B. Reactive arthritis
C. Rheumatoid arthritis
D. Septic arthritis
E. Gout

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. Reactive arthritis is a sterile inflammatory arthritis that develops after an infection, most commonly genitourinary infection with Chlamydia trachomatis or gastrointestinal infection with organisms such as Salmonella, Shigella, Campylobacter, or Yersinia. It typically presents 1 to 4 weeks after the inciting infection with asymmetric oligoarthritis affecting the lower extremities. Associated findings include urethritis, conjunctivitis or anterior uveitis, enthesitis such as Achilles tendon pain, and painless oral ulcers. The inflammatory synovial fluid is culture-negative and lacks crystals.

A is incorrect because disseminated gonococcal infection usually causes migratory polyarthralgia, tenosynovitis, and scattered pustular skin lesions or a purulent monoarthritis. Synovial cultures may be negative, but the preceding chlamydial infection, asymmetric lower-extremity oligoarthritis, conjunctivitis, enthesitis, and oral ulcers favor a postinfectious sterile process.

C is incorrect because rheumatoid arthritis classically causes chronic, symmetric inflammatory polyarthritis involving the small joints of the hands and feet. The acute asymmetric lower-extremity distribution and temporal relationship to urethritis are not typical.

D is incorrect because septic arthritis usually presents as an acutely painful monoarthritis, often with fever and a substantially elevated synovial leukocyte count. Although infection must be excluded in any acute effusion, the negative synovial Gram stain and culture, multiple involved sites, and characteristic extra-articular findings support a sterile inflammatory arthritis.

E is incorrect because gout results from monosodium urate crystal deposition and commonly presents with abrupt, severe monoarthritis. Polarized microscopy would demonstrate needle-shaped, negatively birefringent crystals, which are absent in this patient's synovial fluid.

Question 101  [ID: 48ca6ccb992640bea607b7410b149681]
A 34-year-old man presents with acute lower back pain that began yesterday while he was lifting a heavy box and simultaneously twisting his torso. The pain is localized to the lumbar region and worsens with bending, rotation, and prolonged standing. He denies fever, weight loss, lower-extremity weakness, sensory changes, saddle anesthesia, and bowel or bladder dysfunction. Examination shows bilateral lumbar paraspinal tenderness and palpable muscle tightness. Active range of motion is limited by pain. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and deep tendon reflexes are normal, and straight-leg raising does not reproduce the pain. Lumbar radiographs show normal alignment without fracture or destructive lesions. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's symptoms?

A. Mechanical disruption of paraspinal muscle-tendon fibers and supporting ligamentous fibers with a localized inflammatory response
B. Compression of a lumbar nerve root by posterolateral displacement of nucleus pulposus material
C. Hematogenous bacterial seeding of an intervertebral disc with extension into adjacent vertebral end plates
D. Autoimmune inflammation of the sacroiliac joints and spinal entheses causing progressive ankylosis
E. Loss of vertebral trabecular integrity resulting in an acute compression fracture

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. The abrupt onset of localized pain after lifting and twisting, pain with movement, paraspinal tenderness and tightness, normal neurologic findings, negative straight-leg raising, and normal radiographs indicate a mechanical soft-tissue injury. Excessive tensile loading causes microscopic disruption of muscle-tendon fibers, ligamentous fibers, or both. Damaged cells release inflammatory mediators, producing localized edema, nociceptor activation, pain, and protective muscle spasm.

B is incorrect because lumbar nerve root compression typically causes radiating pain in a dermatomal distribution and may produce sensory loss, weakness, altered reflexes, or a positive straight-leg-raising test.

C is incorrect because infection involving an intervertebral disc and adjacent vertebral end plates generally produces persistent pain and may be associated with fever, elevated inflammatory markers, bacteremia risk factors, or destructive imaging abnormalities.

D is incorrect because an autoimmune enthesitis typically causes chronic inflammatory back pain characterized by morning stiffness, improvement with exercise, nocturnal symptoms, and possible sacroiliac abnormalities rather than abrupt pain after a discrete mechanical event.

E is incorrect because vertebral compression fractures are most common in patients with osteoporosis, advanced age, malignancy, or substantial axial trauma. Such a fracture would generally be visible on imaging and would not primarily produce bilateral paraspinal soft-tissue tenderness with normal vertebral alignment.

Question 102  [ID: 4934c7bafe444feda716ef44176154c9]
A 15-year-old boy is evaluated for a progressively rounded upper back first noticed 2 years ago. He reports mild, aching discomfort between the shoulder blades after prolonged sitting but denies trauma, weakness, paresthesias, or bowel and bladder dysfunction. Examination shows an accentuated posterior thoracic curvature that does not correct with active posture adjustment or spinal hyperextension. Neurologic examination is normal. Serum calcium, phosphate, alkaline phosphatase, and 25-hydroxyvitamin D levels are within reference ranges. Standing radiographs demonstrate a 67-degree posterior thoracic curvature, irregular vertebral endplates, intravertebral disc herniations, and anterior wedging greater than 5 degrees in three adjacent vertebral bodies. Which of the following structural abnormalities most directly produces this patient's spinal contour?

A. Asymmetric vertebral growth resulting in reduced anterior vertebral body height relative to posterior height
B. Bilateral defects of the pars interarticularis causing anterior translation of one vertebral body
C. Degeneration of the nucleus pulposus causing uniform narrowing of multiple intervertebral spaces
D. Failure of fusion of the posterior vertebral arches with protrusion of meningeal tissue
E. Lateral vertebral rotation caused by unequal growth of the right and left sides of the vertebral bodies

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has a rigid, exaggerated posterior thoracic curvature with anterior wedging of at least 5 degrees across three contiguous vertebrae, findings characteristic of structural adolescent kyphosis due to Scheuermann disease. Abnormal growth and endochondral ossification at the vertebral endplates produce relatively diminished anterior vertebral body height. Repeated across adjacent vertebrae, this wedge shape increases the posterior convexity of the thoracic spine. Endplate irregularities and Schmorl nodes, which represent herniation of disc material into adjacent vertebral bodies, commonly accompany this process. B is incorrect because pars interarticularis defects may cause spondylolisthesis, characterized by anterior vertebral translation rather than a multilevel posterior thoracic curvature. C is incorrect because disc degeneration can reduce disc height but does not explain the characteristic anterior wedging of contiguous vertebral bodies in an adolescent. D is incorrect because failure of posterior arch fusion causes spinal dysraphism and may be associated with a meningocele or myelomeningocele. E is incorrect because asymmetric right-to-left vertebral growth and vertebral rotation produce a lateral spinal curvature rather than the sagittal-plane deformity described.

Question 103  [ID: 4a970b4d69e745e4809b50a28eac2d11]
A 38-year-old man presents to the emergency department after landing awkwardly while playing basketball. He reports that his left knee was flexed when he felt a sudden pop and fell to the ground. He has immediate pain and is unable to bear weight. Examination shows swelling and tenderness inferior to the patella with a palpable soft-tissue defect. He cannot actively extend the knee or maintain a straight-leg raise, although passive range of motion is preserved. The extremity is neurovascularly intact. Plain radiographs show no acute fracture but demonstrate a high-riding patella. Which of the following is the most appropriate management?

A. Apply a knee immobilizer in full extension, restrict weight bearing, and arrange urgent orthopedic evaluation for operative repair
B. Apply a compression wrap and permit weight bearing as tolerated with outpatient physical therapy
C. Perform immediate closed reduction under procedural sedation
D. Administer an intra-articular glucocorticoid injection and begin active range-of-motion exercises
E. Place the knee in a hinged brace permitting early flexion and reassess in 6 weeks

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The sudden eccentric loading injury, palpable defect inferior to the patella, inability to actively extend the knee or perform a straight-leg raise, and high-riding patella indicate complete disruption of the patellar tendon. Initial management includes immobilizing the knee in full extension, restricting weight bearing or providing protected weight bearing with crutches, administering analgesia, and obtaining urgent orthopedic consultation. Complete disruption generally requires prompt operative repair to restore active extension. Choice B is inappropriate because compression, unrestricted weight bearing, and physical therapy alone are insufficient for a complete disruption. Choice C is used for a dislocation, which would typically produce obvious deformity and requires radiographic and clinical confirmation; it does not explain the palpable infrapatellar defect and patella alta. Choice D is contraindicated because glucocorticoid injection may impair tendon healing and does not restore continuity. Choice E allows flexion and delays definitive care, which may cause retraction, scarring, and a worse functional outcome.

Question 104  [ID: 4ace6ed83e3f46428254aef26364924e]
A 38-year-old man presents with 5 days of severe lower back pain that began while lifting a heavy box. The pain radiates from the right buttock down the posterolateral thigh and lateral leg to the dorsum of the foot. He reports tingling of the great toe but denies fever, weight loss, urinary retention, fecal incontinence, or perineal numbness. Examination shows decreased sensation over the dorsum of the right foot and weakness of right great-toe extension. Patellar and Achilles reflexes are symmetric. Complete blood count and erythrocyte sedimentation rate are normal. Plain radiographs show no fracture or destructive lesion. Which additional physical examination finding would most strongly support the suspected cause of this patient's symptoms?

A. Reproduction of right leg pain below the knee when the extended right leg is elevated to 40 degrees
B. Anterior thigh pain produced by extension of the right hip with the knee flexed
C. Groin pain and restricted motion during passive internal rotation of the right hip
D. Localized tenderness over the right greater trochanter while lying on the affected side
E. Loss of the right patellar reflex with weakness of knee extension

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has findings consistent with a posterolateral lumbar disk herniation affecting the L5 nerve root: pain radiating to the dorsum of the foot, sensory loss in the same distribution, and weakness of great-toe extension. Reproduction of radicular pain below the knee when the ipsilateral extended leg is elevated between approximately 30 and 70 degrees is a positive straight-leg-raise test and supports lumbosacral nerve root irritation from a herniated disk. Choice B describes a positive femoral nerve stretch test, which is associated with upper lumbar nerve root involvement and typically produces anterior thigh pain. Choice C suggests intra-articular hip disease, such as osteoarthritis. Choice D is characteristic of greater trochanteric pain syndrome and does not account for the neurologic deficits. Choice E indicates L3-L4 nerve root or femoral nerve dysfunction; L5 involvement generally causes weakness of great-toe dorsiflexion without a reliably diminished deep tendon reflex.

Question 105  [ID: 4adec14b3c444f4bb5cf23ebf6ac16a4]
A 62-year-old man presents for a postoperative follow-up visit 2 weeks after sustaining an injury when he stumbled while descending stairs. At the time of injury, he experienced a sudden tearing sensation above the right patella and was unable to straighten the affected lower extremity. Initial examination showed swelling, a palpable suprapatellar defect, and inability to perform a straight-leg raise. Radiographs demonstrated inferior displacement of the patella without fracture. He subsequently underwent operative repair. The incision is now clean and dry, and distal sensation and pulses are intact. He asks how to reduce his risk of disrupting the repair while recovering. Which of the following is the most appropriate patient education?

A. Begin unrestricted active extension exercises immediately to prevent weakness
B. Keep the affected joint continuously flexed to minimize tension on the repair
C. Use the prescribed brace in extension and follow a supervised, gradual range-of-motion and strengthening protocol
D. Avoid all weight-bearing and range-of-motion exercises for at least 6 months
E. Discontinue the brace once pain and swelling have resolved, regardless of functional strength

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The clinical findings are consistent with disruption of the quadriceps portion of the extensor mechanism. Following operative repair, prevention of rerupture requires protection of the repaired tissue with the prescribed brace, typically locked in extension during ambulation, followed by staged increases in range of motion and strengthening under surgical and rehabilitation guidance. Adherence to the individualized rehabilitation protocol is essential because advancing activity too rapidly can disrupt the repair, whereas prolonged immobilization can cause stiffness, weakness, and loss of function.

A is incorrect because immediate unrestricted active extension places excessive tensile stress on the healing repair and increases the risk of failure.

B is incorrect because continuous flexion increases tension across the repaired tissue and does not provide appropriate protection. The brace is generally maintained in extension initially, with flexion introduced gradually.

D is incorrect because complete avoidance of weight-bearing and motion for 6 months is unnecessarily restrictive and can cause severe stiffness, muscle atrophy, and impaired recovery. Protected weight-bearing and controlled motion are usually introduced according to protocol.

E is incorrect because pain relief and reduced swelling do not indicate that the repair has regained adequate tensile strength. Brace discontinuation should be based on healing, strength, functional control, and clearance from the treating clinician.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Question 106  [ID: 4e00e8f78d7441319360d8d072938301]
A 68-year-old woman presents with progressive rounding of her upper back, a 4-cm loss of height, and intermittent midline back discomfort over the past 2 years. She experienced menopause at age 49 and has never received hormone replacement therapy. She does not smoke or drink alcohol. Physical examination shows an accentuated forward thoracic curvature without focal neurologic deficits. Radiographs demonstrate anterior wedging of the T8 vertebral body and increased thoracic sagittal curvature. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry reveals a T-score of -2.8 at the femoral neck. Serum calcium, 25-hydroxyvitamin D, and creatinine levels are normal. She has no history of dysphagia, esophageal disease, or inability to remain upright after taking medications. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy to reduce her risk of additional vertebral fractures and progression of the deformity?

A. Alendronate administered orally once weekly
B. Calcitonin administered intranasally once daily
C. Ibuprofen administered orally on a scheduled basis
D. Raloxifene administered orally once daily
E. Teriparatide administered subcutaneously once daily

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's progressive thoracic curvature and height loss are due to vertebral body wedging in the setting of low bone mineral density. This represents structural kyphosis associated with an osteoporotic vertebral compression fracture. Alendronate, an oral bisphosphonate, is appropriate first-line pharmacologic therapy because it inhibits osteoclast-mediated bone resorption and reduces both vertebral and hip fracture risk. Her renal function is normal, and she has no esophageal disorder or inability to remain upright, so there is no identified contraindication to oral bisphosphonate therapy. Adequate calcium and vitamin D intake should also be ensured. Calcitonin may provide short-term analgesia after an acute vertebral compression fracture but is not preferred for long-term fracture prevention because of limited efficacy. Ibuprofen may reduce discomfort but does not improve bone density or prevent additional fractures. Raloxifene reduces vertebral fracture risk in selected postmenopausal patients but does not reduce hip fracture risk and carries a risk of venous thromboembolism; it is generally not preferred over a bisphosphonate in this setting. Teriparatide is an anabolic agent typically reserved for patients at very high fracture risk, such as those with multiple or recurrent fragility fractures, extremely low bone density, or failure of antiresorptive therapy.

Question 107  [ID: 4e0e4786b8cc444eb1c34e7997ec1366]
A 46-year-old woman presents with 5 months of progressive pain and swelling involving both hands and wrists. She reports stiffness lasting approximately 90 minutes each morning that improves with activity. Examination shows symmetric tenderness and boggy swelling of the wrists and second and third metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints. The distal interphalangeal joints are unaffected. Laboratory studies show a normocytic anemia, an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 58 mm/hr, and a C-reactive protein level of 3.2 mg/dL. Radiographs of the hands demonstrate periarticular osteopenia and small marginal erosions. Which of the following laboratory studies would be most specific for the suspected diagnosis and most useful for predicting progressive erosive disease?

A. Antinuclear antibody
B. Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody
C. Anti-double-stranded DNA antibody
D. Rheumatoid factor
E. Serum uric acid concentration

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody is highly specific for rheumatoid arthritis and may become detectable early in the clinical course. Its presence is also associated with a greater likelihood of persistent, progressive, and erosive disease. The patient's prolonged morning stiffness, symmetric involvement of the wrists, metacarpophalangeal joints, and proximal interphalangeal joints, sparing of the distal interphalangeal joints, elevated inflammatory markers, periarticular osteopenia, and marginal erosions strongly support this diagnosis. Antinuclear antibody is sensitive for several systemic autoimmune conditions but is nonspecific and may be positive in healthy individuals. Anti-double-stranded DNA antibody is relatively specific for systemic lupus erythematosus and is associated particularly with lupus nephritis, but it does not best fit this presentation. Rheumatoid factor is commonly present in rheumatoid arthritis and can provide prognostic information, but it is less specific than anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody because it may also be positive in chronic infections, other autoimmune conditions, malignancy, and healthy older adults. Serum uric acid concentration is neither sufficiently sensitive nor specific for diagnosing crystal-induced disease and does not predict the erosive pattern described here.

Question 108  [ID: 4e4c096e7dd54e71820aec84f937f4f2]
A 13-year-old girl is evaluated for uneven shoulders noticed by her mother during the past 6 months. She has no back pain, weakness, sensory changes, bowel or bladder dysfunction, or history of trauma. Menarche occurred 3 months ago. Examination shows elevation of the right shoulder and asymmetry of the waist. During forward flexion, a prominent right posterior thoracic rib contour is observed. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes are normal. A standing posteroanterior radiograph demonstrates a 28-degree rightward thoracic curvature with rotation of the involved vertebrae. Which of the following anatomic changes most directly produces the prominent posterior rib contour seen on examination?

A. Anterior displacement of the ribs on the convex side due to rotation of the vertebral bodies toward the concavity
B. Posterior displacement of the ribs on the convex side due to rotation of the vertebral bodies toward the convexity
C. Posterior displacement of the ribs on the concave side due to isolated rotation of the spinous processes toward the convexity
D. Symmetric posterior displacement of the ribs due to increased thoracic kyphosis
E. Inferior displacement of the ribs due to shortening of the paraspinal muscles on the convex side

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. A structural lateral curvature is accompanied by axial vertebral rotation. In a rightward thoracic curve, the vertebral bodies rotate toward the convexity, while the spinous processes rotate toward the concavity. This rotational deformity carries the ribs posteriorly on the convex side, producing the characteristic rib prominence that becomes more apparent during forward flexion.

A is incorrect because the vertebral bodies rotate toward the convexity, not the concavity, and the ribs on the convex side are displaced posteriorly rather than anteriorly.

C is incorrect because the prominent posterior ribs occur on the convex side. Spinous-process rotation toward the concavity is a secondary finding and does not independently account for the rib prominence.

D is incorrect because increased thoracic kyphosis would produce a more symmetric sagittal-plane prominence rather than a unilateral rib contour associated with axial rotation.

E is incorrect because asymmetric paraspinal muscle tension may accompany the deformity, but inferior rib displacement from muscle shortening is not the primary mechanism. The visible prominence results principally from vertebral rotation and associated posterior displacement of the ribs on the convex side.

Question 109  [ID: 4f593d16db40423182cc0c9ae034f438]
A 19-year-old collegiate volleyball player presents with right ankle pain and swelling 2 days after landing on another player's foot with the ankle inverted. She was able to bear weight immediately afterward but stopped playing because of pain. Examination shows mild ecchymosis and focal tenderness anterior and inferior to the lateral malleolus. There is no tenderness over the posterior edge of either malleolus, the base of the fifth metatarsal, or the navicular. Anterior drawer testing demonstrates mild laxity with a firm endpoint. Distal pulses, sensation, and motor function are intact. Plain radiographs show no fracture or dislocation. She reports two similar episodes during the previous season and asks how to reduce the likelihood of another episode after she completes rehabilitation. Which of the following is the most appropriate preventive recommendation?

A. Use a lace-up ankle brace during high-risk activities and perform ongoing balance and proprioceptive exercises
B. Avoid weight-bearing exercise for 6 months after symptoms resolve
C. Take an oral nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug before every athletic event
D. Perform static calf stretching alone immediately before competition
E. Wear rigid immobilization continuously throughout the athletic season

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical findings are consistent with a recurrent lateral ankle sprain, most likely involving the anterior talofibular ligament. Previous episodes substantially increase the risk of recurrence because residual deficits in proprioception, neuromuscular control, strength, and dynamic stability may persist after pain resolves. Ongoing balance training, such as single-leg stance or wobble-board exercises, reduces recurrence. A lace-up or semirigid ankle brace during practices and competition provides additional external support and is particularly beneficial for athletes with prior episodes.

B is incorrect because prolonged avoidance of weight-bearing causes deconditioning, weakness, and impaired proprioception. Functional rehabilitation with progressive weight-bearing is preferred once tolerated.

C is incorrect because prophylactic nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug use does not prevent recurrence and may cause gastrointestinal, renal, and cardiovascular adverse effects. Analgesics should not be used to mask symptoms before athletic participation.

D is incorrect because static calf stretching alone does not adequately address the impaired proprioception and neuromuscular control associated with recurrent episodes. Stretching may be one component of a broader conditioning program but is not sufficient by itself.

E is incorrect because continuous rigid immobilization is unnecessary and can cause stiffness, weakness, and reduced function. External support should permit functional activity and should be combined with rehabilitation rather than replace it.

Question 110  [ID: 4f6c9eb1097f482e90c29937548ba84e]
A 68-year-old man with type 2 diabetes mellitus presents with a 2-day history of fever and rapidly worsening pain and swelling of the right knee. He denies trauma. His temperature is 38.7°C (101.7°F), and the right knee is warm, erythematous, and held in slight flexion. Both active and passive range of motion cause severe pain. Arthrocentesis yields cloudy fluid with a leukocyte count of 92,000/mm3, 94% neutrophils, a low glucose concentration, and gram-positive cocci in clusters. Which structural feature most directly facilitates hematogenous entry of the responsible organism into the affected space?

A. Absence of a limiting basement membrane beneath the highly vascular lining
B. Avascularity of the lining and dependence on diffusion from adjacent cartilage
C. Presence of tight junctions forming a blood-tissue barrier
D. Continuous keratinization of the inner lining
E. Direct communication between the affected space and the lymphatic system

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. The synovial lining is highly vascular and lacks a well-defined limiting basement membrane. This anatomy allows circulating microorganisms to pass readily from synovial capillaries into the joint space during hematogenous spread. Once present, organisms stimulate an intense neutrophilic response; inflammatory cytokines, proteolytic enzymes, and bacterial products can rapidly damage articular cartilage. The acute monoarticular presentation, severe pain with passive movement, fever, and markedly elevated neutrophil count in the aspirated fluid support this mechanism. Gram-positive cocci in clusters are particularly suggestive of Staphylococcus aureus. B is incorrect because the synovium is vascular; articular cartilage, not the synovial lining, is avascular. C is incorrect because the synovial microvasculature does not form a restrictive barrier comparable to the blood-brain barrier. D is incorrect because synovium is not a keratinized epithelial surface. E is incorrect because lymphatic drainage does not create direct communication with the joint cavity and is not the primary route by which circulating organisms enter this space.

Question 111  [ID: 4fed8ab16aea485f8e41c1a49f058e10]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 4 months of progressively worsening pain and swelling involving both wrists and the proximal finger joints of both hands. She reports morning stiffness lasting approximately 2 hours and difficulty opening jars and typing. Examination shows symmetric tenderness, warmth, and boggy swelling of the wrists and multiple metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints. The distal interphalangeal joints are spared. Laboratory studies reveal an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, elevated C-reactive protein, positive rheumatoid factor, and high-titer anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies. Radiographs demonstrate periarticular osteopenia and small marginal erosions. A pregnancy test is negative, and her complete blood count, hepatic transaminases, and serum creatinine are normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial disease-modifying intervention?

A. Begin scheduled naproxen monotherapy
B. Begin weekly methotrexate with folic acid supplementation
C. Begin long-term oral prednisone monotherapy
D. Begin adalimumab monotherapy without a conventional agent
E. Begin allopurinol with colchicine prophylaxis

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has rheumatoid arthritis, supported by chronic symmetric involvement of the wrists, metacarpophalangeal joints, and proximal interphalangeal joints; prolonged morning stiffness; elevated inflammatory markers; positive rheumatoid factor and anti-CCP antibodies; and early marginal erosions. A disease-modifying antirheumatic drug should be started promptly to reduce disease activity and prevent progressive structural damage. Methotrexate is the preferred initial conventional disease-modifying agent for most nonpregnant patients with moderate-to-high disease activity and no major contraindications. Folic acid is administered to reduce adverse effects such as stomatitis, gastrointestinal symptoms, cytopenias, and hepatotoxicity. Baseline blood counts, hepatic function, renal function, pregnancy status, and relevant infection screening should be assessed before and during therapy. A is incorrect because naproxen can reduce pain and stiffness but does not prevent erosions or alter long-term disease progression. C is incorrect because glucocorticoids may be used briefly as bridging therapy while a disease-modifying agent takes effect, but long-term monotherapy causes substantial toxicity and does not represent appropriate definitive management. D is incorrect because biologic agents such as adalimumab are generally reserved for patients with an inadequate response or intolerance to conventional disease-modifying therapy; biologic monotherapy is not the preferred initial intervention in this presentation. E is incorrect because allopurinol and colchicine are used in the management of monosodium urate crystal disease, which typically causes episodic acute attacks rather than chronic symmetric small-joint involvement with anti-CCP positivity and marginal erosions.

Question 112  [ID: 518da4338bda43c2a445382bf359ff1f]
A 67-year-old woman presents with 2 years of progressively worsening right knee pain. The pain is exacerbated by walking, climbing stairs, and prolonged standing and improves with rest. She reports stiffness upon awakening that resolves within 15 minutes. She denies fever, recent trauma, joint locking, or episodes of instability. Examination reveals an antalgic gait and decreased knee flexion without erythema or increased warmth. Laboratory studies show a normal leukocyte count and erythrocyte sedimentation rate. Radiographs demonstrate asymmetric joint-space narrowing, subchondral sclerosis, and marginal bony outgrowths. Which additional physical examination finding is most likely in this patient?

A. Hard, nontender enlargement of the joint margins with coarse crepitus during passive motion
B. Boggy swelling with marked tenderness and increased warmth
C. Excessive anterior translation of the tibia during a Lachman test
D. A palpable click with acute joint-line pain during a McMurray test
E. Severe pain with minimal passive motion accompanied by a large, tense effusion

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. The patient's advanced age, activity-related pain relieved by rest, brief morning stiffness, normal inflammatory markers, and radiographic findings of asymmetric joint-space narrowing, subchondral sclerosis, and marginal bony outgrowths are characteristic of osteoarthritis. Typical examination findings include hard bony enlargement, coarse crepitus with active or passive motion, restricted range of motion, and possible varus or valgus deformity.

B is incorrect because boggy swelling, prominent warmth, and tenderness suggest active inflammatory synovitis, as may occur with rheumatoid arthritis or another inflammatory arthropathy.

C is incorrect because excessive anterior tibial translation on the Lachman test indicates anterior cruciate ligament insufficiency, which is more commonly associated with a traumatic twisting or hyperextension injury and subjective instability.

D is incorrect because a painful click during the McMurray test suggests a meniscal tear. Meniscal injury more often presents with joint-line tenderness, locking, catching, or symptoms following a twisting event.

E is incorrect because severe pain with minimal passive motion and a tense effusion raises concern for an acute intra-articular process such as septic arthritis or crystal-induced arthritis. These conditions usually have a more abrupt onset and often demonstrate warmth, significant inflammation, or systemic symptoms.

Question 113  [ID: 51d955d4aea04cb0989c722d2f8db260]
A 29-year-old woman presents with 6 months of intermittent aching in the right shoulder and neck accompanied by numbness and tingling along the medial forearm and fourth and fifth digits. Her symptoms worsen while swimming, carrying a backpack, or working with her arm elevated above her head. She denies recent trauma. Examination reveals mild weakness of right finger abduction and decreased sensation along the medial forearm. The right radial pulse diminishes when the arm is abducted and externally rotated. Maintaining both arms in an elevated, externally rotated position for 3 minutes reproduces her symptoms. A chest radiograph demonstrates an accessory rib arising from C7. MRI of the cervical spine shows no disk herniation or spinal canal stenosis. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Cervical radiculopathy
B. Cubital tunnel syndrome
C. Thoracic outlet syndrome
D. Carpal tunnel syndrome
E. Rotator cuff tendinopathy

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: Thoracic outlet syndrome is the most likely diagnosis. Compression of the brachial plexus and, in some cases, the subclavian vessels occurs as these structures pass between the neck and upper chest. This patient has symptoms involving the lower brachial plexus, including paresthesias along the medial forearm and fourth and fifth digits and weakness of finger abduction. Symptom reproduction with sustained arm elevation, reduction of the radial pulse during arm abduction and external rotation, and the presence of a cervical rib strongly support this diagnosis. Most cases are neurogenic, although vascular compression may coexist.

A. Cervical radiculopathy can cause neck pain, weakness, and sensory changes in a dermatomal distribution, but symptoms are often provoked by cervical spine movement or foraminal compression rather than sustained arm elevation. The normal cervical MRI and diminished radial pulse with positional testing argue against this diagnosis.

B. Cubital tunnel syndrome results from ulnar nerve compression at the elbow and can cause paresthesias in the fourth and fifth digits with intrinsic hand weakness. It does not typically cause medial forearm sensory loss, shoulder or neck discomfort, or positional reduction of the radial pulse.

D. Carpal tunnel syndrome involves median nerve compression at the wrist, producing paresthesias in the thumb, index, middle, and radial half of the ring finger. It would not explain symptoms in the medial forearm and fifth digit or the vascular findings.

E. Rotator cuff tendinopathy commonly causes activity-related shoulder pain and painful or weak abduction but does not produce distal paresthesias, intrinsic hand weakness, or diminished radial pulses with positional maneuvers.

Question 114  [ID: 529ce6c49a8c4ce38f9cf1db034e9e72]
A 24-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe right shoulder pain after being tackled while playing rugby. He landed with his right arm abducted and externally rotated. He supports the affected arm with his opposite hand and is unable to move it because of pain. Examination shows loss of the normal rounded shoulder contour and a prominent acromion. The right arm is held slightly abducted and externally rotated. Sensation over the lateral deltoid is intact, and radial and ulnar pulses are normal. Anteroposterior and scapular-Y radiographs show the humeral head positioned anteroinferior to the glenoid without an associated bony injury. Which of the following is the most appropriate next clinical intervention?

A. Provide analgesia and procedural sedation, perform prompt closed reduction, reassess neurovascular status, and immobilize the arm in a sling
B. Immobilize the arm in a sling without manipulation and arrange outpatient follow-up in 1 week
C. Obtain magnetic resonance imaging before attempting any intervention
D. Begin immediate active range-of-motion exercises to prevent stiffness
E. Proceed directly to open operative repair

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical presentation and radiographic position of the humeral head indicate an anterior glenohumeral dislocation without an associated fracture. Prompt closed reduction is the appropriate intervention. Adequate analgesia, intra-articular anesthetic, and/or procedural sedation should be provided before reduction. Neurovascular function, particularly axillary nerve sensation over the lateral deltoid and distal perfusion, must be documented before and after the procedure. Postreduction radiographs should confirm alignment and exclude an occult fracture, after which the arm is briefly immobilized in a sling with appropriate follow-up and rehabilitation. Choice B is incorrect because delayed reduction can increase pain, muscle spasm, neurovascular injury risk, and difficulty of reduction. Choice C is incorrect because MRI is not required before uncomplicated reduction; it may be obtained later if significant rotator cuff, labral, or other soft-tissue injury is suspected. Choice D is incorrect because immediate active motion before reduction is inappropriate and potentially harmful; rehabilitation begins after alignment is restored and a short period of immobilization is completed. Choice E is incorrect because open operative management is generally reserved for irreducible injuries, open injuries, associated fractures requiring fixation, recurrent instability in selected patients, or persistent neurovascular compromise.

Question 115  [ID: 52d14e60c45b41be8ea3fc71d923817b]
A 15-year-old boy is evaluated for progressive exercise intolerance and a sunken appearance of his anterior torso that has become more noticeable during his adolescent growth spurt. He has no history of trauma or chronic pulmonary disease. Examination shows symmetric posterior displacement of the lower sternum with medial angulation of the adjacent costal margins. Cardiac auscultation reveals a mild leftward displacement of the point of maximal impulse. Pulmonary function testing demonstrates a mild restrictive pattern. CT demonstrates a transverse internal diameter divided by the shortest anteroposterior diameter of 3.6, with mild compression of the right ventricle. Which of the following developmental mechanisms most likely produced this patient's findings?

A. Disproportionate growth of the costal cartilage causing posterior displacement of the sternum
B. Failure of the paired embryonic sternal bars to fuse at the midline
C. Defective mineralization of osteoid at the costochondral junctions
D. Chronic pulmonary hyperinflation causing expansion of the anteroposterior diameter
E. Unilateral absence of the pectoralis major muscle due to disruption of embryonic blood flow

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has pectus excavatum, characterized by posterior depression of the sternum and adjacent costal cartilages. It often becomes more prominent during adolescent growth. The underlying developmental abnormality is disproportionate or abnormal growth of the costal cartilage, which displaces the sternum posteriorly. The CT ratio described is the Haller index; a value greater than approximately 3.25 indicates a severe deformity and may be associated with restrictive physiology, cardiac displacement, or right ventricular compression.

B is incorrect because failure of the embryonic sternal bars to fuse produces a sternal cleft rather than posterior sternal displacement.

C is incorrect because defective osteoid mineralization causes rickets, which may produce costochondral enlargement, bowing of long bones, and a protruding sternum rather than a focal posterior depression.

D is incorrect because chronic pulmonary hyperinflation increases the anteroposterior diameter and produces a barrel-shaped configuration, not posterior displacement of the sternum.

E is incorrect because unilateral absence or hypoplasia of the pectoralis major muscle is characteristic of Poland syndrome, which causes asymmetric soft-tissue and upper-extremity abnormalities rather than a symmetric lower sternal depression.

Question 116  [ID: 53d8be2ab3174bf183a13dbfcb2a1fa6]
A 29-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe right shoulder pain after a witnessed generalized tonic-clonic seizure. He holds his right arm adducted and internally rotated against his torso. Examination reveals a prominent posterior contour of the shoulder and marked restriction of external rotation. Sensation over the lateral deltoid is intact, and radial and ulnar pulses are normal. An anteroposterior radiograph demonstrates a rounded, symmetric appearance of the humeral head with loss of the normal overlap between the humeral head and glenoid. An axillary view shows the humeral head positioned posterior to the glenoid. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Anterior shoulder dislocation
B. Posterior shoulder dislocation
C. Inferior shoulder dislocation
D. Proximal humeral fracture
E. Acromioclavicular joint separation

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. Posterior shoulder dislocation is classically associated with seizures, electrical injury, and high-energy trauma because forceful contraction of the internal rotator muscles drives the humeral head posteriorly. Patients typically hold the arm adducted and internally rotated and have a mechanical block to external rotation. The rounded, symmetric 'lightbulb' appearance on an anteroposterior radiograph and posterior positioning of the humeral head on an axillary view confirm the diagnosis. A is incorrect because anterior dislocation usually causes the arm to be held abducted and externally rotated, with the humeral head displaced anteroinferiorly. C is incorrect because inferior dislocation, or luxatio erecta, presents with the arm fixed overhead in marked abduction. D is incorrect because a proximal humeral fracture would demonstrate a fracture line, cortical disruption, or displaced fragments on imaging and does not typically produce the described fixed internal rotation with posterior humeral head positioning. E is incorrect because acromioclavicular separation causes focal superior shoulder pain, tenderness, and possible elevation of the distal clavicle rather than abnormal positioning of the humeral head relative to the glenoid.

Question 117  [ID: 53ebdac497b74bdab37b139dd7b5e104]
A 29-year-old woman presents with a 6-month history of intermittent aching in the right shoulder and paresthesias along the medial forearm and fourth and fifth digits. Her symptoms worsen when she stocks shelves overhead at work and improve when she lowers her arm. She denies trauma, neck pain, or hand swelling. Examination shows mild weakness of finger abduction and adduction with decreased sensation over the medial forearm and hand. The radial pulse is symmetric and remains palpable with arm elevation. There is no erythema or edema. Complete blood count and serum electrolyte levels are normal. Plain radiographs demonstrate an accessory rib arising from the seventh cervical vertebra. Which of the following structures is most likely being compressed to produce this patient's symptoms?

A. Axillary nerve within the quadrangular space
B. Lower trunk of the brachial plexus
C. Median nerve beneath the flexor retinaculum
D. Radial nerve within the spiral groove
E. Upper trunk of the brachial plexus

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. The sensory symptoms along the medial forearm and fourth and fifth digits, combined with weakness of finger abduction and adduction, localize to fibers derived predominantly from the C8-T1 nerve roots. These fibers travel through the lower trunk of the brachial plexus. An accessory cervical rib can narrow the space through which these neural elements pass, and overhead arm positioning can further increase compression. Preservation of the radial pulse and absence of edema or discoloration favor neural compression rather than clinically significant arterial or venous obstruction.

A is incorrect because axillary nerve compression causes weakness of shoulder abduction and sensory loss over the lateral shoulder, not medial forearm and hand symptoms.

C is incorrect because median nerve compression beneath the flexor retinaculum causes paresthesias in the thumb, index, middle, and radial half of the ring finger, often with thenar weakness. It does not account for medial forearm sensory loss.

D is incorrect because radial nerve injury in the spiral groove typically causes wrist and finger extension weakness with sensory changes over the dorsolateral hand. Finger abduction and adduction are primarily mediated by the ulnar nerve.

E is incorrect because upper trunk involvement predominantly affects C5-C6 fibers, producing weakness of shoulder abduction, external rotation, and elbow flexion, with sensory abnormalities over the lateral upper extremity.

Question 118  [ID: 5485adf1dcfc4d73b1ab1457090203da]
A 34-year-old warehouse employee presents with 2 days of lower back pain that began immediately after he lifted a heavy box while twisting. The pain is localized to the lumbar region and worsens with bending, prolonged standing, and changing positions. It does not radiate into the legs. He denies fever, weight loss, urinary retention, incontinence, saddle anesthesia, lower-extremity weakness, or intravenous drug use. Vital signs are normal. Examination shows a mildly reduced lumbar range of motion and no midline bony tenderness. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes are intact. Urinalysis is normal, and plain radiographs show no acute osseous abnormality. Which of the following additional physical examination findings would most strongly support the leading diagnosis?

A. Reproduction of leg pain below the knee during straight-leg raising
B. Decreased anal sphincter tone with diminished perineal sensation
C. Localized paraspinal tenderness with pain reproduced by active trunk motion
D. Costovertebral angle tenderness accompanied by pain on percussion
E. Focal midline tenderness with pain produced by axial loading

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The acute onset of localized lumbar pain after lifting and twisting, worsening with movement, absence of radicular or systemic symptoms, normal neurologic examination, and lack of an acute osseous abnormality are characteristic of a spinal sprain or strain. Localized paraspinal tenderness and reproduction of pain with active trunk motion further support injury to the paraspinal muscles, tendons, or supporting ligaments. Choice A suggests lumbosacral nerve root irritation, commonly from disk herniation. Choice B indicates possible cauda equina syndrome and requires emergent evaluation. Choice D suggests renal pathology such as pyelonephritis or nephrolithiasis, which is less likely with a normal urinalysis and movement-related pain. Choice E raises concern for a vertebral compression fracture or other osseous injury, particularly when midline tenderness is present.

Question 119  [ID: 54aaadeb6f184853b2587ac7ea565ea7]
A 46-year-old right-hand-dominant man presents with sudden pain near his right elbow after attempting to catch a falling refrigerator. He felt a painful “pop” while his elbow was flexed and immediately lost strength. Examination 2 days later shows swelling and ecchymosis over the front of the elbow, proximal retraction of the muscle belly, and marked weakness with resisted forearm supination. Flexion remains possible but is weaker than on the opposite side. A cordlike structure cannot be palpated in the antecubital region during the hook test. Sensation and distal pulses are intact. Plain radiographs show no fracture or dislocation. The patient works as a carpenter and wishes to regain maximal strength. Which of the following is the most appropriate clinical intervention?

A. Immediate orthopedic referral for early operative repair
B. Immobilization in a long-arm cast for 8 weeks
C. Activity modification and physical therapy without surgical consultation
D. Intra-articular glucocorticoid injection followed by immediate strengthening exercises
E. Closed reduction under procedural sedation

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The acute pop, antecubital ecchymosis, proximal muscle retraction, absent structure on hook testing, and disproportionate loss of supination strength indicate a complete acute distal biceps tendon rupture. In a healthy, active patient who performs manual labor and desires maximal functional recovery, prompt orthopedic referral for operative repair is recommended, ideally within the first few weeks before substantial retraction and scarring develop. Surgical repair provides better restoration of supination strength and endurance than nonoperative care. Choice B is incorrect because prolonged casting does not restore tendon attachment and may cause stiffness. Choice C is more appropriate for older, sedentary, medically high-risk, or low-demand patients willing to accept persistent losses in supination and flexion strength. Choice D is incorrect because glucocorticoid injection does not treat an acute complete rupture and may further impair tendon integrity; immediate strengthening is also inappropriate. Choice E is incorrect because there is no fracture or joint dislocation requiring reduction.

Question 120  [ID: 550cb1d3530d494a87f677413e6d29c4]
A 24-year-old man presents after a basketball struck the tip of his right middle finger while the finger was extended. He reports immediate pain and an inability to straighten the fingertip. Examination shows the distal interphalangeal joint resting in approximately 30 degrees of flexion. He cannot actively extend this joint, but full passive extension is possible. Flexion and extension at the proximal interphalangeal joint are intact, and the finger is neurovascularly intact. Plain radiographs show no fracture or joint subluxation. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial management?

A. Continuous splinting of the distal interphalangeal joint in extension for 6 to 8 weeks
B. Buddy taping the affected finger to the adjacent finger for 2 weeks
C. Immobilization of both interphalangeal joints in flexion for 4 weeks
D. Immediate operative repair with tendon reattachment
E. Early unrestricted range-of-motion exercises and oral NSAID therapy

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The forced flexion injury, loss of active distal interphalangeal joint extension, preserved passive extension, and absence of fracture or subluxation indicate disruption of the terminal extensor mechanism. Closed injuries without a large avulsion fracture or joint instability are treated initially with continuous splinting of the distal interphalangeal joint in full extension or slight hyperextension for 6 to 8 weeks. The joint must remain extended continuously, including during hygiene, to allow healing; the proximal interphalangeal joint should remain mobile. Buddy taping (B) does not adequately maintain distal joint extension and is more appropriate for selected stable collateral ligament injuries. Immobilizing both interphalangeal joints in flexion (C) places the injured structure in a lengthened position and may cause stiffness. Immediate surgery (D) is generally reserved for open injuries, volar subluxation, a large displaced articular fragment, or failure of appropriate conservative treatment. Early unrestricted motion (E) can repeatedly separate the injured ends and lead to a persistent extension deformity despite symptomatic treatment with NSAIDs.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Question 121  [ID: 55a8d7eabaab473c8fded6763b3c1ab3]
A 68-year-old woman presents with acute right wrist pain and swelling after tripping on a rug and falling onto her outstretched hand from standing height. She has no history of malignancy or chronic glucocorticoid use and has never undergone bone mineral density testing. Examination shows tenderness, swelling, and deformity near the wrist; sensation and perfusion of the hand are intact. Radiographs demonstrate cortical disruption and dorsal displacement at the distal end of the radius. Serum calcium, creatinine, and alkaline phosphatase levels are within normal limits. After appropriate acute management, which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation to reduce this patient's risk of future injury?

A. Obtain central dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry, evaluate fall risk, and ensure adequate calcium and vitamin D intake
B. Recommend wrist-strengthening exercises only, because the injury resulted from an accidental fall
C. Begin annual screening with plain radiographs of the spine and both hips
D. Advise prolonged activity restriction to reduce the likelihood of another fall
E. Defer preventive evaluation unless she sustains a second low-impact injury

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: A is correct. A distal radius fracture following a fall from standing height in an adult older than 50 years should raise concern for underlying skeletal fragility. This event is an important opportunity for secondary prevention. Central dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry of the hip and lumbar spine should be obtained to assess bone mineral density, and the patient should receive a fall-risk assessment. Counseling should include adequate calcium and vitamin D intake, weight-bearing and resistance exercise after recovery, smoking cessation if applicable, moderation of alcohol use, home-safety modifications, and review of medications that increase fall risk. Additional evaluation for secondary contributors may be appropriate based on clinical findings. B is incorrect because wrist exercises alone do not address systemic skeletal health or modifiable fall risks. C is incorrect because routine plain radiographs are not recommended for osteoporosis screening and expose the patient to unnecessary radiation. D is incorrect because prolonged inactivity can worsen bone loss, muscle weakness, and fall risk; safe progressive activity is preferred. E is incorrect because a first low-trauma fracture in an older adult is sufficient to prompt evaluation and preventive intervention rather than waiting for another event.

Question 122  [ID: 5672254117d745d4a01c1b97442b558c]
A 34-year-old warehouse employee presents with 2 days of localized lower back pain that began immediately after lifting a heavy box while twisting. The pain worsens with bending and changing positions and improves when lying still. He denies pain radiating into the legs, lower-extremity weakness, numbness, urinary retention, incontinence, fever, weight loss, or prior malignancy. Vital signs are normal. He walks without assistance, and examination shows no visible deformity or midline step-off. Urinalysis is normal, and plain radiographs show preserved vertebral body heights without acute abnormalities. Which of the following additional physical examination findings would most strongly support the leading clinical impression?

A. Localized paraspinal tenderness with pain reproduced by active trunk flexion and extension, accompanied by normal lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes
B. Pain radiating below the knee during straight-leg raising at 35 degrees
C. Saddle hypoesthesia with decreased anal sphincter tone
D. Focal percussion tenderness over a lumbar vertebral body with increased pain during axial loading
E. Costovertebral angle tenderness accompanied by fever and tachycardia

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The acute onset of localized pain after lifting and twisting, mechanical exacerbation with movement, absence of systemic symptoms or neurologic complaints, and normal radiographs are most consistent with a spinal sprain or strain. Localized paraspinal tenderness and pain reproduced by active movement are characteristic examination findings. A normal neurologic examination further supports an uncomplicated mechanical soft-tissue injury. Choice B suggests lumbosacral nerve root irritation, commonly from intervertebral disc herniation. Choice C indicates compression of the cauda equina and requires emergency evaluation. Choice D raises concern for a vertebral compression fracture, particularly when associated with trauma, osteoporosis, or advanced age. Choice E is more consistent with an upper urinary tract infection rather than a mechanically triggered spinal condition.

Question 123  [ID: 5730730d79db454a8b229410ace5a562]
A 16-year-old female competitive gymnast presents with 3 months of progressively worsening low back pain. The pain is exacerbated by back walkovers and other maneuvers involving lumbar extension and improves with rest. She denies fever, weight loss, morning stiffness, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and lower-extremity weakness. Examination reveals increased lumbar lordosis, tight hamstrings, and tenderness over the lower lumbar region. Pain is reproduced by standing on one leg and extending the lumbar spine. Strength, sensation, and deep tendon reflexes are normal in both lower extremities. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein levels are within normal limits. Lateral radiography demonstrates anterior displacement of the L5 vertebral body relative to S1, and oblique views reveal a linear defect through the pars interarticularis of L5. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely produced these findings?

A. Repetitive lumbar hyperextension causing a stress fracture that permits anterior vertebral translation
B. Age-related intervertebral disc dehydration causing posterior vertebral translation
C. Hematogenous bacterial seeding of an intervertebral disc with erosion of adjacent end plates
D. Autoimmune inflammation of the sacroiliac articulations causing progressive vertebral fusion
E. Failure of fusion of the posterior vertebral arches during embryonic development

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Repetitive lumbar hyperextension, particularly in adolescent athletes such as gymnasts, can produce a stress fracture through the pars interarticularis. When the defect is bilateral, the posterior vertebral elements no longer adequately restrain the vertebral body, allowing it to translate anteriorly relative to the vertebra below. The extension-related pain, positive single-leg hyperextension maneuver, normal neurologic examination, pars defect on oblique imaging, and anterior displacement of L5 on S1 support this mechanism. B is incorrect because degenerative disc and facet changes can produce vertebral displacement in older adults, but they do not typically cause a pars defect in an adolescent athlete. C is incorrect because vertebral or disc infection usually causes persistent pain and may be associated with fever, elevated inflammatory markers, and end-plate destruction. D is incorrect because inflammatory sacroiliac disease typically presents with prolonged morning stiffness, improvement with activity, and possible fusion rather than a focal pars defect with anterior translation. E is incorrect because failure of posterior arch fusion is a congenital neural tube closure abnormality and does not explain an acquired extension-related pars stress fracture or the characteristic vertebral displacement seen here.

Question 124  [ID: 57f667bbaf89458c8e15e37e393815d3]
A 72-year-old man presents to the emergency department after falling against the edge of a bathtub. He reports severe left-sided pain that worsens with inspiration, coughing, and movement. His medical history includes hypertension and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease. Temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), blood pressure is 138/82 mm Hg, pulse is 96/min, respirations are 24/min, and oxygen saturation is 91% on room air. Examination reveals shallow respirations, ecchymosis over the left posterolateral thorax, and marked focal tenderness with palpable crepitus over the seventh through ninth costal levels. Breath sounds are slightly diminished on the left. A posteroanterior and lateral radiographic study shows no acute abnormality. Which of the following diagnostic studies is most sensitive for identifying the suspected traumatic injuries and evaluating for associated intrathoracic complications?

A. Repeat posteroanterior and lateral radiographs in 24 hours
B. Computed tomography of the chest
C. Magnetic resonance imaging of the chest
D. Technetium-99m bone scintigraphy
E. Pulmonary ventilation-perfusion scintigraphy

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Computed tomography of the chest is the most sensitive imaging study for detecting acute traumatic disruption of the costal cortex, including nondisplaced or occult injuries that may not be visible on standard radiographs. CT also evaluates clinically important associated complications, such as pneumothorax, hemothorax, pulmonary contusion, and injury to adjacent structures. This patient's advanced age, focal tenderness, crepitus, hypoxemia, and diminished unilateral breath sounds justify additional imaging despite initially normal radiographs. A is incorrect because repeating plain radiographs may still fail to identify nondisplaced injuries and would delay assessment of possible intrathoracic complications. C is incorrect because MRI is not routinely used for acute thoracic trauma; it is slower, less available, and less useful than CT for evaluating cortical disruption and pleural injury. D is incorrect because bone scintigraphy can demonstrate increased metabolic activity at sites of injury but lacks anatomic detail, is not useful for immediate trauma assessment, and cannot adequately evaluate acute intrathoracic complications. E is incorrect because ventilation-perfusion scintigraphy is primarily used to evaluate suspected pulmonary embolism and does not assess traumatic injury of the thoracic wall.
