Question 125  [ID: 5935680ea60944ee95fd22b32cde86d1]
A 67-year-old man presents after falling from a ladder and striking the left side of his torso against a railing. He reports severe focal pain that worsens with deep inspiration, coughing, and movement. His blood pressure is 132/78 mm Hg, pulse is 104/min, respirations are 26/min, and oxygen saturation is 93% on room air. Examination demonstrates shallow respirations, localized tenderness, and palpable crepitus over the left lateral thoracic wall. Breath sounds are present bilaterally. Which of the following additional physical examination findings would most strongly indicate a mechanically unstable injury requiring urgent respiratory assessment?

A. Inward movement of a localized thoracic segment during inspiration
B. Diffuse expiratory wheezing that improves after coughing
C. Pain reproduced by active rotation of the trunk
D. Symmetric expansion with diminished breath sounds at both bases
E. A pleural friction rub heard during inspiration and expiration

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Inward movement of a localized thoracic segment during inspiration, with outward movement during expiration, is paradoxical motion. This finding indicates that a segment has become mechanically disconnected from the surrounding thoracic wall due to multiple adjacent bony disruptions. The unstable segment impairs ventilation and may be associated with underlying pulmonary injury, hypoxemia, and respiratory failure, making urgent respiratory assessment essential. B is incorrect because diffuse expiratory wheezing suggests airflow obstruction rather than mechanical instability after trauma. C is incorrect because pain with trunk rotation is consistent with local injury but does not establish an unstable segment. D is incorrect because symmetric expansion argues against paradoxical motion; bilateral basilar reduction in breath sounds may result from shallow breathing or atelectasis. E is incorrect because a pleural friction rub indicates inflamed pleural surfaces and is not a defining sign of mechanical instability.

Question 126  [ID: 5939f98a21a64087a5e0bcb2cc47ed6a]
A 17-year-old boy presents with a painless, slowly enlarging prominence near his right knee that he first noticed 2 years ago. He denies fever, weight loss, night sweats, nocturnal pain, or recent trauma. Examination shows a firm, immobile, nontender mass over the medial distal thigh. The overlying skin is normal, and the knee has full range of motion. Distal pulses, sensation, and motor function are intact. A complete blood count, serum calcium, alkaline phosphatase, and inflammatory markers are within normal limits. Plain radiographs demonstrate a pedunculated projection arising from the metaphysis of the distal femur and extending away from the adjacent joint. The cortex and medullary cavity of the projection are continuous with those of the underlying femur. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Chondroblastoma
B. Enchondroma
C. Giant cell tumor
D. Osteochondroma
E. Osteosarcoma

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: The correct answer is D. Osteochondroma typically presents during childhood or adolescence as a painless, slowly enlarging, firm mass near the metaphysis of a long bone, especially around the knee. The defining radiographic feature is an exophytic projection whose cortex and medullary cavity are continuous with those of the parent bone. These lesions often project away from the adjacent joint and may stop enlarging after skeletal maturity.

A is incorrect because chondroblastoma typically occurs in the epiphysis of a long bone in an adolescent or young adult. It more often causes localized joint pain and appears as a well-defined lytic lesion rather than an exophytic metaphyseal projection.

B is incorrect because enchondroma is an intramedullary cartilaginous lesion, commonly found in the small bones of the hands and feet. Radiographs typically show a centrally located lucent lesion with stippled or ring-and-arc calcifications, not cortical and medullary continuity with an external projection.

C is incorrect because giant cell tumor generally occurs after physeal closure in adults aged 20 to 40 years. It typically arises in the epiphysis and extends toward the articular surface, producing an expansile, eccentric lytic lesion with a nonsclerotic margin.

E is incorrect because osteosarcoma usually presents with progressive pain and swelling and demonstrates aggressive radiographic findings, such as mixed lytic and sclerotic changes, cortical destruction, a sunburst periosteal reaction, or a Codman triangle. The slow painless course and organized cortical and medullary continuity in this patient favor osteochondroma.

Question 127  [ID: 5beee0bc53c6486eb6618eab4d9ac190]
A 72-year-old man presents with rapidly worsening pain and swelling of the right lower leg after striking it against a table 6 hours ago. He takes warfarin for atrial fibrillation. The pain is severe despite intravenous morphine and increases markedly with passive extension of the toes. Examination shows a tense, swollen lower leg with decreased sensation over the dorsum of the foot. Pedal pulses remain palpable. Laboratory studies reveal a hemoglobin level of 9.4 g/dL, platelet count of 210,000/mm3, and INR of 4.8. An orthopedic surgeon is preparing the patient for immediate operative decompression. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate pharmaceutical intervention?

A. Administer four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate and intravenous vitamin K
B. Administer fresh frozen plasma without vitamin K
C. Administer protamine sulfate
D. Administer oral vitamin K and repeat the INR in 24 hours
E. Administer tranexamic acid and continue warfarin

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has clinical findings of acute compartment syndrome caused or worsened by bleeding into a closed fascial space while excessively anticoagulated with warfarin. Hallmark findings include rapidly progressive pain out of proportion to the injury, pain with passive stretch, tense swelling, and sensory changes. Palpable distal pulses do not exclude the diagnosis. Because emergency operative decompression is required, warfarin must be reversed rapidly. Four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate immediately replaces vitamin K-dependent clotting factors II, VII, IX, and X, while intravenous vitamin K provides sustained reversal as new factors are synthesized. Option B is incorrect because fresh frozen plasma has a slower onset, requires larger infusion volumes, and does not provide sustained reversal without vitamin K; it is generally reserved for situations in which prothrombin complex concentrate is unavailable. Option C is incorrect because protamine sulfate reverses unfractionated heparin and only partially reverses low-molecular-weight heparin; it does not reverse warfarin. Option D is incorrect because oral vitamin K acts too slowly for a limb-threatening emergency requiring immediate surgery. Option E is incorrect because tranexamic acid does not adequately reverse warfarin anticoagulation, and continuing warfarin would promote further bleeding. Pharmacologic reversal must occur concurrently with preparation for emergent fasciotomy and must not delay operative treatment.

Question 128  [ID: 5bf923b9c91844bd9855a0efcfac341e]
A 7-year-old boy is brought to the emergency department for 4 days of fever and progressively worsening pain near his right knee. He has no history of trauma or recent surgery. His temperature is 39.1°C (102.4°F), and he refuses to bear weight on the affected leg. Examination reveals focal warmth, swelling, and tenderness over the proximal right tibia without an overlying wound. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 15,800/mm3, an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 72 mm/hr, and an elevated C-reactive protein level. Blood cultures grow methicillin-sensitive Staphylococcus aureus. MRI demonstrates marrow edema and a localized fluid collection centered in the proximal tibial metaphysis. Which of the following best explains why hematogenous infection preferentially localizes to this anatomic region in children?

A. High oxygen tension in metaphyseal tissue promotes bacterial replication
B. Slow, turbulent blood flow through metaphyseal capillary loops facilitates bacterial deposition
C. Direct communication between the metaphysis and the joint space allows organisms to enter from synovial fluid
D. Absence of phagocytic cells in metaphyseal marrow prevents an effective innate immune response
E. Repetitive contraction of adjacent muscles causes bacterial migration through the periosteum

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This child has acute hematogenous osteomyelitis caused by Staphylococcus aureus. In children, the metaphysis of long bones is particularly susceptible because its vascular supply contains tortuous, hairpin-shaped capillary loops with relatively slow and turbulent blood flow. This vascular anatomy facilitates bacterial deposition and proliferation after hematogenous spread.

A is incorrect because metaphyseal susceptibility is not caused by high oxygen tension; localized vascular stasis and bacterial seeding are the key factors.

C is incorrect because the metaphysis generally does not communicate directly with the joint space. An important exception occurs in certain locations, such as the proximal femur, where the metaphysis may lie within the joint capsule and infection can extend into the joint.

D is incorrect because phagocytic cells are present in marrow. Infection develops despite host defenses due to bacterial seeding, local vascular characteristics, inflammatory injury, and possible vascular compromise.

E is incorrect because muscle contraction does not drive bacteria through the periosteum. Hematogenous spread occurs through the bloodstream, while periosteal extension may develop later as purulent material tracks outward from the infected medullary region.

Question 129  [ID: 5d55d061444448eeac21feb080f62308]
A 67-year-old woman presents with sudden midline thoracic pain that began while lifting a bag of groceries 3 days ago. She reports no fall or direct trauma. Her last menstrual period was at age 44 years. Physical examination shows thoracic kyphosis, a 4-cm decrease from her previously documented height, and focal tenderness over T8. Strength, sensation, and deep tendon reflexes are normal in both lower extremities. Radiographs demonstrate an anterior wedge compression deformity of T8 and diffusely decreased radiographic density. Serum calcium, phosphate, alkaline phosphatase, and creatinine levels are within normal limits. Which additional historical finding would be the most important predisposing factor to elicit in this patient?

A. Daily prednisone use for severe asthma for the past 4 years
B. Treatment with hydrochlorothiazide for hypertension
C. A body mass index of 32 kg/m²
D. Menarche at 11 years of age
E. Participation in weight-bearing exercise three times weekly

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's atraumatic vertebral compression deformity, loss of height, thoracic kyphosis, decreased radiographic density, and normal calcium, phosphate, and alkaline phosphatase levels are characteristic of osteoporosis. When obtaining the history, chronic systemic glucocorticoid exposure is a particularly important predisposing factor. Glucocorticoids decrease osteoblast activity, increase osteocyte and osteoblast apoptosis, reduce intestinal calcium absorption, and increase renal calcium loss, producing rapid loss of bone mass and increased vertebral fracture risk. Therefore, long-term daily prednisone use is the most clinically significant additional historical finding. Hydrochlorothiazide decreases urinary calcium excretion and may have a protective effect on bone density. A higher body mass index generally provides some protection because of greater mechanical loading and increased peripheral estrogen production. Early menarche increases lifetime estrogen exposure and is not a typical risk factor; late menarche would be more concerning. Regular weight-bearing exercise helps preserve bone mass and reduces risk rather than predisposing to this presentation.

Question 130  [ID: 5ecd0830c0e5479ea268a0d810a24f79]
A 32-year-old man presents with severe left ankle pain and inability to bear weight after his planted foot externally rotated while playing soccer. He reports hearing a pop and has pain primarily along the medial aspect of the ankle. Examination shows swelling, medial tenderness, and pain with passive external rotation of the foot. Dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses are intact, and sensation is normal. Ankle radiographs demonstrate widening of the medial clear space without a visible acute osseous abnormality near the ankle. Which additional physical examination assessment is most important in this patient?

A. Palpation of the entire fibula, including the proximal tibiofibular region
B. Performance of the Thompson test while the patient lies prone
C. Assessment for pain with passive flexion, abduction, and external rotation at the hip
D. Performance of the patellar apprehension test
E. Palpation of the plantar fascia at its calcaneal insertion

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. External rotation of a planted foot, medial ankle tenderness, pain with external rotation stress, and widening of the medial clear space suggest disruption of the distal tibiofibular syndesmosis and deltoid ligament. This force may travel proximally through the interosseous membrane and produce a proximal fibular fracture, known as a Maisonneuve fracture. Because patients may localize their symptoms to the ankle and report little proximal pain, the clinician must palpate the entire fibula and examine the proximal tibiofibular region. Failure to do so can result in a missed unstable injury. B is incorrect because the Thompson test evaluates Achilles tendon integrity and is most useful when tendon rupture is suspected. C is incorrect because the flexion-abduction-external rotation maneuver primarily evaluates hip or sacroiliac pathology. D is incorrect because the patellar apprehension test assesses patellar instability and is unrelated to this mechanism. E is incorrect because tenderness at the calcaneal insertion of the plantar fascia is associated with plantar fasciopathy and would not identify the important associated injury suggested by this presentation.

Question 131  [ID: 624b4576499d44a28b2095380f70a7b5]
A 13-year-old boy presents with 3 months of gradually worsening pain just below the right knee. He recently began playing competitive basketball, and the pain is most pronounced during running, jumping, and climbing stairs. He denies fever, night pain, recent trauma, or pain at rest. Examination shows a prominent, tender tibial tubercle without erythema or joint effusion. Pain is reproduced by resisted knee extension and by passive knee flexion. He has full range of motion and a stable ligamentous examination. A radiograph demonstrates fragmentation and soft-tissue swelling at the tibial tubercle without an aggressive bone lesion. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Osgood-Schlatter disease
B. Sinding-Larsen-Johansson syndrome
C. Patellar tendinopathy
D. Tibial tubercle avulsion fracture
E. Osteosarcoma

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Osgood-Schlatter disease is a traction apophysitis of the tibial tubercle caused by repetitive pulling of the patellar tendon on the developing apophysis. It commonly occurs in physically active adolescents during periods of rapid growth. Typical findings include activity-related anterior knee pain, localized tenderness and prominence over the tibial tubercle, pain with resisted knee extension, and radiographic fragmentation or irregularity of the tibial tubercle. Sinding-Larsen-Johansson syndrome causes traction-related pain at the inferior pole of the patella rather than at the tibial tubercle. Patellar tendinopathy usually produces focal tendon pain near the inferior patellar pole and is more common in skeletally mature athletes. A tibial tubercle avulsion fracture presents acutely after forceful quadriceps contraction, often with swelling, inability to extend the knee, and displaced bony injury on imaging. Osteosarcoma may cause progressive pain and swelling, but imaging typically shows an aggressive metaphyseal lesion with destructive changes, periosteal reaction, or a soft-tissue mass rather than isolated tibial tubercle fragmentation.

Question 132  [ID: 6289db70072943178bc60cb4b82d1612]
A 16-year-old boy presents with progressive shortness of breath and reduced endurance during soccer practice. His parents first noticed an inward depression of his anterior thorax during early childhood, and it has become more prominent during his recent growth spurt. He has no history of trauma, chronic illness, or nutritional deficiency. Physical examination shows a symmetric, funnel-shaped depression of the lower sternum with mild anterior displacement of the shoulders. Cardiac auscultation reveals a soft systolic ejection murmur along the left sternal border. Serum calcium, phosphate, alkaline phosphatase, and 25-hydroxyvitamin D levels are normal. CT demonstrates a transverse thoracic diameter-to-anteroposterior diameter ratio of 3.6 and mild compression of the right ventricle. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's structural findings?

A. Disproportionate overgrowth of the costal cartilages causing posterior displacement of the sternum
B. Premature fusion of the sternocostal articulations causing anterior displacement of the sternum
C. Defective mineralization of osteoid at the costochondral junctions
D. Unilateral absence of the pectoralis major muscle due to interruption of embryonic blood supply
E. Vertebral body rotation producing secondary lateral displacement of the sternum

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has pectus excavatum, characterized by posterior depression of the sternum and adjacent costal cartilages. The leading developmental mechanism is disproportionate or abnormal growth of the costal cartilages, which displaces the sternum posteriorly. The deformity often becomes more pronounced during adolescent growth and may reduce the anteroposterior thoracic dimension. A transverse-to-anteroposterior diameter ratio greater than approximately 3.25 indicates a severe deformity and may be associated with right ventricular compression, impaired diastolic filling, reduced stroke volume during exercise, and restrictive ventilatory physiology.

A is correct because abnormal costal cartilage growth produces the characteristic posterior sternal displacement.

B is incorrect because anterior displacement of the sternum describes a protruding rather than depressed configuration; premature sternocostal fusion is not the typical underlying mechanism.

C is incorrect because defective osteoid mineralization causes rickets, which may produce widened costochondral junctions, bowing, and other skeletal abnormalities. The normal calcium, phosphate, alkaline phosphatase, and vitamin D findings argue against this mechanism.

D is incorrect because unilateral absence or hypoplasia of the pectoralis major is associated with Poland syndrome, which causes asymmetric anterior thoracic findings and may occur with ipsilateral upper-extremity anomalies rather than a symmetric lower sternal depression.

E is incorrect because vertebral rotation is characteristic of scoliosis and can cause thoracic asymmetry or a rib prominence, but it does not primarily produce a symmetric, funnel-shaped depression of the lower sternum.

Question 133  [ID: 62d9a7523b6841508d09075f5463c1d6]
A 62-year-old woman presents with 1 year of progressively worsening bilateral knee pain. The pain is exacerbated by walking and climbing stairs and improves with rest. She reports stiffness upon awakening that lasts approximately 15 minutes. Her medical history includes hypertension and obesity. Her body mass index is 34 kg/m². Examination reveals crepitus with passive motion, mild limitation of flexion, and firm enlargement of the joint margins without warmth or erythema. Radiographs show asymmetric joint-space narrowing, marginal osteophytes, and subchondral sclerosis. Which of the following counseling recommendations is most appropriate to improve this patient's symptoms and reduce future functional impairment?

A. Avoid all weight-bearing activity to prevent further structural damage
B. Begin a supervised low-impact aerobic and quadriceps-strengthening program and pursue gradual weight loss
C. Use a knee immobilizer continuously during waking hours
D. Adopt a high-protein diet and begin high-impact running exercises
E. Restrict dietary calcium and vitamin D intake to reduce osteophyte formation

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. This patient's chronic activity-related knee pain, brief morning stiffness, crepitus, firm enlargement, and characteristic radiographic findings are consistent with osteoarthritis. Patient education should emphasize regular low-impact aerobic activity, targeted muscle strengthening, and weight reduction when overweight or obese. Exercise improves pain, mobility, strength, and function without accelerating structural damage. Even modest weight loss can decrease mechanical loading across the knees and improve symptoms.

A is incorrect because prolonged avoidance of weight-bearing activity promotes muscle weakness, deconditioning, reduced mobility, and further functional decline. Activity should be modified rather than eliminated.

C is incorrect because continuous immobilization can cause stiffness, weakness, and loss of function. Bracing may benefit selected patients with instability or unicompartmental disease, but routine continuous immobilization is not recommended.

D is incorrect because high-impact running may aggravate symptoms in a patient who is obese and currently symptomatic. Low-impact activities such as walking, cycling, aquatic exercise, or elliptical training are preferred. A high-protein diet has no specific disease-modifying benefit.

E is incorrect because dietary calcium and vitamin D do not cause osteophyte formation. Restricting these nutrients may increase the risk of reduced bone mineral density and fractures, particularly in an older adult.

Question 134  [ID: 631ddf56bedc4de8a25e17d61833e061]
A 24-year-old woman presents 12 hours after stepping into a hole while playing soccer. Her right foot rolled inward, and she immediately developed pain and swelling near the outer aspect of the ankle. She can bear weight with a limp. Examination shows moderate swelling and ecchymosis anterior and inferior to the lateral malleolus. There is localized tenderness over the surrounding soft tissues but no tenderness along the posterior edge or tip of either malleolus, the base of the fifth metatarsal, or the navicular bone. Anterior drawer testing demonstrates mild laxity with a firm endpoint. Distal sensation, pulses, and capillary refill are normal. Plain radiographs show no fracture or joint incongruity. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial management?

A. Apply a functional ankle brace, use compression and intermittent ice, permit weight-bearing as tolerated, and begin early range-of-motion and rehabilitation exercises
B. Immobilize the ankle in a rigid cast without weight-bearing for 6 weeks
C. Arrange immediate operative repair of the injured soft tissues
D. Inject an intra-articular glucocorticoid and prescribe unrestricted activity
E. Prescribe strict bed rest and delay rehabilitation until all swelling and tenderness have resolved

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical findings indicate an uncomplicated, moderate inversion injury without fracture, neurovascular compromise, gross instability, or joint incongruity. Initial treatment should use functional support with an ankle brace, compression, intermittent ice, elevation as needed, and a short course of analgesia when appropriate. Weight-bearing should be advanced as tolerated, and early protected range-of-motion, strengthening, and proprioceptive exercises should be initiated to promote recovery and reduce recurrence. Choice B is incorrect because prolonged rigid immobilization delays functional recovery and may cause stiffness, weakness, and impaired proprioception; casting is generally reserved for selected severe injuries and, when used, is typically brief. Choice C is incorrect because immediate surgery is not indicated when there is a firm endpoint, preserved function, and no associated fracture or major instability. Choice D is incorrect because glucocorticoid injection is not standard acute therapy and may impair tissue healing; unrestricted activity also increases the risk of further injury. Choice E is incorrect because strict rest and delayed rehabilitation prolong disability. Early protected mobilization produces better functional outcomes than waiting for complete resolution of pain and swelling.

Question 135  [ID: 6358530d8e6a400e9e943bd40888f2d5]
A 15-year-old boy is evaluated for progressive exercise intolerance and concern about his appearance. His parents first noticed an abnormal contour of his anterior torso during early childhood, but it has become more prominent during his recent growth spurt. He denies trauma, fever, cough, or pain. Vital signs are normal. On inspection, the lower sternum and adjacent costal cartilages are displaced posteriorly, and the abnormality becomes more pronounced with inspiration. Cardiac auscultation reveals a soft systolic ejection murmur at the left sternal border. Pulmonary examination is otherwise normal. A lateral radiograph demonstrates posterior displacement of the sternum with mild leftward displacement of the cardiac silhouette. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis based on this history and physical examination?

A. Pectus carinatum
B. Pectus excavatum
C. Flail chest
D. Barrel chest
E. Tietze syndrome

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Pectus excavatum is characterized by posterior displacement of the sternum and adjacent costal cartilages, producing a sunken anterior thoracic contour. It is often recognized in childhood and becomes more prominent during adolescent growth. Patients with more pronounced deformity may report exercise intolerance due to reduced cardiopulmonary efficiency or cardiac displacement. A systolic ejection murmur may occur because of altered cardiac position or compression. Pectus carinatum causes anterior protrusion rather than depression of the sternum. Flail chest follows significant trauma and produces paradoxical movement of an unstable thoracic segment. Barrel chest is a diffuse increase in the anteroposterior diameter, commonly associated with chronic lung hyperinflation. Tietze syndrome causes localized, painful swelling of an upper costochondral junction and does not produce a progressive sternal depression.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Question 136  [ID: 6440638fd0414190b3685fd315b5f35e]
A 22-year-old man presents with severe left shoulder pain after being tackled during a football game. He landed with his left arm extended away from his body. He supports the affected arm with his opposite hand and reports that any attempted movement worsens the pain. Examination shows loss of the normal rounded shoulder contour, a prominent acromion, and the arm held in slight abduction and external rotation. The radial pulse is intact. Radiographs show the humeral head positioned anteroinferior to the glenoid. Which focused physical examination is most important for evaluating the nerve most commonly compromised by this injury?

A. Test sensation over the lateral deltoid and assess deltoid contraction
B. Test thumb opposition and sensation over the palmar index finger
C. Test finger abduction and sensation over the fifth digit
D. Test wrist extension and sensation over the dorsal first web space
E. Test elbow flexion and sensation over the lateral forearm

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The humeral head's anteroinferior position, loss of the rounded shoulder contour, and arm held in abduction and external rotation indicate an anterior glenohumeral dislocation. The axillary nerve is the nerve most commonly injured because it courses around the surgical neck of the humerus near the joint capsule. Axillary nerve function should be documented before and after reduction by testing sensation over the lateral deltoid, often called the regimental badge area, and assessing deltoid contraction or shoulder abduction when tolerated. Choice B evaluates the median nerve, which controls thumb opposition and sensation over the palmar aspect of the index finger. Choice C evaluates the ulnar nerve, which supplies the interossei and sensation over the fifth digit. Choice D evaluates the radial nerve, which controls wrist extension and supplies sensation to the dorsal first web space. Choice E evaluates the musculocutaneous nerve, which contributes to elbow flexion and provides sensation over the lateral forearm through the lateral antebrachial cutaneous nerve.

Question 137  [ID: 649bcc981cfd41a7b71f02d63ddadcb6]
A 12-year-old girl is brought to the clinic for evaluation of uneven shoulders noticed during a school screening. She has no pain, weakness, numbness, bowel or bladder dysfunction, or limitation of physical activity. Menarche occurred 4 months ago. Examination reveals elevation of the right shoulder and a right-sided thoracic prominence during forward bending. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes are normal. Standing posteroanterior radiography demonstrates a 22-degree lateral curvature by Cobb measurement, and the iliac apophysis is graded as Risser 0. Which of the following is the most appropriate counseling and preventive management plan for this patient?

A. Encourage normal physical activity and arrange repeat clinical examination and standing radiography in approximately 6 months to assess for progression
B. Restrict participation in sports and prescribe bed rest until skeletal maturity
C. Begin full-time external bracing immediately and continue it regardless of interval changes
D. Obtain urgent magnetic resonance imaging and refer for operative correction
E. Reassure the family that no additional monitoring is needed after this visit

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has adolescent idiopathic scoliosis with a mild 22-degree curve and substantial remaining growth, as indicated by her recent menarche and Risser 0 status. Curves below approximately 25 degrees are generally managed with observation, serial examinations, and standing radiographs about every 6 months during periods of rapid growth. Continued physical activity should be encouraged because routine sports restriction does not prevent progression. If the curve progresses to approximately 25–40 degrees while significant growth remains, bracing may be indicated to reduce further progression. Choice B is incorrect because bed rest and sports restriction provide no preventive benefit and may negatively affect conditioning and psychosocial health. Choice C is incorrect because immediate bracing is not routinely required for a stable curve of this magnitude; bracing is generally considered when a curve reaches at least 25 degrees or demonstrates meaningful progression in a skeletally immature patient. Choice D is incorrect because urgent MRI and operative referral are not indicated without atypical curve features, significant pain, rapid progression, neurologic abnormalities, or a severe curve. Choice E is incorrect because this patient remains at increased risk for progression due to substantial remaining skeletal growth and therefore requires ongoing surveillance.

Question 138  [ID: 658c753580a74c74a12db5d5aba581e7]
A 3-week-old infant is brought to the clinic because the parents have noticed a persistent abnormal head position since the first week of life. The infant was delivered vaginally after a prolonged breech presentation. Examination shows the head tilted to the left with the chin rotated to the right. A firm, nontender, 2-cm mass is palpable along the left sternocleidomastoid muscle. Passive rotation of the head to the left is limited. The remainder of the neurologic examination is normal. Ultrasonography demonstrates fusiform thickening within the left sternocleidomastoid muscle without a fluid collection, and cervical radiographs show no vertebral abnormality. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this infant's findings?

A. Denervation of the sternocleidomastoid muscle due to congenital injury of the spinal accessory nerve
B. Ischemic injury with subsequent fibrosis and shortening of the sternocleidomastoid muscle
C. Autoimmune destruction of acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction
D. Inflammatory laxity of the transverse ligament causing atlantoaxial instability
E. Congenital failure of segmentation of the cervical vertebrae

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The infant has congenital muscular torticollis, characterized by lateral flexion of the head toward the affected sternocleidomastoid muscle and rotation of the chin away from the affected side. Breech positioning and difficult delivery can cause compression, stretching, or impaired blood flow within the sternocleidomastoid muscle. The resulting ischemic injury and intramuscular hemorrhage lead to fibrosis, shortening, and formation of a firm pseudotumor within the muscle. Ultrasonography commonly shows fusiform sternocleidomastoid thickening. Therefore, option B is correct.

A is incorrect because spinal accessory nerve injury would produce weakness and atrophy of the sternocleidomastoid and trapezius muscles rather than a firm intramuscular mass with shortening.

C is incorrect because autoimmune loss of postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors causes fatigable weakness and may produce ptosis, impaired feeding, or respiratory weakness; it does not cause focal fibrosis or a fixed head posture.

D is incorrect because atlantoaxial instability is associated with conditions such as Down syndrome, juvenile idiopathic arthritis, and trauma. It would not explain fusiform thickening and a palpable mass within the sternocleidomastoid muscle.

E is incorrect because congenital failure of cervical vertebral segmentation causes Klippel-Feil syndrome, which typically presents with a short neck, low posterior hairline, and globally restricted neck motion. The normal cervical radiographs and focal sternocleidomastoid abnormality argue against this mechanism.

Question 139  [ID: 66712659da814e208baef28ab76ebb73]
A 16-year-old boy presents with 3 months of progressively worsening pain and swelling just above his right knee. The pain initially occurred with activity but is now present at night and does not improve with rest. He denies trauma, fever, chills, or weight loss. Examination shows a firm, tender, poorly mobile mass over the distal thigh with decreased knee flexion. Laboratory studies reveal a mildly elevated serum alkaline phosphatase level with normal leukocyte count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, calcium, and phosphate levels. Plain radiographs demonstrate a destructive mixed lytic and sclerotic lesion in the metaphysis of the distal femur, periosteal elevation forming a triangular margin, and radiating spicules extending into the surrounding soft tissue. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Chondrosarcoma
B. Ewing sarcoma
C. Osteomyelitis
D. Osteosarcoma
E. Osteochondroma

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: Osteosarcoma is the most likely diagnosis. It occurs most commonly in adolescents and typically arises in the metaphysis of a long structure, especially near the knee in the distal femur or proximal tibia. Progressive localized pain, swelling, a firm mass, and an elevated alkaline phosphatase level are characteristic. The radiographic findings of a destructive mixed lytic and sclerotic lesion, radiating spicules producing a sunburst pattern, and periosteal elevation creating a Codman triangle strongly support this diagnosis.

A. Chondrosarcoma typically occurs in adults older than 40 years and more often involves the pelvis, proximal femur, or shoulder. Imaging may show rings-and-arcs or popcorn-like calcifications rather than a sunburst pattern.

B. Ewing sarcoma most often affects children and adolescents but usually arises in the diaphysis of a long structure or in the pelvis. It classically produces an onion-skin periosteal reaction and may be associated with fever, leukocytosis, and elevated inflammatory markers.

C. Osteomyelitis may cause localized pain and destructive radiographic changes, but patients commonly have fever, elevated inflammatory markers, leukocytosis, or an identifiable infectious source. The described aggressive periosteal pattern and mixed lytic-sclerotic metaphyseal lesion are more characteristic of osteosarcoma.

E. Osteochondroma is a benign exophytic lesion that usually presents as a painless, slow-growing mass. Imaging demonstrates a cartilage-capped projection with continuity between the cortex and medullary cavity of the lesion and the underlying structure, not an aggressive destructive lesion with periosteal elevation.

Question 140  [ID: 68bf9435240644c98e9ea9f8c186a952]
A 15-year-old boy presents with 3 months of progressive pain and swelling near his left knee. The pain is worse at night and is no longer relieved by ibuprofen. He has no history of trauma, fever, or weight loss. His father underwent enucleation of both eyes during early childhood. Examination shows a firm, tender swelling over the distal thigh with reduced knee flexion. Laboratory studies reveal an elevated alkaline phosphatase level with normal serum calcium and phosphate levels. Plain radiographs demonstrate a mixed lytic and sclerotic lesion in the distal femoral metaphysis with cortical disruption, a radiating periosteal pattern, and elevation of the periosteum at the lesion margin. Which of the following molecular abnormalities most likely contributed to this patient's condition?

A. Biallelic loss of RB1 resulting in failure of G1-to-S cell-cycle checkpoint regulation
B. APC inactivation causing accumulation of beta-catenin and constitutive Wnt signaling
C. EWSR1-FLI1 fusion producing an abnormal transcription factor
D. NF1 inactivation causing persistent activation of RAS signaling
E. EXT1 inactivation causing impaired heparan sulfate synthesis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The adolescent's progressive pain and swelling, metaphyseal location near the knee, mixed lytic-sclerotic appearance, radiating periosteal reaction, and periosteal elevation are characteristic of osteosarcoma. His father's history of bilateral retinoblastoma suggests a germline RB1 pathogenic variant. RB1 encodes the retinoblastoma protein, which normally restrains the G1-to-S transition by binding E2F transcription factors. Biallelic loss releases E2F, permitting uncontrolled cell-cycle progression. Germline RB1 variants markedly increase the risk of osteosarcoma. Choice B is incorrect because APC loss and beta-catenin accumulation are associated with familial adenomatous polyposis and colorectal neoplasia. Choice C is incorrect because the EWSR1-FLI1 fusion, usually produced by t(11;22), is associated with Ewing sarcoma, which more commonly presents as a diaphyseal lesion with an onion-skin periosteal reaction. Choice D is incorrect because NF1 loss increases RAS pathway activity and predisposes to neurofibromas and malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumors. Choice E is incorrect because EXT1 loss impairs heparan sulfate synthesis and causes hereditary multiple exostoses, which predisposes primarily to secondary peripheral chondrosarcoma rather than the lesion described.

Question 141  [ID: 6928abb382c2454cbb8b5f22eb8e526e]
A 38-year-old man presents with 5 days of sharp lower back pain that began after lifting a heavy appliance. The pain radiates down the posterolateral aspect of his left thigh into the lateral foot and worsens with coughing and prolonged sitting. He denies fever, weight loss, urinary retention, fecal incontinence, saddle anesthesia, or recent trauma. His medical history is unremarkable, and he takes no medications. Temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), blood pressure is 126/78 mm Hg, and pulse is 76/min. Examination shows paraspinal tenderness, a positive straight-leg raise on the left, decreased sensation along the lateral left foot, and an absent left Achilles reflex. Lower-extremity strength is 5/5 bilaterally, and rectal tone is normal. MRI demonstrates focal posterolateral displacement of intervertebral material at L5-S1 with contact of the left S1 nerve root. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic treatment for this patient?

A. Naproxen
B. Oxycodone
C. Prednisone
D. Gabapentin
E. Methocarbamol

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Naproxen is the best initial pharmacologic treatment. This patient has acute lower back pain with unilateral nerve-root irritation but no progressive motor deficit, bowel or bladder dysfunction, saddle anesthesia, systemic illness, or other finding requiring urgent intervention. A nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug is appropriate first-line therapy when there are no renal, gastrointestinal, cardiovascular, or hypersensitivity contraindications. NSAIDs reduce pain and inflammation and should be combined with continued activity as tolerated rather than prolonged bed rest. Oxycodone is not first-line because opioids provide limited additional benefit and carry risks of sedation, constipation, misuse, and dependence; they should be reserved for carefully selected patients with severe pain unresponsive to safer therapies. Systemic prednisone is not routinely recommended because any improvement in function is generally modest, pain relief is inconsistent, and adverse effects may occur. Gabapentin is not recommended as initial treatment because evidence does not support meaningful benefit for acute radiating lower-extremity pain, and it can cause dizziness and somnolence. Methocarbamol may provide limited short-term benefit when prominent muscle spasm is present, but it does not directly address nerve-root inflammation and is less appropriate than an NSAID as initial monotherapy in this case.

Question 142  [ID: 6aa3cc2539b149c8a4a853e9210ba231]
A 13-year-old boy presents with 3 months of gradually worsening pain just below the right knee. The pain is aggravated by running, jumping, squatting, and climbing stairs and improves with rest. He plays competitive basketball and reports no acute injury, fever, night pain, or weight loss. Examination shows a prominent, tender tibial tubercle and pain at this site with resisted knee extension. There is no joint effusion, erythema, warmth, or limitation of passive range of motion. The patient walks without a limp. A complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are within normal limits. Which of the following findings is most likely to be seen on a lateral plain radiograph of the affected area?

A. Irregularity and fragmentation of the tibial tubercle with adjacent soft-tissue swelling
B. A well-defined lucent lesion with a sclerotic rim in the femoral epiphysis
C. Joint-space narrowing with marginal osteophyte formation and subchondral sclerosis
D. An aggressive metaphyseal lesion with a sunburst periosteal reaction and soft-tissue mass
E. Widening and displacement through the proximal femoral physis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is characteristic of Osgood-Schlatter disease, a traction apophysitis involving the tibial tubercle in physically active adolescents. Repetitive contraction of the quadriceps transmits force through the patellar tendon to the developing tibial tubercle, producing localized pain, tenderness, and prominence. The diagnosis is usually clinical, but lateral radiographs may show irregularity, sclerosis, or fragmentation of the tibial tubercle, thickening of the patellar tendon, and adjacent soft-tissue swelling. Normal inflammatory markers and the absence of systemic symptoms support a noninfectious, nonmalignant process. Choice B describes an osteochondral lesion, such as osteochondritis dissecans, which typically involves the femoral condyle and may cause swelling, catching, or locking. Choice C describes osteoarthritis, which is unlikely in an adolescent and would not cause isolated tibial tubercle tenderness. Choice D describes an aggressive bone-forming malignancy such as osteosarcoma; this would raise concern for persistent or nocturnal pain, swelling, a mass, and destructive radiographic changes. Choice E describes slipped capital femoral epiphysis, which affects the hip and may present with hip, thigh, or referred knee pain but does not cause focal tibial tubercle tenderness or prominence.

Question 143  [ID: 6bbf4124cf104e2ab5f382603cdd783b]
A 72-year-old man presents with severe left knee pain and swelling that began abruptly 12 hours ago. He denies trauma but reports a similar self-limited episode involving his right wrist 6 months ago. His medical history includes hypertension and hereditary hemochromatosis. His temperature is 37.8°C (100.0°F). Examination shows a warm, erythematous left knee with a large effusion and markedly limited range of motion. The peripheral leukocyte count is 12,800/μL. Radiographs demonstrate linear calcification within the articular cartilage. Arthrocentesis yields cloudy fluid with 18,000 leukocytes/μL, predominantly neutrophils; Gram stain shows no organisms. Which of the following findings is most likely to be seen on compensated polarized light microscopy of the synovial fluid?

A. Needle-shaped crystals with strong negative birefringence
B. Rhomboid-shaped crystals with weak positive birefringence
C. Hexagonal crystals with no birefringence
D. Cholesterol plates with notched corners and strong positive birefringence
E. Maltese cross-shaped lipid droplets with positive birefringence

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. The patient's advanced age, acute inflammatory monoarthritis involving the knee, prior wrist involvement, hereditary hemochromatosis, and linear cartilage calcification are characteristic of calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition. These crystals are typically rhomboid-shaped and weakly positively birefringent under compensated polarized light microscopy. When aligned parallel to the slow axis of the compensator, they appear blue. Hemochromatosis is an important metabolic association because excess iron can impair pyrophosphatase activity and promote calcium pyrophosphate accumulation. A is incorrect because needle-shaped, strongly negatively birefringent monosodium urate crystals are associated with gout; they appear yellow when parallel to the slow axis. C is incorrect because hexagonal, nonbirefringent crystals are composed of cystine and are found in urine in cystinuria, not in an acutely inflamed joint. D is incorrect because cholesterol crystals are large plates with notched corners and may occur in chronic effusions, particularly with longstanding inflammatory disease, but they do not match this clinical and radiographic presentation. E is incorrect because Maltese cross-shaped lipid droplets may be observed in lipid-containing fluids but are not the expected cause of this acute crystal-induced inflammatory episode.

Question 144  [ID: 6d6a5f98be514fcfa18d5484ac2c730c]
A 72-year-old man presents with 8 months of progressive aching pain and heaviness in both buttocks and thighs. His symptoms occur after walking approximately two blocks and improve when he sits or leans forward over a shopping cart. He denies pain at rest, fever, weight loss, recent trauma, and bowel or bladder dysfunction. Physical examination shows normal lower-extremity strength, symmetric patellar reflexes, mildly diminished Achilles reflexes, and intact distal pulses. Straight-leg raise testing is negative bilaterally. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein levels are within normal limits. Plain radiographs demonstrate multilevel degenerative changes, reduced intervertebral disk height, and facet joint hypertrophy. Which of the following diagnostic studies is most appropriate to confirm the cause of this patient's symptoms and define the extent of neural compression?

A. Ankle-brachial index measurement
B. Electromyography and nerve conduction studies
C. Magnetic resonance imaging without contrast
D. Radionuclide bone scan
E. Computed tomography myelography

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The patient's exertional bilateral buttock and thigh discomfort, relief with sitting or forward flexion, preserved distal pulses, and degenerative findings on radiographs are characteristic of neurogenic claudication caused by lumbar spinal stenosis. Magnetic resonance imaging without contrast is the preferred confirmatory study because it provides detailed visualization of the spinal canal, intervertebral disks, ligamentum flavum, facet joints, nerve roots, and degree of neural compression. Contrast is generally unnecessary unless infection, malignancy, inflammatory disease, or prior surgery is suspected. An ankle-brachial index (A) is useful when peripheral arterial disease is suspected; however, the positional relief with flexion and normal pulses favor neurogenic rather than vascular claudication. Electromyography and nerve conduction studies (B) may help distinguish radiculopathy from peripheral neuropathy when the diagnosis is uncertain, but they do not directly define anatomic narrowing or compression. A radionuclide bone scan (D) is more useful for detecting occult fracture, metastatic disease, or osteomyelitis and is not the preferred test in this presentation. Computed tomography myelography (E) can delineate neural compression but is invasive and is generally reserved for patients who cannot undergo MRI or when MRI findings are inconclusive.

Question 145  [ID: 6e016890540a4318b9b948c34a65fa56]
A 67-year-old woman presents to the emergency department 1 hour after falling onto her outstretched right hand. She reports severe wrist pain and swelling. Medical history includes hypertension and osteoporosis. Examination shows a closed, dorsally angulated deformity of the wrist with intact skin. Capillary refill is less than 2 seconds, and sensation and finger movement are preserved. Radiographs demonstrate cortical disruption through the distal radial metaphysis with dorsal displacement and angulation of the distal segment. Closed manipulation and immobilization are planned. The patient is alert, cooperative, and hemodynamically stable. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacologic intervention to provide targeted analgesia before the procedure while minimizing systemic adverse effects?

A. Inject lidocaine without epinephrine into the localized collection of blood at the injury site
B. Administer oral acetaminophen immediately before manipulation
C. Inject lidocaine with epinephrine circumferentially into the fingers
D. Administer intravenous ketorolac as the sole agent immediately before manipulation
E. Administer intravenous morphine followed by discharge without post-procedure monitoring

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The radiographic and clinical findings indicate a displaced distal radius fracture requiring closed reduction. A hematoma block using lidocaine without epinephrine provides effective, targeted anesthesia by placing local anesthetic directly into the fracture hematoma. It minimizes systemic opioid exposure and avoids the cardiopulmonary risks and monitoring requirements associated with procedural sedation.

B is incorrect because oral acetaminophen does not provide sufficiently rapid or profound analgesia for closed manipulation of a displaced fracture.

C is incorrect because circumferential digital injection is not the appropriate anesthetic technique for a distal radius reduction. Epinephrine is also unnecessary for this procedure and may increase the risk of local ischemic complications in areas with limited collateral circulation.

D is incorrect because ketorolac may help with general pain control but is inadequate as the sole agent for a painful closed reduction. It also carries risks of gastrointestinal bleeding, renal injury, and platelet dysfunction.

E is incorrect because intravenous morphine can provide analgesia but may cause respiratory depression, hypotension, nausea, and sedation. A patient receiving intravenous opioids requires appropriate monitoring and reassessment before discharge; therefore, discharge without post-administration monitoring is unsafe.

Question 146  [ID: 6e8359cba230400baff451f6c0e6e973]
A 7-year-old girl is brought to the clinic because of 4 months of swelling and stiffness of the right knee and left ankle. Her symptoms are most pronounced in the morning and improve after approximately 1 hour of activity. She has no history of trauma, fever, rash, weight loss, or recent infection. Examination shows warmth, effusion, and decreased range of motion at the affected sites without overlying erythema. Laboratory studies show a mildly elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, a positive antinuclear antibody titer, and negative rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies. Synovial fluid contains 9,000 leukocytes/mm3 with negative Gram stain and culture. Radiographs show soft-tissue swelling and effusions without erosions. Symptoms have persisted despite 6 weeks of appropriately dosed naproxen. Which of the following is the most appropriate next clinical intervention?

A. Begin long-term oral prednisone therapy
B. Administer intra-articular triamcinolone injections
C. Begin intravenous ceftriaxone therapy
D. Initiate etanercept therapy
E. Perform arthroscopic synovectomy

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This child has oligoarticular juvenile rheumatoid arthritis, now more commonly termed oligoarticular juvenile idiopathic arthritis. The presentation is characterized by onset before age 16, persistent symptoms for more than 6 weeks, involvement of four or fewer joints, prolonged morning stiffness, sterile inflammatory synovial fluid, and exclusion of infectious and other causes. After an inadequate response to an NSAID, intra-articular glucocorticoid injection is an appropriate next intervention for limited persistent joint involvement. Triamcinolone, particularly triamcinolone hexacetonide when available, can provide rapid and prolonged local control while minimizing systemic adverse effects. A is incorrect because long-term systemic prednisone can cause growth suppression, osteoporosis, weight gain, hypertension, and other toxicities; systemic glucocorticoids are generally reserved for severe systemic or refractory manifestations. C is incorrect because the chronic course, relatively low synovial leukocyte count, and negative Gram stain and culture do not support bacterial infection. D is incorrect because a tumor necrosis factor inhibitor is generally reserved for disease that remains active despite intra-articular therapy and conventional disease-modifying treatment such as methotrexate. E is incorrect because surgical synovectomy is not an appropriate early intervention and is reserved for rare, refractory cases with persistent functional impairment.

Question 147  [ID: 6ee72c40b2754076be82040c9b4398d8]
A 42-year-old man presents with 4 months of progressively worsening deep right groin pain. The pain increases with weight-bearing and occasionally radiates to the anterior thigh. He has systemic lupus erythematosus and has taken prednisone 20 to 40 mg daily for several years. He drinks three to four alcoholic beverages nightly. Examination reveals an antalgic gait and pain with passive internal rotation of the right hip. There is no erythema, warmth, or focal tenderness. His temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), leukocyte count is 7,800/mm3, erythrocyte sedimentation rate is 12 mm/hr, and C-reactive protein is normal. An anteroposterior radiograph of the pelvis shows no fracture, joint-space narrowing, or other abnormality. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic study to obtain next?

A. Magnetic resonance imaging of the right hip without contrast
B. Computed tomography of the right hip without contrast
C. Ultrasonography of the right hip
D. Technetium-99m radionuclide bone scan
E. Fluoroscopically guided aspiration of the right hip

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Magnetic resonance imaging without contrast is the most sensitive and specific study for detecting early avascular necrosis when plain radiographs are normal. This patient has progressive weight-bearing groin pain, painful limitation of internal rotation, chronic glucocorticoid exposure, and substantial alcohol use, all of which strongly support this diagnosis. MRI can identify early marrow changes before subchondral collapse or other radiographic abnormalities develop and can also evaluate the contralateral side, where clinically silent disease may be present.

B is incorrect because computed tomography is less sensitive than MRI for early disease. CT is more useful for defining subchondral fracture, articular surface collapse, and later-stage structural changes.

C is incorrect because ultrasonography may detect a joint effusion but cannot reliably identify early ischemic changes within the femoral head.

D is incorrect because radionuclide bone scanning can demonstrate altered uptake but is less sensitive and less specific than MRI. It may be considered when MRI is contraindicated or unavailable.

E is incorrect because aspiration is primarily indicated when septic or crystal-induced arthritis is suspected. This patient is afebrile, has normal inflammatory markers, and lacks warmth or acute severe pain, making an infectious or inflammatory joint process less likely.

Question 148  [ID: 6efb9dbbbdb74ba699c6f992569480e2]
A 6-year-old girl is brought for follow-up after 4 months of intermittent swelling of the right knee and left ankle, morning stiffness lasting 1 hour, and limping that improves throughout the day. She has no fever, rash, weight loss, or history of trauma. Examination shows a small right knee effusion and decreased flexion without erythema or tenderness. Laboratory studies show a positive antinuclear antibody titer of 1:320, negative rheumatoid factor, and mildly elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate. She is receiving naproxen and weekly methotrexate with improvement in her symptoms. She denies eye pain, redness, photophobia, or visual changes. Which of the following preventive measures is most important to recommend for this patient?

A. Annual visual acuity testing by her primary care clinician
B. Regular slit-lamp examinations by an ophthalmologist
C. Screening colonoscopy beginning at age 10 years
D. Annual radiographs of all symptomatic joints
E. Prophylactic topical ophthalmic glucocorticoids

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This young, antinuclear antibody-positive girl with chronic oligoarticular inflammation is at high risk for chronic anterior uveitis, an important extra-articular complication of juvenile rheumatoid arthritis. The ocular inflammation is frequently asymptomatic until complications such as cataracts, glaucoma, band keratopathy, or permanent vision loss develop. Therefore, regular screening with slit-lamp examination by an ophthalmologist is essential even when the patient has no eye pain, redness, photophobia, or visual changes. High-risk children may require examinations approximately every 3 months, with the interval adjusted according to age at onset, antinuclear antibody status, disease duration, and clinical course. Choice A is incorrect because routine visual acuity testing does not reliably detect early anterior chamber inflammation. Choice C is incorrect because this condition does not independently warrant childhood colonoscopy screening. Choice D is incorrect because serial radiographs of every symptomatic joint are not routinely recommended as a preventive measure and expose the child to unnecessary radiation; imaging is guided by clinical indications. Choice E is incorrect because topical ophthalmic glucocorticoids are used to treat confirmed ocular inflammation and should not be given prophylactically due to risks such as cataracts, glaucoma, and infection.

Question 149  [ID: 6fd2ab584f01415cb19ef1b1ff13100f]
A 47-year-old man presents with sudden onset of severe pain and swelling of the right knee that began 12 hours ago. He reports two similar self-limited episodes during the past year. He has no history of trauma and takes no daily medications. His temperature is 37.4°C (99.3°F). Examination shows a warm, erythematous, markedly tender right knee with a moderate effusion and limited range of motion. Synovial fluid analysis reveals 28,000 leukocytes/mm3 with neutrophil predominance and weakly positively birefringent rhomboid-shaped crystals; Gram stain and culture are negative. Radiographs demonstrate linear calcification within the articular cartilage. Because of the patient's age and recurrent episodes, which of the following is the most appropriate health maintenance measure after the acute episode resolves?

A. Evaluate for associated metabolic conditions with iron studies, serum calcium, magnesium, phosphorus, alkaline phosphatase, and thyroid testing
B. Begin lifelong allopurinol therapy and monitor serum uric acid every 6 months
C. Recommend strict avoidance of all calcium-containing foods and supplements
D. Prescribe indefinite daily colchicine without evaluating for contributing conditions
E. Reassure the patient that no additional evaluation is indicated because recurrent episodes are expected at his age

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The synovial fluid findings of weakly positively birefringent rhomboid-shaped crystals and radiographic cartilage calcification indicate calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition. Because this patient is younger than 60 years and has recurrent episodes, evaluation for associated metabolic disorders is appropriate. Important associations include hemochromatosis, hyperparathyroidism, hypomagnesemia, hypophosphatasia, and hypothyroidism. Suggested testing includes iron studies, calcium, magnesium, phosphorus, alkaline phosphatase, and thyroid function; parathyroid hormone testing is appropriate when calcium is elevated or suspicion remains high. Identifying and treating an underlying disorder is an important preventive and health maintenance measure. Option B is incorrect because allopurinol lowers urate and prevents recurrent monosodium urate crystal attacks; it does not remove calcium pyrophosphate crystals. Option C is incorrect because dietary calcium restriction does not prevent crystal deposition and may adversely affect skeletal health. Option D is incorrect because low-dose colchicine may be considered for frequent recurrences, but indefinite prophylaxis without first evaluating this unusually young patient for contributing conditions is not the best next step. Option E is incorrect because this condition is predominantly associated with older age; presentation before age 60 should prompt evaluation for familial or secondary metabolic causes.

Question 150  [ID: 704f5c68abda4712bc5bfe4a9069f151]
A 67-year-old man presents with 8 months of aching pain in the lower back, buttocks, and posterior thighs that occurs after walking approximately two blocks. The discomfort is accompanied by bilateral leg heaviness and resolves within several minutes when he sits or leans forward over a shopping cart. He has no bowel or bladder dysfunction, saddle anesthesia, fever, recent trauma, or history of malignancy. Lower-extremity strength is 5/5 bilaterally, sensation is intact, and pedal pulses are normal. Straight-leg raise testing is negative. MRI demonstrates degenerative facet and ligamentous hypertrophy with reduction of the anteroposterior diameter of the central canal at L3-L4 and L4-L5. He has no history of peptic ulcer disease, chronic kidney disease, or anticoagulant use. In addition to activity modification and supervised physical therapy, which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy?

A. Naproxen
B. Oxycodone
C. Prednisone taken orally for 6 weeks
D. Methotrexate
E. Cyclobenzaprine taken indefinitely

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Naproxen is the best initial pharmacologic option. This patient's exertional bilateral leg discomfort, relief with sitting or forward flexion, normal peripheral pulses, and degenerative reduction in central canal caliber are characteristic of neurogenic claudication. In the absence of renal disease, peptic ulcer disease, anticoagulant use, or another contraindication, a short trial of a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug can be used with physical therapy and activity modification to reduce pain and improve function. Oxycodone is inappropriate as initial therapy because opioids have substantial risks, including sedation, constipation, dependence, and falls, and should not be routinely used for chronic nonmalignant pain. Prolonged systemic prednisone is not recommended because sustained benefit is unproven and adverse effects include hyperglycemia, infection, osteoporosis, and adrenal suppression. Methotrexate is a disease-modifying antirheumatic drug used for inflammatory conditions and has no role in treating degenerative canal narrowing. Cyclobenzaprine may provide brief benefit for acute muscle spasm, but indefinite use is inappropriate, particularly in older adults, because of sedation, anticholinergic effects, and fall risk.

Question 151  [ID: 70ea0aa14e554830a48603628e1c7f7b]
A 68-year-old woman presents with acute midline back pain that began after lifting a bag of groceries 3 days ago. She has no history of major trauma. Menopause occurred at age 44, and she has never received estrogen therapy. She also completed several prolonged courses of prednisone for severe asthma. Physical examination shows thoracic kyphosis, decreased height compared with prior records, and focal tenderness over T12 without neurologic deficits. Plain radiographs demonstrate an anterior wedge compression deformity of T12. Serum calcium, phosphorus, creatinine, alkaline phosphatase, thyroid-stimulating hormone, and 25-hydroxyvitamin D levels are within normal limits. Which of the following additional diagnostic findings independently meets the densitometric criterion for the suspected underlying condition?

A. Lumbar spine T-score of −1.8 on central dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry
B. Femoral neck T-score of −2.6 on central dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry
C. Lumbar spine Z-score of −2.1 on central dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry
D. Calcaneal T-score of −2.3 on quantitative ultrasonography
E. Total hip T-score of −0.9 on central dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. In a postmenopausal woman, osteoporosis is diagnosed densitometrically by a T-score of −2.5 or lower at the femoral neck, total hip, lumbar spine, or, in selected circumstances, the 33% radius using central dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry. A femoral neck T-score of −2.6 therefore meets the diagnostic threshold. This patient also has a low-trauma vertebral compression fracture, which is sufficient for a clinical diagnosis regardless of bone mineral density. Choice A is incorrect because a T-score between −1.0 and −2.5 indicates low bone mass but does not meet the densitometric threshold for osteoporosis. Choice C is incorrect because Z-scores compare a patient with age-, sex-, and ethnicity-matched controls and are primarily used when evaluating premenopausal women, men younger than 50 years, and children; a Z-score of −2.0 or lower is described as below the expected range for age but does not establish osteoporosis in this postmenopausal patient. Choice D is incorrect because quantitative ultrasonography may help estimate fracture risk but cannot establish the diagnosis or replace central dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry. Choice E is incorrect because a T-score of −0.9 is within the expected normal range.

Question 152  [ID: 71aaec46f44a4ed6ad9b927f3b8ccda2]
A 46-year-old woman presents with 8 months of progressive stiffness and swelling of both hands. She reports that her fingers become pale and then blue when exposed to cold. She also has increasing difficulty swallowing solid foods, frequent heartburn, and exertional dyspnea. Physical examination shows shiny, thickened skin over the fingers, hands, forearms, and face; decreased oral aperture; scattered facial telangiectasias; and a healing ulcer on the tip of the right index finger. Fine inspiratory crackles are heard at both lung bases. Laboratory studies reveal a positive antinuclear antibody with a nucleolar pattern and antibodies directed against DNA topoisomerase I. Pulmonary function testing demonstrates a restrictive pattern with reduced diffusing capacity. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Dermatomyositis
B. Eosinophilic fasciitis
C. Mixed connective tissue disease
D. Systemic lupus erythematosus
E. Systemic sclerosis

Correct Answer: E
Explanation: Systemic sclerosis is the most likely diagnosis. The patient has progressive skin thickening extending proximal to the hands, cold-induced triphasic digital color changes, digital ulceration, telangiectasias, decreased oral aperture, esophageal dysfunction, and interstitial lung involvement. Antibodies against DNA topoisomerase I, also called anti-Scl-70 antibodies, are strongly associated with diffuse cutaneous involvement and an increased risk of interstitial lung disease. Dermatomyositis typically causes symmetric proximal muscle weakness, elevated muscle enzymes, a heliotrope eruption, and Gottron papules rather than distal skin tightening and digital ischemia. Eosinophilic fasciitis causes painful, symmetric induration of the extremities with a peau d'orange appearance and groove sign; it generally spares the fingers and lacks the characteristic vascular and visceral findings described here. Mixed connective tissue disease may include overlapping features and cold-induced digital color changes, but it is most strongly associated with high-titer anti-U1 ribonucleoprotein antibodies and usually includes findings from several connective tissue disorders. Systemic lupus erythematosus may cause arthralgias and cold-sensitive digital color changes, but it does not typically cause progressive skin tightening, sclerodactyly, or this antibody profile.

Question 153  [ID: 7258d07583dd45a7a8d0cab82c1f41dd]
A 46-year-old woman presents with a 2-day history of severe headache, blurred vision, and decreased urine output. For the past year, she has experienced episodic finger pallor and cyanosis with cold exposure, progressive difficulty opening her mouth, heartburn, and tightening of the skin over her hands and forearms. Examination reveals a blood pressure of 218/124 mm Hg, retinal flame hemorrhages, perioral skin tightening, digital tip ulcers, and thickened, shiny skin extending proximal to the metacarpophalangeal joints. Laboratory studies show a serum creatinine level of 3.1 mg/dL, increased from 0.8 mg/dL 1 month ago; hemoglobin of 9.2 g/dL; thrombocytopenia; and schistocytes on peripheral smear. Urinalysis demonstrates mild proteinuria and microscopic hematuria. Testing for anti-RNA polymerase III antibodies is positive. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate pharmacologic intervention?

A. Captopril
B. High-dose intravenous methylprednisolone
C. Intravenous cyclophosphamide
D. Amlodipine
E. Plasmapheresis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Captopril is the best immediate treatment. This patient has abrupt malignant hypertension, rapidly progressive acute kidney injury, microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, and thrombocytopenia in the setting of characteristic vasospastic episodes, progressive skin thickening, gastrointestinal involvement, digital ischemia, and anti-RNA polymerase III antibodies. These findings indicate an acute renal vascular complication driven by severe arteriolar narrowing and activation of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system. An angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor should be started immediately and titrated aggressively, even if the serum creatinine initially rises. Captopril is often selected because its short half-life permits rapid titration. Dialysis may be required temporarily, but ACE inhibitor therapy should be continued because renal recovery can occur.

B is incorrect because high-dose glucocorticoids can precipitate or worsen this acute renal complication and should be avoided unless required for another compelling indication.

C is incorrect because cyclophosphamide may be used for selected patients with progressive inflammatory interstitial lung involvement, but it does not provide the urgent blood pressure and renin-angiotensin control required here.

D is incorrect because amlodipine may help episodic digital vasospasm and can lower blood pressure, but it does not specifically inhibit the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system and is not the preferred lifesaving therapy for this presentation.

E is incorrect because plasmapheresis is used for disorders such as thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura. Although this patient has schistocytes and thrombocytopenia, the accompanying malignant hypertension, acute kidney injury, characteristic skin and vascular findings, and anti-RNA polymerase III antibodies support a hypertensive renal microangiopathy for which immediate ACE inhibition is indicated.

Question 154  [ID: 7314847d2cc04307bbe2f1a6469c6857]
A 72-year-old woman presents with 6 weeks of bilateral shoulder and hip aching that is most severe upon awakening. She reports morning stiffness lasting approximately 90 minutes and difficulty dressing, rising from a chair, and getting out of bed. She denies recent trauma or a new medication. Examination demonstrates limited active range of motion of both shoulders because of pain, but passive range of motion is nearly intact. Strength is 5/5 when pain is overcome, and there is no joint swelling or rash. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 78 mm/hour, elevated C-reactive protein, normal creatine kinase, and negative rheumatoid factor. Shoulder ultrasonography demonstrates bilateral subdeltoid bursitis without tendon rupture. Which additional historical finding is most important to elicit urgently in this patient?

A. Pain radiating from the neck into the thumb and index finger
B. Progressive difficulty swallowing and climbing stairs
C. New headache, jaw fatigue while chewing, or transient visual loss
D. Shoulder pain worsened by overhead activity and lying on the affected side
E. Cold intolerance, constipation, and recent weight gain

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: This patient's age, subacute bilateral shoulder and hip girdle pain, prolonged morning stiffness, elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein, normal creatine kinase, preserved objective strength, and bilateral bursitis are characteristic of polymyalgia rheumatica. During history taking, clinicians must urgently ask about new headache, scalp tenderness, jaw claudication, and visual symptoms because polymyalgia rheumatica is strongly associated with giant cell arteritis. Transient visual loss may precede irreversible blindness and requires immediate evaluation and high-dose glucocorticoid therapy. Choice A suggests cervical radiculopathy, which would more often produce dermatomal pain, sensory abnormalities, weakness, or altered reflexes. Choice B suggests an inflammatory myopathy, but that condition typically causes true proximal weakness and may elevate creatine kinase. Choice D is more typical of rotator cuff disease or subacromial impingement, which is usually localized and mechanically provoked rather than associated with prolonged bilateral morning stiffness and markedly elevated inflammatory markers. Choice E suggests hypothyroidism, which may cause generalized aches but does not best explain this characteristic presentation or identify the most urgent associated condition.

Question 155  [ID: 734c55a05a9d449e8e252944b060af58]
A 76-year-old man presents with 18 hours of severe right knee pain and swelling that began 2 days after an elective abdominal procedure. He cannot bear weight and denies trauma. His medical history includes stage 4 chronic kidney disease, hypertension, and atrial fibrillation treated with warfarin. His temperature is 37.2°C (99.0°F), blood pressure is 138/82 mm Hg, and heart rate is 88/min. Examination shows a large, warm right knee effusion with erythema and markedly limited range of motion. Laboratory studies reveal a leukocyte count of 10,800/mm3, serum creatinine of 3.1 mg/dL, and INR of 2.3. Arthrocentesis yields cloudy fluid with 28,000 leukocytes/mm3 and 85% neutrophils. Polarized microscopy demonstrates rhomboid-shaped crystals with weak positive birefringence. Gram stain is negative, and the synovial fluid culture shows no growth after 48 hours. Radiography demonstrates linear calcification within the articular cartilage. Which of the following is the most appropriate intervention for this patient?

A. Begin oral indomethacin at full anti-inflammatory dosing
B. Administer intra-articular triamcinolone after joint aspiration
C. Begin oral allopurinol with a target serum urate level below 6 mg/dL
D. Administer high-dose oral colchicine until gastrointestinal adverse effects occur
E. Begin intravenous vancomycin and ceftriaxone

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The synovial fluid finding of rhomboid-shaped, weakly positively birefringent crystals, along with linear cartilage calcification, indicates calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition causing an acute inflammatory attack. For a single affected joint, aspiration followed by an intra-articular glucocorticoid injection is an effective intervention. It is particularly appropriate here because advanced chronic kidney disease and anticoagulation increase the risks associated with systemic anti-inflammatory medications. Infection has been reasonably excluded by the clinical presentation, negative Gram stain, and negative culture.

A is incorrect because indomethacin and other NSAIDs can worsen renal function and increase bleeding risk in a patient with stage 4 chronic kidney disease who is taking warfarin.

C is incorrect because allopurinol lowers uric acid and is used for long-term prevention of recurrent monosodium urate crystal disease. It does not treat calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition and is not initiated as the primary intervention for this presentation.

D is incorrect because high-dose colchicine has substantial gastrointestinal and systemic toxicity. Colchicine clearance is reduced in advanced kidney disease, increasing the risk of neuromyopathy, bone marrow suppression, and other adverse effects. If used, it would require cautious dose reduction.

E is incorrect because empiric antibiotics are appropriate when septic arthritis remains strongly suspected. In this case, the characteristic crystals, cartilage calcification, absence of systemic toxicity, and negative synovial culture support a noninfectious crystal-induced inflammatory process.
