Question 156  [ID: 73e1e08cfb70409f80385d37c9b8380c]
A 46-year-old woman presents with an 18-month history of episodic finger pallor and cyanosis triggered by cold exposure, progressive difficulty swallowing solid foods, and tightening of the skin over her hands and face. She also reports increasing difficulty fully extending her fingers. Examination shows shiny, thickened skin distal to the elbows, reduced oral aperture, scattered facial telangiectasias, and firm subcutaneous nodules over several fingertips. Cardiac and pulmonary examinations are unremarkable. Antinuclear antibody testing is positive at a high titer with a centromere pattern. Hand radiographs show resorption of the distal phalangeal tufts and small periarticular calcium deposits. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's findings?

A. Immune-mediated endothelial injury with fibroblast activation and excessive extracellular matrix deposition
B. Deposition of circulating immune complexes with complement activation in small vessels
C. Cytotoxic T-lymphocyte destruction of striated muscle fibers expressing major histocompatibility complex class I molecules
D. Autoantibody-mediated stimulation of osteoclast differentiation with formation of an invasive synovial pannus
E. Deposition of monosodium urate crystals that activates the NLRP3 inflammasome and recruits neutrophils

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. This patient has systemic sclerosis, specifically a limited cutaneous phenotype, as indicated by cold-induced digital vasospasm, distal skin thickening, reduced oral aperture, telangiectasias, calcific deposits, esophageal dysmotility, and anticentromere antibodies. The foundational pathophysiology involves endothelial cell injury and dysregulated vascular tone, followed by immune activation and release of profibrotic mediators such as transforming growth factor-beta. These mediators activate fibroblasts, causing excessive synthesis and deposition of collagen and other extracellular matrix proteins. The resulting vasculopathy and fibrosis produce digital ischemia, skin tightening, contractures, gastrointestinal dysmotility, and progressive internal-organ involvement. B is incorrect because immune-complex deposition with complement activation is characteristic of type III hypersensitivity disorders such as systemic lupus erythematosus and does not primarily explain progressive fibrosis. C is incorrect because cytotoxic T-cell-mediated injury to muscle fibers is associated with inflammatory myopathies and would more commonly cause symmetric proximal muscle weakness and elevated muscle enzymes. D is incorrect because osteoclast activation and invasive pannus formation describe the joint destruction of rheumatoid arthritis, which typically presents with inflammatory synovitis and marginal erosions rather than skin tightening and vasospastic episodes. E is incorrect because urate crystal activation of the NLRP3 inflammasome causes acute gouty inflammation, usually presenting as episodic severe monoarthritis and potentially tophi, but it does not account for the patient's vascular, cutaneous, and esophageal findings.

Question 157  [ID: 74b68c6d691143c69dcebc0160efd935]
A 42-year-old man presents with severe lower back pain that began 2 days ago while lifting a heavy appliance. The pain radiates down the posterior aspect of both legs and has worsened despite ibuprofen. During the past 8 hours, he has developed difficulty initiating urination and numbness around the perineum. Examination shows decreased rectal tone, diminished sensation over the perianal region, bilateral ankle plantar-flexion weakness, and absent Achilles reflexes. A bladder scan reveals 650 mL of retained urine. CBC and erythrocyte sedimentation rate are within normal limits. MRI demonstrates a large central extradural extrusion at L5-S1 with marked compression of the descending nerve roots. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate intervention?

A. Strict bed rest and oral opioid therapy for 7 days
B. Epidural glucocorticoid injection
C. Emergent surgical decompression
D. Outpatient physical therapy and scheduled NSAID therapy
E. Oral prednisone followed by reassessment in 2 weeks

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The patient has acute bilateral radicular symptoms, urinary retention, perineal sensory loss, decreased rectal tone, lower-extremity weakness, and MRI evidence of severe descending nerve-root compression. These findings indicate cauda equina compression, a surgical emergency. Emergent decompression is required to relieve neural compression and reduce the risk of permanent bladder, bowel, sexual, sensory, and motor dysfunction. Bladder catheterization should also be performed for acute urinary retention, but it does not replace definitive decompression. Choice A is incorrect because bed rest and analgesics are inappropriate when progressive neurologic deficits and sphincter dysfunction are present. Choice B is incorrect because epidural glucocorticoid injections may provide temporary relief in selected patients with radicular pain but are contraindicated as a substitute for urgent surgery in a compressive neurologic emergency. Choice D is incorrect because NSAIDs, continued activity as tolerated, and physical therapy are appropriate for uncomplicated symptoms without red-flag findings. Choice E is incorrect because systemic glucocorticoids do not adequately relieve severe mechanical compression, and delaying intervention may result in irreversible neurologic injury.

Question 158  [ID: 7529517a4fba40098a736ebc1e97be06]
A 68-year-old woman presents with several months of gradually worsening right knee pain. The pain is exacerbated by walking and climbing stairs and improves with rest. She reports stiffness lasting approximately 10 minutes after awakening and denies fever, rash, or involvement of other joints. Her medical history includes stage 3 chronic kidney disease and a prior hospitalization for a bleeding gastric ulcer. Examination shows crepitus, mild bony enlargement, and tenderness along the joint line without erythema or significant warmth. There are no overlying skin lesions. Laboratory studies show a normal leukocyte count and erythrocyte sedimentation rate. Plain radiographs demonstrate asymmetric joint-space narrowing, marginal osteophytes, and subchondral sclerosis. She has had inadequate relief with exercise, weight reduction, and physical therapy. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. Oral naproxen
B. Oral prednisone
C. Topical diclofenac
D. Methotrexate
E. Oral oxycodone

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: This patient's activity-related knee pain, brief morning stiffness, crepitus, bony enlargement, and characteristic radiographic findings are consistent with osteoarthritis. A topical nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as diclofenac is an appropriate initial pharmacologic treatment for localized knee symptoms, particularly in an older patient at high risk for systemic NSAID toxicity. Topical administration provides clinically meaningful local pain relief with substantially less systemic exposure than oral NSAIDs. Oral naproxen is inappropriate because her chronic kidney disease and history of gastrointestinal bleeding place her at increased risk for renal injury and recurrent ulcer bleeding. Oral prednisone is not recommended for chronic management and can cause significant systemic adverse effects. Methotrexate is a disease-modifying antirheumatic drug used for inflammatory arthritides and does not treat the degenerative process described. Oxycodone is not first-line therapy because opioids have limited long-term benefit and substantial risks, including sedation, constipation, dependence, and overdose.

Question 159  [ID: 764bec7610dc4b058c12d31c56134767]
A 17-year-old boy presents with 4 months of progressively worsening focal pain along the anterior aspect of his right lower leg. He denies preceding trauma, fever, weight loss, or recent illness. The pain occurs daily and frequently awakens him from sleep. Examination reveals well-localized tenderness over the proximal tibia without erythema, warmth, swelling, or a palpable mass. He has full range of motion of the adjacent joints, and strength and sensation are intact. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are within normal limits. Plain radiographs demonstrate a 7-mm, well-circumscribed lucent cortical focus surrounded by dense reactive sclerosis. Which additional historical finding would most strongly support the suspected diagnosis?

A. Rapid and substantial pain relief after taking ibuprofen
B. Pain that occurs only with running and resolves with rest
C. Morning stiffness lasting longer than 1 hour
D. Recent pharyngitis followed by migratory joint pain
E. Progressive pain accompanied by fever and night sweats

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is characteristic of an osteoid osteoma, a small benign bone-forming tumor that most commonly occurs in adolescents and young adults. Typical findings include focal pain that is worse at night, localized tenderness, and a small radiolucent nidus surrounded by reactive sclerosis. The pain is mediated by high concentrations of prostaglandins within the lesion and therefore often improves rapidly and dramatically with nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs such as ibuprofen. Choice B is more suggestive of a stress fracture or exertional overuse injury, which typically produces activity-related pain that improves with rest. Choice C suggests an inflammatory arthropathy and would be less consistent with a focal cortical lesion and normal inflammatory markers. Choice D suggests a postinfectious process such as acute rheumatic fever, which typically causes migratory polyarthritis rather than persistent focal pain. Choice E raises concern for infection or malignancy; constitutional symptoms and elevated inflammatory markers may be present in those conditions but are absent in this patient.

Question 160  [ID: 774150bc605d469abf7a8de2a8603245]
A 32-year-old woman presents with 4 months of intermittent aching in the right shoulder and neck accompanied by numbness and tingling along the medial forearm and fourth and fifth digits. Symptoms worsen while stocking shelves overhead at work and when carrying a heavy shoulder bag. She denies trauma, fever, joint swelling, or persistent weakness. Examination shows rounded shoulders and reproduction of symptoms when the right arm is elevated and externally rotated. Grip strength, deep tendon reflexes, and distal pulses are normal at rest. Complete blood count and metabolic studies are normal. Plain radiographs show no acute abnormality, and electrodiagnostic testing reveals no fixed peripheral nerve injury. In addition to a supervised rehabilitation program, which of the following counseling recommendations is most appropriate to reduce this patient's symptoms and risk of recurrence?

A. Avoid repetitive overhead activity, modify workplace ergonomics, improve posture, and perform prescribed shoulder-girdle stretching and strengthening exercises
B. Use a tight shoulder brace continuously to depress the clavicle and limit movement
C. Continue overhead work without modification while taking a daily opioid before each shift
D. Begin prolonged bed rest and avoid all upper-extremity exercise until symptoms completely resolve
E. Perform repetitive overhead resistance exercises to reproduce symptoms and build tolerance

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's position-dependent pain and paresthesias, occupational exposure to repetitive overhead activity, postural abnormalities, and absence of fixed neurologic or vascular deficits are consistent with thoracic outlet syndrome, most likely the neurogenic form. Conservative management is first-line in patients without progressive weakness, vascular compromise, or tissue-threatening ischemia. Education should emphasize avoiding or modifying provocative overhead activities, improving workstation ergonomics, correcting posture, avoiding heavy shoulder loads, and adhering to physical therapy focused on stretching shortened anterior structures and strengthening the shoulder-girdle stabilizers. These measures decrease compression and help prevent recurrence.

A is correct because activity modification, ergonomic changes, postural correction, and prescribed rehabilitation directly address modifiable contributors.

B is incorrect because a tight brace may increase local compression and is not recommended for continuous use.

C is incorrect because opioids do not correct the precipitating mechanical factors, and routine prophylactic opioid use is inappropriate for chronic nonmalignant pain.

D is incorrect because prolonged rest promotes deconditioning and is less effective than gradual, supervised rehabilitation.

E is incorrect because repeatedly provoking symptoms with overhead resistance may aggravate compression; exercise should be individualized, controlled, and focused on posture, flexibility, and stabilization rather than symptom reproduction.

Question 161  [ID: 7741fec1ac56443cb13ced0b936567d6]
A 16-year-old boy presents with 3 months of progressive pain and swelling near his left knee. The pain initially occurred with activity but is now present at rest and frequently awakens him at night. He denies recent trauma, fever, or weight loss. Examination shows a firm, tender swelling over the distal thigh with reduced knee flexion. There is no overlying erythema or warmth. Laboratory studies show an alkaline phosphatase level of 380 U/L, erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 18 mm/hr, and normal leukocyte count. Plain radiographs demonstrate a poorly defined metaphyseal lesion of the distal femur with mixed areas of sclerosis and lucency, cortical destruction, periosteal elevation, and radiating mineralized spicules extending into the surrounding soft tissue. Which of the following is the most appropriate next clinical intervention?

A. Begin empiric intravenous vancomycin and ceftriaxone
B. Perform an immediate unplanned excisional biopsy in the primary care setting
C. Immobilize the extremity and repeat radiographs in 6 weeks
D. Refer promptly to an orthopedic oncologist for local staging imaging followed by a carefully planned biopsy
E. Administer an intra-articular glucocorticoid injection for symptom relief

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: The adolescent's progressive pain, firm swelling, metaphyseal location, mixed destructive and sclerotic appearance, periosteal elevation, and radiating mineralized spicules are characteristic of osteosarcoma. The appropriate intervention is prompt referral to an orthopedic oncologist. Local staging with MRI should be completed before a carefully planned core needle or incisional biopsy. Biopsy placement is critical because the tract must be removed during definitive resection; an improperly placed biopsy can contaminate uninvolved compartments and compromise limb-sparing surgery. Chest imaging is also required because the lungs are the most common site of metastasis. Treatment generally consists of multiagent chemotherapy and wide surgical resection after histologic confirmation and staging. A is incorrect because the absence of fever, leukocytosis, and inflammatory findings makes bacterial infection less likely, and empiric antibiotics could delay definitive evaluation. B is incorrect because an unplanned excisional biopsy may contaminate surrounding tissue and interfere with definitive resection. C is incorrect because aggressive radiographic features require urgent evaluation rather than observation. E is incorrect because a glucocorticoid injection would not treat the underlying process and could delay diagnosis or complicate subsequent management.

Question 162  [ID: 779f15343d00423489b397d986056e29]
A 52-year-old woman presents with 8 weeks of progressive difficulty climbing stairs, rising from a chair, and reaching overhead to wash her hair. She denies numbness, joint pain, recent strenuous exercise, or new medications. Examination shows symmetric 3/5 strength with shoulder abduction and hip flexion, 5/5 strength distally, intact sensation, and normal deep tendon reflexes. No skin eruption is present. Laboratory studies reveal a creatine kinase level of 4,800 U/L and elevated aldolase and aminotransferase levels. Which additional question should be prioritized to identify a potentially serious associated manifestation of her condition?

A. Do your eyelids droop more toward the end of the day?
B. Have you experienced numbness that began in your feet?
C. Do you cough or choke when swallowing food or liquids?
D. Do you develop severe calf pain after walking a predictable distance?
E. Have you had brief attacks of severe pain and redness in a single joint?

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The presentation is consistent with polymyositis, an inflammatory myopathy characterized by subacute, symmetric proximal weakness, markedly elevated creatine kinase and aldolase levels, preserved sensation, and generally preserved reflexes. Oropharyngeal involvement can cause dysphagia, coughing or choking with meals, aspiration, and aspiration pneumonia. Asking specifically about swallowing difficulty is therefore an important component of the history. A is more characteristic of myasthenia gravis, which commonly causes fatigable ocular and bulbar weakness and typically does not markedly elevate creatine kinase. B suggests a length-dependent peripheral neuropathy, which would usually produce distal sensory abnormalities rather than isolated symmetric proximal weakness. Question 163  [ID: 78df48d0c0f24f9ebc6c8b9ea4274f34]
A 17-year-old boy presents with 5 months of progressively worsening focal pain along the proximal right tibia. The pain is most severe at night and improves markedly within 30 minutes of taking ibuprofen. He denies trauma, fever, weight loss, or fatigue. Examination reveals localized tenderness without erythema, warmth, swelling, or limitation of knee motion. A complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are normal. Plain radiographs demonstrate focal cortical thickening and surrounding sclerosis with a subtle 6-mm central lucency. Which of the following diagnostic studies would best characterize the abnormality and confirm the suspected diagnosis?

A. Thin-section computed tomography of the affected area
B. Magnetic resonance imaging with intravenous gadolinium
C. Technetium-99m radionuclide scan
D. Ultrasonography with color Doppler
E. Repeat plain radiographs in 6 months

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Thin-section computed tomography is the best study for confirming an osteoid osteoma because it clearly demonstrates the small radiolucent nidus, which may contain central mineralization, as well as the surrounding reactive cortical sclerosis. The characteristic clinical pattern is focal nocturnal pain that is rapidly relieved by an NSAID due to high prostaglandin production within the nidus. MRI may show extensive surrounding marrow and soft-tissue edema that can obscure the nidus and lead to an incorrect interpretation. A technetium-99m scan is highly sensitive and can localize increased activity, sometimes producing a double-density sign, but it is less anatomically specific than CT. Ultrasonography is not reliable for evaluating a small intracortical abnormality. Delaying evaluation with repeat radiographs is inappropriate because the current clinical and radiographic findings warrant definitive characterization.

Question 164  [ID: 7a2a37d0e1414f07a8eeca614cfd95fd]
A 16-year-old competitive gymnast presents with 6 weeks of focal low back pain that worsens during back walkovers and other maneuvers requiring repetitive extension. She denies fever, nocturnal pain, lower-extremity weakness, paresthesias, and bowel or bladder dysfunction. Examination shows focal tenderness over the lower lumbar region and pain reproduced by single-leg hyperextension. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, reflexes, and straight-leg raise testing are normal. Complete blood count and inflammatory markers are within reference ranges. Lumbar imaging demonstrates a small cortical defect through the L5 pars interarticularis without vertebral displacement. In addition to short-term symptom management, which of the following counseling recommendations is most appropriate to promote healing and reduce the risk of recurrence or progression?

A. Continue full athletic participation as tolerated because the imaging finding is benign and self-limited
B. Avoid all physical activity and remain on strict bed rest until follow-up imaging shows complete bony union
C. Temporarily discontinue pain-provoking extension activities, begin supervised core stabilization and flexibility exercises, and return gradually when pain-free
D. Use a lumbar extension program daily to strengthen the posterior spinal elements before returning to competition
E. Pursue immediate surgical fixation to prevent future vertebral displacement

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The imaging and activity-related extension pain are consistent with spondylolysis, a stress injury of the pars interarticularis commonly seen in adolescent athletes participating in sports involving repetitive lumbar extension and rotation. Initial management is conservative. Patient education should emphasize temporary cessation or modification of pain-provoking activities, particularly repetitive hyperextension, followed by supervised rehabilitation focusing on neutral-spine core stabilization, hamstring and hip flexibility, and correction of sport-specific biomechanics. A gradual return to activity is appropriate once the patient is pain-free with daily activities and sport-specific movements and has recovered strength and range of motion. A is incorrect because continued participation through pain can delay healing and increase the risk of progression to vertebral slippage. B is incorrect because prolonged bed rest causes deconditioning and is unnecessary; relative rest and guided rehabilitation are preferred. D is incorrect because repeated extension exercises reproduce the mechanism of injury and may worsen symptoms. E is incorrect because surgery is generally reserved for persistent symptoms despite an adequate course of conservative treatment or for selected cases with progression, instability, or neurologic compromise.

Question 165  [ID: 7af1c3db35de4d74a686ec294639a4cd]
A 72-year-old man presents with 8 months of aching in the lower back, buttocks, and both calves that begins after walking approximately 2 blocks. The discomfort improves within several minutes when he sits or leans forward over a shopping cart. He denies pain at rest, recent trauma, fever, weight loss, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and saddle numbness. His medical history includes obesity, hypertension, and osteoarthritis. Examination shows normal lower-extremity strength, symmetric reflexes, intact sensation, and palpable pedal pulses. Straight-leg raising does not reproduce his symptoms. MRI demonstrates an anteroposterior canal diameter of 8 mm at L3-L4 and L4-L5 with facet joint hypertrophy and thickening of adjacent supporting ligaments. No acute compression or destructive lesion is present. Which of the following is the most appropriate patient education recommendation to help preserve mobility and reduce symptom burden?

A. Avoid walking and other weight-bearing activity until symptoms completely resolve
B. Begin a supervised flexion-based conditioning program with core strengthening and low-impact aerobic exercise such as stationary cycling
C. Perform repeated lumbar extension exercises to increase tolerance for upright posture
D. Use a rigid brace continuously during waking hours to prevent further structural narrowing
E. Restrict caloric and protein intake to accelerate weight loss and reduce axial loading

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient's exertional bilateral buttock and calf discomfort, relief with sitting or forward flexion, preserved peripheral pulses, and imaging findings are characteristic of lumbar spinal stenosis causing neurogenic claudication. In the absence of progressive neurologic deficits or bowel or bladder dysfunction, initial management emphasizes education and conservative measures that maintain function. A supervised flexion-based program, core strengthening, and low-impact aerobic exercise such as stationary cycling can improve conditioning and walking tolerance while avoiding positions that exacerbate symptoms. Weight management should also be encouraged when appropriate.

A is incorrect because prolonged activity avoidance causes deconditioning, weakness, and further loss of mobility. Activity should be modified rather than eliminated.

C is incorrect because lumbar extension generally decreases the available canal space and can worsen neurogenic claudication; flexion commonly provides relief.

D is incorrect because continuous rigid bracing has not been shown to prevent progression and may promote core muscle weakness and dependence.

E is incorrect because gradual weight reduction may be beneficial in an obese patient, but excessive caloric and protein restriction risks malnutrition and loss of muscle mass. Sustainable dietary modification with adequate protein is preferred.

Question 166  [ID: 7b519704947b40e1aed79f0504baf2b1]
A 24-year-old man presents to the emergency department after falling while playing volleyball. He landed with his right arm abducted and externally rotated. He reports immediate severe pain and an inability to move the affected arm. On examination, he supports the arm in slight abduction and external rotation. The normal rounded contour below the acromion is absent, and there is a prominent fullness anteriorly. The radial pulse is 2+, and capillary refill is normal. Before and after definitive management, which of the following focused examination findings is most important to assess and document?

A. Sensation over the lateral shoulder and strength of shoulder abduction
B. Sensation of the little finger and strength of finger abduction
C. Sensation of the palmar index finger and strength of thumb opposition
D. Sensation of the dorsal first web space and strength of wrist extension
E. Sensation of the medial forearm and strength of elbow flexion

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The mechanism, arm position, loss of the normal rounded shoulder contour, and anterior fullness are characteristic of an anterior glenohumeral dislocation. The axillary nerve is the peripheral nerve most commonly injured because it courses near the inferior aspect of the joint and around the surgical neck of the humerus. Its function should be assessed before and after reduction by testing sensation over the lateral shoulder, often called the regimental badge area, and deltoid-mediated shoulder abduction. Option B evaluates the ulnar nerve, which supplies sensation to the little finger and motor function to the interossei. Option C evaluates the median nerve, including sensation over the palmar index finger and thenar motor function. Option D evaluates the radial nerve, including sensation in the dorsal first web space and wrist extension. Option E primarily assesses the musculocutaneous nerve, which supplies sensation to the lateral rather than medial forearm and contributes to elbow flexion; therefore, the pairing is anatomically inconsistent and is not the priority in this presentation.

Question 167  [ID: 7b8041ebc17246ccbc4b143d3b2d6507]
A 34-year-old man presents with 2 days of localized lower back pain that began immediately after lifting a heavy box while moving. The pain worsens with bending and twisting and improves when he lies still. He denies pain radiating into the legs, numbness, weakness, saddle anesthesia, urinary retention, fever, weight loss, or recent trauma. His temperature is 36.8°C (98.2°F), blood pressure is 124/78 mm Hg, and pulse is 76/min. Examination reveals tenderness and increased tone adjacent to the lower lumbar vertebrae. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes are normal, and the straight-leg raise test is negative bilaterally. Urinalysis is normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. Naproxen taken orally for a short course as needed with food
B. Gabapentin with gradual dose titration
C. Oral prednisone administered as a taper
D. Oxycodone-acetaminophen administered on a scheduled basis
E. Methocarbamol combined with diazepam

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has an acute spinal sprain or strain characterized by pain after lifting, localized paraspinal tenderness and spasm, pain with movement, and the absence of neurologic deficits or red-flag findings. A short course of an oral nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as naproxen is appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy, provided there are no contraindications such as active peptic ulcer disease, significant kidney disease, anticoagulant use, or NSAID hypersensitivity. Continued activity as tolerated and avoidance of prolonged bed rest should also be recommended. Gabapentin is primarily used for neuropathic pain and is not indicated because the patient has no radicular symptoms. Systemic corticosteroids do not provide meaningful benefit for uncomplicated, nonradicular acute lower back pain and carry avoidable adverse effects. Scheduled opioid therapy is inappropriate because expected benefits are limited and risks include sedation, constipation, dependence, and overdose. Skeletal muscle relaxants may occasionally be considered for short-term use when first-line therapy is inadequate, but combining methocarbamol with diazepam causes additive central nervous system depression and is not an appropriate initial regimen.

Question 168  [ID: 7cf5f45566b94d5e89fdfeb17087c5ee]
A 62-year-old man with poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus and peripheral neuropathy presents with worsening drainage from an ulcer beneath his right first metatarsal head. The ulcer has been present for 6 weeks despite local wound care. He reports increasing swelling but denies significant pain. His temperature is 38.1°C (100.6°F), blood pressure is 138/82 mm Hg, and pulse is 96/min. Examination reveals a 2.5-cm plantar ulcer with surrounding erythema and purulent drainage. A sterile metal probe passed through the ulcer contacts a firm underlying structure. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 13,200/mm3, erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 92 mm/hr, and C-reactive protein level of 11 mg/dL. Plain radiographs demonstrate focal cortical erosion and periosteal reaction of the first metatarsal. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Charcot neuroarthropathy
B. Osteomyelitis
C. Cellulitis
D. Septic arthritis
E. Gout

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. Osteomyelitis is strongly suggested by a chronic diabetic foot ulcer that probes to bone, systemic and local inflammatory findings, markedly elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein, and radiographic evidence of cortical erosion with periosteal reaction. Contiguous spread from a chronic diabetic foot ulcer is a common mechanism, and pain may be minimal because of peripheral neuropathy. Charcot neuroarthropathy can cause swelling, warmth, deformity, and bony fragmentation in patients with diabetic neuropathy, but purulent ulcer drainage, fever, markedly elevated inflammatory markers, a positive probe-to-bone finding, and focal cortical erosion favor infection. Cellulitis is limited primarily to skin and subcutaneous tissue and does not produce cortical erosion or periosteal reaction. Septic arthritis typically causes severe pain with joint movement, restricted range of motion, and an effusion centered on a joint; the findings here localize to the first metatarsal beneath a chronic ulcer. Gout usually presents as an acutely painful, erythematous monoarthritis, often involving the first metatarsophalangeal joint, but it does not explain a chronic purulent ulcer, positive probe-to-bone test, fever, and periosteal reaction.

Question 169  [ID: 7d0ec688efae48998f7691eb6151127d]
An 8-year-old boy is brought to the emergency department for 3 days of fever and progressively worsening pain near his right knee. He has no history of trauma and now refuses to bear weight. His temperature is 39.1°C (102.4°F), pulse rate is 118/min, and blood pressure is 104/68 mm Hg. Examination shows warmth, swelling, and marked tenderness over the proximal tibia without crepitus or an open wound. Laboratory studies reveal a leukocyte count of 15,800/mm3, an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 72 mm/hr, and an elevated C-reactive protein concentration. Plain radiographs show mild soft-tissue swelling without fracture. MRI demonstrates localized marrow edema in the proximal tibial metaphysis with cortical disruption and a small subperiosteal fluid collection. Blood cultures grow methicillin-sensitive Staphylococcus aureus. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms best explains the typical localization of this condition in this patient?

A. Hematogenous seeding of metaphyseal capillary loops characterized by sluggish blood flow and limited phagocytic activity
B. Deposition of circulating immune complexes within the epiphyseal articular surface
C. Autoantibody-mediated destruction of synovial lining cells following streptococcal infection
D. Ischemic necrosis caused by interruption of the epiphyseal blood supply after repetitive microtrauma
E. Calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition within periarticular cartilage

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is consistent with acute hematogenous osteomyelitis. In children, organisms circulating in the bloodstream commonly localize in the metaphysis of long bones. Metaphyseal vessels form tortuous capillary loops with sluggish flow, facilitating bacterial deposition. Relative paucity of effective phagocytic activity in this region further permits bacterial proliferation. The resulting inflammatory response increases intramedullary pressure, compromises local perfusion, disrupts the cortex, and may elevate the periosteum to form a subperiosteal abscess. Staphylococcus aureus is the most common causative organism. Option B is incorrect because immune-complex deposition does not explain a focal metaphyseal infection with positive blood cultures and a subperiosteal collection. Option C is incorrect because autoantibody-mediated synovial injury would primarily produce inflammatory joint findings rather than focal metaphyseal marrow and cortical abnormalities. Option D describes an ischemic mechanism such as avascular necrosis; this typically affects the epiphysis and does not account for fever, inflammatory marker elevation, or bacteremia. Option E describes a crystal arthropathy, which usually affects older adults and causes acute synovitis rather than focal metaphyseal infection.

Question 170  [ID: 7eeda43550d24860a7a8cfb5fbdd3b5c]
A 13-year-old girl is brought to the clinic after her parent noticed that one shoulder appears higher than the other. She has no pain, weakness, sensory changes, bowel or bladder dysfunction, or history of trauma. Menarche occurred 6 months ago. Physical examination shows elevation of the right shoulder, prominence of the right scapula, and asymmetric waist contours. During the forward-bend test, a right thoracic prominence is observed. Scoliometer measurement over the area of greatest prominence is 9 degrees. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes are normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic study to confirm and quantify this patient's deformity?

A. Standing posteroanterior and lateral full-length radiographs with measurement of the Cobb angle
B. Supine noncontrast MRI to measure the angle of trunk rotation
C. CT scan with three-dimensional reconstruction as the initial confirmatory study
D. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry to determine vertebral bone mineral density
E. Technetium-99m bone scintigraphy to evaluate vertebral metabolic activity

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The examination findings and scoliometer measurement suggest a structural lateral curvature with vertebral rotation. A scoliometer angle of trunk rotation of approximately 5 to 7 degrees or greater generally warrants radiographic evaluation. Standing posteroanterior and lateral full-length radiographs are the preferred confirmatory studies because weight-bearing images demonstrate the magnitude and pattern of the curvature. The Cobb angle is measured between the superior end plate of the upper end vertebra and the inferior end plate of the lower end vertebra; an angle of at least 10 degrees establishes the radiographic diagnosis. The posteroanterior projection also reduces radiation exposure to radiosensitive anterior tissues compared with an anteroposterior projection. B is incorrect because MRI is not routinely required for an otherwise typical presentation with a normal neurologic examination; it is reserved for atypical curvature patterns, rapid progression, significant pain, neurologic abnormalities, or concern for an underlying neural-axis lesion. MRI also does not use the angle of trunk rotation as the standard measurement of curve magnitude. C is incorrect because CT exposes the patient to substantially more ionizing radiation and is not the initial study for routine evaluation. D is incorrect because bone-density testing assesses osteopenia or osteoporosis and does not quantify this deformity. E is incorrect because bone scintigraphy may evaluate occult fracture, infection, or a metabolically active lesion but is not used to confirm or measure the curvature.

Question 171  [ID: 805cff074ef94d2ca29d3f6848fb9786]
A 6-week-old firstborn girl is brought for a routine well-child visit. She was delivered by cesarean section after remaining in frank breech presentation during the final trimester. Her mother has noticed that the infant's thigh creases appear uneven. Examination shows that the left knee rests lower than the right when the knees are flexed and the feet are placed flat on the examination table. Passive abduction of the left proximal lower extremity is decreased compared with the right. Which additional physical examination maneuver and finding would most strongly support the suspected diagnosis?

A. Adduction of the flexed thigh with posterior pressure produces a palpable clunk
B. Abduction of the flexed thigh with gentle anterior lifting produces a palpable clunk
C. Extension of the knee while the ankle is dorsiflexed reproduces posterior leg pain
D. Medial displacement of the patella produces apprehension
E. Compression of the forefoot produces a palpable click between the metatarsal heads

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The infant has several major risk factors and examination findings associated with developmental dysplasia of the hip, including female sex, firstborn status, breech presentation, asymmetric thigh folds, apparent limb shortening, and restricted abduction. The maneuver described in choice B is the Ortolani maneuver. With the infant supine and the thighs and knees flexed, the examiner abducts the thigh while applying gentle anterior pressure over the greater trochanter. A palpable clunk indicates reduction of a posteriorly displaced femoral head into the acetabulum and strongly supports the diagnosis. Choice A describes the Barlow maneuver, which attempts to dislocate an unstable but currently reduced femoral head by adducting the thigh and applying posterior pressure; it assesses instability rather than reduction of an already displaced femoral head. Choice C describes a straight-leg-raise-type maneuver used to evaluate lumbosacral nerve root irritation and is not appropriate in this infant. Choice D evaluates patellar instability. Choice E describes a maneuver used to assess interdigital nerve irritation, such as a Morton neuroma, and is unrelated to the infant's findings.

Question 172  [ID: 81104295c4544c0b91440c6d4b5a926c]
A 67-year-old man presents after falling against the edge of a bathtub 6 hours ago. He reports severe, focal pain along his left lateral torso that worsens with inspiration, coughing, and movement. He denies loss of consciousness, abdominal pain, or hemoptysis. His temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), blood pressure is 138/82 mm Hg, pulse is 88/min, respirations are 22/min, and oxygen saturation is 95% on room air. Examination reveals localized tenderness and ecchymosis over the left lateral thoracic wall without paradoxical motion or crepitus. Breath sounds are equal bilaterally, but inspiration is shallow because of pain. Imaging demonstrates minimally displaced cortical discontinuities involving the left fifth through seventh ribs without pleural air, fluid accumulation, or pulmonary opacity. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial management?

A. Apply circumferential compression wrapping and prescribe bed rest
B. Provide multimodal analgesia, encourage incentive spirometry, and promote early mobilization
C. Perform immediate tube thoracostomy
D. Proceed with operative fixation of the involved structures
E. Administer prophylactic broad-spectrum antibiotics and systemic glucocorticoids

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has uncomplicated, minimally displaced rib fractures without pneumothorax, hemothorax, pulmonary contusion, respiratory failure, or flail chest. Initial management is conservative and centers on adequate multimodal analgesia, such as acetaminophen and an NSAID when not contraindicated, with cautious use of opioids or regional anesthesia for persistent severe pain. Incentive spirometry, coughing, deep-breathing exercises, and early mobilization reduce splinting and help prevent atelectasis and pneumonia, which are particularly important complications in older adults.

A is incorrect because circumferential wrapping restricts ventilation and increases the risk of atelectasis and pneumonia; prolonged bed rest is also harmful. C is incorrect because tube thoracostomy is indicated for clinically significant pneumothorax or hemothorax, neither of which is present. D is incorrect because operative fixation is generally reserved for selected patients with severe displacement, flail segments, respiratory compromise, refractory pain, or failure of conservative management. E is incorrect because closed, uncomplicated injuries do not require prophylactic antibiotics, and systemic glucocorticoids do not improve healing or prevent pulmonary complications.

Question 173  [ID: 81577af0975a4ea485d01a5f2c502080]
A 24-year-old woman presents with 3 months of fatigue, intermittent low-grade fever, and pain and morning stiffness involving both hands and wrists. She reports that the symptoms worsen after sun exposure and that she has recently developed painless sores in her mouth. Physical examination reveals an erythematous rash over the cheeks that spares the nasolabial folds, two shallow oral ulcers, and tenderness and swelling of several metacarpophalangeal joints. Laboratory studies show hemoglobin of 10.4 g/dL, leukocyte count of 3,200/mm3, platelet count of 118,000/mm3, serum creatinine of 1.3 mg/dL, and urinalysis with 2+ protein and red blood cell casts. Antinuclear antibody testing is positive at a titer of 1:640. Radiographs of the hands show no erosions. Which of the following additional serologic findings would be most specific for the underlying condition?

A. Anti-Smith antibodies
B. Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies
C. Anticentromere antibodies
D. Anti-Jo-1 antibodies
E. Anti-topoisomerase I antibodies

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Anti-Smith antibodies are highly specific for systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), although their sensitivity is relatively low. This patient has multiple characteristic findings, including photosensitivity, a malar-pattern rash that spares the nasolabial folds, painless oral ulcers, nonerosive inflammatory polyarthritis, pancytopenia, proteinuria, and red blood cell casts. A positive antinuclear antibody test is highly sensitive but not specific; anti-Smith antibodies provide strong diagnostic specificity. Anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies would also be highly specific and would correlate more closely with renal disease activity, but they are not listed. Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies are strongly associated with rheumatoid arthritis, which typically causes erosive symmetric polyarthritis and does not explain the photosensitive rash, oral ulcers, cytopenias, and nephritic urinary sediment. Anticentromere antibodies are associated with limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis. Anti-Jo-1 antibodies are associated with antisynthetase syndrome, which commonly presents with inflammatory myopathy, interstitial lung disease, mechanic's hands, and arthritis. Anti-topoisomerase I antibodies, also called anti-Scl-70 antibodies, are associated with diffuse cutaneous systemic sclerosis and an increased risk of interstitial lung disease.

Question 174  [ID: 8244eae9cc8c48fc9d807d7d14b947e8]
A 46-year-old woman presents with an 18-month history of episodic finger color changes triggered by cold exposure. Her fingers initially become white, then blue, and finally red as they warm. She also reports progressive tightening of the skin over her fingers, frequent heartburn, and difficulty swallowing solid foods. Physical examination shows smooth, thickened skin extending from the fingertips to the elbows, reduced oral aperture, scattered dilated superficial vessels on the face and hands, and small depressed scars at several fingertips. There is no proximal muscle weakness. Laboratory studies show an antinuclear antibody titer of 1:640 with a centromere staining pattern and a normal creatine kinase level. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Dermatomyositis
B. Limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis
C. Mixed connective tissue disease
D. Systemic lupus erythematosus
E. Eosinophilic fasciitis

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. Limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis is characterized by episodic triphasic digital color changes with cold exposure, progressive skin thickening limited primarily to the distal extremities and face, fingertip pitting scars, telangiectasias, reduced oral aperture, and esophageal dysmotility. A centromere-pattern antinuclear antibody strongly supports this diagnosis. Patients should also be monitored for pulmonary arterial hypertension.

A is incorrect because dermatomyositis typically causes symmetric proximal muscle weakness, elevated muscle enzymes, and characteristic cutaneous findings such as heliotrope discoloration, Gottron papules, and a shawl or V-sign eruption. This patient has normal muscle strength and creatine kinase.

C is incorrect because mixed connective tissue disease commonly produces overlapping features accompanied by high-titer anti-U1 ribonucleoprotein antibodies. Although episodic digital color changes and swollen hands may occur, the distal skin thickening, fingertip scars, telangiectasias, esophageal symptoms, and centromere antibody pattern are more specific for limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis.

D is incorrect because systemic lupus erythematosus more often presents with photosensitive eruptions, oral ulcers, inflammatory polyarthritis, serositis, cytopenias, or renal involvement. Progressive distal skin tightening and a centromere antibody pattern are not typical.

E is incorrect because eosinophilic fasciitis causes painful, symmetric swelling and induration of the extremities, often after strenuous exercise. The fingers and face are usually spared, a groove sign may be present, and peripheral eosinophilia is common. Episodic digital color changes, fingertip scars, telangiectasias, and esophageal dysmotility favor limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis.

Question 175  [ID: 82d208a39be54beeae28afd4c0df5d95]
A 27-year-old man presents with 5 days of pain and swelling in his right knee and left ankle. Three weeks ago, he had a self-limited episode of fever and bloody diarrhea after eating undercooked poultry. He also reports redness of both eyes and pain at the insertion of the right Achilles tendon. He denies current gastrointestinal symptoms, urethral discharge, or recent sexual activity. Examination shows bilateral conjunctival injection without visual impairment, a warm right knee with a moderate effusion, swelling of the left ankle, and tenderness over the right Achilles tendon insertion. Arthrocentesis of the knee yields cloudy fluid with 18,000 leukocytes/mm3, predominantly neutrophils. Gram stain and culture are negative, and no crystals are identified. Radiographs show soft-tissue swelling without erosions. Which of the following is the best initial pharmacologic intervention for this patient's current symptoms?

A. Naproxen
B. Methotrexate
C. Ceftriaxone
D. Allopurinol
E. Infliximab

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has reactive arthritis following a gastrointestinal infection, supported by the acute asymmetric lower-extremity oligoarthritis, enthesitis, conjunctivitis, and sterile inflammatory synovial fluid. A nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as naproxen is the preferred initial pharmacologic therapy for symptomatic control. Methotrexate is reserved for persistent or chronic disease that does not respond adequately to NSAIDs or local glucocorticoid therapy. Ceftriaxone is used for gonococcal infection but is not indicated when synovial cultures are negative and the presentation follows a resolved enteric infection. Antibiotic treatment of a resolved gastrointestinal infection does not generally shorten the subsequent arthritis. Allopurinol is used for long-term urate-lowering therapy in gout, but no crystals were present. Infliximab may be considered for severe, refractory chronic disease but is inappropriate as initial therapy.

Question 176  [ID: 830ab0d8bd9e4624aa0efcd1a4391de5]
A 48-year-old man presents with 2 months of progressive fatigue, fever, unintentional weight loss, diffuse muscle pain, and aching in both knees and ankles. He also reports episodic postprandial abdominal pain and new burning pain with weakness of the right foot. His blood pressure is 168/96 mm Hg. Examination shows retiform, violaceous discoloration of the lower legs, decreased sensation over the lateral right calf, and weakness of right foot dorsiflexion. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 92 mm/hr, elevated C-reactive protein, serum creatinine of 1.8 mg/dL, and negative antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies. Urinalysis shows trace protein without hematuria or red blood cell casts. Testing for hepatitis B and C is negative. Abdominal angiography demonstrates multiple small aneurysms and irregular narrowing of the renal and mesenteric arteries. Which of the following is the most appropriate induction pharmacotherapy for this patient?

A. High-dose systemic glucocorticoids plus cyclophosphamide
B. Colchicine monotherapy
C. Hydroxychloroquine plus a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug
D. Methotrexate plus folic acid
E. Tenofovir plus prolonged high-dose systemic glucocorticoids

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has a severe, systemic medium-vessel inflammatory disorder characterized by constitutional symptoms, myalgias, arthralgias, livedoid skin changes, hypertension, mesenteric ischemic symptoms, mononeuritis multiplex, renal arterial involvement without glomerulonephritis, and angiographic microaneurysms. Severe disease with neurologic, renal, or gastrointestinal involvement requires remission-induction therapy with high-dose systemic glucocorticoids plus cyclophosphamide. Once remission is achieved, cyclophosphamide is generally replaced with a less toxic maintenance agent, such as azathioprine or methotrexate. Colchicine is used primarily for gout and selected autoinflammatory conditions and is inadequate for this life-threatening presentation. Hydroxychloroquine and nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs may treat symptoms in milder inflammatory arthropathies but do not control necrotizing arterial inflammation. Methotrexate may be considered for maintenance or selected nonsevere cases but is insufficient as induction therapy for major neurologic, renal arterial, and gastrointestinal involvement. Tenofovir would be appropriate only if active hepatitis B infection were present; hepatitis B-associated cases are generally treated with antiviral therapy, a short glucocorticoid course, and often plasma exchange rather than prolonged immunosuppression. This patient's viral testing is negative.

Question 177  [ID: 87084593fe844df4acfbcb06bec37914]
A 38-year-old man presents with acute right anterior knee pain after forcefully jumping to block a shot during a basketball game. He felt a sudden pop as he pushed off and immediately fell, after which he was unable to bear weight or straighten the affected leg. Examination shows swelling, tenderness, and a palpable defect inferior to the patella. He cannot perform a straight-leg raise. Passive range of motion is preserved. Lateral radiography demonstrates a high-riding patella without fracture. Disruption of which anatomic structure best explains these findings?

A. Distal quadriceps tendon
B. Medial patellofemoral ligament
C. Patellar tendon
D. Anterior cruciate ligament
E. Lateral meniscus

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The patellar tendon connects the inferior pole of the patella to the tibial tuberosity and transmits force generated by the quadriceps to permit active knee extension. Its disruption produces a palpable infrapatellar defect, inability to perform a straight-leg raise, and patella alta because the intact quadriceps pulls the patella proximally. A distal quadriceps tendon disruption also impairs active extension but typically causes a defect superior to the patella and patella baja. Medial patellofemoral ligament injury is associated with lateral patellar instability or dislocation and does not usually abolish active extension. An anterior cruciate ligament injury causes instability, hemarthrosis, and positive Lachman testing but does not produce a high-riding patella or loss of the straight-leg raise. A lateral meniscal injury may cause joint-line pain, locking, or catching but does not disrupt force transmission required for active extension.

Question 178  [ID: 875b073c6e5144db9f800e6e021a8a51]
A 16-year-old boy presents with progressive exertional dyspnea, reduced exercise tolerance, and intermittent palpitations. His parents report that an inward depression of his lower anterior chest has become more prominent during his recent growth spurt. He has no history of trauma or chronic pulmonary disease. Temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), pulse is 82/min, respirations are 16/min, and oxygen saturation is 99% on room air. Examination demonstrates a symmetric concavity involving the lower sternum and adjacent costal margins. Cardiac auscultation reveals a soft systolic ejection murmur at the left sternal border. Complete blood count and comprehensive metabolic panel are normal. Spirometry shows a mild restrictive pattern, and plain radiographs demonstrate posterior displacement of the sternum with leftward displacement of the cardiac silhouette. Which of the following diagnostic studies and measurements would best objectively quantify the anatomic severity of this patient's condition and help determine candidacy for corrective intervention?

A. Standing spinal radiography with calculation of the Cobb angle
B. Computed tomography with calculation of the ratio of the transverse internal diameter to the shortest anteroposterior diameter
C. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry with calculation of a lumbar spine Z-score
D. Radionuclide bone scanning to assess focal osteoblastic activity
E. Ultrasonography with measurement of diaphragmatic excursion during inspiration

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Computed tomography can define the degree of posterior sternal displacement and associated compression or displacement of intrathoracic structures. The ratio of the widest transverse internal diameter to the shortest distance between the sternum and vertebral body is the Haller index. A normal value is approximately 2.5, whereas a value greater than approximately 3.25 generally indicates a severe deformity and may support consideration of operative correction when accompanied by symptoms or cardiopulmonary impairment. A is incorrect because the Cobb angle quantifies spinal curvature and does not measure the severity of the anterior chest depression. C is incorrect because dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry evaluates bone mineral density and is not used to characterize this structural abnormality. D is incorrect because radionuclide bone scanning detects areas of altered bone turnover, such as occult fracture, infection, or malignancy, but does not provide the required anatomic measurement. E is incorrect because diaphragmatic ultrasonography may evaluate diaphragmatic motion but cannot quantify the relationship between the transverse and anteroposterior dimensions needed to grade this deformity.

Question 179  [ID: 89a30225b5674566965ff31d3313da4d]
A 27-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a high-speed motorcycle collision. He reports severe posterior neck pain and inability to move his legs. Examination shows a blood pressure of 76/42 mm Hg, pulse of 48/min, respirations of 20/min, and oxygen saturation of 97% on supplemental oxygen. His skin is warm and dry. He has 2/5 strength in both upper extremities, 0/5 strength in both lower extremities, and diminished sensation below the nipple line. FAST examination is negative, and there is no external hemorrhage. Hemoglobin is 14.2 g/dL. CT demonstrates comminution of the C5 vertebral body with retropulsion into the canal. His blood pressure remains low after cautious administration of isotonic crystalloid. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacologic therapy for this patient's persistent hemodynamic instability?

A. High-dose intravenous methylprednisolone
B. Intravenous norepinephrine infusion
C. Intravenous phenylephrine infusion
D. Intravenous furosemide
E. Subcutaneous enoxaparin

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has hypotension, marked bradycardia, warm skin, and major neurologic deficits after a high cervical injury, with no evidence of hemorrhage. These findings indicate loss of sympathetic vascular tone with unopposed vagal activity. After limited crystalloid resuscitation, norepinephrine is the preferred vasopressor because its alpha-1 activity increases systemic vascular resistance and its beta-1 activity supports heart rate and cardiac output. It is commonly used to maintain adequate mean arterial pressure and preserve perfusion in this setting. A is incorrect because routine high-dose methylprednisolone is not recommended; evidence of meaningful neurologic benefit is limited, while risks include infection, gastrointestinal bleeding, hyperglycemia, and impaired wound healing. C is less appropriate because phenylephrine is a pure alpha-1 agonist that may cause reflex bradycardia and reduce cardiac output in a patient who is already bradycardic. D is incorrect because furosemide would reduce intravascular volume and worsen hypotension. E is important for venous thromboembolism prophylaxis once bleeding risk and procedural plans have been assessed, but it does not treat the patient's immediate hemodynamic instability.

Question 180  [ID: 89bc8c9815d64c6bb9f2fbef23fc4eb2]
A 68-year-old woman presents with acute left wrist pain and swelling after falling from standing height onto her outstretched hand. She has no history of cancer or chronic glucocorticoid use and has never undergone bone-density screening. Examination shows swelling, tenderness, and deformity near the wrist; sensation, capillary refill, and finger movement are intact. Radiographs demonstrate loss of cortical continuity through the distal radial metaphysis with dorsal angulation of the distal segment. After appropriate realignment and immobilization, which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation to reduce this patient's risk of future injury?

A. Obtain dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry, assess for secondary contributors to reduced bone density, and provide counseling on fall prevention and adequate calcium and vitamin D intake
B. Repeat wrist radiographs annually to identify recurrent structural injury before symptoms develop
C. Recommend prolonged activity restriction and avoidance of weight-bearing exercise
D. Begin intranasal calcitonin indefinitely without additional risk assessment
E. Defer bone-density evaluation unless she experiences a second low-energy injury

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient sustained a distal radius fracture after a fall from standing height, which is a sentinel fragility injury and should prompt evaluation for osteoporosis and modifiable fall risks. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry is appropriate because all women age 65 years and older should undergo osteoporosis screening, and her low-energy injury further increases concern. Evaluation should also address potential secondary contributors, such as vitamin D deficiency, hyperparathyroidism, thyroid disease, renal dysfunction, medication effects, and inadequate calcium intake. Counseling should include adequate calcium and vitamin D intake, weight-bearing and resistance exercise when medically appropriate, smoking cessation, moderation of alcohol use, vision assessment, home-safety modification, and review of medications that increase fall risk. B is incorrect because routine annual wrist imaging does not assess systemic skeletal fragility or prevent future injuries. C is incorrect because prolonged inactivity can worsen bone loss, muscle weakness, and fall risk; progressive weight-bearing and resistance exercise should be encouraged after healing. D is incorrect because calcitonin is not preferred long-term therapy for osteoporosis and should not be initiated without appropriate evaluation. E is incorrect because a low-energy distal radius fracture in an older adult warrants immediate bone-health and fall-risk assessment rather than waiting for another injury.

Question 181  [ID: 89c3f1932b7c4f3984cbd961ed010266]
A 29-year-old man presents with 10 months of progressive low back and buttock pain. His symptoms are worse in the early morning, improve with activity, and are associated with approximately 1 hour of morning stiffness. He reports intermittent pain that alternates between the right and left buttocks. Examination shows reduced lumbar flexion, limited chest expansion, and tenderness over both sacroiliac regions. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein are elevated. Plain radiographs demonstrate bilateral sacroiliac erosions and subchondral sclerosis. In addition to appropriate pharmacologic treatment, which of the following counseling recommendations is most appropriate to help preserve long-term function and reduce complications?

A. Avoid physical activity during asymptomatic periods to prevent structural damage
B. Perform regular low-impact aerobic activity with postural, spinal-extension, and deep-breathing exercises
C. Use a thoracolumbar brace continuously to maintain spinal alignment
D. Perform repetitive spinal-flexion exercises to reduce morning stiffness
E. Undergo routine high-velocity spinal manipulation to maintain mobility

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The presentation is characteristic of ankylosing spondylitis, including onset before age 40, inflammatory-pattern back pain that improves with activity, prolonged morning stiffness, alternating buttock pain, limited lumbar motion and chest expansion, and bilateral sacroiliitis on imaging. Regular exercise is a central component of long-term management. Low-impact aerobic activity, spinal-extension and postural exercises, stretching, and deep-breathing exercises help maintain mobility, preserve upright posture, support chest expansion, and reduce functional limitation. Physical therapy may be helpful in developing an individualized program.

A is incorrect because prolonged inactivity promotes stiffness, reduced mobility, deconditioning, and postural decline. Patients should remain physically active within safe limits.

C is incorrect because continuous bracing is not routinely recommended. It may contribute to deconditioning and does not prevent progressive structural changes.

D is incorrect because repetitive flexion can reinforce the characteristic forward-flexed posture. Exercise programs should emphasize extension and neutral alignment.

E is incorrect because high-velocity spinal manipulation may cause serious injury, including fracture or neurologic compromise, particularly when structural rigidity or reduced bone density is present.

Question 182  [ID: 8a77cfe575da4b259e734bc23f63fa87]
A 13-year-old boy presents with 3 months of gradually worsening pain just below the right knee. He plays soccer and basketball, and the pain is aggravated by running, jumping, kneeling, and climbing stairs. He denies fever, night pain, recent trauma, or pain at rest. Examination shows a prominent, tender tibial tubercle without erythema or warmth. Pain is reproduced by resisted knee extension, but the joint has full range of motion and no effusion or instability. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein levels are normal. A lateral radiograph demonstrates mild fragmentation and soft-tissue swelling at the tibial tubercle without fracture or destructive changes. In addition to temporary activity modification and local ice application, which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy?

A. Oral ibuprofen as needed for pain
B. Intra-articular triamcinolone injection
C. Oral prednisone for 7 days
D. Oral cephalexin for 10 days
E. Oral oxycodone before athletic activity

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical presentation is characteristic of Osgood-Schlatter disease, an overuse-related traction apophysitis involving the tibial tubercle in a skeletally immature athlete. Initial management is conservative and includes relative rest from provoking activities, ice, stretching and strengthening exercises, and short-term use of a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as ibuprofen for symptomatic relief. NSAIDs reduce pain and allow participation in rehabilitation, although they do not alter the underlying self-limited course. Intra-articular triamcinolone is inappropriate because the condition is extra-articular, and corticosteroid injection near the patellar tendon may weaken the tendon and cause local tissue atrophy. Systemic prednisone is not indicated for this localized mechanical overuse condition and exposes the patient to unnecessary adverse effects. Cephalexin is inappropriate because there are no clinical or laboratory findings suggesting infection. Oxycodone is not appropriate for this generally mild, self-limited condition; opioid use introduces substantial risks and could mask pain that should guide activity modification.

Question 183  [ID: 8a9091f06dcb473595813fb1c985751c]
A 72-year-old man presents with 8 months of bilateral buttock and posterior thigh pain that occurs after walking approximately 2 blocks. The discomfort is accompanied by tingling in both feet and resolves within several minutes when he sits or leans forward over a shopping cart. Standing upright without walking can also provoke his symptoms. He has hypertension and hyperlipidemia. Physical examination shows normal lower-extremity strength, symmetric patellar reflexes, mildly diminished Achilles reflexes, and intact distal pulses. Straight-leg raising does not reproduce the pain. MRI demonstrates degenerative facet joint enlargement, thickening of the ligamentum flavum, and decreased space available for the cauda equina at L3-L5. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms best explains why forward flexion relieves this patient's symptoms?

A. Flexion increases the dimensions of the central canal and neural foramina, reducing mechanical compression and ischemia of the nerve roots
B. Flexion increases lumbar lordosis, preventing posterior displacement of the intervertebral discs
C. Flexion decreases arterial perfusion pressure in the lower extremities, reducing metabolic demand in exercising muscles
D. Flexion stretches the sciatic nerves, restoring axoplasmic transport through demyelinated peripheral nerve segments
E. Flexion stabilizes the sacroiliac joints, decreasing inflammatory stimulation of adjacent sensory fibers

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has lumbar spinal stenosis causing neurogenic claudication. Degenerative facet hypertrophy, ligamentum flavum thickening, and other age-related changes reduce the space available for the cauda equina. Lumbar extension further decreases the cross-sectional area of the central canal and neural foramina and promotes inward buckling of the ligamentum flavum, increasing nerve-root compression and transient ischemia. Flexion has the opposite effect: it enlarges the canal and foramina, reduces neural compression, and relieves symptoms. This accounts for the characteristic improvement with sitting or leaning over a shopping cart. Choice B is incorrect because flexion decreases, rather than increases, lumbar lordosis. Choice C describes neither the biomechanics nor the physiology of this condition; vascular claudication is typically related to inadequate arterial flow during exertion and is not reliably relieved by lumbar flexion. Choice D is incorrect because nerve stretching does not restore axoplasmic transport and would be more likely to provoke symptoms in a tension-sensitive nerve-root disorder. Choice E is incorrect because sacroiliac joint inflammation does not explain bilateral exertional leg symptoms, posture-dependent relief, or the MRI findings.

Question 184  [ID: 8b3ec53f733e4253877e90506dcfdea2]
A 24-year-old man presents with 5 days of pain and swelling in his right knee and left ankle. Three weeks ago, he had dysuria and scant urethral discharge that resolved without treatment. He now also reports redness and irritation of both eyes. Examination shows bilateral nonpurulent conjunctival injection, a small painless ulcer on the glans penis, and tenderness at the insertion of the right Achilles tendon. The right knee has a moderate effusion and limited range of motion. Synovial fluid analysis shows 18,000 leukocytes/mm3 with 65% neutrophils; Gram stain and culture are negative. A nucleic acid amplification test of first-catch urine is positive for Chlamydia trachomatis. Which of the following mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's current clinical findings?

A. Deposition of circulating immune complexes containing bacterial antigens within synovial tissue
B. Immune-mediated inflammation triggered by a distant infection in a genetically susceptible host
C. Direct invasion and replication of viable bacteria within the affected joints
D. Formation of monosodium urate crystals due to reduced renal uric acid excretion
E. Autoantibody-mediated destruction of citrullinated proteins within the synovium

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The patient has reactive arthritis, characterized by acute asymmetric oligoarthritis of the lower extremities, enthesitis, conjunctivitis, and genitourinary findings occurring several weeks after an infection, commonly Chlamydia trachomatis. The underlying mechanism is sterile, immune-mediated inflammation triggered by microbial antigens at a distant site. Genetic susceptibility, particularly HLA-B27, is associated with increased risk and more severe or persistent disease.

A is incorrect because classic immune-complex deposition is associated with conditions such as serum sickness, disseminated gonococcal infection, and some viral arthritides; it is not the primary mechanism of reactive arthritis.

B is correct because the joint inflammation is an immune-mediated response following infection elsewhere in the body. Synovial cultures are sterile, distinguishing this condition from septic arthritis.

C is incorrect because direct bacterial invasion would cause septic arthritis. Although microbial components may sometimes be detected in synovial tissue, viable organisms do not typically replicate within the affected joints, and routine synovial cultures are negative.

D is incorrect because monosodium urate deposition causes gout, which typically presents as an intensely painful monoarthritis and is confirmed by needle-shaped, negatively birefringent crystals in synovial fluid. It does not explain the preceding urethritis, conjunctivitis, or enthesitis.

E is incorrect because antibodies to citrullinated proteins are strongly associated with rheumatoid arthritis, which usually causes a chronic, symmetric inflammatory polyarthritis involving small joints rather than an acute asymmetric lower-extremity oligoarthritis following infection.

Question 185  [ID: 8b4fd04720d7430cbba7a259b49bde49]
A 72-year-old man presents with gradually worsening pain in his right hip and lower back for 8 months. He also reports that his hats no longer fit and that he has had progressive hearing difficulty. Examination reveals an enlarged skull, mild frontal bossing, and warmth over the right tibia. Laboratory studies show an alkaline phosphatase level of 680 U/L, with normal serum calcium, phosphorus, and parathyroid hormone levels. Radiographs of the pelvis demonstrate cortical thickening, coarsened trabeculae, and enlargement of the right hemipelvis. Skull radiographs show patchy areas of sclerosis. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Acromegaly
B. Osteomalacia
C. Paget disease of bone
D. Primary hyperparathyroidism
E. Osteoporosis

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The correct answer is C. Paget disease of bone is characterized by excessive, disorganized remodeling that produces enlarged but structurally abnormal skeletal tissue. It typically affects adults older than 55 years and commonly involves the pelvis, spine, skull, and long bones. Classic findings include pain, increasing hat size due to skull enlargement, hearing loss from cranial nerve compression, localized warmth from increased vascularity, and radiographic cortical thickening with coarse trabeculae. Patchy skull sclerosis may produce a cotton-wool appearance. Markedly elevated alkaline phosphatase with normal calcium and phosphorus levels is characteristic. A is incorrect because acromegaly causes enlarged hands and feet, coarse facial features, prognathism, and elevated insulin-like growth factor 1; it does not typically cause this pattern of focal radiographic remodeling. B is incorrect because osteomalacia usually causes diffuse discomfort, proximal muscle weakness, low vitamin D, and abnormalities in calcium or phosphorus, with Looser zones on imaging. D is incorrect because primary hyperparathyroidism typically produces hypercalcemia, elevated parathyroid hormone, and findings such as subperiosteal resorption or osteitis fibrosa cystica. E is incorrect because osteoporosis causes reduced density and fragility fractures but usually has normal laboratory values and does not cause skeletal enlargement, cortical thickening, or elevated alkaline phosphatase in the absence of a recent fracture.

Question 186  [ID: 8b6b9ce0d271444db2ff1f09e11c1faa]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 9 months of diffuse aching pain involving both sides of her body above and below the waist. She also reports nonrestorative sleep, fatigue, difficulty concentrating, and intermittent headaches. Her symptoms began after a prolonged period of emotional stress and have persisted despite reduced physical activity. She denies joint swelling, focal weakness, fever, rash, or weight loss. Examination demonstrates widespread tenderness to moderate digital pressure without synovitis, muscle weakness, or limitation of joint motion. Complete blood count, comprehensive metabolic panel, thyroid-stimulating hormone, creatine kinase, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein levels are within normal limits. Plain radiographs show no abnormalities. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's symptoms?

A. Central amplification of nociceptive signaling with impaired descending pain inhibition
B. Autoantibody-mediated destruction of postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors
C. Immune complex deposition within synovial tissue causing chronic inflammation
D. Calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition with activation of the inflammasome
E. Immune-mediated necrosis of skeletal muscle fibers with release of intracellular enzymes

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's chronic widespread pain, fatigue, nonrestorative sleep, cognitive symptoms, diffuse tenderness, and normal inflammatory markers, creatine kinase level, and imaging are characteristic of fibromyalgia. Its primary pathophysiologic basis is altered central pain processing, often called central sensitization. Increased excitability of pain-processing pathways, reduced descending inhibitory signaling, and changes in neurotransmitters such as substance P, glutamate, serotonin, and norepinephrine can cause normally nonpainful or mildly painful stimuli to be perceived as significantly painful. There is no primary inflammatory, structural, or destructive abnormality of muscle or joint tissue.

A is correct because central amplification of nociceptive input and impaired endogenous pain inhibition best explain widespread pain despite normal laboratory and imaging findings.

B is incorrect because postsynaptic acetylcholine receptor destruction causes myasthenia gravis, which presents with fatigable weakness, often involving ocular, bulbar, or proximal muscles, rather than diffuse pain and tenderness.

C is incorrect because immune complex-mediated synovial inflammation is associated with inflammatory arthritides, which typically produce joint swelling, prolonged morning stiffness, synovitis, and potentially elevated inflammatory markers.

D is incorrect because calcium pyrophosphate deposition causes acute or chronic inflammatory arthritis, commonly affecting the knees or wrists, and may demonstrate chondrocalcinosis on imaging. It does not cause chronic widespread tenderness with normal joint findings.

E is incorrect because immune-mediated muscle fiber necrosis causes prominent proximal weakness and markedly elevated creatine kinase levels. This patient has normal strength and a normal creatine kinase level.
