Question 187  [ID: 8c6459cf5fd2445fa12cb8feaaf9debd]
A 72-year-old woman presents with 6 weeks of bilateral shoulder and hip aching. She has difficulty getting out of bed, rising from a chair, and brushing her hair because of pain and stiffness, which are most severe in the morning and last approximately 90 minutes. She reports fatigue and a 2.3-kg (5-lb) unintentional weight loss. She denies headache, jaw pain, visual changes, joint swelling, rash, or recent medication changes. Examination shows painful limitation of active shoulder and hip movement, but passive range of motion is preserved. Strength is 5/5 when discomfort is overcome. There is no synovitis or focal neurologic deficit. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 78 mm/hr, elevated C-reactive protein, normal creatine kinase, and negative rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies. Ultrasonography demonstrates bilateral subdeltoid bursitis. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Elderly-onset rheumatoid arthritis
B. Fibromyalgia
C. Polymyalgia rheumatica
D. Polymyositis
E. Rotator cuff tendinopathy

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: Polymyalgia rheumatica is the most likely diagnosis. It typically occurs in adults older than 50 years and causes abrupt or subacute bilateral shoulder and hip girdle pain with prolonged morning stiffness. Constitutional symptoms, markedly elevated inflammatory markers, preserved strength, normal creatine kinase, and bilateral subdeltoid bursitis further support the diagnosis. Patients may report functional weakness because pain limits movement, but objective strength is intact. Elderly-onset rheumatoid arthritis may cause prolonged stiffness and elevated inflammatory markers, but it more commonly produces objective peripheral synovitis and may have positive rheumatoid factor or anti-CCP antibodies. Fibromyalgia causes diffuse pain and fatigue but does not typically cause markedly elevated inflammatory markers or inflammatory bursitis. Polymyositis causes true symmetric proximal muscle weakness and usually an elevated creatine kinase rather than pain-limited movement with preserved strength. Rotator cuff tendinopathy can cause shoulder pain and restricted active movement, but it does not explain bilateral hip symptoms, prolonged systemic morning stiffness, constitutional symptoms, or substantially elevated inflammatory markers.

Question 188  [ID: 8cb0815c482e4ce8b6b634721e3f14f1]
A 72-year-old man presents with 10 months of progressively worsening bilateral buttock and posterior thigh pain that occurs after walking approximately 100 meters. The discomfort is accompanied by numbness in both feet and improves within several minutes of sitting or leaning forward over a shopping cart. He denies pain at rest, fever, weight loss, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and saddle anesthesia. Treatment with activity modification, acetaminophen, a structured physical therapy program, and an epidural glucocorticoid injection has provided minimal relief. On examination, lower-extremity pulses are 2+ and symmetric. Strength is 5/5, and sensation is mildly decreased over both lateral feet. Straight-leg raise testing is negative bilaterally. MRI of the lower back demonstrates severe central canal narrowing at L3-L4 and L4-L5 due to ligamentous and facet hypertrophy, without vertebral slippage or dynamic instability. Which of the following is the most appropriate next clinical intervention?

A. Decompressive laminectomy without fusion
B. Long-term oral opioid therapy
C. Lumbar fusion without decompression
D. Urgent intravenous glucocorticoid therapy
E. Continued observation without additional treatment

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has neurogenic claudication caused by lumbar spinal stenosis, as demonstrated by exertional bilateral leg symptoms that improve with sitting or forward flexion and MRI evidence of severe central canal narrowing. His symptoms significantly limit function and have persisted despite appropriate conservative treatment. Decompressive laminectomy is the most appropriate next intervention. Fusion is generally added only when there is associated instability, deformity, or spondylolisthesis, none of which is present here.

A is correct because surgical decompression is indicated for persistent, function-limiting neurogenic claudication that has not responded to nonsurgical management.

B is incorrect because long-term opioid therapy does not correct the mechanical neural compression and carries substantial risks, including dependence, sedation, falls, and overdose.

C is incorrect because fusion alone does not adequately decompress the affected neural structures. In addition, there is no radiographic instability or vertebral slippage to support fusion.

D is incorrect because intravenous glucocorticoids are not standard treatment for chronic degenerative canal narrowing. They may be considered in select acute inflammatory or malignant compressive processes, which are not suggested by this presentation.

E is incorrect because observation is inappropriate given the patient's progressive, function-limiting symptoms and failure of multiple conservative therapies.

Question 189  [ID: 8dc2d0fc9928430f8ede2fdf75cfbd4c]
A 27-year-old woman presents with 4 months of symmetric pain and morning stiffness in her hands and wrists. She also reports fatigue, intermittent painless oral ulcers, and a facial rash that worsens after sun exposure. Examination reveals tenderness and mild swelling of several metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints without deformity. Laboratory studies show hemoglobin 10.8 g/dL, leukocyte count 3,400/mm3, positive antinuclear antibodies at a titer of 1:640, and positive anti-double-stranded DNA and anti-Smith antibodies. Urinalysis shows no protein or cellular casts, and radiographs of the hands show no erosions. Hydroxychloroquine is initiated. Which of the following is the most appropriate preventive measure to recommend at this time to reduce the risk of medication-related morbidity?

A. Obtain a baseline ophthalmologic examination for retinal toxicity screening
B. Begin monthly complete blood cell counts to screen for agranulocytosis
C. Obtain dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry every year
D. Administer a live attenuated herpes zoster vaccine before the next dose
E. Discontinue the medication if the patient plans to become pregnant

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical findings are consistent with systemic lupus erythematosus, and hydroxychloroquine is recommended for most patients because it reduces disease activity, flares, tissue damage, and thrombotic risk. Its most important cumulative toxicity is irreversible retinal injury. A baseline ophthalmologic evaluation should be obtained soon after treatment is initiated to identify preexisting retinal disease and establish a reference for future screening. Subsequent screening frequency depends on treatment duration and risk factors such as daily dosage greater than 5 mg/kg of actual body weight, renal impairment, concurrent tamoxifen use, and preexisting retinal disease. Choice B is incorrect because severe agranulocytosis is not a common toxicity requiring monthly blood counts during hydroxychloroquine therapy. Choice C is incorrect because routine annual bone-density testing is not indicated in a young premenopausal patient who is not receiving prolonged glucocorticoid therapy and has no additional osteoporosis risk factors. Choice D is incorrect because hydroxychloroquine alone does not cause clinically significant immunosuppression requiring live-vaccine administration before treatment; additionally, routine herpes zoster vaccination is generally given as a recombinant, nonlive vaccine when indicated. Choice E is incorrect because hydroxychloroquine is generally continued during pregnancy; discontinuation increases the risk of maternal disease flares and adverse pregnancy outcomes.

Question 190  [ID: 8f78365ad800428c859e0417ebba04c5]
A 19-year-old man presents with a gradually enlarging, painless prominence near his right knee that he first noticed 4 years ago. During the past 3 months, he has developed intermittent numbness in the right foot and calf discomfort while running. He denies fever, night sweats, weight loss, or recent trauma. Examination reveals a firm, fixed prominence over the posteromedial distal thigh. The right foot is warm, but the dorsalis pedis pulse diminishes when the knee is flexed. Strength is normal, and sensation is mildly decreased over the plantar aspect of the foot. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, C-reactive protein, calcium, and alkaline phosphatase levels are within normal limits. Radiographs show a pedunculated, cartilage-capped projection arising from the distal femoral metaphysis, with uninterrupted continuity between the cortex and medullary cavity of the projection and those of the adjacent bone. MRI demonstrates a 7-mm cartilaginous cap without a soft-tissue mass but shows displacement of the adjacent neurovascular bundle. Which of the following is the most appropriate intervention?

A. Observation with periodic clinical evaluation only
B. Marginal surgical excision of the lesion at its base
C. Neoadjuvant multiagent chemotherapy followed by wide resection
D. Empiric intravenous antibiotics with operative drainage
E. External-beam radiation therapy

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The imaging finding of a cartilage-capped bony projection with continuity of both the cortex and medullary cavity is characteristic of an osteochondroma. Although asymptomatic lesions can generally be observed, this patient has neurovascular compromise, demonstrated by sensory symptoms, exertional calf discomfort, positional reduction of the distal pulse, and MRI-confirmed displacement of the neurovascular bundle. Symptomatic osteochondromas should be treated with marginal surgical excision, including removal of the cartilage cap and perichondrium at the lesion's base. A is incorrect because observation is appropriate only when the lesion is asymptomatic and lacks concerning growth or complications. C is incorrect because chemotherapy and wide resection are reserved for selected malignant bone tumors; the thin cartilage cap, absence of a soft-tissue mass, and characteristic imaging findings do not suggest malignant transformation. D is incorrect because normal inflammatory markers, lack of systemic symptoms, and absence of imaging findings associated with an abscess or infection make antimicrobial therapy and drainage inappropriate. E is incorrect because radiation therapy has no routine role in treating an osteochondroma and would expose a young patient to unnecessary toxicity.

Question 191  [ID: 91a77ef15c7d4cd5928312c04b32fe3e]
A 10-year-old girl is brought to the clinic for 3 months of pain, swelling, and stiffness involving both wrists, several metacarpophalangeal joints, both knees, and the left ankle. Her stiffness lasts approximately 90 minutes each morning and improves with activity. She has no fever, evanescent rash, recent infection, or weight loss. Examination shows warmth, swelling, and restricted range of motion in eight joints. Laboratory studies reveal an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, positive antinuclear antibodies, negative rheumatoid factor, and normal serum uric acid. Plain radiographs show periarticular soft-tissue swelling without erosions. A trial of naproxen has provided only partial symptomatic relief. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacologic therapy to control disease activity and reduce the risk of progressive joint damage?

A. Continue naproxen as sole therapy
B. Begin weekly methotrexate with folic acid supplementation
C. Begin long-term daily oral prednisone
D. Begin etanercept as first-line monotherapy
E. Begin colchicine therapy

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This child has juvenile rheumatoid arthritis, also termed juvenile idiopathic arthritis, with polyarticular involvement and persistent active disease despite treatment with a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug. Methotrexate is the preferred conventional disease-modifying antirheumatic drug for active polyarticular disease because it controls inflammation and reduces the risk of progressive joint damage. Folic acid is given to reduce adverse effects such as stomatitis, gastrointestinal intolerance, and cytopenias. CBC, hepatic transaminases, and renal function should be monitored during therapy. A is incorrect because NSAIDs may reduce pain and stiffness but generally do not adequately prevent structural damage when multiple joints remain actively involved. C is incorrect because systemic glucocorticoids can be used briefly for severe manifestations or as bridging therapy, but long-term use in children is avoided due to growth suppression, osteoporosis, infection risk, and other toxicities. D is incorrect because tumor necrosis factor inhibitors such as etanercept are generally added when disease remains active despite an adequate trial of methotrexate or when specific high-risk features justify earlier biologic therapy; they are not the usual first disease-modifying treatment in this presentation. E is incorrect because colchicine is used for conditions such as crystal-induced inflammation and familial Mediterranean fever and does not treat this chronic polyarticular condition.

Question 192  [ID: 9414d3c3e35944609ba6a6662fd9efa4]
A 27-year-old man presents with 9 months of progressive lower back and buttock pain. The discomfort is worse during the second half of the night, causes morning stiffness lasting approximately 90 minutes, and improves after exercise but not with rest. He denies preceding trauma. Three months ago, he was treated for a painful, red, photophobic eye. Examination reveals tenderness over both sacroiliac joints and reduced chest expansion. The erythrocyte sedimentation rate is elevated, and pelvic radiographs show bilateral erosive changes and subchondral sclerosis adjacent to the sacroiliac joints. Which of the following additional physical examination findings is most likely in this patient?

A. Reproduction of radicular pain with ipsilateral straight-leg raising
B. An increase of less than 5 cm between lumbar skin markings during forward flexion
C. Localized tenderness over the greater trochanter with preserved spinal motion
D. Pain and palpable crepitus during passive rotation of the hip
E. A positive Thompson test with absent plantar flexion

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The patient's young age, chronic back and alternating buttock symptoms that improve with activity, prolonged morning stiffness, prior episode of anterior uveitis, reduced chest expansion, and bilateral sacroiliac erosions are characteristic of ankylosing spondylitis. Progressive limitation of lumbar flexion is a typical examination finding. The modified Schober test assesses lumbar mobility by measuring the change in distance between skin markings during forward flexion. An increase of less than 5 cm indicates restricted lumbar flexion and supports this diagnosis.

A is incorrect because radicular pain during straight-leg raising suggests lumbosacral nerve root irritation, commonly from lumbar disk herniation, rather than the axial stiffness described here.

C is incorrect because focal greater trochanteric tenderness with preserved spinal motion is more consistent with greater trochanteric pain syndrome.

D is incorrect because pain and crepitus with passive hip rotation suggest degenerative joint disease. Hip involvement may occur in advanced cases, but this is less characteristic than restricted lumbar flexion in this presentation.

E is incorrect because an absent plantar-flexion response during the Thompson test indicates rupture of the Achilles tendon and does not explain the patient's chronic axial symptoms.

Question 193  [ID: 941ea1888ba44340b3f1f6e839a3d725]
A 29-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a high-speed motor vehicle collision in which his flexed knee struck the dashboard. He reports severe left groin and buttock pain and is unable to move the affected limb. Examination shows that the left lower extremity is shortened, adducted, and internally rotated. The dorsalis pedis pulse is palpable, but sensation is decreased over the dorsum of the foot. An anteroposterior pelvic radiograph demonstrates the left femoral head positioned superior and posterior to the acetabulum without an associated fracture. The patient is hemodynamically stable, and no other immediately life-threatening injuries are identified. Which of the following is the most appropriate next intervention?

A. Obtain magnetic resonance imaging before attempting treatment
B. Perform urgent closed reduction under procedural sedation
C. Apply skeletal traction and reassess in 24 hours
D. Discharge with crutches, analgesics, and outpatient follow-up
E. Begin physical therapy with active range-of-motion exercises

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The limb position, mechanism of dashboard impact, and radiographic displacement of the femoral head are characteristic of a posterior hip dislocation. Urgent closed reduction under adequate procedural sedation or anesthesia is required, ideally within 6 hours, to reduce the risks of femoral head osteonecrosis, progressive sciatic nerve injury, and articular cartilage damage. Neurovascular function should be documented before and after reduction. Post-reduction radiographs and computed tomography are then obtained to confirm concentric reduction and evaluate for occult acetabular or femoral head fragments.

A is incorrect because MRI is not required before reduction and would unnecessarily delay time-sensitive treatment. MRI may be useful later for selected soft-tissue or osteonecrosis evaluations.

C is incorrect because skeletal traction does not provide definitive urgent treatment and delaying reduction increases the risk of osteonecrosis and neurologic injury.

D is incorrect because this is an emergency requiring immediate reduction, not outpatient management.

E is incorrect because active range-of-motion exercises before reduction could worsen pain and neurovascular or articular injury. Rehabilitation is considered only after successful reduction, confirmation of joint stability, and evaluation for associated injuries.

Question 194  [ID: 94cd8a6974184464bf9687c445707baa]
A 42-year-old warehouse employee presents with 5 days of severe lower back pain that began while lifting a heavy box. The pain radiates through the left buttock and posterior thigh to the lateral foot and is worsened by coughing and sitting. Examination shows reduced sensation along the lateral aspect of the left foot, weakness of left ankle plantar flexion, and a diminished left Achilles reflex. Raising the extended left leg reproduces the radiating pain at 35 degrees. Complete blood count and inflammatory markers are within normal limits. MRI demonstrates a focal left posterolateral soft-tissue projection contiguous with the L5-S1 interspace that narrows the lateral recess. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's findings?

A. Disruption of the annulus fibrosus with posterior displacement of nucleus pulposus material and compression of the traversing S1 nerve root
B. Hypertrophy of the ligamentum flavum causing symmetric compression of multiple lumbosacral nerve roots
C. Autoimmune destruction of peripheral nerve myelin producing diffuse, ascending motor weakness
D. Compression of the common fibular nerve at the fibular head causing impaired ankle dorsiflexion
E. Degeneration of the femoral head due to interruption of its retinacular blood supply

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's radiating pain, sensory loss over the lateral foot, plantar-flexion weakness, diminished Achilles reflex, and positive straight-leg-raise test localize the lesion to the left S1 nerve root. At the L5-S1 level, a posterolateral defect in the annulus fibrosus can permit nucleus pulposus material to move posteriorly and compress the traversing S1 root. Both mechanical compression and local inflammatory mediators contribute to radicular pain and neurologic deficits. Choice B is incorrect because ligamentum flavum hypertrophy usually contributes to central canal narrowing, producing chronic bilateral symptoms, neurogenic claudication, or multilevel deficits rather than an acute unilateral S1 pattern. Choice C is incorrect because autoimmune peripheral demyelination typically causes symmetric ascending weakness and generalized areflexia rather than pain and sensory findings confined to a single dermatomal distribution. Choice D is incorrect because common fibular nerve compression causes weakness of dorsiflexion and eversion with sensory loss over the dorsum of the foot; it does not cause reduced plantar flexion or a diminished Achilles reflex. Choice E is incorrect because compromised femoral head perfusion causes hip or groin pain and progressive joint dysfunction, not dermatomal radiating pain with an S1 motor and reflex deficit.

Question 195  [ID: 96c65f65d07c4bee94a04597717f42f6]
A 62-year-old man with poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus and peripheral neuropathy presents with 3 weeks of worsening drainage from an ulcer beneath his right first metatarsal head. He reports minimal pain but has recently developed malaise and intermittent chills. His temperature is 38.1°C (100.6°F). Examination shows a 2-cm plantar ulcer with surrounding erythema and purulent drainage. A sterile metal probe inserted into the ulcer contacts a firm underlying surface. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 13,200/mm3, erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 92 mm/hr, and C-reactive protein level of 11 mg/dL. Plain radiographs demonstrate focal cortical irregularity and periosteal reaction near the ulcer. MRI shows decreased T1 signal and increased T2 signal within the adjacent first metatarsal, with surrounding marrow enhancement. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Acute Charcot neuroarthropathy
B. Cellulitis
C. Gouty arthritis
D. Osteomyelitis
E. Septic arthritis

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: Osteomyelitis is the most likely diagnosis. This patient has a chronic diabetic foot ulcer with purulent drainage, systemic inflammatory findings, a positive probe-to-bone test, markedly elevated inflammatory markers, and imaging abnormalities involving the adjacent cortex and marrow. MRI findings of decreased T1 signal, increased T2 signal, and marrow enhancement are highly suggestive of infection extending into the first metatarsal. Cellulitis involves the skin and subcutaneous tissues and would not explain the positive probe-to-bone test or marrow signal abnormalities. Acute Charcot neuroarthropathy can cause warmth, swelling, and bony destruction in patients with diabetic neuropathy, but it more commonly produces periarticular fragmentation, subluxation, and midfoot collapse without a draining ulcer, purulence, or systemic inflammatory findings. Gouty arthritis typically presents with abrupt, severe joint pain and may show erosions or tophi, but it does not typically cause contiguous marrow involvement from a purulent plantar ulcer. Septic arthritis causes infection within a joint space, usually with severe pain and restricted range of motion; the imaging findings here are centered in the metatarsal adjacent to the ulcer rather than within a joint.

Question 196  [ID: 9739d118b07c4352bef22f2d9ff728a0]
A 27-year-old woman presents with 4 months of bilateral hand and wrist pain accompanied by morning stiffness lasting 90 minutes. She also reports fatigue, intermittent painless oral ulcers, and a facial rash that worsens after sun exposure. Examination reveals tenderness and mild swelling of the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints without deformity or erosive changes. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 3,200/mm3, positive antinuclear antibodies, elevated anti-double-stranded DNA antibody titers, and low serum complement levels. Serum creatinine and urinalysis are normal. She has no neurologic, cardiopulmonary, or hematologic instability. Which of the following is the most appropriate long-term pharmacologic intervention for this patient?

A. Hydroxychloroquine
B. Cyclophosphamide
C. Indomethacin alone
D. High-dose intravenous methylprednisolone
E. Belimumab

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Hydroxychloroquine is the preferred foundational long-term therapy for patients with this clinical presentation, particularly when manifestations include inflammatory joint symptoms, photosensitive skin findings, oral ulcers, and constitutional symptoms without major organ involvement. It reduces disease activity and flare frequency and improves long-term survival. A baseline ophthalmologic examination and periodic retinal screening are required because of the risk of retinal toxicity. Cyclophosphamide is reserved for severe or organ-threatening manifestations, such as proliferative nephritis, severe neuropsychiatric involvement, or pulmonary hemorrhage. Indomethacin may provide temporary symptomatic relief but does not adequately control the underlying disease or prevent future flares and complications. High-dose intravenous methylprednisolone is appropriate for severe, acute, organ-threatening exacerbations rather than stable outpatient disease. Belimumab may be added when active disease persists despite standard therapy, but it is not the preferred initial long-term medication in this patient.

Question 197  [ID: 97b454fbe2b64969a47941d69663a820]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 9 months of diffuse aching throughout her body, nonrestorative sleep, fatigue, difficulty concentrating, and intermittent headaches. Her symptoms worsen during periods of emotional stress and inactivity. She denies joint swelling, prolonged morning stiffness, fever, rash, weight loss, or focal weakness. Examination shows widespread soft-tissue tenderness without synovitis, deformity, muscle weakness, or neurologic deficits. Complete blood count, comprehensive metabolic panel, thyroid-stimulating hormone, creatine kinase, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein levels are within normal limits. After discussing the chronic nature of her symptoms and establishing realistic treatment goals, which of the following recommendations is most appropriate to improve long-term function and quality of life?

A. Begin a graded low-impact aerobic exercise program, reinforce consistent sleep habits, and consider cognitive behavioral therapy
B. Avoid physical activity during symptomatic periods to prevent tissue injury
C. Use long-term opioid therapy to achieve complete elimination of pain
D. Undergo serial inflammatory marker testing every 3 months to monitor disease activity
E. Adopt prolonged bed rest followed by high-intensity resistance training once symptoms resolve

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's chronic widespread pain, fatigue, nonrestorative sleep, cognitive symptoms, diffuse tenderness, normal strength, absence of synovitis, and normal laboratory studies are characteristic of fibromyalgia. Patient education should emphasize that the condition is chronic but manageable and does not represent ongoing tissue destruction. A gradual, individualized program of low-impact aerobic exercise is among the most effective measures for improving pain, physical function, fatigue, and quality of life. Consistent sleep habits and cognitive behavioral therapy can further improve coping, sleep, and symptom burden. A is correct because it reflects an evidence-based, multidisciplinary self-management strategy. B is incorrect because inactivity promotes deconditioning and may worsen pain and functional impairment. C is incorrect because long-term opioid therapy has limited benefit in this condition and carries substantial risks, including dependence, overdose, and opioid-induced hyperalgesia. D is incorrect because inflammatory markers are generally normal and serial testing does not monitor symptom severity or guide routine management. E is incorrect because prolonged bed rest worsens deconditioning, and abrupt high-intensity exercise may exacerbate symptoms; exercise should instead begin at a tolerable level and increase gradually.

Question 198  [ID: 98a188e1dc2c4b71af75c147bf8c3842]
A 34-year-old man presents with 2 days of aching pain across his lower back that began immediately after he lifted a heavy box while moving. The pain worsens with bending and twisting but does not radiate into his legs. He denies fever, weight loss, urinary retention, incontinence, saddle anesthesia, weakness, or numbness. He has no history of malignancy, injection drug use, chronic glucocorticoid therapy, or recent infection. Examination shows localized bilateral paraspinal tenderness and reduced range of motion due to pain. Lower-extremity strength is 5/5, sensation is intact, reflexes are symmetric, and straight-leg raising produces no radicular symptoms. He has no midline vertebral tenderness. Urinalysis is normal, and plain radiographs obtained at an urgent care center show no acute osseous abnormality. Which of the following is the best initial clinical intervention?

A. Encourage continued activity as tolerated, apply superficial heat, and begin a short course of an NSAID
B. Prescribe strict bed rest for 1 week and an oral opioid analgesic
C. Obtain urgent MRI and refer for surgical evaluation
D. Begin a prolonged taper of systemic glucocorticoids
E. Apply a rigid thoracolumbar brace continuously for 6 weeks

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has acute, uncomplicated mechanical pain following a lifting injury, with localized paraspinal tenderness, pain with movement, a normal neurologic examination, and no red-flag features. Initial management is conservative and should include remaining active as tolerated, avoiding prolonged bed rest, using superficial heat, and administering a short course of an NSAID if there are no contraindications. Most cases improve substantially within several days to a few weeks. A is correct because early mobilization and nonopioid analgesia promote recovery and reduce disability. B is incorrect because prolonged bed rest delays functional recovery, and opioids are not first-line treatment for uncomplicated acute pain. C is incorrect because MRI and surgical referral are reserved for severe or progressive neurologic deficits, suspected cauda equina syndrome, infection, malignancy, fracture, or persistent symptoms despite appropriate conservative care. D is incorrect because systemic glucocorticoids have no established benefit for uncomplicated nonradicular pain and can cause significant adverse effects. E is incorrect because prolonged rigid bracing is unnecessary, may contribute to deconditioning, and does not improve outcomes in an uncomplicated soft-tissue injury.

Question 199  [ID: 990b1f0271294551a6ad0854e9d12a42]
A 6-year-old girl is brought to the clinic because of 10 weeks of swelling of the right knee and left ankle. Her mother reports that the child walks stiffly for approximately 1 hour each morning but becomes more active later in the day. There is no history of trauma, fever, weight loss, rash, or recent infection. Examination shows a right knee effusion, mild swelling of the left ankle, warmth, and decreased range of motion without overlying erythema. Laboratory studies show a mildly elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, a normal complete blood count, a positive antinuclear antibody test, and a negative rheumatoid factor. Plain radiographs show soft-tissue swelling without fracture or bony erosion. Based on this presentation, which additional examination should be prioritized to evaluate for an important associated condition that may initially be asymptomatic?

A. Dilated funduscopic examination for retinal hemorrhages
B. Schirmer testing for decreased tear production
C. Slit-lamp examination for anterior chamber inflammation
D. Weber and Rinne testing for sensorineural hearing loss
E. Oral examination for painless mucosal ulcerations

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: This child has an oligoarticular pattern of juvenile rheumatoid arthritis, currently termed juvenile idiopathic arthritis. Young age, female sex, involvement of four or fewer joints, prolonged morning stiffness, symptom improvement with activity, and positive antinuclear antibodies are characteristic. Children with this pattern are at increased risk for chronic anterior uveitis, which is frequently asymptomatic until ocular damage has occurred. Regular slit-lamp examinations are therefore essential because routine inspection of the eyes may be normal. A dilated funduscopic examination evaluates the retina and posterior segment rather than anterior chamber inflammation. Schirmer testing assesses tear production and is used when evaluating keratoconjunctivitis sicca. Weber and Rinne tests assess hearing and are not routinely indicated in this presentation. Painless oral ulcers may suggest systemic lupus erythematosus, but an oral examination does not screen for the major asymptomatic complication associated with this child's presentation.

Question 200  [ID: 9962aa349b00485abb8c6092f712b466]
A 72-year-old woman presents with 6 weeks of bilateral shoulder and hip girdle aching accompanied by morning stiffness lasting approximately 90 minutes. She has difficulty getting out of bed, rising from a chair, and lifting her arms to brush her hair. She denies headache, scalp tenderness, jaw pain with chewing, visual changes, fever, or recent trauma. Examination demonstrates reduced active range of motion of both shoulders because of pain, but passive range of motion is preserved. Muscle strength is 5/5 throughout, and there is no joint swelling or rash. Laboratory studies reveal an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 82 mm/hr, C-reactive protein level of 6.4 mg/dL, normal creatine kinase level, and negative rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies. Which of the following is the best initial pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. Prednisone 15 mg orally once daily
B. Ibuprofen 600 mg orally three times daily
C. Methotrexate 15 mg orally once weekly
D. Methylprednisolone 1 g intravenously daily for 3 days
E. Hydroxychloroquine 200 mg orally twice daily

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. This older patient has subacute, symmetric shoulder and hip girdle pain, prolonged morning stiffness, markedly elevated inflammatory markers, preserved muscle strength, and a normal creatine kinase level. Initial treatment is a low-dose oral glucocorticoid, typically prednisone 12.5-25 mg daily. Symptoms often improve dramatically within several days, after which the medication is gradually tapered according to the clinical response and inflammatory marker levels. Patients receiving prolonged glucocorticoid therapy should also be assessed for osteoporosis risk and may require calcium, vitamin D, and antiresorptive therapy. B is incorrect because nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs generally provide inadequate disease control and are not recommended as sole initial therapy. C is incorrect because methotrexate is not first-line monotherapy; it may be added as a glucocorticoid-sparing agent in patients with frequent relapses, high glucocorticoid requirements, or substantial risk from glucocorticoid toxicity. D is incorrect because pulse-dose intravenous glucocorticoids are excessive in the absence of ischemic ocular or neurologic complications. E is incorrect because hydroxychloroquine is used for selected inflammatory arthritides but is not effective initial therapy for this presentation.

Question 201  [ID: 9967c9cb53304e86aa2c385dc60589fc]
A 27-year-old woman presents with 3 months of fatigue and episodic pain and swelling of the wrists, metacarpophalangeal joints, and knees. Morning stiffness lasts approximately 90 minutes. She also reports painless oral ulcers and a facial rash that worsens after sun exposure. Examination shows tenderness and mild swelling of both wrists and several metacarpophalangeal joints without deformities. There is an erythematous eruption over the cheeks and nasal bridge that spares the nasolabial folds. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 3,200/mm3, hemoglobin of 10.8 g/dL, serum creatinine of 1.2 mg/dL, and urinalysis with 2+ protein and microscopic hematuria. Antinuclear antibodies are present at a titer of 1:640, antibodies to double-stranded DNA are detected, and serum C3 and C4 levels are decreased. Radiographs of the hands show no erosions. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Dermatomyositis
B. Mixed connective tissue disease
C. Rheumatoid arthritis
D. Systemic lupus erythematosus
E. Systemic sclerosis

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: The correct answer is D. Systemic lupus erythematosus is supported by symmetric, nonerosive inflammatory joint involvement combined with photosensitive malar erythema that spares the nasolabial folds, painless oral ulcers, leukopenia, anemia, proteinuria, microscopic hematuria, high-titer antinuclear antibodies, anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies, and hypocomplementemia. Anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies are relatively specific and often correlate with renal disease activity. Decreased complement levels reflect immune-complex consumption. A is incorrect because dermatomyositis typically causes symmetric proximal muscle weakness, elevated muscle enzymes, a heliotrope eruption, and Gottron papules rather than nonerosive small-joint inflammation with renal findings. B is incorrect because mixed connective tissue disease characteristically has high-titer anti-U1 ribonucleoprotein antibodies and overlapping features such as Raynaud phenomenon, swollen hands, myositis, and esophageal dysmotility. C is incorrect because rheumatoid arthritis can produce symmetric inflammatory involvement of the wrists and metacarpophalangeal joints, but prolonged disease commonly causes marginal erosions and does not explain the photosensitive malar eruption, oral ulcers, hypocomplementemia, or anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies. E is incorrect because systemic sclerosis is associated with skin thickening, sclerodactyly, Raynaud phenomenon, telangiectasias, and gastrointestinal dysmotility, none of which are present here.

Question 202  [ID: 99af44149ebb4b1a9c54b9a64e48a20a]
A 68-year-old woman presents for follow-up after sustaining a distal radius fracture when she tripped and fell from standing height 6 weeks ago. She underwent menopause at age 49 and has never received hormone therapy. She does not smoke or drink alcohol. Physical examination shows normal healing of the fracture and no focal vertebral tenderness. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry demonstrates T-scores of -2.8 at the femoral neck and -2.6 at the lumbar spine. Serum calcium, 25-hydroxyvitamin D, creatinine, thyroid-stimulating hormone, and alkaline phosphatase levels are within normal limits. Her estimated glomerular filtration rate is 72 mL/min/1.73 m2. She has no history of esophageal disease and can remain upright after taking medication. In addition to recommending weight-bearing exercise and adequate calcium and vitamin D intake, which of the following is the best initial pharmacologic intervention?

A. Oral alendronate
B. Intranasal calcitonin
C. Raloxifene
D. Teriparatide
E. Systemic estrogen therapy

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has postmenopausal osteoporosis demonstrated by T-scores at or below -2.5 and a low-trauma fracture. Oral alendronate, a bisphosphonate, is an appropriate first-line pharmacologic therapy because it reduces vertebral, nonvertebral, and hip fracture risk. She has adequate renal function, no esophageal disorder, and can remain upright after administration, so she has no major contraindication to oral bisphosphonate therapy. Calcitonin is not preferred for chronic fracture prevention because its antifracture efficacy is limited; it is used primarily for short-term analgesia after an acute vertebral compression fracture. Raloxifene reduces vertebral fracture risk but has not demonstrated comparable reduction in hip fracture risk and increases the risk of venous thromboembolism. Teriparatide is generally reserved for patients at very high fracture risk, such as those with multiple vertebral fractures, extremely low T-scores, or failure of antiresorptive therapy. Systemic estrogen can prevent bone loss but is not first-line treatment in a 68-year-old woman because the cardiovascular, thromboembolic, and malignancy risks generally outweigh the skeletal benefits when initiated long after menopause.

Question 203  [ID: 9a1916b83fc846a4a88e6d5033ff4593]
A 16-year-old competitive gymnast presents with 3 months of activity-related low back pain that worsens with tumbling and back-extension maneuvers. She denies pain at rest, bowel or bladder dysfunction, lower-extremity weakness, or numbness. Examination demonstrates increased lumbar lordosis, tight hamstrings, and pain reproduced by single-leg hyperextension. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, reflexes, and gait are normal. Standing lateral radiography demonstrates approximately 20% anterior translation of the L5 vertebral body relative to S1. Which of the following is the most appropriate counseling to reduce symptoms and minimize the risk of further vertebral displacement?

A. Continue unrestricted participation because neurologic findings are absent
B. Avoid all athletic activity permanently, even after symptoms resolve
C. Temporarily avoid painful hyperextension and impact activities, begin supervised core stabilization and hamstring flexibility exercises, and return gradually when pain-free
D. Remain on strict bed rest until follow-up imaging demonstrates complete anatomic correction
E. Wear a rigid brace continuously for life to prevent progression

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The imaging and clinical findings are consistent with low-grade spondylolisthesis in an adolescent athlete, commonly associated with repetitive lumbar extension. Initial management and preventive counseling are conservative when there are no neurologic deficits or high-grade displacement. The patient should temporarily stop activities that provoke pain, particularly repetitive hyperextension and high-impact loading, and participate in physical therapy emphasizing core stabilization, neutral-spine mechanics, and hamstring flexibility. A gradual return to sports is appropriate after the patient is pain-free and has recovered functional strength and range of motion. A is incorrect because unrestricted participation during symptomatic periods may worsen pain and contribute to progression. B is incorrect because permanent restriction is generally unnecessary for low-grade disease that responds to conservative care. D is incorrect because prolonged bed rest causes deconditioning and is not required; complete radiographic correction is not an expected treatment goal. E is incorrect because bracing may be used selectively for persistent symptoms, but lifelong continuous bracing is neither routinely indicated nor preventive standard care.

Question 204  [ID: 9a51515fa1a24a488c8bcd020d4b6333]
A 72-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after falling forward and striking his forehead on a countertop. He reports severe posterior neck pain and burning sensations in both hands. He has a history of progressive neck stiffness but was ambulatory without assistance before the fall. On examination, he is alert and has midline posterior neck tenderness. Strength is 2/5 in both hands, 3/5 with elbow extension, and 4+/5 in both lower extremities. Sensation to pinprick is diminished in the hands and forearms but relatively preserved in the lower extremities and perineum. Rectal tone is intact. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis based on this history and physical examination?

A. Anterior cord syndrome
B. Brown-Séquard syndrome
C. Cauda equina syndrome
D. Central cord syndrome
E. Conus medullaris syndrome

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: Central cord syndrome is characterized by disproportionately greater motor impairment in the upper extremities than in the lower extremities, often with variable sensory abnormalities and sacral sparing. It commonly occurs in older adults with preexisting cervical canal narrowing after a hyperextension mechanism, such as striking the forehead during a forward fall. The marked hand and arm weakness with relatively preserved lower-extremity strength and perineal sensation is the key examination pattern. Anterior cord syndrome causes bilateral motor paralysis and loss of pain and temperature sensation below the lesion, with preservation of vibration and proprioception. Brown-Séquard syndrome produces ipsilateral motor, vibration, and proprioception loss with contralateral pain and temperature loss below the lesion. Cauda equina syndrome typically causes asymmetric lower-extremity weakness, radicular pain, saddle anesthesia, reduced reflexes, and bowel or bladder dysfunction. Conus medullaris syndrome more often causes symmetric lower-extremity deficits, early bowel or bladder dysfunction, and saddle anesthesia.

Question 205  [ID: 9b3f4e4e02c64701b808830e86c394e6]
A 14-year-old girl presents with intermittent mid-back aching that occurs after prolonged standing and athletic activity. She denies nocturnal pain, weakness, paresthesias, bowel or bladder dysfunction, fever, and recent trauma. Menarche occurred 18 months ago. Examination shows asymmetric shoulder height and prominence of the right posterior ribs during forward flexion. Strength, sensation, reflexes, and gait are normal. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are within normal limits. Standing radiographs demonstrate an 18-degree right thoracic lateral curvature with vertebral rotation and no congenital vertebral abnormalities. Observation and an exercise-based physical therapy program are planned. Which of the following medications is most appropriate for short-term relief of this patient's activity-related discomfort?

A. Ibuprofen
B. Alendronate
C. Prednisone
D. Calcitonin
E. Oxycodone

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Ibuprofen is the most appropriate medication for short-term relief of mild, activity-related mechanical discomfort in this otherwise healthy adolescent. A nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug can be used as needed in conjunction with observation, activity modification, and physical therapy. It may reduce pain but does not correct the curvature or prevent progression. Alendronate is a bisphosphonate used primarily for osteoporosis and has no role in treating this patient's curvature or mechanical discomfort. Prednisone is not indicated because there is no evidence of an inflammatory, autoimmune, or compressive process; systemic corticosteroids also carry substantial adverse effects with repeated use. Calcitonin may occasionally be used for pain associated with acute osteoporotic vertebral compression fractures, which are not present here. Oxycodone is inappropriate for mild intermittent pain because the risks of sedation, dependence, and respiratory depression outweigh any potential benefit.

Question 206  [ID: 9c7b709f37434b19b8c704897cb33313]
A 5-week-old boy is brought to the clinic because his parents have noticed that he persistently holds his head tilted toward the right with his chin rotated toward the left. He was delivered vaginally at 39 weeks after a prolonged labor complicated by breech positioning. He is afebrile and feeds normally. Examination reveals limited passive rotation of the head toward the right and a firm, nontender, 1.5-cm fusiform swelling along the right sternocleidomastoid muscle. There is no erythema, cervical lymphadenopathy, stridor, or focal neurologic deficit. The remainder of the examination is normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic study to characterize the palpable abnormality and support the suspected diagnosis?

A. Ultrasonography of the neck
B. Contrast-enhanced CT of the neck
C. MRI of the brain and cervical spine
D. Anteroposterior and open-mouth odontoid radiographs
E. Electromyography of the sternocleidomastoid muscle

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The infant's characteristic head posture, restricted passive range of motion, breech positioning, and fusiform sternocleidomastoid mass are consistent with congenital muscular torticollis associated with fibrotic shortening of the sternocleidomastoid muscle. Although the diagnosis is often clinical, ultrasonography is the preferred study when imaging is needed to characterize a palpable mass. It is noninvasive, does not expose the infant to radiation, and can demonstrate focal or diffuse sternocleidomastoid thickening and fibrosis while helping exclude other soft-tissue lesions. Contrast-enhanced CT is unnecessary in a well-appearing infant without findings suggesting deep infection, trauma, or malignancy and involves ionizing radiation. MRI of the brain and cervical spine is reserved for atypical presentations, neurologic abnormalities, suspected central pathology, or failure to improve with appropriate therapy. Odontoid radiographs may be used when atlantoaxial instability or rotatory subluxation is suspected, typically after trauma or in an older child with acute painful symptoms; they do not adequately characterize this soft-tissue mass. Electromyography is not routinely used to diagnose this condition and would not be appropriate as the initial diagnostic study.

Question 207  [ID: 9d6967131383473fa6636a62d48c3fc1]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 5 months of progressive pain and swelling involving both wrists and the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints. She reports morning stiffness lasting approximately 2 hours and difficulty opening jars and typing. Examination demonstrates symmetric joint tenderness, boggy swelling, and reduced grip strength. The distal interphalangeal joints are spared. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 58 mm/hr, elevated C-reactive protein, and strongly positive anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies. Radiographs reveal periarticular osteopenia and small marginal erosions. Renal and hepatic function are normal, hepatitis screening is negative, and a pregnancy test is negative. She uses reliable contraception and has no history of pulmonary disease. Which of the following is the best initial long-term pharmacologic therapy to reduce disease activity and prevent further structural damage?

A. Hydroxychloroquine monotherapy
B. Weekly methotrexate with folic acid supplementation
C. Adalimumab monotherapy
D. Daily prednisone as indefinite monotherapy
E. Naproxen monotherapy

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The clinical findings are consistent with rheumatoid arthritis of moderate-to-high activity, including symmetric involvement of the wrists, metacarpophalangeal joints, and proximal interphalangeal joints; prolonged morning stiffness; elevated inflammatory markers; positive anti-CCP antibodies; and marginal erosions. Methotrexate is the preferred initial conventional disease-modifying antirheumatic drug for most patients with moderate-to-high disease activity. It reduces symptoms, limits radiographic progression, and improves long-term function. Folic acid is given to reduce adverse effects such as stomatitis, gastrointestinal intolerance, cytopenias, and hepatotoxicity. CBC, hepatic function, and renal function should be monitored, and pregnancy must be avoided. Hydroxychloroquine monotherapy is more appropriate for mild disease and is unlikely to be sufficient in a patient with erosions and substantial activity. Adalimumab is an effective biologic agent but is generally added after an inadequate response to conventional therapy rather than used as initial monotherapy. Prednisone may be used briefly as bridging therapy but is inappropriate for indefinite monotherapy because of serious long-term toxicities and inadequate protection against structural progression when used alone. Naproxen can reduce pain and stiffness but does not alter the underlying disease course or prevent joint damage.

Question 208  [ID: 9df89c2e677442af917999eb3cc540eb]
A 46-year-old man presents with sudden pain near the back of his left ankle that began while he was accelerating to return a serve during a tennis match. He reports hearing a pop and feeling as though another player kicked him from behind, although no one was nearby. He has difficulty walking and cannot rise onto his toes on the affected side. Examination shows swelling and ecchymosis proximal to the heel, weak plantar flexion, and a palpable gap several centimeters above the calcaneus. No imaging has yet been obtained. Which of the following additional physical examination findings would most strongly support the suspected cause of this patient's symptoms?

A. Absence of plantar flexion when the calf is squeezed with the patient lying prone
B. Increased anterior translation of the talus when the heel is pulled forward
C. Pain over the distal tibiofibular region when the mid-calf is compressed
D. Increased inversion of the heel when the foot is tilted medially
E. Reproduction of plantar heel pain with passive extension of the great toe

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's sudden posterior ankle pain, audible pop, sensation of being kicked, inability to perform a single-leg heel raise, weak plantar flexion, and palpable tendon gap are characteristic of an Achilles tendon rupture. The Thompson test is performed with the patient prone and the feet extending beyond the examination table. Squeezing the gastrocnemius-soleus complex normally produces passive plantar flexion. Failure of the foot to plantar-flex indicates disruption of the Achilles tendon and strongly supports the diagnosis.

A is correct because absent plantar flexion during calf compression is a positive Thompson test, the key bedside maneuver for suspected Achilles tendon rupture.

B is incorrect because increased anterior translation during the anterior drawer test suggests injury to the anterior talofibular ligament, typically caused by an inversion ankle injury.

C is incorrect because pain near the distal tibiofibular syndesmosis during calf compression is a positive squeeze test, which supports a high ankle sprain.

D is incorrect because excessive inversion during the talar tilt test suggests injury to the calcaneofibular ligament or other lateral ankle stabilizers.

E is incorrect because reproduction of plantar heel pain with passive great-toe extension describes the windlass test, which is associated with plantar fasciopathy.

Question 209  [ID: 9e334802dc464eaba026ab5f4f182396]
A 12-year-old girl is brought to the clinic after her parent notices that one shoulder is higher than the other. She has no pain, weakness, sensory changes, or bowel or bladder dysfunction. Menarche occurred 4 months ago. Examination shows elevation of the right shoulder and prominence of the right posterior ribs during forward bending. Neurologic examination is normal. Standing posteroanterior radiography demonstrates a 29-degree lateral curvature with vertebral rotation. The Risser grade is 1. Which of the following is the most appropriate intervention to reduce the risk of curve progression?

A. Observation with repeat standing radiography in 12 months
B. Full-time thoracolumbosacral bracing with periodic clinical and radiographic monitoring
C. Physical therapy alone with core-strengthening exercises
D. Immediate operative correction with posterior spinal fusion
E. Strict activity restriction until skeletal maturity

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has adolescent idiopathic scoliosis with a 29-degree curve and substantial remaining skeletal growth, as indicated by a Risser grade of 1 and recent menarche. Curves measuring approximately 25 to 40 degrees in skeletally immature patients are generally managed with thoracolumbosacral bracing. Bracing does not reliably reverse the existing curvature, but it can reduce the risk of progression to a magnitude that would require surgery. Periodic clinical evaluation and standing radiographs are necessary to monitor progression. Choice A is incorrect because observation alone is generally appropriate for curves less than approximately 20 to 25 degrees; waiting 12 months in this high-risk patient could allow significant progression. Choice C is incorrect because exercise and physical therapy may improve conditioning, posture, and symptoms but have not replaced bracing as the primary intervention for a moderate curve in a growing patient. Choice D is incorrect because operative correction is generally reserved for progressive curves approaching or exceeding 45 to 50 degrees or for selected severe deformities. Choice E is incorrect because routine activity restriction does not prevent progression; patients should generally remain physically active unless another contraindication is present.

Question 210  [ID: 9e42469e8a7244c8a72b68daa80a923f]
A 62-year-old man with poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus and peripheral neuropathy presents with 3 weeks of increasing drainage from an ulcer beneath the right first metatarsal head. He reports malaise but denies severe pain. His temperature is 38.1°C (100.6°F), blood pressure is 132/78 mm Hg, and pulse is 96/min. Examination shows a 2.5-cm plantar ulcer with surrounding erythema and purulent drainage. A sterile metal probe advanced through the ulcer contacts a hard, gritty surface. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 13,200/mm3, erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 92 mm/hr, and C-reactive protein level of 11 mg/dL. MRI demonstrates a sinus tract extending from the ulcer to the first metatarsal, with cortical disruption, marrow edema, and a 1.5-cm adjacent fluid collection. Blood cultures are obtained. Which of the following is the most appropriate clinical intervention?

A. Apply topical mupirocin and perform daily dressing changes
B. Begin oral cephalexin for 5 days without additional procedures
C. Perform urgent surgical drainage and debridement with deep tissue or osseous cultures, followed by culture-directed antimicrobial therapy
D. Administer an intra-articular glucocorticoid injection
E. Recommend strict off-loading and reassess after 4 weeks without antimicrobial therapy

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The chronic draining ulcer, positive probe-to-hard-surface finding, markedly elevated inflammatory markers, and MRI evidence of cortical disruption and marrow involvement indicate a deep infection involving the first metatarsal. The adjacent fluid collection and devitalized tissue require source control through surgical drainage and debridement. Deep tissue or osseous specimens should be obtained for culture because superficial swab cultures may not identify the causative organism accurately. Antimicrobial therapy should then be tailored to culture results and is generally prolonged; empiric systemic therapy should not be delayed in a clinically infected patient after appropriate cultures have been collected. A is incorrect because topical therapy cannot adequately penetrate or eradicate a deep-seated infection. B is incorrect because a short course of oral cephalexin is inadequate for cortical and marrow involvement, may not cover likely polymicrobial pathogens, and does not address the abscess or necrotic tissue. D is incorrect because glucocorticoid injection is contraindicated in the presence of active infection and could worsen it. E is incorrect because off-loading is an important adjunct for ulcer healing but is not sufficient when there is purulence, systemic inflammation, deep structural involvement, and an adjacent fluid collection.

Question 211  [ID: a1dfc2e92b9f4f43bbb55733a3a41cda]
A 48-year-old man presents with 6 weeks of progressively worsening bilateral calf pain, fatigue, and unintentional weight loss. The pain is present at rest and worsens with walking. He also reports episodic abdominal pain after meals and new numbness of the right foot. His medical history is significant for untreated chronic hepatitis B infection. His temperature is 38.1°C (100.6°F), blood pressure is 168/96 mm Hg, and pulse is 92/min. Examination shows tender calf muscles, retiform violaceous patches on both legs, and decreased right ankle dorsiflexion with diminished sensation over the dorsum of the foot. Laboratory studies show an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate and serum creatinine of 1.8 mg/dL. Urinalysis reveals mild proteinuria without red blood cell casts. Antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibody testing is negative. Abdominal angiography demonstrates multiple small aneurysms and irregular areas of narrowing in the renal and mesenteric arteries. Which of the following pathologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's findings?

A. Granulomatous inflammation of large arterial walls with fragmentation of the internal elastic lamina
B. Transmural necrotizing inflammation of medium-sized arteries with fibrinoid necrosis and lesions of varying ages
C. Pauci-immune necrotizing inflammation of small vessels with frequent glomerular capillary involvement
D. Noninflammatory thrombosis of arteries and veins caused by antiphospholipid antibodies
E. Eosinophil-rich granulomatous inflammation associated with asthma and peripheral eosinophilia

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. This patient has polyarteritis nodosa, characterized by segmental, transmural necrotizing inflammation of medium-sized muscular arteries. Fibrinoid necrosis weakens the arterial wall, producing microaneurysms, while healing and intimal proliferation cause irregular narrowing and tissue ischemia. Lesions at different stages of development are typically present simultaneously. Ischemia involving arteries that supply skeletal muscle can cause severe myalgias and calf tenderness; involvement of peripheral nerves can cause mononeuritis multiplex, such as foot drop. Renal arterial disease causes hypertension and renal dysfunction without glomerulonephritis, explaining the absence of red blood cell casts. Mesenteric involvement causes postprandial abdominal pain. Hepatitis B infection is a recognized association, often involving immune-complex deposition.

A is incorrect because granulomatous inflammation of large arteries with disruption of the elastic lamina is characteristic of giant cell arteritis or Takayasu arteritis. These disorders do not typically produce widespread medium-artery microaneurysms.

C is incorrect because pauci-immune small-vessel inflammation is characteristic of ANCA-associated conditions. These commonly involve glomerular capillaries, resulting in hematuria and red blood cell casts, and may involve pulmonary capillaries. This patient's negative ANCA testing, spared glomeruli, and medium-artery aneurysms argue against this mechanism.

D is incorrect because antiphospholipid antibody syndrome causes a noninflammatory hypercoagulable state with arterial or venous thrombosis. It does not characteristically cause systemic inflammation, fibrinoid arterial necrosis, or multiple microaneurysms.

E is incorrect because eosinophil-rich granulomatous inflammation is associated with eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis. That condition typically occurs in patients with asthma, chronic rhinosinusitis, and marked eosinophilia, none of which are present here.

Question 212  [ID: a429e0e6b0834cfcb7d6e8c77fdcd954]
A 68-year-old man presents with 18 hours of rapidly worsening right knee pain, swelling, and inability to bear weight. He denies trauma. His medical history includes type 2 diabetes mellitus and end-stage renal disease treated with hemodialysis. His temperature is 38.7°C (101.7°F), blood pressure is 108/66 mm Hg, pulse is 104/min, and respirations are 18/min. The right knee is diffusely swollen, warm, and erythematous with a palpable effusion. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 15,800/mm3, an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 78 mm/hr, and an elevated C-reactive protein level. Plain radiographs show an effusion without fracture or chondrocalcinosis. Which of the following additional physical examination findings would most strongly indicate that the primary abnormality is within the knee rather than in the overlying tissues?

A. Focal fluctuance directly anterior to the patella with preserved passive range of motion
B. Pain localized to the tibial tubercle during resisted knee extension
C. Severe pain and marked restriction during both active and passive range of motion
D. Increased anterior translation of the tibia during Lachman testing
E. Tenderness along the medial joint line with pain during McMurray testing

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The correct answer is C. Severe pain and marked restriction with both active and passive range of motion strongly indicate an intra-articular process. In septic arthritis, inflammation and increased pressure within the joint capsule make even gentle passive motion intensely painful. The acute atraumatic onset, fever, inability to bear weight, effusion, elevated inflammatory markers, diabetes, and hemodialysis further heighten concern for septic arthritis and warrant urgent arthrocentesis. A is incorrect because focal prepatellar fluctuance with preserved passive motion suggests prepatellar bursitis, a periarticular condition. B is incorrect because pain at the tibial tubercle during resisted extension is more consistent with pathology involving the patellar tendon or its insertion. D is incorrect because increased anterior translation on Lachman testing suggests an anterior cruciate ligament injury, which is typically associated with trauma and does not explain the systemic findings. E is incorrect because medial joint-line tenderness and a positive McMurray test suggest a meniscal injury; these findings may cause mechanical pain or locking but do not typically produce fever, diffuse warmth, and markedly elevated inflammatory markers.

Question 213  [ID: a62bbaee17d34450aea4e0c4d2dedd3a]
A 27-year-old woman presents with 4 months of intermittent aching in the right shoulder and paresthesias along the medial forearm and fourth and fifth fingers. Her symptoms worsen while swimming, carrying a backpack, or working with her arm above her head. She has recently noticed reduced grip endurance but denies trauma, fever, or joint swelling. Examination shows mild weakness of finger abduction and decreased sensation along the medial forearm. The radial pulse is symmetric at rest. Spurling testing and percussion over the cubital tunnel are negative. A complete blood count and metabolic panel are normal. Cervical spine radiographs show no acute abnormalities. Which of the following physical examination maneuvers would be most appropriate to further evaluate the suspected cause of her symptoms?

A. Abduct and externally rotate both shoulders to 90 degrees, flex the elbows to 90 degrees, and repeatedly open and close the hands
B. Extend and laterally rotate the neck while applying downward axial pressure to the head
C. Flex the affected elbow maximally and maintain the position for several minutes
D. Flex the wrist while pressing the dorsal surfaces of both hands together
E. Passively flex, abduct, and externally rotate the hip while stabilizing the opposite side of the pelvis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. The elevated arm stress test, also called the Roos test, is performed with the shoulders abducted and externally rotated and the elbows flexed to 90 degrees while the patient repeatedly opens and closes the hands. Reproduction of upper-extremity pain, paresthesias, heaviness, or weakness supports thoracic outlet syndrome. This patient's symptoms are provoked by overhead activity and involve a lower brachial plexus distribution, particularly C8-T1, which is characteristic of neurogenic compression at the thoracic outlet.

B is incorrect because this describes the Spurling test, which evaluates cervical nerve root compression. It is already negative in this patient, and cervical radiculopathy is less likely given the strong association with overhead arm positioning.

C is incorrect because sustained elbow flexion is used to evaluate ulnar nerve entrapment at the cubital tunnel. Cubital tunnel syndrome can cause paresthesias in the fourth and fifth fingers, but it does not typically cause symptoms that are specifically worsened by shoulder elevation or overhead activity.

D is incorrect because this describes the Phalen maneuver, which evaluates median nerve compression at the wrist. Median nerve compression typically affects the thumb, index finger, middle finger, and radial half of the ring finger rather than the medial forearm and fifth finger.

E is incorrect because this describes the FABER test, which assesses the hip and sacroiliac region and is unrelated to the patient's upper-extremity symptoms.

Question 214  [ID: a65b77ead4824636863768761de8954b]
A 34-year-old warehouse employee presents with 2 days of lower back pain that began immediately after lifting a heavy box while twisting. The pain is aching, remains localized, and worsens with bending, rotation, and prolonged standing. He denies fever, weight loss, urinary symptoms, lower-extremity weakness, saddle numbness, and bowel or bladder dysfunction. Examination shows a mildly limited range of motion due to pain and tenderness with palpable tightness over the bilateral paraspinal soft tissues. There is no focal midline tenderness or step-off. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes are normal, and straight-leg raising is negative bilaterally. Urinalysis is normal. Plain radiographs show preserved vertebral body heights and alignment without acute osseous abnormalities. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Acute lumbar musculoligamentous strain
B. Lumbar intervertebral disc herniation
C. Vertebral compression fracture
D. Cauda equina syndrome
E. Acute pyelonephritis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Acute lumbar musculoligamentous strain is the most likely diagnosis. The pain began after lifting with rotation, is localized, is exacerbated by movement, and is accompanied by paraspinal tenderness and muscle tightness without neurologic deficits or abnormal radiographic findings. Lumbar intervertebral disc herniation typically causes radicular pain, sensory changes, weakness, reflex abnormalities, or a positive straight-leg-raising test. Vertebral compression fracture is more likely after significant trauma or in a patient with osteoporosis and generally produces focal midline tenderness and vertebral height loss on imaging. Cauda equina syndrome presents with severe neurologic findings such as saddle anesthesia, urinary retention or incontinence, bowel dysfunction, and lower-extremity weakness. Acute pyelonephritis typically causes fever, flank pain, costovertebral angle tenderness, urinary symptoms, and an abnormal urinalysis.

Question 215  [ID: a8651816199c4986ab3601cdf47a85cf]
A 16-year-old boy presents with 4 months of progressively worsening focal pain in the right lower leg. The pain is most severe at night and frequently awakens him from sleep. He reports substantial relief within 30 minutes of taking over-the-counter ibuprofen. He denies trauma, fever, weight loss, or recent illness. Examination reveals focal tenderness over the midshaft of the right tibia without erythema, warmth, or a palpable mass. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are within normal limits. Plain radiographs demonstrate localized cortical thickening and surrounding sclerosis. Noncontrast CT reveals a well-circumscribed 7-mm radiolucent focus with central mineralization. The patient and his parents elect to defer percutaneous intervention. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. Scheduled naproxen therapy
B. Weekly oral alendronate therapy
C. Oral prednisone therapy
D. Intravenous cefazolin therapy
E. Subcutaneous methotrexate therapy

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This presentation is characteristic of an osteoid osteoma, a benign bone-forming neoplasm commonly occurring in adolescents and young adults. The classic findings are focal nocturnal pain that improves dramatically with nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs and CT evidence of a small radiolucent nidus, often with central mineralization and surrounding reactive sclerosis. The lesion produces high levels of prostaglandins, which stimulate local nociceptors and cause pain. Scheduled NSAID therapy, such as naproxen, inhibits cyclooxygenase-mediated prostaglandin synthesis and is an appropriate pharmacologic option when symptoms are controlled and the patient defers definitive treatment. Percutaneous radiofrequency ablation is often preferred for persistent or intolerable symptoms. Alendronate is used for conditions involving excessive bone resorption, such as osteoporosis, and does not address the prostaglandin-mediated pain in this case. Prednisone is not indicated and may cause substantial adverse effects with prolonged use. Cefazolin would be appropriate for certain bacterial infections, but the absence of fever, inflammatory marker elevation, and destructive imaging findings argues against infection. Methotrexate is used for selected inflammatory and neoplastic disorders but has no role in treating this lesion.

Question 216  [ID: a899173905f04fea8c8aa495ecc60056]
A 27-year-old man presents with 9 months of progressive lower back and buttock pain. His pain is most severe in the early morning, is associated with approximately 1 hour of stiffness, and improves with exercise but not with rest. He occasionally awakens during the second half of the night because of pain. He denies preceding trauma, fever, weight loss, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and limb weakness. Examination shows reduced forward flexion of the lumbar spine, limited chest expansion, and tenderness over both sacroiliac joints. Muscle strength, sensation, and deep tendon reflexes are normal. Laboratory studies show an elevated C-reactive protein level, negative rheumatoid factor, and normal renal function. Pelvic radiographs demonstrate bilateral sacroiliac joint erosions and subchondral sclerosis. Which of the following is the best initial pharmacologic intervention for this patient?

A. Scheduled treatment with a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug
B. Long-term oral prednisone therapy
C. Weekly oral methotrexate therapy
D. Immediate initiation of adalimumab therapy
E. Long-term opioid analgesic therapy

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient's young age, chronic inflammatory lower back pain, prolonged morning stiffness, improvement with activity, reduced spinal mobility, limited chest expansion, and bilateral sacroiliac erosions are characteristic of ankylosing spondylitis. A scheduled nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug is the preferred initial pharmacologic treatment. NSAIDs reduce pain and stiffness and should generally be combined with regular exercise and physical therapy to preserve posture, spinal mobility, and chest expansion. Long-term systemic glucocorticoids are not recommended because their benefit for axial symptoms is limited and chronic toxicity is substantial. Methotrexate may help selected patients with peripheral joint involvement but is generally ineffective for predominantly axial disease. A tumor necrosis factor inhibitor such as adalimumab is appropriate when active symptoms persist despite an adequate trial of NSAIDs and nonpharmacologic therapy; it is not usually the initial intervention. Long-term opioid therapy does not address the underlying inflammatory process and carries risks of dependence, tolerance, and adverse effects.

Question 217  [ID: aa66f78bdff14ea0b4e8347b66246542]
A 52-year-old woman presents with several months of fatigue, intermittent pain and morning stiffness in both hands, a persistent gritty sensation in her eyes, and difficulty swallowing dry foods without drinking water. She has developed three dental caries during the past year despite no prior history of significant dental disease. Examination shows reduced salivary pooling, bilateral nontender parotid enlargement, and tenderness of several metacarpophalangeal joints without swelling. Schirmer testing demonstrates decreased tear production. Laboratory studies reveal a positive antinuclear antibody and elevated anti-Ro/SSA and anti-La/SSB antibodies. Which of the following patient-education measures is most appropriate to reduce her risk of further dental complications?

A. Use topical fluoride regularly, maintain meticulous oral hygiene, and receive routine dental examinations
B. Use an alcohol-containing mouthwash several times daily to reduce oral bacterial growth
C. Suck on sugar-containing acidic candies throughout the day to stimulate saliva production
D. Take prophylactic oral antibiotics before routine dental cleanings
E. Limit fluid intake between meals to decrease parotid gland stimulation

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Sjögren syndrome causes lymphocytic dysfunction and destruction of the salivary glands, resulting in xerostomia. Reduced salivary flow diminishes oral buffering, lubrication, and antimicrobial activity, substantially increasing the risk of dental caries, periodontal disease, and oral candidiasis. Preventive counseling should emphasize meticulous oral hygiene, regular dental examinations, topical fluoride use, adequate hydration, avoidance of tobacco and excess sugar, and use of sugar-free gum or lozenges to stimulate residual salivary flow. Alcohol-free mouth rinses and saliva substitutes may also be helpful.

B is incorrect because alcohol-containing mouthwash can further dry and irritate the oral mucosa, potentially worsening symptoms and dental risk.

C is incorrect because sugar-containing candies promote dental caries, while acidic candies can contribute to enamel erosion. Sugar-free gum or lozenges, preferably containing xylitol, are better options for stimulating saliva.

D is incorrect because prophylactic antibiotics are not indicated before routine dental procedures solely because of this condition. Antibiotic prophylaxis is reserved for selected patients with specific high-risk cardiac conditions.

E is incorrect because adequate hydration helps relieve oral dryness. Fluid restriction would worsen xerostomia and increase the risk of dental complications.
