Question 218  [ID: aa73559153ca4bacbf509fcccfff52f1]
A 29-year-old woman presents with 8 months of intermittent aching in the right shoulder and arm accompanied by numbness of the fourth and fifth fingers. Symptoms worsen when she stocks shelves overhead at work and improve when she lowers the arm. She denies trauma, fever, or joint swelling. Examination reveals mild weakness of right finger abduction and decreased sensation along the medial forearm and hand. Symptoms are reproduced by sustained elevation of both arms. Radial pulses remain symmetric, and there is no arm discoloration or edema. Complete blood count, metabolic panel, and erythrocyte sedimentation rate are normal. Plain radiographs show no acute abnormality or accessory rib, and cervical spine MRI shows no disc herniation or neural foraminal narrowing. Which of the following diagnostic studies is most appropriate to objectively localize the suspected neurologic dysfunction?

A. Electromyography with nerve conduction studies
B. Duplex ultrasonography of the upper-extremity veins
C. CT angiography of the chest with the arm elevated
D. MRI of the rotator cuff
E. Serum creatine kinase measurement

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Electromyography with nerve conduction studies is the most appropriate study to objectively evaluate and localize neurologic dysfunction in this patient. Her position-dependent pain and paresthesias, sensory changes in the medial forearm and hand, and weakness of finger abduction suggest involvement of the lower brachial plexus, particularly the C8-T1 fibers. Electrodiagnostic testing can identify a lower-trunk plexopathy and help distinguish it from cervical radiculopathy, ulnar neuropathy, or other peripheral nerve disorders. A normal study does not completely exclude intermittent neurologic compression, but it is the best option provided for objective localization. Duplex ultrasonography of the upper-extremity veins is indicated when venous obstruction is suspected, typically in a patient with unilateral arm swelling, cyanosis, venous distention, or thrombosis. CT angiography with provocative positioning is more appropriate for ischemic findings such as diminished pulses, pallor, coolness, or digital embolic changes. MRI of the rotator cuff evaluates structural shoulder pathology but would not explain the medial forearm sensory loss and intrinsic hand weakness. Serum creatine kinase may be elevated in primary muscle injury or inflammatory myopathy but does not localize a focal neurologic lesion.

Question 219  [ID: aac6cf3ee1ad4fc8850d9548548ceae3]
A 15-year-old boy is brought by his parents because of a gradually increasing rounded appearance of his upper back over the past year. He denies pain, weakness, paresthesias, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and difficulty breathing. He spends several hours daily using a computer and participates in little physical activity. Examination shows prominent forward rounding of the upper back that largely corrects when he stands upright on command and with passive extension. Strength, sensation, reflexes, and gait are normal. Standing lateral radiographs demonstrate a 48-degree curvature without vertebral wedging, endplate irregularities, or congenital abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate counseling and preventive management for this patient?

A. Encourage postural training, upper-back extensor strengthening, regular physical activity, and periodic clinical reassessment
B. Begin full-time rigid bracing until skeletal maturity regardless of progression
C. Recommend strict avoidance of sports and physical education activities
D. Refer immediately for operative correction to prevent neurologic compromise
E. Prescribe long-term calcium and vitamin D supplementation as the primary intervention

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has flexible postural kyphosis, suggested by mild increased upper-back curvature that corrects with active and passive extension and the absence of vertebral wedging or endplate abnormalities on imaging. The appropriate preventive approach is education regarding posture, regular physical activity, extension-strengthening exercises, stretching when indicated, and periodic reassessment for progression or development of pain or neurologic symptoms. These measures promote postural awareness and conditioning while avoiding unnecessary intervention. Choice B is incorrect because rigid bracing is generally reserved for selected skeletally immature patients with progressive, structurally fixed deformity, such as Scheuermann-type changes, rather than a mild flexible curve. Choice C is incorrect because activity restriction is unnecessary; exercise and conditioning are generally encouraged. Choice D is incorrect because surgery is reserved for severe, progressive, rigid deformity associated with refractory pain, cardiopulmonary impairment, neurologic compromise, or major functional or cosmetic concerns. Choice E is incorrect because calcium and vitamin D do not correct a flexible postural deformity and are not the primary management in an otherwise healthy adolescent without evidence of deficiency or reduced bone density.

Question 220  [ID: ac27244add114048b1eb8de1cdfd4510]
A 46-year-old woman presents with 4 weeks of aching lower lumbar pain that worsens with prolonged standing and extension and improves with sitting. She denies recent trauma, fever, weight loss, bowel or bladder dysfunction, saddle anesthesia, and lower-extremity weakness. She has no history of gastrointestinal bleeding, kidney disease, or medication allergies. Examination reveals localized lower lumbar tenderness and pain with extension. Lower-extremity strength is 5/5 bilaterally, sensation is intact, reflexes are symmetric, and straight-leg raise testing is negative. A standing lateral radiograph demonstrates 20% anterior translation of L4 relative to L5 without fracture or destructive changes. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy for this patient's pain while activity modification and supervised rehabilitation are initiated?

A. Naproxen
B. Gabapentin
C. Oral prednisone
D. Oxycodone
E. Alendronate

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The radiographic anterior translation of one vertebral body relative to the adjacent vertebra, together with extension-related axial pain and a normal neurologic examination, is consistent with low-grade spondylolisthesis without a neurologic emergency. Initial management is conservative and includes activity modification, physical therapy emphasizing stabilization and flexibility, and a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as naproxen for short-term analgesic and anti-inflammatory therapy when no renal, gastrointestinal, allergic, or other contraindications are present. Gabapentin is primarily used for neuropathic pain and is not indicated for isolated axial pain without radicular symptoms. Systemic prednisone is not routinely recommended because evidence of benefit is limited and adverse effects may outweigh benefit. Oxycodone is inappropriate as initial therapy for uncomplicated, nonmalignant pain due to risks of sedation, dependence, and overdose. Alendronate treats osteoporosis and does not provide appropriate symptomatic therapy for this condition.

Question 221  [ID: ac75b24cf5ec4013acb672cbf47bfa09]
A 68-year-old man presents with sudden pain and swelling above his right knee after stepping off a curb. He reports hearing a “pop” and is now unable to straighten the affected leg or bear weight. Five days ago, he began treatment for acute pyelonephritis. His medical history includes type 2 diabetes mellitus, hypertension, and hyperlipidemia. Current medications are levofloxacin, metformin, lisinopril, atorvastatin, and acetaminophen as needed. Examination shows a palpable defect immediately superior to the patella, a large effusion, and inability to perform a straight-leg raise. Radiographs show no acute fracture but demonstrate inferior displacement of the patella. Ultrasonography reveals discontinuity of the fibrillar structure above the patella. Which of the following medications should be discontinued immediately because of its association with this presentation?

A. Atorvastatin
B. Levofloxacin
C. Lisinopril
D. Metformin
E. Acetaminophen

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Levofloxacin should be discontinued immediately. Fluoroquinolones carry a boxed warning for tendinitis and tendon rupture. The risk is increased in adults older than 60 years, patients with renal disease or transplants, and those receiving systemic glucocorticoids. Although the Achilles tendon is affected most often, other tendons, including the structure above the patella responsible for active knee extension, may also be involved. Symptoms can occur during treatment or months after exposure. This patient’s sudden pain, palpable suprapatellar defect, inability to perform a straight-leg raise, inferior patellar displacement, and ultrasonographic discontinuity indicate a complete disruption requiring immobilization and urgent surgical consultation in addition to stopping the offending drug. Atorvastatin can cause myalgias, myopathy, and rarely rhabdomyolysis, but it is not characteristically associated with this focal structural disruption. Lisinopril may cause cough, angioedema, hyperkalemia, or kidney dysfunction but does not explain these findings. Metformin is associated primarily with gastrointestinal effects and, rarely, lactic acidosis. Acetaminophen can cause dose-dependent hepatotoxicity but is not associated with tendon injury.

Question 222  [ID: acd49f7fd2114341a57aaa17ff35907b]
A 68-year-old man with type 2 diabetes mellitus and chronic kidney disease presents after stumbling while descending a staircase. He reports that he forcefully contracted his right thigh to prevent a fall, felt a sudden pop above the kneecap, and immediately developed pain and difficulty walking. Examination shows swelling and ecchymosis superior to the patella, a palpable defect approximately 2 cm above its upper border, and inability to actively extend the knee or perform a straight-leg raise. Passive range of motion is intact. Lateral radiography demonstrates inferior displacement of the patella without acute fracture. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Anterior cruciate ligament tear
B. Patellar tendon rupture
C. Quadriceps tendon rupture
D. Patellar fracture
E. Meniscal tear

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The most likely diagnosis is a quadriceps tendon rupture. This injury commonly occurs in adults older than 40 years after a sudden eccentric contraction of the quadriceps with the knee flexed. Diabetes mellitus and chronic kidney disease increase the risk of tendon degeneration. Key findings include a palpable suprapatellar defect, inability to actively extend the knee or perform a straight-leg raise, and inferior patellar displacement (patella baja) on lateral radiography. An anterior cruciate ligament tear typically causes rapid hemarthrosis, instability, and positive Lachman testing but does not usually abolish active knee extension. A patellar tendon rupture produces a palpable defect below the patella and superior displacement of the patella (patella alta). A displaced patellar fracture can disrupt active extension, but radiography would demonstrate the fracture. A meniscal tear causes joint-line pain, mechanical locking or catching, and provocative test findings without a palpable suprapatellar defect or loss of the straight-leg raise.

Question 223  [ID: ad01f38a55bd410397690cfc9d5f5e67]
A 48-year-old woman presents with 6 months of aching and morning stiffness in both hands and wrists. The stiffness lasts approximately 45 minutes, but she has not noticed persistent joint swelling. She also reports a gritty, burning sensation in both eyes and difficulty swallowing crackers unless she drinks water. She has developed several dental caries during the past year. Examination shows mild tenderness of the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints without synovitis or deformity. Oral examination reveals dry, erythematous mucosa and scant salivary pooling. Laboratory studies show a positive antinuclear antibody and antibodies to Ro/SSA. Which of the following additional physical examination findings is most likely in this patient?

A. Bilateral, nontender enlargement of the parotid glands
B. Fixed flexion deformities of the distal interphalangeal joints
C. Erythematous plaques with silvery scale over the extensor surfaces
D. Painless oral ulcers involving the hard palate
E. Tophaceous deposits along the helix of the ear

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's keratoconjunctivitis sicca symptoms, xerostomia, increased dental caries, nonerosive inflammatory arthralgias, and anti-Ro/SSA antibodies are characteristic of Sjögren syndrome. Chronic lymphocytic infiltration of the salivary glands can cause bilateral, typically nontender parotid gland enlargement. This is an important examination finding and may be recurrent. Persistent or asymmetric gland enlargement warrants evaluation for lymphoma, for which affected patients are at increased risk. Choice B describes fixed joint deformities and is not typical of the usually nonerosive arthropathy associated with Sjögren syndrome. Choice C suggests psoriasis and would support psoriatic arthritis. Choice D is more characteristic of systemic lupus erythematosus; although secondary Sjögren syndrome may occur with lupus, painless hard-palate ulcers are not the most likely additional finding in this presentation. Choice E indicates chronic tophaceous gout due to monosodium urate deposition and does not explain the prominent ocular and oral dryness.

Question 224  [ID: ae30dd97b6aa43ffb44d93a2a33f4ee0]
A 48-year-old woman presents with 4 months of progressively worsening difficulty climbing stairs, rising from a chair, and placing objects on high shelves. She denies diplopia, sensory changes, joint pain, dysphagia, or symptoms that fluctuate throughout the day. She takes no prescription medications. Examination demonstrates symmetric 3/5 strength with shoulder abduction and hip flexion, with preserved distal strength, sensation, and deep tendon reflexes. No rash or other cutaneous abnormalities are present. Serum creatine kinase is 6,200 U/L, and aldolase and aminotransferase levels are elevated. Electromyography shows short-duration, low-amplitude, polyphasic motor unit potentials with spontaneous fibrillation potentials. Biopsy of the left quadriceps demonstrates endomysial mononuclear infiltration composed predominantly of CD8-positive T lymphocytes invading non-necrotic fibers. There is no perifascicular atrophy or rimmed vacuole formation. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Dermatomyositis
B. Inclusion body myositis
C. Myasthenia gravis
D. Polymyositis
E. Polymyalgia rheumatica

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: Polymyositis is characterized by subacute, symmetric proximal weakness, markedly elevated creatine kinase, myopathic electromyographic findings, and endomysial inflammation with CD8-positive T cells invading otherwise non-necrotic fibers. The absence of cutaneous findings and perifascicular atrophy helps distinguish it from dermatomyositis. Dermatomyositis typically causes characteristic skin findings, such as a heliotrope eruption or Gottron papules, and biopsy demonstrates perifascicular atrophy with predominantly perivascular or perifascicular inflammation. Inclusion body myositis usually affects adults older than 50 years, progresses slowly, and often causes asymmetric weakness involving the finger flexors and quadriceps; biopsy shows rimmed vacuoles. Myasthenia gravis causes fatigable weakness that may involve ocular or bulbar function, but sensation remains intact and creatine kinase is generally normal. Polymyalgia rheumatica occurs in adults older than 50 years and causes proximal aching and morning stiffness rather than objective weakness; creatine kinase levels are typically normal.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Question 225  [ID: b09059e95c854574a0c405346d4161ce]
A 44-year-old woman presents with 8 months of diffuse aching involving both sides of her body, fatigue, nonrestorative sleep, and difficulty concentrating. Her symptoms have persisted despite regular low-impact exercise, sleep-hygiene measures, and stress reduction. She also reports depressed mood and loss of interest but denies fever, weight loss, joint swelling, or prolonged morning stiffness. Examination shows tenderness to palpation in multiple regions without synovitis, deformity, erythema, or objective weakness. Complete blood count, comprehensive metabolic panel, thyroid-stimulating hormone, creatine kinase, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein are within normal limits. Which of the following medications is the most appropriate pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. Duloxetine
B. Methotrexate
C. Naproxen
D. Oxycodone
E. Prednisone

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has fibromyalgia, characterized by chronic widespread pain accompanied by fatigue, nonrestorative sleep, and cognitive symptoms in the absence of inflammatory, endocrine, or primary muscle abnormalities. Duloxetine is a serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitor approved for fibromyalgia and can reduce pain while also treating her comorbid depressive symptoms. It is appropriate when nonpharmacologic measures alone have been insufficient. Methotrexate is a disease-modifying antirheumatic drug used for inflammatory disorders such as rheumatoid arthritis; this patient has no synovitis or elevated inflammatory markers. Naproxen may help nociceptive pain from inflammatory or structural conditions but generally has limited benefit for the centrally mediated pain in fibromyalgia. Oxycodone and other opioids are not recommended because they have poor long-term efficacy and carry risks of dependence, overdose, and opioid-induced hyperalgesia. Prednisone is not indicated because there is no evidence of an inflammatory process, and long-term glucocorticoid therapy would expose the patient to substantial adverse effects without meaningful benefit.

Question 226  [ID: b0b05f40dd1c4b65971ea1a0212ed8ce]
A 27-year-old woman presents with 4 months of fatigue, intermittent low-grade fevers, and pain and morning stiffness involving both wrists, several metacarpophalangeal joints, and both knees. Her symptoms are worse after sun exposure. She has no history of thrombosis, pregnancy loss, seizures, or renal disease. Physical examination shows a faint erythematous eruption over the cheeks that spares the nasolabial folds, two painless oral ulcers, and tenderness with mild swelling of the wrists and metacarpophalangeal joints. No joint deformities are present. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 3,200/mm3, a positive antinuclear antibody titer of 1:640, elevated anti-double-stranded DNA antibody levels, and low serum C3 and C4 concentrations. Serum creatinine and urinalysis are normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate long-term pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. Allopurinol
B. Hydroxychloroquine
C. Infliximab
D. Methotrexate
E. Prednisone 60 mg daily

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Hydroxychloroquine is the preferred long-term foundational therapy for this patient's autoimmune inflammatory condition, particularly when manifestations include constitutional symptoms, photosensitive skin findings, oral ulcers, cytopenia, and nonerosive inflammatory polyarticular symptoms without major renal or neurologic involvement. It decreases disease activity and flare frequency and improves long-term survival. Before or soon after initiation, the patient should receive a baseline ophthalmologic examination because cumulative exposure can cause retinal toxicity; dosing should generally not exceed 5 mg/kg/day of actual body weight. Allopurinol is used to reduce uric acid in patients with gout and does not treat this presentation. Infliximab is a tumor necrosis factor inhibitor used for selected inflammatory diseases but is not standard therapy here and may provoke autoantibody formation or a drug-induced autoimmune syndrome. Methotrexate can be added for persistent inflammatory joint or skin symptoms that do not respond adequately to foundational therapy, but it is not the preferred initial long-term agent in this uncomplicated presentation. High-dose prednisone is reserved for severe or organ-threatening manifestations; prolonged use at this dose carries substantial risks, including infection, osteoporosis, hyperglycemia, hypertension, and avascular necrosis.

Question 227  [ID: b177cc3fddad48299c894f528a92f66d]
A 42-year-old warehouse worker presents with 5 days of severe lower back pain that began immediately after lifting a heavy box. The pain radiates from the right buttock down the lateral leg to the dorsum of the foot and is worsened by coughing, sneezing, and prolonged sitting. He denies fever, weight loss, urinary retention, fecal incontinence, and perineal numbness. Examination reveals decreased sensation over the first dorsal web space of the right foot and weakness of right great-toe extension. Patellar and Achilles reflexes are symmetric. Passive elevation of the extended right leg reproduces the radiating pain at 40 degrees. Plain radiographs show no fracture, destructive lesion, or vertebral displacement. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Lumbar disk herniation at L4-L5 causing L5 radiculopathy
B. Lumbar spinal stenosis causing neurogenic claudication
C. Piriformis syndrome causing sciatic nerve compression
D. Isthmic spondylolisthesis at L5-S1
E. Osteoporotic vertebral compression fracture

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The acute onset after heavy lifting, radicular pain extending to the dorsum of the foot, pain exacerbation with coughing or sneezing, and a positive straight-leg raise test indicate nerve root irritation from lumbar disk herniation. Weakness of great-toe extension and sensory loss in the first dorsal web space localize the deficit to the L5 nerve root, which is most commonly compressed by an L4-L5 disk herniation. Lumbar spinal stenosis typically occurs in older adults and causes exertional bilateral leg discomfort relieved by sitting or lumbar flexion. Piriformis syndrome may produce buttock and posterior leg pain but does not typically cause back pain exacerbated by Valsalva maneuvers or a dermatomal L5 motor and sensory deficit. Isthmic spondylolisthesis is often associated with repetitive lumbar hyperextension and would generally be evident as vertebral displacement on radiographs. A vertebral compression fracture is more likely in an older patient with osteoporosis or significant trauma and would cause focal axial pain rather than a clear L5 radiculopathy.

Question 228  [ID: b1b30737f0aa47b5ac17be0d4dc61cfd]
A 24-year-old man is preparing for discharge to an inpatient rehabilitation facility after diving into a shallow pool 3 weeks ago. He reports persistent inability to move his legs and loss of sensation below the umbilicus. Examination shows intact upper-extremity strength, 0/5 strength in both lower extremities, absent lower-extremity sensation, and urinary retention requiring intermittent catheterization. MRI obtained after the incident demonstrated anterior displacement of T11 relative to T12 with associated edema and disruption of adjacent neural tissue. His vital signs and laboratory results are currently normal. He will use a wheelchair for mobility. Which of the following instructions is most important for reducing his risk of pressure-related skin injury?

A. Perform wheelchair pressure-relief maneuvers every 15 to 30 minutes and inspect the skin daily
B. Massage any erythematous areas over bony prominences twice daily
C. Limit dietary protein intake to reduce urinary calcium excretion
D. Use a donut-shaped cushion beneath the sacrum while seated
E. Remain seated for most of the day to increase tolerance to sustained pressure

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Loss of mobility and sensation places this patient at high risk for pressure injuries because he cannot reliably perceive discomfort or independently reposition himself. Preventive education should include pressure-relief maneuvers approximately every 15 to 30 minutes while seated, scheduled repositioning when in bed, daily inspection of the skin with a mirror or caregiver assistance, moisture control, appropriate pressure-redistributing surfaces, and adequate nutrition. Early recognition of nonblanching erythema can prevent progression to deeper tissue injury.

B is incorrect because massaging erythematous skin over a bony prominence can worsen tissue damage through additional friction and shear. Persistent erythema should instead prompt immediate pressure relief and clinical assessment.

C is incorrect because protein restriction impairs tissue maintenance and wound healing. Adequate protein and caloric intake are important for preserving skin integrity.

D is incorrect because donut-shaped cushions concentrate pressure around their edges and may impair local circulation. A properly fitted pressure-redistributing wheelchair cushion is preferred.

E is incorrect because prolonged uninterrupted sitting increases tissue ischemia and pressure-injury risk. Sitting time should be advanced gradually and combined with frequent pressure relief and regular skin assessment.

Question 229  [ID: b201ebfff4fc40b5b711534f6d8f8000]
A 10-year-old girl is evaluated for 3 months of progressive pain and swelling involving both wrists, several metacarpophalangeal joints, and both knees. Her symptoms are most severe in the morning and improve after approximately 90 minutes of activity. She has had intermittent fatigue but no recent infection, rash, or trauma. Examination reveals symmetric swelling, warmth, tenderness, and decreased range of motion at the affected sites. Laboratory studies show an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, positive antinuclear antibodies, and positive rheumatoid factor. Plain radiographs demonstrate periarticular soft-tissue swelling and early juxta-articular osteopenia. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's findings?

A. Activation of CD4-positive T lymphocytes with release of proinflammatory cytokines, synovial proliferation, and pannus formation
B. Deposition of monosodium urate crystals with activation of the NLRP3 inflammasome
C. Postinfectious antibody cross-reactivity against type II collagen caused by molecular mimicry
D. Defective lysosomal degradation of homogentisic acid with pigment deposition in connective tissues
E. Loss of dystrophin resulting in progressive degeneration and necrosis of skeletal myocytes

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The prolonged symmetric inflammatory symptoms, morning stiffness, objective swelling, elevated inflammatory markers, and positive rheumatoid factor in a child are consistent with polyarticular juvenile rheumatoid arthritis, also termed juvenile idiopathic arthritis. The underlying mechanism involves dysregulated adaptive immunity, particularly activation of CD4-positive T lymphocytes and production of proinflammatory cytokines such as tumor necrosis factor-alpha, interleukin-1, and interleukin-6. These mediators promote synovial hyperplasia, angiogenesis, inflammatory-cell recruitment, and pannus formation, which can eventually erode cartilage and bone. B is incorrect because monosodium urate deposition causes gout, which is extremely uncommon in children and typically presents as episodic acute monoarticular inflammation. C is incorrect because postinfectious molecular mimicry may contribute to reactive or rheumatic syndromes, but these usually follow a recognizable infection and do not characteristically cause persistent symmetric small-joint inflammation with this serologic pattern. D is incorrect because homogentisic acid accumulation occurs in alkaptonuria and produces ochronosis and degenerative changes later in life. E is incorrect because dystrophin deficiency causes Duchenne or Becker muscular dystrophy, which presents with progressive proximal weakness rather than inflammatory swelling and prolonged morning stiffness.

Question 230  [ID: b24b1989b66047148068ca9f248934b2]
A 26-year-old man presents with 5 days of worsening pain and swelling of the right knee and left ankle. He also reports pain at the insertion of the right Achilles tendon, burning with urination, and redness of both eyes. Temperature is 37.4°C (99.3°F). Examination shows asymmetric swelling and limited range of motion of the affected joints, tenderness at the Achilles tendon insertion, and bilateral nonpurulent conjunctival injection. Several shallow, painless oral ulcers are present. Synovial fluid analysis shows 18,000 leukocytes/mm3 with neutrophil predominance, no crystals, and negative Gram stain and culture. Urine nucleic acid amplification testing for Neisseria gonorrhoeae and Chlamydia trachomatis is negative. Which of the following additional historical findings would most strongly support the suspected unifying diagnosis?

A. Recurrent attacks of severe first metatarsophalangeal pain after alcohol consumption
B. A self-limited episode of diarrhea 3 weeks before symptom onset
C. A recent tick bite followed by an expanding annular rash
D. Progressive symmetric hand stiffness lasting more than 1 hour each morning
E. A family history of recurrent bleeding into large joints

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The combination of acute asymmetric oligoarticular involvement of the lower extremities, Achilles enthesitis, urethral symptoms, conjunctival inflammation, and painless oral ulcers is characteristic of reactive arthritis. This condition typically develops 1–4 weeks after a gastrointestinal or genitourinary infection. Common gastrointestinal triggers include Campylobacter, Salmonella, Shigella, and Yersinia. The triggering infection may have resolved before the joint manifestations appear, so specifically asking about a recent self-limited diarrheal illness is essential. Negative synovial fluid cultures support a sterile inflammatory process, and a negative current infectious test does not exclude a preceding infection.

A is incorrect because episodic podagra after alcohol consumption suggests gout, which would typically demonstrate monosodium urate crystals in synovial fluid and would not explain conjunctival or urethral findings.

C is incorrect because an expanding annular rash after a tick bite suggests Lyme disease. Lyme arthritis commonly causes intermittent or persistent monoarthritis of a large joint, especially the knee, but this presentation's enthesitis, urethral symptoms, and conjunctival inflammation favor another diagnosis.

D is incorrect because prolonged morning stiffness with progressive symmetric involvement of the small joints suggests rheumatoid arthritis. Rheumatoid arthritis is generally a chronic symmetric polyarthritis and does not typically cause the triad of asymmetric lower-extremity joint involvement, conjunctival inflammation, and urethral symptoms.

E is incorrect because recurrent hemarthroses in male relatives suggest an inherited coagulation disorder such as hemophilia. Hemarthrosis does not account for the associated ocular, urethral, oral, and entheseal findings.

Question 231  [ID: b2c624d411204a5e8545e45ffa32f592]
A 29-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after falling approximately 6 meters from a construction platform. He reports severe mid-back pain and an inability to move his legs. Blood pressure is 118/74 mm Hg, pulse is 104/min, and respirations are 18/min. Examination shows tenderness and a palpable step-off over the lower thoracic region. Strength is 0/5 in both lower extremities. Sensation to pinprick and temperature is absent below the T8 dermatome, whereas vibration and joint-position sensation are preserved. Initial lower-extremity reflexes are absent. Rectal tone is decreased. Hemoglobin, platelet count, and coagulation studies are within normal limits. CT demonstrates comminution of the T8 vertebral body with posterior displacement of an osseous fragment into the canal. MRI shows ventral compression and signal abnormality predominantly involving the anterior two-thirds of the neural tissue at this level. Which of the following neuroanatomic mechanisms best explains this patient's pattern of deficits?

A. Bilateral disruption of the dorsal columns with preservation of the corticospinal and spinothalamic tracts
B. Disruption of the corticospinal and spinothalamic tracts with relative preservation of the dorsal columns
C. Unilateral disruption of the corticospinal tract and dorsal columns with contralateral spinothalamic tract involvement
D. Compression of peripheral nerve roots below the termination of the conus medullaris
E. Selective injury to centrally located fibers supplying the upper extremities with sparing of lower-extremity fibers

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The patient has findings consistent with traumatic anterior cord syndrome caused by ventral compression from a retropulsed vertebral fragment. The anterior two-thirds of the cord contains the descending corticospinal tracts, which mediate voluntary motor function, and the ascending spinothalamic tracts, which carry pain and temperature sensation. Injury to these pathways produces bilateral paralysis and loss of pain and temperature sensation below the lesion. The dorsal columns are located posteriorly and have a relatively distinct posterior arterial supply, allowing vibration and proprioception to remain intact. Choice A is incorrect because dorsal column injury would impair vibration and position sensation while preserving pain, temperature, and motor function. Choice C describes the tract pattern of a hemicord lesion, which causes ipsilateral motor and proprioceptive loss with contralateral pain and temperature loss. Choice D is incorrect because injury to nerve roots below the conus would produce a lower motor neuron pattern with asymmetric weakness, radicular sensory loss, and possible saddle anesthesia rather than a defined bilateral tract pattern. Choice E describes the classic distribution associated with central cord injury, in which upper-extremity motor impairment is greater than lower-extremity impairment, typically following cervical hyperextension.

Question 232  [ID: b326198a42cf4d1e8ecafa5c02fc42c4]
A 42-year-old man presents with 4 months of progressively worsening right groin pain. The pain is exacerbated by weight-bearing and occasionally radiates to the anterior thigh. He has systemic lupus erythematosus and has taken prednisone intermittently for several years, including a recent 3-month course of high-dose therapy. He denies fever, recent trauma, or intravenous drug use. Examination reveals an antalgic gait and pain with passive internal rotation of the right hip. Range of motion is mildly restricted, but there is no overlying erythema or warmth. The leukocyte count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are within normal limits. Plain radiographs demonstrate patchy sclerosis of the superior femoral head with a crescent-shaped subchondral lucency and early flattening of the articular surface. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Avascular necrosis of the femoral head
B. Femoral neck stress fracture
C. Primary osteoarthritis of the hip
D. Septic arthritis of the hip
E. Transient osteoporosis of the hip

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Avascular necrosis of the femoral head is the most likely diagnosis. Long-term or high-dose glucocorticoid exposure is a major risk factor because it can impair blood supply to subchondral bone. Patients typically develop progressive groin or anterior thigh pain that worsens with weight-bearing, along with limited and painful internal rotation. Patchy sclerosis, a subchondral crescent sign, and flattening of the femoral head indicate subchondral collapse and are characteristic radiographic findings. A femoral neck stress fracture generally occurs after repetitive loading or increased physical activity and appears as a linear fracture or focal marrow abnormality rather than a crescent-shaped subchondral lucency. Primary osteoarthritis usually develops gradually in older patients and produces joint-space narrowing, osteophytes, and subchondral cysts. Septic arthritis typically causes acute severe pain, marked limitation of motion, fever, and elevated inflammatory markers; urgent arthrocentesis would be indicated if suspected. Transient osteoporosis causes self-limited hip pain with diffuse osteopenia and bone marrow edema but does not produce focal sclerosis, a crescent sign, or articular surface collapse.

Question 233  [ID: b377a5f54a0f491f9adfa45953aedf0a]
A 24-year-old woman presents with 3 months of pain and morning stiffness involving both wrists and several metacarpophalangeal joints. She also reports fatigue, intermittent painless oral ulcers, and a facial rash that worsens after sun exposure. Physical examination reveals mild, symmetric swelling and tenderness of the wrists and metacarpophalangeal joints without deformity. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 3,200/mm3, platelet count of 118,000/mm3, serum creatinine of 0.9 mg/dL, and a positive antinuclear antibody titer of 1:640. Urinalysis shows no protein or blood. Which of the following additional laboratory findings would be most specific for the suspected diagnosis?

A. Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies
B. Anti-centromere antibodies
C. Anti-histone antibodies
D. Anti-Smith antibodies
E. Anti-Jo-1 antibodies

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: Anti-Smith antibodies are highly specific for systemic lupus erythematosus, although they are present in only a minority of affected patients. The combination of symmetric inflammatory polyarticular symptoms, photosensitivity, oral ulcers, leukopenia, thrombocytopenia, and a strongly positive antinuclear antibody supports this diagnosis. Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies are highly specific for rheumatoid arthritis, which would not explain the photosensitive rash and hematologic abnormalities as well. Anti-centromere antibodies are associated with limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis. Anti-histone antibodies are most strongly associated with drug-induced lupus, which typically causes arthralgias and serositis but less commonly produces cytopenias or renal involvement. Anti-Jo-1 antibodies are associated with antisynthetase syndrome, characterized by inflammatory myopathy, interstitial lung disease, arthritis, Raynaud phenomenon, and mechanic's hands.

Question 234  [ID: b4f6fd5c58604fff943e33b8b91f4041]
A 23-year-old woman presents with 2 days of severe right knee pain, swelling, and inability to bear weight. During the preceding week, she experienced intermittent pain in both wrists and ankles and noted several painless pustules on her distal extremities. She is sexually active with two male partners and uses condoms inconsistently. Her temperature is 38.2°C (100.8°F). Examination shows a warm, markedly swollen right knee with severe pain on passive motion. Arthrocentesis yields cloudy fluid containing 68,000 leukocytes/mm3 with 90% neutrophils. Culture of the fluid grows oxidase-positive gram-negative diplococci. She improves after drainage and appropriate antimicrobial therapy. Which of the following discharge recommendations is most important for preventing reinfection and transmission?

A. Advise her to avoid all sexual activity for 7 days after she and her recent partners have completed treatment
B. Recommend antibiotic prophylaxis before future dental procedures
C. Administer long-term suppressive oral antibiotics for 6 months
D. Recommend avoiding weight-bearing exercise for 3 months
E. Delay evaluation of her sexual partners unless they develop symptoms

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has septic arthritis caused by disseminated Neisseria gonorrhoeae infection, suggested by migratory polyarthralgia, pustular skin lesions, acute monoarticular inflammation, and oxidase-positive gram-negative diplococci. Prevention requires evaluation and presumptive treatment of sexual partners from the preceding 60 days. The patient should abstain from sexual activity until 7 days after treatment has been completed and until all partners have been treated. She should also receive counseling regarding consistent condom use and testing for chlamydia, syphilis, and HIV. Choice B is incorrect because routine dental antibiotic prophylaxis does not prevent recurrence in this setting. Choice C is incorrect because prolonged suppressive antibiotics are not indicated after adequate drainage and completion of the recommended antimicrobial course. Choice D is incorrect because prolonged avoidance of weight-bearing activity does not prevent reinfection and may impair recovery; activity should be advanced as symptoms permit. Choice E is incorrect because sexual partners may be asymptomatic but can still transmit infection, so partner evaluation and presumptive treatment should not be delayed.

Question 235  [ID: b5337fb410d64b6ca6b5ede82d4b8cc5]
A 46-year-old woman presents with a 2-day history of severe headache, blurred vision, and decreased urine output. For the past 18 months, she has experienced episodic finger discoloration with cold exposure, progressive tightening of the skin over her hands and forearms, difficulty swallowing solid foods, and painful swelling of the finger joints. Examination reveals a blood pressure of 198/116 mm Hg, bibasilar crackles, digital pitting scars, and thickened, shiny skin extending proximal to the elbows. Laboratory studies show a serum creatinine level of 3.2 mg/dL, increased from 0.9 mg/dL 1 month ago. Hemoglobin is 9.1 g/dL, platelet count is 88,000/mm3, and a peripheral blood smear demonstrates schistocytes. Urinalysis shows mild proteinuria and microscopic hematuria. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate pharmacologic therapy?

A. Amlodipine
B. Captopril
C. Cyclophosphamide
D. High-dose intravenous methylprednisolone
E. Intravenous labetalol

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has systemic sclerosis complicated by scleroderma renal crisis, characterized by abrupt severe hypertension, rapidly progressive acute kidney injury, and microangiopathic hemolytic anemia with thrombocytopenia and schistocytes. An angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor, particularly short-acting captopril, is the treatment of choice and should be initiated immediately and rapidly titrated to control blood pressure. Therapy should generally be continued despite an initial increase in serum creatinine because it significantly improves renal recovery and survival. Amlodipine may help Raynaud phenomenon or provide adjunctive blood pressure control but does not adequately treat the renin-mediated renal crisis. Cyclophosphamide may be used for severe interstitial lung involvement but has no role in the immediate management of this presentation. High-dose glucocorticoids can precipitate or worsen renal crisis and should be avoided when possible. Intravenous labetalol can lower blood pressure but does not directly suppress the marked renin-angiotensin activation driving this complication and is not preferred over an ACE inhibitor.

Question 236  [ID: b5a11da0271c4916ac656820704ea9fa]
A 58-year-old woman presents with 3 months of progressive difficulty climbing stairs, rising from a chair, and lifting objects overhead. She has no rash, sensory loss, diplopia, or recent medication changes. Examination demonstrates symmetric weakness of the shoulder and hip girdles with preserved sensation and deep tendon reflexes. Serum creatine kinase is 4,800 U/L, and testing for antinuclear antibodies is positive. MRI of the thighs shows bilateral muscle edema. Biopsy demonstrates endomysial mononuclear inflammation with invasion of non-necrotic fibers. Treatment with high-dose prednisone and methotrexate is planned. Which of the following is the most appropriate preventive recommendation before or during this therapy?

A. Administer indicated inactivated and recombinant vaccines, including influenza, pneumococcal, and recombinant zoster vaccines, while avoiding live vaccines during significant immunosuppression
B. Administer the live attenuated zoster vaccine after high-dose prednisone has been initiated
C. Defer all vaccinations until prednisone and methotrexate have been permanently discontinued
D. Begin daily prophylactic amoxicillin to prevent common bacterial infections
E. Discontinue routine age-appropriate cancer screening because the biopsy establishes a nonmalignant cause of her symptoms

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical findings are consistent with polymyositis, which is commonly treated with high-dose glucocorticoids and a steroid-sparing immunosuppressant such as methotrexate. Vaccination status should be reviewed before immunosuppression whenever possible. Indicated inactivated and recombinant vaccines, including annual influenza vaccination, appropriate pneumococcal vaccination, and recombinant zoster vaccination, should be administered. Live vaccines should generally be avoided during significant immunosuppression because of the risk of vaccine-associated infection. B is incorrect because a live attenuated zoster vaccine should not be administered after initiation of high-dose immunosuppressive therapy; recombinant zoster vaccine is preferred. C is incorrect because inactivated and recombinant vaccines can be administered during immunosuppressive therapy, although immune responses may be reduced. D is incorrect because routine antibacterial prophylaxis is not recommended; selected patients receiving prolonged high-dose glucocorticoids combined with other immunosuppressants may instead require risk-based Pneumocystis jirovecii prophylaxis. E is incorrect because routine age-appropriate malignancy screening should continue, as idiopathic inflammatory myopathies can be associated with malignancy.

Question 237  [ID: b6aa1b9f5eb243e5b8589f76ee672d8a]
A 74-year-old woman presents with sudden onset of severe right knee pain and swelling that began 1 day after undergoing a parathyroidectomy. She has no history of trauma. Her temperature is 37.8°C (100.0°F), blood pressure is 132/78 mm Hg, and pulse is 94/min. Examination shows a warm, erythematous right knee with a large effusion and markedly limited range of motion. Laboratory studies reveal a leukocyte count of 12,400/mm3 and a serum uric acid level of 5.2 mg/dL. Plain radiographs demonstrate linear calcifications within the articular cartilage. Arthrocentesis yields cloudy fluid with 28,000 leukocytes/mm3, predominantly neutrophils. Gram stain is negative, and polarized light microscopy shows rhomboid-shaped crystals with weak positive birefringence. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease
B. Gout
C. Septic arthritis
D. Reactive arthritis
E. Osteoarthritis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease is the most likely diagnosis. An acute attack commonly affects a large joint, especially the knee, in an older adult and may be triggered by surgery or acute illness. Hyperparathyroidism is an associated metabolic condition. The diagnostic findings are rhomboid-shaped, weakly positively birefringent calcium pyrophosphate crystals in synovial fluid and chondrocalcinosis on radiographs. Gout is caused by monosodium urate crystals, which are needle-shaped and strongly negatively birefringent; it more commonly affects the first metatarsophalangeal joint, although other joints may be involved. Septic arthritis can cause an acutely warm, swollen joint with neutrophil-predominant synovial fluid, but the negative Gram stain, moderate synovial leukocyte count, characteristic crystals, and chondrocalcinosis favor calcium pyrophosphate deposition; synovial fluid culture should still be obtained because infection can coexist with crystals. Reactive arthritis typically follows a gastrointestinal or genitourinary infection and often presents with asymmetric lower-extremity involvement accompanied by enthesitis, urethritis, or conjunctivitis. Osteoarthritis causes chronic, activity-related pain and may produce joint-space narrowing and osteophytes, but it does not typically cause an abrupt, intensely inflammatory effusion with characteristic birefringent crystals.

Question 238  [ID: b7b20b9051404184895f9cb65a2308e6]
A 68-year-old woman presents with several years of gradually worsening pain in both hands. The pain is aggravated by gardening and opening jars and improves with rest. She reports stiffness lasting approximately 10 minutes after awakening. Examination shows firm, minimally tender enlargement of several distal and proximal interphalangeal joints without warmth or erythema. Grip strength is mildly reduced. The metacarpophalangeal joints are not swollen, and there is no nail pitting. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein levels are normal, and testing for rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies is negative. Which of the following radiographic findings would most strongly support the suspected diagnosis?

A. Symmetric marginal erosions with periarticular osteopenia
B. Nonuniform joint-space narrowing with marginal osteophytes and subchondral sclerosis
C. Punched-out erosions with sclerotic overhanging edges
D. Chondrocalcinosis with hook-like osteophytes at the metacarpal heads
E. Erosive changes with pencil-in-cup deformities and ankylosis

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The clinical pattern is most consistent with osteoarthritis: chronic activity-related pain, brief morning stiffness, firm enlargement of the distal and proximal interphalangeal joints, and an absence of systemic inflammation. Typical radiographic findings include nonuniform joint-space narrowing caused by focal cartilage loss, marginal osteophyte formation, subchondral sclerosis, and sometimes subchondral cysts.

A is incorrect because symmetric marginal erosions and periarticular osteopenia are characteristic of rheumatoid arthritis, which usually involves the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints while sparing the distal interphalangeal joints. Prolonged morning stiffness and inflammatory swelling would also be expected.

C is incorrect because punched-out erosions with overhanging edges are characteristic of chronic tophaceous gout. Gout more commonly causes episodic, intensely painful inflammatory attacks and may be associated with tophi.

D is incorrect because chondrocalcinosis and hook-like osteophytes at the metacarpal heads suggest calcium pyrophosphate deposition or an associated metabolic arthropathy such as hemochromatosis. This pattern does not best match the distal and proximal interphalangeal distribution described.

E is incorrect because pencil-in-cup deformities, ankylosis, and erosive changes are associated with psoriatic arthritis. Nail pitting, dactylitis, psoriasis, and prominent inflammatory findings may also be present.

Question 239  [ID: b93b29ca330c49068f318b7e9c916a2d]
A 52-year-old woman presents with 3 months of progressively worsening difficulty climbing stairs, rising from a chair, and lifting objects above her head. She also reports mild difficulty swallowing solid foods. She takes no prescription medications and denies recent infection, toxin exposure, or family history of similar symptoms. Examination shows symmetric weakness of the shoulder and hip girdles with preserved distal strength, sensation, and deep tendon reflexes. No skin eruption is present. Serum creatine kinase is 4,800 U/L, and testing for antinuclear antibodies is positive. Electromyography demonstrates short-duration, low-amplitude motor unit potentials with spontaneous fibrillation activity. Which of the following pathologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's findings?

A. CD8-positive T-lymphocyte infiltration of the endomysium with direct injury to major histocompatibility complex class I-expressing fibers
B. Complement-mediated injury of perifascicular capillaries causing ischemia and perifascicular atrophy
C. Autoantibody-mediated destruction of postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction
D. Accumulation of misfolded proteins within rimmed vacuoles accompanied by endomysial inflammation
E. Glucocorticoid-induced selective atrophy of type II fibers without inflammatory infiltration

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has polymyositis, characterized by subacute, symmetric proximal weakness, markedly elevated creatine kinase, myopathic electromyographic findings, possible dysphagia, and absence of characteristic cutaneous findings. Its principal pathophysiologic feature is cell-mediated cytotoxicity: CD8-positive T lymphocytes invade the endomysium and directly injure fibers that aberrantly express major histocompatibility complex class I molecules. B describes dermatomyositis, in which complement-mediated microangiopathy produces perifascicular ischemia and atrophy and is commonly associated with characteristic skin findings. C describes myasthenia gravis, which typically causes fluctuating fatigable weakness, often involving ocular or bulbar function, without substantial creatine kinase elevation. D describes inclusion body myositis, which generally has an insidious course in older adults, often causes asymmetric weakness involving the finger flexors and quadriceps, and is associated with rimmed vacuoles. E describes glucocorticoid-induced myopathy, which causes proximal weakness but usually has a normal creatine kinase level and lacks inflammatory infiltrates; this patient also reports no glucocorticoid exposure.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Question 240  [ID: b9fa93c5050e485991a4db67b5d1dfe0]
A 68-year-old man presents with 8 months of progressive aching and heaviness in both buttocks and thighs. The discomfort occurs after standing for several minutes or walking approximately two blocks and is accompanied by intermittent numbness in both feet. His symptoms improve when he sits or leans forward over a shopping cart but do not resolve simply by standing still. He denies fever, weight loss, recent trauma, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and saddle anesthesia. Examination shows mildly reduced lower-extremity reflexes and intact strength and sensation at rest. Pedal pulses are 2+ and symmetric. Straight-leg raising does not reproduce his symptoms. MRI demonstrates multilevel degenerative changes with ligamentous thickening, facet joint hypertrophy, and decreased space available for the cauda equina at L3-L4 and L4-L5. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Lumbar spinal stenosis
B. Peripheral arterial disease
C. Lumbar disk herniation
D. Degenerative hip osteoarthritis
E. Cauda equina syndrome

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Lumbar spinal stenosis is the most likely diagnosis. Degenerative facet hypertrophy and ligamentous thickening reduce the space available for neural structures, producing neurogenic claudication. The characteristic pattern is bilateral buttock or leg discomfort triggered by walking or prolonged standing and relieved by sitting or lumbar flexion, such as leaning over a shopping cart. Peripheral arterial disease can cause exertional leg pain, but symptoms typically improve by stopping activity even if the patient remains standing; diminished pulses and other evidence of impaired perfusion may also be present. Lumbar disk herniation more often causes acute or subacute unilateral radicular pain in a dermatomal distribution, frequently with a positive straight-leg raise test. Hip osteoarthritis commonly produces activity-related groin pain, restricted hip motion, and pain with internal rotation rather than posture-dependent bilateral neurologic symptoms. Cauda equina syndrome is an emergency characterized by acute bowel or bladder dysfunction, saddle anesthesia, and significant lower-extremity weakness, none of which is present here.

Question 241  [ID: bb2d75b18c664ae89934378bc753baa2]
A 15-year-old boy presents with progressive shortness of breath and reduced exercise tolerance during soccer practice. His parents report that an inward contour of his lower sternum has become more pronounced during his recent growth spurt. He denies trauma, fever, cough, or nocturnal symptoms. Physical examination shows a thin adolescent with a symmetric, funnel-shaped depression involving the lower sternum and adjacent costal cartilages. Cardiac auscultation reveals a soft systolic ejection murmur. Pulmonary function testing demonstrates a mild restrictive pattern. Computed tomography shows transverse displacement of the heart and a ratio of the internal transverse diameter to the shortest anteroposterior diameter of 3.6. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Pectus carinatum
B. Pectus excavatum
C. Poland syndrome
D. Flail chest
E. Thoracic scoliosis

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Pectus excavatum is characterized by posterior displacement of the sternum and adjacent costal cartilages, producing a symmetric or asymmetric funnel-shaped depression. It often becomes more apparent during adolescent growth and may cause reduced exercise tolerance, restrictive pulmonary physiology, cardiac displacement, and a benign systolic ejection murmur. The CT-derived Haller index is calculated by dividing the transverse internal diameter by the shortest anteroposterior diameter; a value greater than approximately 3.25 indicates a severe deformity and may support surgical evaluation. Pectus carinatum causes anterior protrusion rather than depression of the sternum. Poland syndrome typically features unilateral absence or hypoplasia of the pectoralis major muscle, often with ipsilateral hand abnormalities. Flail chest occurs after significant trauma and is characterized by paradoxical motion of a segment created by multiple rib fractures. Thoracic scoliosis produces lateral spinal curvature and rotational asymmetry rather than an isolated funnel-shaped sternal depression.

Question 242  [ID: bb6df4967fda481eb4a5f18409b3636d]
A 6-week-old boy is brought to the clinic because his parents have noticed that he consistently holds his head tilted to the right with his chin turned to the left. He was delivered vaginally at 39 weeks after a prolonged labor complicated by breech positioning. He is feeding normally and has no fever, vomiting, irritability, or history of trauma. Physical examination reveals a firm, nontender, 1.5-cm fusiform mass along the right sternocleidomastoid muscle. Passive rotation of the head to the right and lateral flexion to the left are limited. The remainder of the neurologic examination is normal. Ultrasonography demonstrates focal thickening of the right sternocleidomastoid muscle without a fluid collection, and cervical radiographs show no vertebral abnormalities. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Congenital muscular torticollis
B. Atlantoaxial rotatory subluxation
C. Klippel-Feil syndrome
D. Sandifer syndrome
E. Ocular torticollis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Congenital muscular torticollis is caused by unilateral shortening and fibrosis of the sternocleidomastoid muscle, often associated with intrauterine malposition or birth-related injury. Affected infants characteristically tilt the head toward the involved side and rotate the chin away from it. A firm, nontender fusiform mass within the sternocleidomastoid muscle and ultrasonographic evidence of focal muscle thickening strongly support this diagnosis. Atlantoaxial rotatory subluxation typically presents acutely after trauma, infection, or surgery and would not cause a sternocleidomastoid mass. Klippel-Feil syndrome results from congenital fusion of cervical vertebrae and is associated with a short neck, low posterior hairline, and restricted neck movement; vertebral abnormalities would be visible on radiographs. Sandifer syndrome causes episodic dystonic neck posturing associated with gastroesophageal reflux, usually during or after feeding, rather than a fixed posture with a palpable muscle mass. Ocular torticollis is compensatory head positioning due to strabismus or another ocular disorder and generally has normal passive neck range of motion without sternocleidomastoid thickening.

Question 243  [ID: bc67de430937430089a233659430b223]
A 76-year-old man presents with 18 hours of abrupt, severe pain and swelling of the right knee. He denies trauma, fever, or recent illness. His medical history includes stage 4 chronic kidney disease, hypertension, peptic ulcer disease complicated by a prior gastrointestinal bleed, and atrial fibrillation treated with warfarin. His temperature is 37.1°C (98.8°F). The right knee is warm, erythematous, swollen, and markedly tender with restricted range of motion. Arthrocentesis yields cloudy fluid with a leukocyte count of 22,000/mm3 and 85% neutrophils. Polarized light microscopy demonstrates rhomboid-shaped crystals with weak positive birefringence. Gram stain is negative, and the synovial fluid culture shows no growth. Radiographs demonstrate linear calcification within the articular cartilage. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacologic treatment for this patient's current symptoms?

A. Allopurinol orally
B. Indomethacin orally
C. Intra-articular triamcinolone
D. Probenecid orally
E. Intravenous vancomycin

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The crystal morphology, weak positive birefringence, and articular cartilage calcification indicate an acute calcium pyrophosphate crystal–induced inflammatory episode. Intra-articular glucocorticoid therapy, such as triamcinolone, is appropriate for severe inflammation limited to one accessible joint after infection has been reasonably excluded. It is particularly advantageous in this patient because chronic kidney disease, prior gastrointestinal bleeding, and warfarin therapy make systemic nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs high risk.

A is incorrect because allopurinol lowers urate production and is used for long-term prevention of recurrent monosodium urate crystal deposition; it does not treat this calcium pyrophosphate crystal–mediated episode or provide rapid symptom relief.

B is incorrect because indomethacin can treat acute crystal-induced inflammation, but this patient's advanced kidney disease, prior gastrointestinal hemorrhage, and anticoagulation create substantial risks of acute kidney injury and recurrent bleeding.

D is incorrect because probenecid increases renal urate excretion and is used for selected patients requiring chronic urate-lowering therapy. It is ineffective for calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition, does not treat an acute flare, and is generally ineffective or contraindicated in advanced kidney disease.

E is incorrect because intravenous vancomycin would be appropriate when bacterial infection is suspected. The identified crystals, negative Gram stain, negative culture, absence of systemic toxicity, and characteristic imaging findings support a sterile crystal-induced inflammatory process rather than bacterial infection.

Question 244  [ID: bd9d4ab955634545b60613c4d19663e4]
A 46-year-old right-handed carpenter presents with 6 weeks of pain along the outer aspect of his right elbow. The pain began after he started a project requiring prolonged use of a screwdriver and is worsened by gripping tools, shaking hands, and lifting objects with the palm facing downward. He denies direct trauma, fever, joint swelling, numbness, or neck pain. Examination shows focal tenderness just distal to the lateral epicondyle and reproduction of pain with resisted wrist extension while the elbow is extended. Strength, sensation, reflexes, and range of motion are otherwise normal. Plain radiographs show no fracture, degenerative changes, or joint effusion. In addition to short-term symptom management, which of the following counseling recommendations is most appropriate to reduce the risk of recurrence?

A. Avoid all use of the affected arm for at least 3 months
B. Use tools with a larger grip, maintain the wrist in a neutral position, take frequent breaks, and begin gradual eccentric strengthening as symptoms improve
C. Wear a counterforce strap continuously, including during sleep, to prevent further episodes
D. Receive scheduled corticosteroid injections every 3 months regardless of symptoms
E. Perform forceful wrist-extension stretching through pain before each work shift

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This presentation is consistent with lateral epicondylitis, an overuse condition involving the common wrist extensor origin. Recurrence prevention centers on correcting occupational mechanics and gradually improving load tolerance. Using tools with larger, ergonomically designed grips reduces required gripping force; maintaining a neutral wrist limits repetitive extensor loading; scheduled breaks reduce cumulative strain; and progressive eccentric strengthening can improve tendon capacity once acute pain subsides. A is incorrect because prolonged complete avoidance can cause deconditioning and is unnecessary; relative activity modification followed by graded return is preferred. C is incorrect because a counterforce strap may provide temporary symptom relief during activity, but continuous use, especially during sleep, is not recommended and does not correct contributing mechanics. D is incorrect because prophylactic corticosteroid injections are inappropriate; repeated injections may weaken local tissue and are associated with poor long-term outcomes. E is incorrect because forceful stretching through pain may aggravate symptoms; stretching and strengthening should be gradual and performed within tolerable limits.

Question 245  [ID: bde01895a71347c8a19db88490df8f2b]
A 16-year-old boy presents with 2 months of progressive pain and swelling just above the right knee. The pain is worse at night and no longer improves with ibuprofen. He plays basketball but denies a specific injury. Examination shows a firm, tender enlargement over the distal thigh with reduced knee flexion. There is no erythema or fever. Laboratory studies show a normal leukocyte count and mildly elevated alkaline phosphatase. Plain radiographs demonstrate a destructive metaphyseal lesion of the distal femur with mixed sclerosis and lucency, cortical disruption, radiating periosteal new formation, and elevation of the periosteum. Which of the following is the most appropriate next diagnostic study to define the local extent of the lesion and guide tissue-sampling planning?

A. Contrast-enhanced MRI of the entire femur and adjacent joints
B. Noncontrast CT of the affected knee
C. Technetium-99m radionuclide scan as the sole next study
D. Ultrasonography of the distal thigh
E. Repeat plain radiographs in 6 weeks

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The clinical and radiographic findings are highly concerning for osteosarcoma, which commonly occurs in adolescents and frequently involves the metaphysis of the distal femur. Radiating periosteal new formation and periosteal elevation are classic aggressive imaging features. Contrast-enhanced MRI of the entire involved structure, including the adjacent joints, is the best study for defining intramedullary involvement, soft-tissue extension, neurovascular relationships, joint involvement, and discontinuous skip lesions. MRI should be performed before biopsy so that the biopsy tract can be planned with the definitive surgical approach. A multidisciplinary orthopedic oncology team should coordinate tissue sampling. Choice B is incorrect because CT is less sensitive than MRI for determining intramedullary and soft-tissue extent, although CT may help characterize mineralized matrix or evaluate the chest for metastases. Choice C is incorrect because radionuclide scanning may be used during staging to identify additional sites, but it does not adequately define local anatomy or replace MRI. Choice D is incorrect because ultrasonography cannot reliably characterize the full local extent of this deep, aggressive lesion. Choice E is incorrect because the aggressive clinical and radiographic features require prompt evaluation rather than interval observation.

Question 246  [ID: be0ace9b0c164bc484e3e3e882d9010f]
A 72-year-old man presents with several months of dull pelvic discomfort and gradually worsening hearing. He reports that his hats have become too tight over the past several years. He has no recent trauma, fever, weight loss, or history of malignancy. Examination shows frontal bossing and reduced hearing bilaterally. There is mild tenderness over the right hemipelvis without erythema or warmth. Radiographs of the skull demonstrate patchy areas of sclerosis and lucency with cortical thickening, and pelvic imaging shows enlargement of the right hemipelvis with coarse trabeculae. Which of the following laboratory profiles is most likely in this patient?

A. Elevated alkaline phosphatase with normal serum calcium and phosphate levels
B. Elevated alkaline phosphatase with elevated serum calcium and decreased parathyroid hormone levels
C. Decreased alkaline phosphatase with decreased serum calcium and phosphate levels
D. Normal alkaline phosphatase with elevated serum calcium and parathyroid hormone levels
E. Elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate with positive rheumatoid factor and normal alkaline phosphatase

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's advanced age, increasing head size, hearing impairment, pelvic discomfort, cortical thickening, coarse trabeculae, and mixed areas of sclerosis and lucency are characteristic of Paget disease of bone. Accelerated osteoclastic resorption followed by disorganized osteoblastic formation produces increased bone turnover. Consequently, serum alkaline phosphatase is elevated, while serum calcium and phosphate levels generally remain normal. Option B is incorrect because hypercalcemia is not typical unless the patient is immobilized or has another disorder; suppressed parathyroid hormone would suggest a non-parathyroid cause of hypercalcemia. Option C is incorrect because low alkaline phosphatase is inconsistent with increased osteoblastic activity, and concurrent hypocalcemia and hypophosphatemia suggest another metabolic abnormality. Option D is incorrect because elevated calcium and parathyroid hormone levels are more consistent with primary hyperparathyroidism, which may produce subperiosteal resorption and osteitis fibrosa cystica rather than the described radiographic pattern. Option E is incorrect because inflammatory markers and rheumatoid factor are not the key diagnostic laboratory findings in this condition. Measurement of serum alkaline phosphatase is useful for assessing disease activity and monitoring response to therapy.

Question 247  [ID: be3358b4a0ac40f7b6f63650caa8422a]
A 29-year-old man presents with pain and deformity of the right middle finger after a baseball struck the tip of the finger while he was attempting to catch it 2 hours ago. He reports that the fingertip has remained bent since the incident. Examination shows mild swelling and tenderness over the dorsal aspect of the distal interphalangeal joint. The distal phalanx rests in flexion, and the patient cannot actively extend it; passive extension is intact. Sensation and capillary refill are normal. Plain radiographs show no fracture or joint subluxation. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial intervention?

A. Continuous splinting of the distal interphalangeal joint in extension for 6 to 8 weeks
B. Buddy taping the affected finger to the adjacent finger for 2 weeks
C. Immobilization of the proximal interphalangeal joint in flexion for 4 weeks
D. Immediate open surgical repair
E. Early unrestricted range-of-motion exercises with oral NSAID therapy

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has a mallet finger caused by disruption of the terminal extensor tendon following forced flexion of an actively extended distal interphalangeal joint. The characteristic findings are a distal phalanx resting in flexion, inability to actively extend the distal interphalangeal joint, and preserved passive extension. In the absence of a large avulsion fracture or volar joint subluxation, the appropriate initial treatment is continuous splinting of the distal interphalangeal joint in full extension or slight hyperextension for 6 to 8 weeks. The splint must remain in place continuously because any flexion during the healing period can disrupt tendon healing and restart the treatment interval. Buddy taping does not adequately maintain distal interphalangeal extension. Immobilizing the proximal interphalangeal joint in flexion does not address the affected terminal extensor mechanism and may cause unnecessary stiffness. Immediate surgery is generally reserved for open injuries, significant joint subluxation, large articular avulsion fractures, or failure of appropriate conservative therapy. Early unrestricted motion would prevent tendon healing and may result in a persistent extension lag or secondary deformity.

Question 248  [ID: beb0c50fd09f4624898faa1708438c57]
An 18-month-old boy is brought to the clinic for delayed walking and progressive prominence along the front of his thorax. He was exclusively breastfed until 12 months of age without supplementation and now consumes a restrictive diet with minimal dairy products. Physical examination shows frontal bossing, widening of both wrists, mild bilateral bowing of the legs, and symmetric beadlike enlargement of the costochondral junctions. Laboratory studies reveal a serum calcium level of 8.3 mg/dL, phosphorus level of 2.4 mg/dL, markedly elevated alkaline phosphatase level, elevated parathyroid hormone level, and low 25-hydroxyvitamin D level. Radiographs demonstrate metaphyseal cupping and fraying. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy?

A. Oral cholecalciferol plus elemental calcium
B. Intranasal calcitonin monotherapy
C. Oral phosphate salts plus calcitriol
D. Intravenous zoledronic acid
E. Oral calcium carbonate monotherapy

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The child has clinical, biochemical, and radiographic evidence of defective mineralization caused by nutritional vitamin D deficiency. Findings include costochondral enlargement, frontal bossing, wrist widening, lower-extremity bowing, low phosphorus, elevated alkaline phosphatase and parathyroid hormone, low 25-hydroxyvitamin D, and metaphyseal cupping and fraying. Initial treatment is oral vitamin D, typically cholecalciferol, together with adequate elemental calcium. Calcium should be provided because dietary calcium deficiency may coexist and sufficient calcium is required for remineralization. B is incorrect because calcitonin inhibits osteoclast activity and is not used to correct vitamin D deficiency. C is used for certain renal phosphate-wasting disorders, such as X-linked hypophosphatemia; this patient's low 25-hydroxyvitamin D level and nutritional history support a nutritional deficiency instead. D is incorrect because bisphosphonates inhibit bone resorption and may worsen impaired mineralization. E is incomplete because calcium alone does not correct the underlying vitamin D deficiency.

Question 249  [ID: bed1705001cf47a5b866fa1c91ebd584]
A 42-year-old man presents with 4 months of progressively worsening deep right groin pain. The pain is exacerbated by standing and walking and occasionally radiates to the anterior thigh. He denies recent trauma, fever, or morning stiffness. His medical history includes systemic lupus erythematosus treated with multiple courses of high-dose prednisone. He walks with an antalgic gait. There is no erythema, swelling, or tenderness over the greater trochanter. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are within normal limits. Plain radiographs show no acute abnormality, but MRI demonstrates a serpiginous subchondral low-signal line with surrounding marrow edema in the right femoral head. Which of the following additional physical examination findings is most likely in this patient?

A. Pain and weakness with resisted hip abduction accompanied by lateral tenderness
B. Painful limitation of passive internal rotation of the hip
C. Reproduction of radicular pain with ipsilateral straight-leg raising
D. Groin pain with a palpable bulge that increases during the Valsalva maneuver
E. Tenderness at the pubic symphysis with pain during resisted thigh adduction

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The clinical presentation is characteristic of avascular necrosis of the femoral head. Major risk factors include prolonged or high-dose glucocorticoid exposure, excessive alcohol use, sickle cell disease, trauma, and systemic lupus erythematosus. Patients typically report gradually progressive deep groin, buttock, or anterior thigh pain that worsens with weight bearing. Examination commonly reveals an antalgic gait and painful restriction of hip range of motion, particularly internal rotation and abduction. MRI is the most sensitive test for early disease and may show a serpiginous subchondral signal abnormality before plain radiographs become abnormal. Therefore, painful limitation of passive internal rotation is the most likely additional finding. Choice A suggests greater trochanteric pain syndrome, which causes lateral tenderness and pain with resisted abduction rather than deep groin pain. Choice C suggests lumbosacral radiculopathy, typically accompanied by radiating neuropathic pain, sensory changes, or reflex abnormalities. Choice D is consistent with an inguinal hernia, which produces a palpable groin bulge accentuated by increased intra-abdominal pressure. Choice E suggests an adductor strain or athletic pubalgia, which causes localized tenderness and pain with resisted adduction.
