Question 250  [ID: c12dd5574fa24c7997e476dfc25fd6f7]
A 13-year-old boy is brought to the clinic for 2 months of gradually worsening pain just below his right kneecap. He recently began playing on both his school soccer and basketball teams. The pain is exacerbated by running, jumping, squatting, and climbing stairs and improves with rest. He denies fever, nocturnal pain, joint swelling, or a specific traumatic event. Examination shows localized tenderness and a firm prominence over the right tibial tubercle. Pain is reproduced with resisted knee extension, but the knee has full range of motion without instability or effusion. Plain radiographs show mild fragmentation and soft-tissue swelling at the tibial tubercle without fracture or destructive lesion. Which of the following is the most appropriate counseling recommendation for this patient and his parents?

A. He should permanently discontinue all running and jumping activities to prevent progressive deformity.
B. He may continue activities as tolerated, with temporary activity modification, ice after exercise, stretching of the quadriceps and hamstrings, and short-term use of an NSAID if needed.
C. He should undergo immediate surgical excision of the symptomatic bony prominence before returning to sports.
D. He should be placed in a rigid long-leg cast for 6 weeks regardless of symptom severity.
E. He should continue unrestricted sports participation because pain does not indicate a risk of symptom progression.

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This presentation is characteristic of Osgood-Schlatter disease, an overuse traction injury involving the tibial tubercle apophysis in a physically active adolescent. Management is conservative, and education should emphasize that the condition is generally self-limited as skeletal maturity occurs. The patient may participate in sports as tolerated, but painful activities should be reduced temporarily. Ice, quadriceps and hamstring stretching, protective padding over the tubercle, and short courses of NSAIDs can improve symptoms. A is incorrect because permanent avoidance of sports is unnecessary; activity can usually resume gradually as pain permits. C is incorrect because surgery is rarely indicated and is generally reserved for persistent symptoms from an ossicle after skeletal maturity. D is incorrect because routine casting is unnecessary; brief immobilization may be considered only for unusually severe, refractory pain. E is incorrect because unrestricted activity despite significant pain may worsen symptoms and prolong recovery, so participation should be guided by discomfort and functional limitations.

Question 251  [ID: c17c81b918ce44caae3de970cce6bc06]
A 34-year-old man presents with 2 days of lower back pain that began immediately after he lifted a heavy box while moving. The pain is localized to the lower lumbar region and worsens with bending, twisting, and prolonged standing. He denies fever, weight loss, nighttime pain, abdominal pain, dysuria, urinary retention, incontinence, saddle anesthesia, lower-extremity weakness, or intravenous drug use. He has no history of malignancy, chronic glucocorticoid therapy, or recent trauma other than the lifting event. His temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), blood pressure is 126/78 mm Hg, pulse is 76/min, and respirations are 14/min. Examination shows localized paraspinal tenderness and reduced lumbar range of motion due to pain. There is no midline percussion tenderness or palpable deformity. Lower-extremity strength is 5/5, sensation is intact, reflexes are symmetric, and the straight-leg raise test is negative bilaterally. Urinalysis is normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic approach at this time?

A. No additional laboratory testing or imaging is indicated initially
B. Anteroposterior and lateral radiographs of the lumbar region
C. Noncontrast computed tomography of the lumbar region
D. Magnetic resonance imaging of the lumbar region
E. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate, C-reactive protein level, and blood cultures

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. The acute onset after lifting, pain provoked by movement, localized paraspinal tenderness, and normal neurologic examination support an uncomplicated mechanical soft-tissue injury. There are no red-flag findings suggesting fracture, malignancy, infection, cauda equina compression, or another condition requiring immediate testing. In such patients, the diagnosis is clinical, and routine laboratory studies or imaging do not improve outcomes. Further evaluation is warranted if symptoms persist despite appropriate conservative management or if red-flag features develop. B is incorrect because plain radiographs are primarily indicated when fracture is suspected, such as after significant trauma or in patients with osteoporosis, advanced age, or chronic glucocorticoid exposure. C is incorrect because computed tomography exposes the patient to substantial radiation and is generally reserved for detailed assessment of suspected bony injury when radiographs are inadequate or abnormal. D is incorrect because magnetic resonance imaging is indicated for severe or progressive neurologic deficits, suspected infection or malignancy, cauda equina features, or persistent symptoms when invasive intervention is being considered. E is incorrect because inflammatory markers and blood cultures are appropriate when infection is suspected; this patient has no fever, systemic illness, immunocompromising condition, intravenous drug use, or focal midline tenderness.

Question 252  [ID: c1ee79d32d2c4485bec3811c46987ad0]
A 72-year-old woman presents with 6 weeks of bilateral shoulder and hip aching accompanied by morning stiffness lasting 90 minutes. She has difficulty rising from a chair and brushing her hair. Examination shows painful active range of motion of both shoulders without objective muscle weakness, joint swelling, or rash. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 82 mm/hr, elevated C-reactive protein, normal creatine kinase, and negative anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies. Her symptoms improve dramatically within 4 days of starting low-dose prednisone, and a prolonged taper is planned. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry shows a femoral neck T-score of −2.2. Which of the following is the most appropriate preventive measure for this patient?

A. Begin calcium and vitamin D supplementation alone
B. Begin calcium, vitamin D, and an oral bisphosphonate
C. Administer intramuscular testosterone every month
D. Delay preventive therapy until the T-score is −2.5 or lower
E. Discontinue prednisone and substitute long-term opioid therapy

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This presentation is characteristic of polymyalgia rheumatica, which is generally treated with prolonged low-dose glucocorticoid therapy. Older age, anticipated glucocorticoid exposure for more than 3 months, and a femoral neck T-score of −2.2 place this patient at increased risk for glucocorticoid-induced osteoporosis and fracture. She should receive adequate calcium and vitamin D, counseling regarding weight-bearing exercise and fall prevention, and pharmacologic osteoporosis prophylaxis with a bisphosphonate such as alendronate, assuming no contraindications. A is incorrect because calcium and vitamin D alone are inadequate for a patient with substantial fracture risk. C is incorrect because testosterone is not appropriate osteoporosis prophylaxis in this patient. D is incorrect because glucocorticoid-related fracture risk can warrant treatment before the T-score reaches the osteoporosis threshold of −2.5. E is incorrect because prednisone is effective first-line therapy for the underlying condition, whereas opioids neither treat the inflammatory process nor prevent glucocorticoid-associated bone loss.

Question 253  [ID: c307ef7585da4a888c05a1700f0b2b01]
A 48-year-old woman presents with 3 months of progressive difficulty climbing stairs, rising from a chair, and lifting objects above her head. She also reports mild difficulty swallowing solid foods. She takes no medications and has no recent history of trauma or strenuous exercise. Physical examination reveals symmetric 3/5 strength with shoulder abduction and hip flexion; distal strength and sensation are preserved. No skin eruption is present. Laboratory studies show a creatine kinase level of 4,900 U/L, elevated aldolase, and mildly elevated aminotransferase levels. Electromyography demonstrates short-duration, low-amplitude motor unit potentials with spontaneous fibrillations. A biopsy shows endomysial mononuclear inflammatory infiltrates surrounding and invading non-necrotic fibers. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic intervention?

A. Hydroxychloroquine
B. Methotrexate
C. Oral prednisone
D. Pyridostigmine
E. Riluzole

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The patient has polymyositis, characterized by progressive, symmetric proximal weakness, elevated creatine kinase and aldolase levels, myopathic electromyographic findings, and endomysial inflammation with invasion of non-necrotic fibers. The absence of a characteristic cutaneous eruption helps distinguish polymyositis from dermatomyositis. High-dose systemic glucocorticoid therapy, typically oral prednisone at approximately 1 mg/kg/day, is the standard initial pharmacologic treatment. The dose is gradually tapered after clinical strength and laboratory markers improve. Methotrexate (B) is commonly used as a glucocorticoid-sparing agent or for disease that responds inadequately to glucocorticoids, but it is not generally the initial monotherapy. Hydroxychloroquine (A) is most useful for cutaneous manifestations associated with dermatomyositis and does not adequately treat significant proximal weakness. Pyridostigmine (D) is used for symptomatic treatment of myasthenia gravis, which typically causes fluctuating and fatigable weakness without marked creatine kinase elevation. Riluzole (E) may modestly slow progression of amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, which produces upper and lower motor neuron findings rather than this pattern of symmetric proximal weakness and inflammatory biopsy changes.

Question 254  [ID: c30c69fece3d4e12968ef7bd06a2e9fc]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 8 months of diffuse, bilateral pain involving her neck, shoulders, back, hips, arms, and legs. She also reports nonrestorative sleep, daytime fatigue, frequent headaches, and difficulty concentrating. Her symptoms have caused her to reduce her usual physical activity. She denies joint swelling, fever, rash, weight loss, or focal weakness. Examination demonstrates widespread soft-tissue tenderness without synovitis, joint deformity, muscle weakness, or neurologic deficits. Complete blood count, comprehensive metabolic panel, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, C-reactive protein, thyroid-stimulating hormone, and creatine kinase levels are within normal limits. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial management plan for this patient?

A. Begin a graded aerobic exercise program, provide sleep education, and consider cognitive behavioral therapy
B. Prescribe long-term opioid therapy with dose escalation based on pain severity
C. Initiate oral prednisone and taper according to the clinical response
D. Recommend strict activity restriction until the pain has completely resolved
E. Begin methotrexate therapy with folic acid supplementation

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has fibromyalgia, characterized by chronic widespread pain accompanied by fatigue, nonrestorative sleep, cognitive symptoms, diffuse tenderness, and an absence of objective inflammatory, neurologic, or muscle abnormalities. Initial management should emphasize education and a multidisciplinary, nonpharmacologic approach. Graded low-impact aerobic exercise is among the most effective interventions and can improve pain, physical function, fatigue, and sleep. Sleep hygiene and cognitive behavioral therapy may further improve coping, mood, and symptom burden. Medications such as duloxetine, milnacipran, pregabalin, or low-dose amitriptyline may be added when symptoms remain functionally limiting despite foundational nonpharmacologic therapy. Choice B is incorrect because long-term opioids are generally ineffective for this condition and carry substantial risks, including dependence, overdose, and opioid-induced hyperalgesia. Choice C is incorrect because corticosteroids do not treat the central pain-processing abnormality responsible for these symptoms and expose the patient to unnecessary adverse effects. Choice D is incorrect because inactivity can worsen pain, deconditioning, fatigue, and functional impairment; activity should instead be introduced gradually and increased as tolerated. Choice E is incorrect because methotrexate is used for selected inflammatory disorders, whereas this patient has no synovitis, elevated inflammatory markers, or other evidence of an inflammatory process.

Question 255  [ID: c31f305145f44e3c9bd839a223360553]
A 2-month-old infant is brought to the clinic because of a persistent abnormal head position first noticed several weeks after birth. The infant was delivered vaginally after a breech presentation. The parents report that the infant consistently tilts the head to the right and turns the chin to the left. Examination reveals decreased passive rotation of the head to the right and a firm, nontender 1.5-cm mass within the right sternocleidomastoid muscle. The remainder of the neurologic examination is normal. There is no fever, irritability, cervical lymphadenopathy, or history of trauma. Which of the following is the best initial intervention for this patient?

A. Caregiver-directed passive stretching, repositioning, and referral for physical therapy
B. Immediate surgical release of the affected sternocleidomastoid muscle
C. Botulinum toxin injection into the affected sternocleidomastoid muscle
D. Rigid cervical immobilization for 6 weeks
E. Oral diazepam and nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory therapy

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The infant's characteristic head tilt toward the affected side, chin rotation away from the affected side, restricted cervical range of motion, and firm sternocleidomastoid mass are typical of congenital muscular torticollis. The best initial management is conservative therapy with gentle passive stretching, increased supervised prone positioning, environmental repositioning to encourage active rotation toward the restricted side, and early physical therapy. Treatment initiated during the first several months of life is highly effective and helps prevent plagiocephaly and facial asymmetry. Choice B is incorrect because surgical release is reserved for persistent functional limitation despite prolonged conservative treatment, generally in older infants or children. Choice C is incorrect because botulinum toxin may be considered for refractory cases but is not first-line treatment. Choice D is incorrect because rigid immobilization does not correct sternocleidomastoid shortening and may worsen stiffness or delay motor development. Choice E is incorrect because diazepam and anti-inflammatory medication may be used selectively for painful acute muscle spasm in older patients, but they are inappropriate for this painless congenital presentation.

Question 256  [ID: c38b3848faa0437d8ed0e775d145ff8a]
A 24-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe right shoulder pain after being tackled during a rugby match. He landed with his right arm abducted and externally rotated. The patient supports the affected arm in slight abduction and refuses to move it. Examination reveals loss of the normal rounded shoulder contour and a prominent acromion. Radial and ulnar pulses are intact. He reports numbness over the lateral aspect of the proximal upper arm. Radiographs show the humeral head positioned anteroinferior to the glenoid. Based on the anatomic relationship of the involved structures, which neural structure is most likely injured?

A. Axillary nerve
B. Long thoracic nerve
C. Musculocutaneous nerve
D. Radial nerve
E. Suprascapular nerve

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The mechanism, abnormal contour, and anteroinferior position of the humeral head indicate an anterior glenohumeral dislocation. The axillary nerve passes through the quadrangular space and courses around the surgical neck of the humerus, placing it at risk when the humeral head is displaced anteroinferiorly. Injury produces sensory loss over the lateral proximal upper arm in the superior lateral cutaneous nerve distribution and may cause deltoid weakness. The long thoracic nerve innervates the serratus anterior; injury causes medial scapular winging rather than lateral upper-arm sensory loss. The musculocutaneous nerve supplies the anterior compartment of the arm and provides sensation to the lateral forearm; injury would impair elbow flexion and forearm supination. The radial nerve courses near the humeral shaft and is more commonly injured with midshaft humeral fractures, producing wrist drop and dorsal hand sensory deficits. The suprascapular nerve innervates the supraspinatus and infraspinatus and may cause weakness of abduction initiation and external rotation, but it does not provide cutaneous sensation over the lateral proximal arm.

Question 257  [ID: c3b75c5f7bf64844a353e9733dd00663]
A 48-year-old man presents with 2 months of progressive fatigue, unintentional weight loss, severe aching in both calves, and episodic postprandial abdominal pain. He recently developed burning pain in the right foot followed by difficulty dorsiflexing it. His blood pressure is 168/96 mm Hg. Examination reveals a reticular violaceous discoloration of both legs, scattered tender subcutaneous nodules, and right foot drop. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 88 mm/hr, serum creatinine of 1.8 mg/dL, and positive hepatitis B surface antigen. Urinalysis demonstrates mild proteinuria and microscopic hematuria without red blood cell casts. Antinuclear antibody, cytoplasmic antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibody, and perinuclear antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibody testing are negative. Chest radiography is normal. Which of the following diagnostic findings would most strongly support the suspected unifying diagnosis?

A. Multiple microaneurysms and irregular constrictions of the renal and mesenteric arteries on angiography
B. Perifascicular atrophy with perivascular inflammation on skeletal muscle biopsy
C. Linear immunoglobulin G deposition along the glomerular basement membrane on renal biopsy
D. Monosodium urate crystals demonstrating strong negative birefringence in synovial fluid
E. Marginal erosions and positive anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The combination of systemic symptoms, calf myalgias, livedo reticularis, tender subcutaneous nodules, mononeuritis multiplex manifested by foot drop, postprandial abdominal pain, hypertension, renal dysfunction without an active glomerular sediment, and hepatitis B infection strongly suggests polyarteritis nodosa. Angiography classically demonstrates microaneurysms, segmental narrowing, and irregular constrictions involving medium-sized renal, mesenteric, or hepatic arteries. These findings can establish the diagnosis when tissue biopsy is unavailable or impractical. Pulmonary involvement and antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies are typically absent. B describes dermatomyositis, which generally presents with symmetric proximal weakness and characteristic cutaneous findings rather than foot drop, abdominal ischemic symptoms, or arterial microaneurysms. C is characteristic of anti-glomerular basement membrane disease, which typically produces rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis with red blood cell casts and may cause pulmonary hemorrhage. D confirms gout and does not explain the multisystem findings. E supports rheumatoid arthritis, which presents primarily with symmetric inflammatory polyarthritis and does not account for the neuropathy, abdominal pain, hypertension, and livedo pattern in this vignette.

Question 258  [ID: c49264ef4a49470b8e754245016fb9ec]
A 27-year-old man is evaluated 6 hours after sustaining a closed, comminuted tibial shaft fracture in a motorcycle collision. The fracture was reduced and immobilized in a circumferential cast. He now reports rapidly worsening, deep pain in the affected leg despite intravenous opioid administration. Examination reveals a tense, swollen lower leg and severe pain with passive extension of the toes. Sensation is decreased over the dorsum of the foot, and ankle dorsiflexion is weak. The foot is warm, with palpable dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses. Serum creatine kinase is 1,200 U/L. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate intervention?

A. Elevate the affected extremity above the level of the heart and apply ice
B. Remove the cast and arrange immediate surgical decompression
C. Obtain contrast-enhanced CT angiography of the affected extremity
D. Increase intravenous opioid dosing and reassess in 2 hours
E. Begin intravenous heparin and consult vascular surgery

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. Rapidly progressive pain out of proportion to the injury, pain with passive stretching, tense swelling, sensory changes, and motor weakness after a tibial fracture indicate dangerously increased pressure within a confined anatomic space. Palpable distal pulses do not exclude this emergency because arterial flow may persist until late. External constriction should be removed immediately, and urgent surgical decompression is required to restore tissue perfusion and prevent irreversible nerve injury, muscle necrosis, rhabdomyolysis, renal failure, and limb loss. Pressure measurement may help when the examination is equivocal, but treatment should not be delayed when clinical findings are compelling. A is incorrect because elevation above heart level may further reduce arterial perfusion, and ice does not relieve the underlying pressure. C is incorrect because CT angiography evaluates vascular injury but would delay definitive treatment; the warm foot and palpable pulses also make acute arterial occlusion less likely. D is incorrect because escalating analgesia and observation delays time-sensitive decompression and may mask progression. E is incorrect because anticoagulation is used for thrombotic disease and does not correct pressure-related tissue ischemia; it may also increase bleeding into the affected space.

Question 259  [ID: c54c0500388b40219f31af4eff916b05]
A 3-month-old girl is brought to the clinic because her parents noticed that her thigh creases appear uneven during diaper changes. She was delivered vaginally at 39 weeks after a frank breech presentation. Physical examination demonstrates asymmetric thigh folds, apparent shortening of the left lower extremity, and limited abduction of the left thigh. With the infant supine, gentle abduction and anterior lifting of the left proximal femur produces a palpable clunk. Ultrasonography demonstrates a shallow left acetabulum with incomplete coverage and lateral displacement of the femoral head. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Developmental dysplasia of the hip
B. Legg-Calvé-Perthes disease
C. Slipped capital femoral epiphysis
D. Septic arthritis
E. Transient synovitis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Developmental dysplasia of the hip encompasses acetabular dysplasia, instability, subluxation, and dislocation of the femoral head. Major risk factors in this patient include female sex and breech presentation. Limited abduction, asymmetric thigh folds, apparent limb shortening, and a palpable clunk with reduction are characteristic examination findings. Ultrasonography is the preferred imaging study in young infants because the femoral head has not yet fully ossified; a shallow acetabulum with inadequate femoral head coverage supports the diagnosis.

B is incorrect because Legg-Calvé-Perthes disease is avascular necrosis of the femoral head that usually presents in children ages 4–8 years with an atraumatic limp, activity-related pain, and restricted internal rotation and abduction. It does not typically present in infancy or produce a reducible clunk.

C is incorrect because slipped capital femoral epiphysis most often occurs in overweight adolescents and presents with groin, thigh, or referred knee pain, a limp, and obligatory external rotation during flexion. The patient's age and ultrasonographic findings are incompatible with this condition.

D is incorrect because septic arthritis generally causes acute pain, fever, irritability, refusal to move the affected extremity, and marked discomfort with passive motion. Inflammatory markers are often elevated. This infant's risk factors, painless examination findings, and chronic structural abnormalities favor a congenital instability rather than infection.

E is incorrect because transient synovitis typically occurs after a viral illness in children ages 3–8 years and causes acute limping and discomfort with movement. It is self-limited and does not cause acetabular shallowing, persistent lateral displacement of the femoral head, asymmetric folds, or a palpable reduction clunk.

Question 260  [ID: c6880cd2a4ea43b1ae86d1a15bc8f7bf]
A 13-year-old boy presents with 3 months of gradually worsening pain over the front of his right knee. The pain is exacerbated by running, jumping, kneeling, and climbing stairs and improves with rest. He recently began participating in both basketball and track. He denies fever, nighttime pain, trauma, or joint swelling. Examination shows a prominent, tender tibial tubercle without erythema or warmth. He has full knee range of motion, and pain is reproduced by resisted knee extension. There is no joint-line tenderness, effusion, or ligamentous instability. Plain radiographs demonstrate mild fragmentation and soft-tissue swelling at the tibial tubercle without an acute fracture. Which of the following is the best initial management?

A. Activity modification, ice, a short course of an NSAID, and quadriceps and hamstring stretching
B. Immediate arthroscopic debridement of the affected area
C. Strict non-weight-bearing with long-leg casting for 6 weeks
D. Intra-articular glucocorticoid injection followed by unrestricted activity
E. Empiric intravenous antibiotics and surgical drainage

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This adolescent has Osgood-Schlatter disease, a traction apophysitis caused by repetitive stress from the patellar tendon on the developing tibial tubercle. The typical presentation is activity-related anterior knee pain, focal tibial tubercle tenderness and prominence, and pain with resisted knee extension in a skeletally immature athlete. Initial treatment is conservative and includes relative rest or activity modification, ice, a short course of an NSAID when appropriate, and stretching and strengthening of the quadriceps and hamstrings. A patellar tendon strap may also reduce symptoms. Participation may continue as tolerated if pain does not cause limping or functional impairment. Symptoms usually resolve after physeal closure. Option B is incorrect because surgery is rarely indicated and is generally reserved for persistent symptoms from an ossicle after skeletal maturity. Option C is incorrect because routine casting and strict non-weight-bearing are unnecessary; brief immobilization is considered only for severe, refractory pain. Option D is incorrect because glucocorticoid injection is not recommended and may weaken the patellar tendon or injure local tissues. Option E is incorrect because the absence of fever, warmth, erythema, systemic illness, and destructive imaging findings argues strongly against septic arthritis or osteomyelitis.

Question 261  [ID: c90f3026bef449e0a8a72bb32250d657]
A 14-year-old boy is evaluated for a progressively rounded upper back first noticed 18 months ago. He reports intermittent aching between the shoulder blades after prolonged sitting but denies fever, nocturnal pain, weakness, paresthesias, or bowel or bladder dysfunction. Examination shows a fixed, sharply angulated posterior curvature that does not correct with active extension or when supine. Strength, sensation, reflexes, and gait are normal. CBC, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein are within normal limits. Standing lateral radiography demonstrates a 64-degree curvature with anterior wedging of 7 degrees in three consecutive vertebral bodies and irregular vertebral endplates. His Risser grade is 1. Which of the following is the most appropriate intervention at this time?

A. Observation with annual radiographs and no activity modification
B. Extension-based thoracolumbosacral bracing combined with supervised physical therapy
C. Immediate posterior spinal fusion with instrumentation
D. Strict bed rest followed by gradual return to activity
E. Long-term systemic glucocorticoid therapy

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This adolescent has structural kyphosis characterized by a rigid curvature, anterior wedging of at least 5 degrees across three consecutive vertebral bodies, and endplate irregularities. Because he remains skeletally immature and has a curve between approximately 60 and 80 degrees without neurologic compromise, extension-based bracing is appropriate to limit progression and potentially improve the deformity during growth. Physical therapy emphasizing spinal extension, posture, and flexibility is used as an adjunct. Choice A is incorrect because observation alone is generally reserved for smaller, minimally symptomatic curves, particularly those less than approximately 60 degrees. Choice C is incorrect because operative correction is typically reserved for severe or progressive curves, often greater than 75 to 80 degrees, or for refractory pain, major functional impairment, cardiopulmonary compromise, or neurologic deficits. Choice D is incorrect because bed rest does not correct the structural deformity and may cause deconditioning. Choice E is incorrect because glucocorticoids have no therapeutic role and may worsen bone health.

Question 262  [ID: c937a277f14149fd8e7b7c38099dafc4]
A 24-year-old man presents with 5 days of pain and swelling in his right knee and left ankle. Two weeks earlier, he developed dysuria and scant urethral discharge after having unprotected intercourse with a new partner. He also reports bilateral eye redness without vision changes. Examination shows conjunctival injection, a swollen and warm right knee, tenderness at the left Achilles tendon insertion, and several shallow painless oral ulcers. Synovial fluid contains 18,000 leukocytes/mm3 with negative Gram stain, culture, and crystal analysis. A urine nucleic acid amplification test is positive for Chlamydia trachomatis. Radiographs show soft-tissue swelling without erosions. In addition to treating the patient's current infection, which of the following counseling recommendations is most appropriate to reduce his risk of reinfection and future episodes?

A. Avoid all weight-bearing exercise indefinitely
B. Take prophylactic doxycycline before each sexual encounter
C. Ensure that recent sexual partners are evaluated and treated, and abstain from sexual activity until treatment has been completed by all parties
D. Recommend HLA-B27 testing for all first-degree relatives
E. Begin a lifelong gluten-free diet

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The presentation of acute asymmetric lower-extremity synovitis, enthesitis, conjunctival inflammation, oral lesions, and recent Chlamydia trachomatis infection is characteristic of reactive arthritis. Chlamydial reinfection can trigger recurrent episodes. Recent sexual partners should be notified, evaluated, and presumptively treated according to current sexually transmitted infection guidelines. The patient should abstain from sexual activity until he and his partners have completed therapy and the recommended post-treatment interval has passed; consistent condom use and appropriate repeat testing should also be discussed. A is incorrect because permanent avoidance of weight-bearing activity is unnecessary and may worsen deconditioning; activity can be modified during acute symptoms and advanced as tolerated. B is incorrect because antibiotic prophylaxis before sexual intercourse is not recommended and may promote adverse effects and antimicrobial resistance. C is correct because partner management and temporary abstinence reduce transmission, reinfection, and the risk of future infection-triggered episodes. D is incorrect because HLA-B27 may be associated with more severe or recurrent disease, but routine screening of asymptomatic relatives provides no preventive benefit. E is incorrect because dietary gluten restriction does not prevent recurrence unless the patient has a separate indication such as confirmed celiac disease.

Question 263  [ID: c9a322a2a1f644c681cca70286eada76]
A 68-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe posterior neck pain and bilateral arm weakness after falling forward and striking his forehead on a countertop. He did not lose consciousness. His medical history includes hypertension and cervical degenerative changes. Temperature is 36.8°C (98.2°F), blood pressure is 148/86 mm Hg, pulse is 88/min, and respirations are 16/min. Examination shows midline posterior neck tenderness. Strength is 2/5 in both hands, 3/5 in the proximal upper extremities, and 4/5 in both lower extremities. Sensation to pinprick is decreased in the hands and forearms. Rectal tone is diminished. Complete blood count and serum electrolyte levels are within normal limits. Noncontrast CT of the head shows no acute abnormality, and CT of the cervical region shows degenerative narrowing without fracture or dislocation. Which of the following is the most appropriate next diagnostic study?

A. Electromyography and nerve conduction studies
B. MRI of the cervical region without contrast
C. Repeat CT of the cervical region with intravenous contrast
D. Technetium-99m bone scintigraphy
E. Flexion-extension radiographs of the cervical region

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: MRI of the cervical region without contrast is the most appropriate next study. The patient has objective neurologic deficits after a hyperextension-type mechanism despite the absence of fracture or dislocation on CT. MRI is superior to CT for evaluating the neural tissue, intervertebral discs, ligaments, epidural space, and soft-tissue edema. It can identify cord compression, edema, hemorrhage, traumatic disc herniation, ligamentous disruption, or an epidural hematoma and should be obtained urgently while immobilization is maintained.

A is incorrect because electromyography and nerve conduction studies are generally used to evaluate peripheral nerve, plexus, or nerve-root dysfunction after the acute period. They do not adequately assess an unstable structural lesion or acute neural tissue compression.

C is incorrect because intravenous contrast does not significantly improve CT detection of acute ligamentous or neural tissue abnormalities. Contrast-enhanced CT may be useful for selected vascular or infectious processes, but MRI is preferred for the findings in this patient.

D is incorrect because bone scintigraphy is not used for the urgent evaluation of acute neurologic deficits following injury. It is more useful for detecting occult bone turnover associated with stress injury, infection, or malignancy.

E is incorrect because flexion-extension radiographs should not be performed in a patient with acute pain and neurologic deficits. Manipulation could worsen an unstable injury, and these radiographs are less sensitive than MRI for ligamentous and neural tissue abnormalities.

Question 264  [ID: c9ad736410e3493c81b05cc137c6dabf]
A 47-year-old man presents after attempting to catch a falling air-conditioning unit 2 days ago. He felt a sudden pop in the front of his dominant elbow followed by immediate pain. Since then, he has noticed swelling, bruising, and difficulty using a screwdriver. Examination shows ecchymosis over the antecubital region, proximal retraction of the muscle contour, and marked weakness with forearm supination against resistance. Elbow flexion is mildly weakened. Plain radiographs show no fracture or dislocation. Which of the following additional physical examination findings is most likely in this patient?

A. Inability to palpate a cordlike structure when the examiner hooks a finger beneath it from the lateral side of the antecubital fossa
B. Reproduction of lateral elbow pain with resisted wrist extension while the elbow is extended
C. Increased medial joint-space opening with a valgus force applied at 30 degrees of elbow flexion
D. Paresthesias in the fourth and fifth digits elicited by percussion posterior to the medial epicondyle
E. Inability to maintain thumb adduction while grasping a sheet of paper between the thumb and index finger

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The abrupt eccentric load, audible pop, antecubital ecchymosis, proximal change in muscle contour, and pronounced loss of supination strength indicate a distal biceps tendon rupture. The hook test is performed with the elbow flexed and the forearm supinated; the examiner attempts to hook an index finger beneath the intact distal tendon from the lateral side. Failure to palpate or hook the cordlike tendon supports a complete rupture. Choice B describes pain provoked by resisted wrist extension, which is associated with common extensor tendon injury at the lateral epicondyle. Choice C indicates medial collateral ligament insufficiency, typically associated with repetitive valgus stress or throwing. Choice D is a positive Tinel sign over the ulnar nerve and suggests ulnar neuropathy at the elbow. Choice E describes Froment sign, which reflects weakness of the ulnar-innervated adductor pollicis rather than injury at the anterior elbow.

Question 265  [ID: cb42e639f7024a88851f41f452a7d12e]
A 48-year-old man presents with 6 weeks of progressive bilateral calf pain, fatigue, and an unintentional 5-kg (11-lb) weight loss. The pain is worse with activity and has not improved with over-the-counter analgesics. He also reports intermittent postprandial abdominal pain and new burning pain with weakness of the right foot. His medical history is notable for untreated chronic hepatitis B infection. Temperature is 37.8°C (100.0°F), blood pressure is 168/96 mm Hg, and pulse is 92/min. Examination reveals tender calf muscles, retiform violaceous discoloration of both legs, and several tender subcutaneous nodules. Right ankle dorsiflexion is weak, and sensation is decreased over the dorsum of the right foot. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 74 mm/hr, mildly elevated serum creatinine, and a normal creatine kinase level. Urinalysis shows no erythrocyte casts. Pulmonary examination and chest radiography are normal. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Dermatomyositis
B. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis
C. Polyarteritis nodosa
D. Polymyalgia rheumatica
E. Microscopic polyangiitis

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: Polyarteritis nodosa is the most likely diagnosis. The history and examination demonstrate a characteristic combination of constitutional symptoms, activity-related myalgias with normal creatine kinase, livedo-type skin changes, tender subcutaneous nodules, hypertension, postprandial abdominal pain, and asymmetric peripheral nerve dysfunction consistent with mononeuritis multiplex. Chronic hepatitis B infection is an important associated historical finding. Renal involvement typically results from ischemic injury to medium-sized renal arteries, producing hypertension and impaired kidney function without glomerulonephritis; therefore, erythrocyte casts are generally absent. Pulmonary involvement is also characteristically absent. Dermatomyositis causes symmetric proximal weakness, elevated muscle enzymes, and characteristic cutaneous findings such as heliotrope rash and Gottron papules rather than mononeuritis multiplex and postprandial abdominal pain. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis commonly causes upper airway disease, pulmonary abnormalities, and rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis. Polymyalgia rheumatica occurs almost exclusively in adults older than 50 years and causes proximal shoulder and hip girdle pain and stiffness without nodules, neuropathy, or abdominal ischemic symptoms. Microscopic polyangiitis commonly produces glomerulonephritis and pulmonary capillaritis, findings absent in this patient.

Question 266  [ID: cc6f48e489a544d48afbd7fa9f8c001f]
A 15-year-old boy is evaluated for a progressively rounded upper back first noticed 2 years ago. He reports mild aching between the shoulder blades after prolonged sitting but denies trauma, weakness, paresthesias, bowel or bladder dysfunction, fever, or weight loss. Examination shows an exaggerated posterior thoracic curvature, forward-positioned shoulders, and tight hamstrings. Neurologic examination is normal. The prominence becomes more apparent when he bends forward. Which of the following additional physical examination findings would most strongly support a fixed structural deformity rather than a flexible postural abnormality?

A. Complete correction of the curvature when the patient lies supine
B. Normalization of the curvature with active thoracic extension
C. Persistence of the curvature during hyperextension over a firm bolster
D. Asymmetric elevation of one side of the rib cage during forward bending
E. Reproduction of radicular pain with passive straight-leg raising

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: Persistence of the exaggerated posterior curvature during active or passive hyperextension indicates a rigid structural deformity, as occurs with vertebral wedging in adolescent structural kyphosis. The examination should assess curve flexibility by asking the patient to extend while standing or by positioning the patient supine over a bolster. A: Complete correction while supine indicates a flexible postural curvature rather than a fixed deformity. B: Normalization with active extension also supports a flexible postural abnormality. D: Asymmetric rib elevation during forward bending suggests vertebral rotation associated with scoliosis, not an isolated fixed sagittal-plane deformity. E: Radicular pain during straight-leg raising suggests lumbosacral nerve root irritation and does not assess the flexibility of the thoracic curvature.

Question 267  [ID: ce2c7a2781df4231a8bbb3fa13e37239]
A 16-year-old boy presents with 2 months of worsening pain near his left knee. He initially noticed the discomfort after basketball practice, but he denies a specific injury. The pain now occurs at rest, awakens him at night, and has not improved with ibuprofen. Examination reveals localized tenderness and subtle swelling over the distal thigh without erythema. He has a mild antalgic gait. Plain radiographs demonstrate a mixed lytic and sclerotic lesion centered in the distal femoral metaphysis with cortical disruption and an aggressive periosteal reaction. Which additional finding elicited during the history and physical examination would most strongly support the leading clinical concern?

A. A progressively enlarging, firm, fixed mass at the site of pain
B. Brief morning stiffness that resolves after several minutes of activity
C. Pain reproduced only with resisted knee extension
D. Diffuse joint-line tenderness with clicking during knee rotation
E. Intermittent pain that resolves completely with rest and activity modification

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is most concerning for osteosarcoma, a malignant primary bone tumor that commonly occurs in adolescents and typically involves the metaphysis of a long bone, especially around the knee. Progressive pain that occurs at rest or at night, localized swelling, and a firm, fixed mass are important historical and physical examination findings. The mixed lytic and sclerotic appearance, cortical disruption, and aggressive periosteal reaction further support this diagnosis. A progressively enlarging, firm, fixed mass would therefore most strongly reinforce the clinical concern.

B is incorrect because brief morning stiffness improving with activity is more suggestive of a benign mechanical or mild inflammatory condition and does not specifically support an aggressive neoplasm.

C is incorrect because pain occurring only with resisted knee extension is more consistent with a quadriceps muscle or tendon injury.

D is incorrect because joint-line tenderness and clicking with rotation suggest intra-articular pathology, such as a meniscal injury.

E is incorrect because pain that resolves completely with rest and activity modification is characteristic of an overuse injury. Pain from osteosarcoma typically becomes progressive, persists at rest, and may awaken the patient at night.

Question 268  [ID: ce924f6df2e94d3280651f343c0a9812]
A 68-year-old woman presents with acute mid-back pain that began after lifting a bag of groceries 2 days ago. She has no history of major trauma. Her medical history is significant for menopause at age 44 and rheumatoid arthritis treated with prednisone for the past 6 years. She does not smoke or drink alcohol. Vital signs are normal. Physical examination demonstrates focal tenderness over the lower thoracic vertebrae without erythema or warmth. Strength, sensation, and deep tendon reflexes are intact in both lower extremities. Radiographs demonstrate an anterior wedge compression fracture of T11 without a destructive lesion. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry reveals a T-score of -2.8 at the femoral neck. Serum calcium, phosphate, alkaline phosphatase, creatinine, and 25-hydroxyvitamin D levels are within normal limits. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Osteomalacia
B. Osteoporosis
C. Primary hyperparathyroidism
D. Paget disease of bone
E. Multiple myeloma

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. This patient has a low-trauma vertebral compression fracture and a femoral neck T-score of -2.8, which meets the densitometric criterion for osteoporosis of a T-score at or below -2.5. Her risk factors include advanced age, postmenopausal status, early menopause, rheumatoid arthritis, and prolonged glucocorticoid therapy. Normal calcium, phosphate, alkaline phosphatase, and vitamin D levels are also typical because osteoporosis involves reduced bone mass with preserved mineralization. A is incorrect because osteomalacia results from impaired mineralization, commonly due to vitamin D deficiency, and is typically associated with diffuse bone pain, proximal muscle weakness, low phosphate or calcium, and elevated alkaline phosphatase. C is incorrect because primary hyperparathyroidism generally causes hypercalcemia, hypophosphatemia, and an elevated parathyroid hormone level. D is incorrect because Paget disease usually causes focal skeletal enlargement or deformity and a markedly elevated alkaline phosphatase level with normal calcium and phosphate. E is incorrect because multiple myeloma may cause pathologic fractures but is more likely to produce lytic lesions, anemia, renal dysfunction, hypercalcemia, or a monoclonal protein abnormality.

Question 269  [ID: ceb2dd4de5804f8a93fa642dddde9f5d]
A 36-year-old man presents after landing awkwardly with his right leg flexed while playing basketball. He felt a sudden pop followed by immediate anterior pain and an inability to continue playing. Examination shows a large effusion, superior displacement of the patella, and a palpable defect immediately inferior to the patella. He is unable to actively extend the leg or perform a straight-leg raise, although passive motion is preserved. Distal pulses and sensation are intact. A complete blood count and metabolic panel are unremarkable. Initial anteroposterior and lateral radiographs show no acute fracture. Which of the following additional diagnostic findings would most strongly support the suspected cause of this patient's presentation?

A. Point-of-care ultrasonography showing complete fiber discontinuity between the inferior patellar pole and tibial tubercle with an intervening hematoma
B. Magnetic resonance imaging showing disruption of the anterior cruciate ligament with anterior translation of the tibia
C. Magnetic resonance imaging showing a vertical tear involving the posterior horn of the medial meniscus
D. Point-of-care ultrasonography showing fiber discontinuity proximal to the superior patellar pole
E. Lateral radiography showing inferior displacement of the patella with an Insall-Salvati ratio less than 0.8

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The sudden eccentric load on a flexed leg, palpable defect below the patella, superiorly displaced patella, and inability to perform active extension or a straight-leg raise indicate disruption of the patellar tendon. Ultrasonography can rapidly demonstrate loss of tendon continuity, retracted fibers, and an intervening hematoma. Lateral radiographs may also show patella alta, typically with an Insall-Salvati ratio greater than 1.2. Choice B is incorrect because an anterior cruciate ligament tear may cause a pop, effusion, and instability but does not typically produce a palpable infrapatellar defect or loss of active extension. Choice C is incorrect because a meniscal tear more commonly causes joint-line tenderness, locking, or catching without loss of the straight-leg raise. Choice D describes disruption of the quadriceps tendon, which occurs superior to the patella and typically produces inferior displacement of the patella. Choice E is incorrect because inferior patellar displacement and an Insall-Salvati ratio below 0.8 indicate patella baja, which is more consistent with a quadriceps tendon injury than with the lesion described here.

Question 270  [ID: cee92daf283c4384adc37dcc27eab88e]
A 24-year-old man presents with 10 months of progressive low back and buttock pain. The pain is worse during the early morning, is associated with approximately 1 hour of stiffness, and improves with exercise but not with rest. He denies preceding trauma. Examination reveals limited lumbar flexion, decreased chest expansion, and tenderness over both sacroiliac regions. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein are elevated; rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies are negative. Plain radiographs of the lumbar spine show no significant abnormalities. Which of the following additional diagnostic findings would most strongly support the suspected diagnosis at this stage?

A. Lumbar MRI showing posterolateral disc herniation with nerve root compression
B. MRI of the sacroiliac joints showing bilateral subchondral bone marrow edema
C. Serum uric acid elevation with negatively birefringent crystals in synovial fluid
D. Bone scintigraphy showing focal increased uptake in a lumbar vertebral body
E. Pelvic radiograph showing superior migration and joint-space narrowing of the femoral head

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The patient's young age, insidious inflammatory back pain that improves with activity, prolonged morning stiffness, reduced lumbar mobility, decreased chest expansion, sacroiliac tenderness, and elevated inflammatory markers suggest axial inflammatory disease. MRI of the sacroiliac joints is the most sensitive study for early disease and may demonstrate active inflammation as bilateral subchondral bone marrow edema before structural changes become visible on plain radiographs. A is incorrect because a disc herniation typically causes radicular pain with neurologic findings and does not explain prolonged morning stiffness or decreased chest expansion. C is incorrect because monosodium urate crystals indicate gout, which usually presents as episodic acute peripheral monoarthritis. D is incorrect because focal vertebral uptake is more concerning for a localized process such as fracture, infection, or malignancy rather than symmetric sacroiliac inflammation. E is incorrect because superior femoral head migration and hip joint-space narrowing are characteristic of degenerative or inflammatory hip arthropathy but do not best confirm this early axial presentation.

Question 271  [ID: cf9cd7d744d241d28cba9e8e00ed3d2d]
A 16-year-old male competitive gymnast presents with 3 months of gradually worsening focal low back pain. The pain increases during tumbling and dismounts, particularly when he extends or rotates his trunk, and improves with rest. He denies fever, nocturnal pain, lower-extremity weakness, paresthesias, and bowel or bladder dysfunction. Examination demonstrates focal tenderness over the lower lumbar region and reproduction of pain when he stands on one leg and hyperextends his trunk. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes are normal. An oblique radiograph demonstrates a linear lucency through the region between the superior and inferior articular processes of the fifth lumbar vertebra without vertebral displacement. Which of the following best describes the pathophysiologic basis of this patient's condition?

A. Repetitive extension and rotation causing a stress fracture of the pars interarticularis
B. Degeneration of the nucleus pulposus causing posterolateral displacement through the annulus fibrosus
C. Failure of vertebral end-plate ossification causing anterior vertebral body wedging
D. Inflammation of the sacroiliac articulations due to immune-mediated enthesitis
E. Hematogenous bacterial seeding of an intervertebral disc and adjacent vertebral end plates

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. The pars interarticularis is the portion of the vertebral neural arch located between the superior and inferior articular processes. Repetitive lumbar extension and rotation, particularly in adolescent gymnasts, football linemen, and other athletes, can produce cyclic mechanical loading and a stress fracture at this site. Typical findings include focal low back pain worsened by extension, a positive single-leg hyperextension test, a normal neurologic examination, and a lucency through the pars region on imaging. The absence of vertebral displacement indicates that associated anterior translation has not occurred. B describes intervertebral disc herniation, which more commonly causes radicular pain, sensory changes, weakness, or altered reflexes when a nerve root is compressed. C describes the developmental end-plate abnormality associated with juvenile kyphosis, which produces anterior wedging of multiple contiguous vertebral bodies rather than a focal posterior-element lucency. D describes inflammatory sacroiliitis, which typically presents with inflammatory back pain, prolonged morning stiffness, improvement with activity, and possible extra-articular manifestations. E describes vertebral osteomyelitis with disc-space infection, which is more likely to cause constant pain, systemic symptoms, elevated inflammatory markers, and destructive changes involving the disc space and adjacent end plates.

Question 272  [ID: d07b308a937f43db9a6b4295dcda62b6]
A 28-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a high-speed motor vehicle collision. He reports severe right upper thoracic pain and numbness in his right hand. Blood pressure is 104/68 mm Hg, pulse is 112/min, respirations are 24/min, and oxygen saturation is 95% on room air. Examination reveals right supraclavicular swelling and tenderness. The right upper extremity is cooler than the left, and the right radial pulse is diminished. Breath sounds are equal bilaterally. Hemoglobin is 11.2 g/dL. A portable radiograph demonstrates a linear lucency with cortical discontinuity through the posterior aspect of the right first rib without pneumothorax. Which of the following is the most appropriate next diagnostic study?

A. CT angiography of the chest and thoracic outlet with intravenous contrast
B. Dedicated rib radiographs with oblique views
C. Noncontrast CT of the chest
D. MRI of the brachial plexus
E. Duplex ultrasonography of the right upper extremity veins

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Injury to the first rib generally requires substantial force and is associated with damage to nearby structures, particularly the subclavian artery and vein. This patient has concerning findings for arterial injury, including supraclavicular swelling, a cool extremity, and a diminished radial pulse. CT angiography with intravenous contrast is the preferred rapid, noninvasive study for evaluating the subclavian and other thoracic outlet vessels while also identifying active bleeding and associated traumatic injuries. Dedicated rib radiographs (B) may better characterize the osseous abnormality but would not assess the suspected vascular complication and would not change the immediate priority. Noncontrast CT (C) can delineate osseous injuries and pulmonary complications but cannot adequately evaluate arterial patency, contrast extravasation, or pseudoaneurysm. MRI of the brachial plexus (D) may be useful later if a persistent neurologic deficit suggests plexus injury, but vascular compromise must be evaluated urgently. Venous duplex ultrasonography (E) assesses venous thrombosis and is limited for visualizing the proximal subclavian vessels because of overlying structures; it also does not adequately assess the suspected arterial injury.

Question 273  [ID: d0eb4305a50a4121bc1e7858ffba17ae]
A 76-year-old woman presents to the emergency department with severe left groin pain and inability to bear weight after falling from standing height onto her left side. She has osteoporosis and takes alendronate. Examination shows no obvious deformity, but passive internal rotation of the left hip and axial loading reproduce severe pain. Distal pulses, sensation, and motor function are intact. Complete blood count and serum electrolyte levels are within normal limits. Anteroposterior pelvic and cross-table lateral radiographs show no acute abnormality. Which of the following is the most appropriate next diagnostic study?

A. Repeat plain radiographs in 7 to 10 days
B. MRI of the hip without contrast
C. CT angiography of the pelvis
D. Diagnostic ultrasonography of the hip
E. Technetium-99m bone scintigraphy

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: MRI without contrast is the preferred next study when an occult femoral neck fracture is strongly suspected despite normal initial radiographs. This patient's advanced age, osteoporosis, inability to bear weight, groin pain, and pain with passive rotation and axial loading create a high pretest probability. MRI has excellent sensitivity and specificity and can identify nondisplaced injuries, associated marrow edema, and alternative causes of pain. A is incorrect because delaying imaging may allow displacement and increase the risks of avascular necrosis, nonunion, and morbidity from immobilization. C is incorrect because CT angiography evaluates vascular injury, which is not suggested by her intact pulses and neurologic examination. Standard noncontrast CT may be considered when MRI is unavailable or contraindicated, but angiography is inappropriate here. D is incorrect because ultrasonography may detect a joint effusion but cannot reliably exclude an occult osseous injury. E is incorrect because bone scintigraphy may detect increased metabolic activity but is less specific, may remain negative early after injury, and has largely been replaced by MRI for this indication.

Question 274  [ID: d13f736ff1694606b07394c29f355423]
A 24-year-old man presents to the emergency department with rapidly worsening right lower-leg pain 6 hours after sustaining a closed tibial shaft fracture in a motorcycle collision. The fracture was reduced, and the extremity was immobilized in a circumferential cast. He now reports severe pain despite intravenous opioids and new tingling over the dorsum of the foot. Examination shows a tense, swollen lower leg with marked pain during passive extension of the great toe. Dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses are palpable, and capillary refill is 2 seconds. Radiographs show maintained fracture alignment without new displacement. Serum creatine kinase is 1,200 U/L. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms best explains this patient's worsening symptoms?

A. Arterial vasospasm causing transient reduction in distal blood flow despite normal tissue pressure
B. Rising pressure within a noncompliant fascial space causing venous outflow obstruction, reduced capillary perfusion, and tissue ischemia
C. Disruption of the sympathetic nerve supply causing loss of vascular tone and dependent edema
D. Immune-mediated destruction of peripheral myelin causing progressive sensory and motor dysfunction
E. Occlusion of a proximal deep vein causing increased hydrostatic pressure without impairment of arterial inflow

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The patient has acute compartment syndrome following a tibial fracture and circumferential casting. Bleeding and tissue edema within a closed, noncompliant fascial compartment progressively increase local pressure. Venous outflow is impaired first, which raises venous and interstitial pressures and further worsens edema. As tissue pressure approaches or exceeds capillary perfusion pressure, oxygen delivery declines, producing muscle and nerve ischemia. This explains the severe pain out of proportion to the injury, pain with passive muscle stretch, tense swelling, paresthesia, and elevated creatine kinase. Palpable distal pulses do not exclude the diagnosis because arterial pressure may remain sufficient to preserve pulses until late in the course. Choice A is incorrect because isolated arterial vasospasm would more likely cause coolness, pallor, diminished pulses, and reduced distal perfusion rather than a tense swollen extremity with prominent pain on passive stretch. Choice C is incorrect because sympathetic denervation does not produce rapidly rising tissue pressure and ischemic injury after fracture and casting. Choice D is incorrect because an immune-mediated demyelinating neuropathy typically causes progressive, often symmetric weakness and sensory changes rather than acute focal pain and swelling after trauma. Choice E is incorrect because deep venous thrombosis may cause swelling and discomfort but does not typically cause severe pain with passive stretch, a tense fascial space, or rapid muscle ischemia in this setting.

Question 275  [ID: d2a8e50d3195487e9f411bd53b20307f]
A 24-year-old woman presents to the emergency department with sudden painful twisting of her neck and difficulty returning her head to a neutral position. Earlier today, she received intravenous prochlorperazine for severe nausea. Two hours later, she developed sustained contraction of the left sternocleidomastoid muscle, causing her chin to rotate toward the right. She is alert and afebrile, and her airway is patent. Strength, sensation, and deep tendon reflexes are normal in all extremities. Complete blood count and serum electrolyte levels are within normal limits. Radiographs of the cervical spine show no fracture, dislocation, or prevertebral soft-tissue swelling. Which of the following is the best immediate pharmacologic treatment?

A. Intravenous diphenhydramine
B. Oral baclofen
C. Intramuscular haloperidol
D. Oral carbidopa-levodopa
E. Subcutaneous sumatriptan

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has acute torticollis caused by a medication-induced dystonic reaction after receiving prochlorperazine, a dopamine D2 receptor antagonist. Acute dystonic reactions often occur within hours to several days of exposure and may produce sustained, painful contraction of the cervical muscles. Immediate treatment is a parenteral anticholinergic agent, such as diphenhydramine or benztropine. Intravenous diphenhydramine typically produces rapid improvement and is widely available in emergency settings. An oral course may be continued briefly to prevent recurrence because the causative drug may have a longer half-life. Baclofen is used for chronic spasticity and selected persistent dystonias but does not provide the rapid anticholinergic effect needed here. Haloperidol is another dopamine antagonist and may worsen the reaction. Carbidopa-levodopa is primarily used for Parkinson disease and certain dopamine-responsive dystonias, not an acute medication-induced reaction. Sumatriptan is used for migraine and cluster headache and has no role in treating sustained involuntary cervical muscle contraction.

Question 276  [ID: d2d13c4f8ab94b0793df38dc69d2daa9]
A 46-year-old man presents after abruptly changing direction while playing basketball. He reports hearing a pop and feeling as though he had been kicked in the back of the ankle. He has difficulty walking and is unable to push off with the affected foot. Examination shows swelling and ecchymosis posterior to the ankle, a palpable defect approximately 4 cm proximal to the calcaneal insertion, and absent plantar flexion when the calf is squeezed. Plain radiographs show no acute fracture. Which of the following diagnostic studies is most appropriate to confirm the suspected injury and assess the degree of tissue discontinuity?

A. Dynamic ultrasonography of the affected area
B. Computed tomography without contrast
C. Electromyography and nerve conduction studies
D. Venous duplex ultrasonography
E. Repeat plain radiographs with weight-bearing views

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Dynamic ultrasonography is the most appropriate confirmatory study. It can rapidly demonstrate fiber discontinuity, measure the gap between retracted ends, distinguish partial from complete disruption, and assess approximation during ankle movement. The described mechanism, posterior defect, impaired push-off, and absent plantar flexion with calf compression strongly support an Achilles tendon rupture. Although this diagnosis is often clinical, ultrasonography is useful when confirmation or characterization is needed. Computed tomography is primarily useful for detailed evaluation of bone and is less sensitive for this type of soft-tissue injury. Electromyography and nerve conduction studies evaluate peripheral nerve dysfunction and would not define acute structural disruption. Venous duplex ultrasonography evaluates deep venous thrombosis, which may cause swelling and pain but does not explain the acute pop, palpable defect, or abnormal calf-squeeze test. Repeat weight-bearing radiographs may identify occult bony alignment abnormalities but will not adequately characterize the injured tissue.

Question 277  [ID: d3815e5d3e5d4b81a0258de046dd8005]
A 46-year-old woman presents with a 3-day history of severe headache, blurred vision, and decreased urine output. For the past 2 years, she has experienced progressive tightening of the skin over her hands and face, episodic finger discoloration with cold exposure, difficulty swallowing solid foods, and shortness of breath with exertion. She recently completed a 2-week course of high-dose prednisone for worsening hand discomfort. Her blood pressure is 214/126 mm Hg, pulse is 96/min, and respirations are 18/min. Examination shows taut facial skin, reduced oral aperture, and firm, shiny skin extending proximal to the metacarpophalangeal joints. Funduscopic examination reveals retinal hemorrhages and cotton-wool spots. Laboratory studies show a serum creatinine of 3.1 mg/dL, increased from 0.8 mg/dL one month ago. Urinalysis demonstrates mild proteinuria and microscopic hematuria. A peripheral blood smear shows schistocytes, and the platelet count is 92,000/mm3. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate pharmacologic intervention?

A. Captopril
B. High-dose intravenous methylprednisolone
C. Nifedipine
D. Plasma exchange
E. Intravenous labetalol

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Captopril is the treatment of choice for scleroderma renal crisis, which is characterized by abrupt severe hypertension, rapidly progressive acute kidney injury, mild urinary abnormalities, and microangiopathic hemolytic anemia with thrombocytopenia. ACE inhibition should be initiated immediately and titrated aggressively, even if serum creatinine initially continues to rise. Captopril is often preferred initially because its short duration of action permits rapid dose adjustment. Recent high-dose glucocorticoid exposure is an important precipitating factor. B is incorrect because high-dose glucocorticoids can precipitate or worsen scleroderma renal crisis. C is incorrect because nifedipine may help vasospastic digital symptoms but does not adequately address the renin-mediated renal vascular injury. D is incorrect because the schistocytes and thrombocytopenia result from hypertension-associated microangiopathy; plasma exchange is used for thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura and is not first-line therapy here. E is incorrect because intravenous labetalol can lower blood pressure but does not inhibit the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system, which is central to this emergency. Other antihypertensives may be added if needed, but an ACE inhibitor is essential.

Question 278  [ID: d43b3fb57bb248448953e8a635d61335]
A 34-year-old man presents with severe right ankle pain and inability to bear weight after his planted foot was forcefully externally rotated during a soccer game. Examination shows swelling and tenderness over the medial ankle, pain with compression of the tibia and fibula at the mid-calf, and tenderness near the proximal fibula. The skin is intact, and distal pulses and sensation are normal. Anteroposterior ankle radiography demonstrates widening of the medial clear space and separation between the distal tibia and fibula. A full-length lower-leg radiograph shows an oblique lucent line with cortical disruption through the proximal third of the fibula. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Isolated lateral ankle sprain
B. Maisonneuve fracture
C. Isolated distal tibiofibular syndesmotic sprain
D. Trimalleolar fracture
E. Achilles tendon rupture

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. A Maisonneuve fracture results from a forceful external-rotation mechanism and consists of disruption of the distal tibiofibular syndesmosis with an associated proximal fibular fracture. Medial clear-space widening suggests deltoid ligament disruption or medial instability, while proximal fibular tenderness and cortical disruption identify the characteristic proximal injury. This unstable pattern may be missed if imaging is limited to the ankle, so the entire tibia and fibula should be evaluated. A is incorrect because an isolated lateral ankle sprain typically follows an inversion mechanism and causes lateral ligament tenderness without syndesmotic widening or proximal fibular injury. C is incorrect because an isolated syndesmotic sprain can cause pain with tibiofibular compression and external rotation, but it does not include the proximal fibular cortical disruption seen here. D is incorrect because a trimalleolar fracture involves the medial, lateral, and posterior malleoli at the ankle; the imaging instead localizes the fibular injury proximally. E is incorrect because an Achilles tendon rupture causes posterior ankle pain, weakness of plantar flexion, a palpable tendon defect, and an abnormal Thompson test rather than medial clear-space widening and proximal fibular injury.

Question 279  [ID: d4dd0a7a350042d2a543d41f8de103f8]
A 13-year-old boy with obesity presents with 3 weeks of progressive left groin and anterior thigh pain that occasionally radiates to the knee. He has developed a limp and now has difficulty bearing weight. There is no history of trauma or fever. Examination shows the left leg resting in external rotation, reduced hip flexion, and markedly limited internal rotation. Flexion of the hip produces obligatory external rotation. Leukocyte count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are normal. An anteroposterior pelvic radiograph shows widening and irregularity of the proximal femoral growth plate, with the epiphysis displaced posteromedially relative to the metaphysis. The patient is immediately placed on strict non–weight-bearing status, and urgent operative stabilization is planned. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacologic therapy for symptom control while the patient awaits definitive treatment?

A. Oral ibuprofen
B. Intra-articular triamcinolone
C. Intravenous cefazolin as sole therapy
D. Subcutaneous somatropin
E. Oral alendronate

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This adolescent has the characteristic presentation and radiographic findings of slipped capital femoral epiphysis. Definitive management requires urgent in situ operative fixation, and the patient must remain strictly non–weight-bearing to reduce the risk of further displacement and avascular necrosis. An NSAID such as ibuprofen is appropriate for short-term analgesia while definitive treatment is arranged, assuming there are no renal, gastrointestinal, bleeding, or hypersensitivity contraindications. Ibuprofen provides pain relief but does not replace urgent surgical stabilization. Intra-articular triamcinolone is not indicated and could delay definitive care or introduce infection. Cefazolin may be used for perioperative antimicrobial prophylaxis, but antibiotic therapy alone does not treat this noninfectious condition or provide adequate symptom control. Somatropin is inappropriate because growth hormone therapy can increase the risk of proximal femoral physeal displacement and is not indicated in an adolescent with obesity and no demonstrated deficiency. Alendronate is used for selected disorders of abnormal bone resorption and has no therapeutic role in acute physeal displacement.

Question 280  [ID: d55849e1f4394e08ab0a660df0087f4d]
A 74-year-old man presents with 18 hours of abrupt, severe pain and swelling of the right knee. He denies trauma, recent surgery, or prior similar episodes. His medical history includes hypertension, primary hyperparathyroidism, and chronic kidney disease. His temperature is 37.8°C (100.0°F), blood pressure is 138/82 mm Hg, and pulse is 92/min. Examination shows a warm, erythematous, markedly swollen right knee with a large effusion and severe pain during passive movement. Laboratory studies reveal a leukocyte count of 12,800/mm3 and serum calcium of 11.2 mg/dL. Plain radiographs demonstrate linear calcification within the articular cartilage. Arthrocentesis is performed. Which of the following synovial fluid findings would most strongly support the suspected diagnosis?

A. Needle-shaped crystals with strong negative birefringence under polarized light
B. Rhomboid-shaped crystals with weak positive birefringence under polarized light
C. Nonbirefringent spherical deposits that stain with alizarin red
D. Plate-like crystals with notched corners and strong positive birefringence
E. Gram-positive cocci with a synovial leukocyte count greater than 50,000/mm3

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The abrupt onset of severe monoarticular pain and swelling in an older adult, together with hyperparathyroidism and linear calcification of articular cartilage, strongly suggests pseudogout caused by calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate deposition. The characteristic synovial fluid finding is rhomboid-shaped crystals with weak positive birefringence under compensated polarized light. Choice A describes monosodium urate crystals, which are associated with gout and are needle-shaped with strong negative birefringence. Choice C describes basic calcium phosphate deposition; these crystals are generally not visible with routine polarized microscopy but may be detected with alizarin red staining. Choice D describes cholesterol crystals, which may occur in chronic effusions and appear as large plates with notched corners. Choice E suggests septic arthritis. Infection must remain in the differential diagnosis for any acute hot, swollen joint, and synovial fluid should be sent for Gram stain and culture; however, the radiographic calcification and associated metabolic risk factor favor calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition.

Question 281  [ID: d57aeb33f562487693da915036d438f8]
A 67-year-old woman presents for an annual wellness examination without acute complaints. She underwent menopause at age 49 years and has never used hormone therapy. She has a 20-pack-year smoking history, drinks one glass of wine nightly, and performs little regular exercise. She has no history of low-impact fractures. Her BMI is 19 kg/m². Physical examination shows mild thoracic kyphosis but no focal tenderness or gait instability. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry reveals T-scores of −2.7 at the femoral neck and −2.6 at the lumbar spine. Serum calcium, creatinine, alkaline phosphatase, and thyroid-stimulating hormone levels are within normal limits, and the serum 25-hydroxyvitamin D level is 32 ng/mL. Which of the following is the most appropriate preventive management plan to reduce this patient's risk of future low-trauma fractures?

A. Begin weekly oral alendronate; encourage smoking cessation, weight-bearing and resistance exercise, fall-prevention measures, and a total daily calcium intake of approximately 1,200 mg with adequate vitamin D
B. Recommend calcium and vitamin D supplementation alone and repeat dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry in 5 years
C. Begin systemic estrogen-progestin therapy and advise avoidance of weight-bearing exercise
D. Begin intranasal calcitonin as first-line long-term preventive therapy and restrict dietary calcium
E. Provide fall-prevention counseling without medication because she has no history of fracture

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's T-scores are at or below −2.5, establishing osteoporosis and indicating a sufficiently elevated fracture risk to warrant pharmacologic therapy even without a prior fragility fracture. In a patient without esophageal disease, inability to remain upright, or significant renal impairment, an oral bisphosphonate such as alendronate is appropriate first-line therapy. Comprehensive prevention also includes smoking cessation, regular weight-bearing and resistance exercise, fall-risk reduction, adequate dietary calcium totaling approximately 1,200 mg/day for women older than 50 years, and sufficient vitamin D intake. Choice B is incorrect because calcium and vitamin D alone do not adequately reduce fracture risk when bone density meets criteria for pharmacologic treatment; waiting 5 years to reassess is also inappropriate. Choice C is incorrect because systemic menopausal hormone therapy is not first-line treatment in a 67-year-old woman due to thromboembolic, cardiovascular, and malignancy risks, and weight-bearing exercise should be encouraged rather than avoided. Choice D is incorrect because calcitonin has limited antifracture efficacy and is not preferred for long-term first-line prevention; calcium restriction is also inappropriate. Choice E is incorrect because the absence of a prior fracture does not eliminate the need for medication when the T-score is −2.5 or lower.
