Question 282  [ID: d5a0bb75e1fb40b68245e3ee0ee73ec4]
A 27-year-old man presents with 9 months of progressive lower back and buttock pain. The pain is worse during the early morning, awakens him during the second half of the night, and improves with exercise but not with rest. He reports morning stiffness lasting approximately 90 minutes. He has no history of trauma, fever, weight loss, or bowel or bladder dysfunction. Physical examination reveals reduced lumbar flexion, limited chest expansion, and tenderness over both sacroiliac regions. The erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein level are elevated. Testing for rheumatoid factor is negative. Pelvic radiographs demonstrate bilateral sacroiliac joint-space narrowing with subchondral erosions and sclerosis. He has no history of gastrointestinal bleeding, chronic kidney disease, or cardiovascular disease. Which of the following is the best initial pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. Naproxen administered at an anti-inflammatory dosage
B. Methotrexate administered once weekly
C. Infliximab administered by intravenous infusion
D. Prednisone administered as long-term daily therapy
E. Oxycodone administered as needed for pain

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has inflammatory axial pain characterized by onset before age 40, prolonged morning stiffness, nocturnal symptoms, improvement with activity, restricted spinal mobility, reduced chest expansion, elevated inflammatory markers, and bilateral radiographic sacroiliitis. A nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as naproxen, given at an adequate anti-inflammatory dosage, is the preferred initial pharmacologic treatment when no contraindication exists. NSAIDs reduce pain and stiffness and improve function; they should be combined with regular exercise and physical therapy. Methotrexate may help selected patients with significant peripheral joint involvement but has little benefit for predominantly axial symptoms. Infliximab, a tumor necrosis factor inhibitor, is appropriate for persistent active disease despite adequate trials of NSAIDs and nonpharmacologic therapy, rather than as routine initial treatment. Long-term systemic prednisone is not recommended because of limited efficacy for axial disease and substantial risks, including osteoporosis, infection, hyperglycemia, and adrenal suppression. Opioids do not treat the underlying inflammatory process and should not be used as first-line therapy for chronic inflammatory back pain.

Question 283  [ID: d675b485355f4f41a25a0d44d9e0d234]
A 58-year-old man presents with severe right shoulder pain and weakness after forcefully lifting a heavy box above his head 2 days ago. He felt a sudden tearing sensation and has since been unable to raise the affected arm independently. Examination shows no visible deformity. Passive range of motion is nearly full, but active abduction is markedly limited. The drop-arm test is positive, and external rotation strength is decreased. Sensation and distal pulses are intact. Plain radiographs show no fracture, dislocation, or significant degenerative changes. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic study to characterize the extent of this patient's injury and guide definitive management?

A. Computed tomography of the shoulder without contrast
B. Magnetic resonance imaging of the shoulder without contrast
C. Electromyography and nerve conduction studies
D. Radionuclide bone scan
E. Repeat plain radiographs with additional views

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The acute traumatic onset, preserved passive range of motion, profound weakness with active abduction, positive drop-arm test, and weakness with external rotation strongly suggest an acute full-thickness rotator cuff tear. MRI without contrast is the preferred study for defining tear location, size, tendon retraction, muscle atrophy, and associated soft-tissue abnormalities. These findings help determine whether early operative repair is appropriate. A is incorrect because CT is most useful for detailed evaluation of bone and does not characterize rotator cuff integrity as well as MRI. C is incorrect because electrodiagnostic testing evaluates peripheral nerve injury or cervical radiculopathy; this patient's acute traumatic mechanism and examination findings localize the problem to the shoulder rather than a neurologic lesion. D is incorrect because a bone scan is primarily used to detect occult osseous pathology, infection, or metastatic disease and does not adequately define acute soft-tissue disruption. E is incorrect because additional radiographs may identify subtle bony abnormalities but cannot determine the presence, size, or degree of retraction of a full-thickness tear.

Question 284  [ID: d7513ee40593488e9c053e6316c0fc0c]
A 67-year-old man presents with 18 hours of severe right knee pain and swelling. He is unable to bear weight and reports chills. His medical history includes type 2 diabetes mellitus and chronic kidney disease. Temperature is 38.6°C (101.5°F), blood pressure is 132/78 mm Hg, pulse is 104/min, and respirations are 18/min. The right knee is warm, erythematous, and markedly swollen, with severe pain during both active and passive range of motion. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 16,800/mm3, erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 72 mm/hr, and C-reactive protein level of 18 mg/dL. Plain radiographs demonstrate a large effusion without fracture or bony erosion. Which of the following is the most appropriate next diagnostic study?

A. Urgent arthrocentesis with synovial fluid cell count, differential, crystal analysis, Gram stain, and culture
B. Serum uric acid measurement
C. Contrast-enhanced MRI of the knee
D. Antinuclear antibody and rheumatoid factor testing
E. Repeat plain radiographs in 7 to 10 days

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. This patient has an acute, intensely painful monoarticular presentation with fever, inability to bear weight, pain with passive motion, leukocytosis, and markedly elevated inflammatory markers. Urgent arthrocentesis is the key diagnostic study when septic arthritis is suspected. Synovial fluid should be sent for white blood cell count with differential, crystal analysis, Gram stain, and culture. A high synovial leukocyte count with neutrophil predominance supports the diagnosis, but culture is required to identify the causative organism and guide antimicrobial therapy. Crystal analysis is also necessary because crystal-induced arthritis can closely mimic this presentation, and crystals may coexist with infection. Blood cultures should also be obtained, but they do not replace synovial fluid analysis. Serum uric acid measurement is neither sufficiently sensitive nor specific for acute gout and cannot exclude septic arthritis. MRI may identify adjacent osteomyelitis or deep soft-tissue involvement but would delay definitive fluid analysis and is not the best initial diagnostic study. Antinuclear antibody and rheumatoid factor testing are not useful in evaluating an acute febrile monoarticular presentation. Repeating radiographs would delay diagnosis and treatment; early radiographs commonly show only an effusion.

Question 285  [ID: d89470378323434ab743819d1bc5c494]
A 46-year-old woman presents with 4 months of progressively worsening difficulty climbing stairs, rising from a chair, and lifting objects above her head. She denies sensory changes, ocular symptoms, dysphagia, or recent medication use. Physical examination shows symmetric 4/5 strength of the shoulder and hip girdles, with preserved distal strength and normal deep tendon reflexes. No skin abnormalities are present. Laboratory studies reveal a creatine kinase level of 4,800 U/L, elevated aldolase, and mild elevations of aspartate aminotransferase and alanine aminotransferase. Thyroid-stimulating hormone and serum electrolyte levels are normal. Electromyography demonstrates increased insertional activity, fibrillation potentials, and short-duration, low-amplitude motor unit potentials. Which of the following findings is most likely on biopsy of affected tissue?

A. Endomysial infiltration by CD8-positive T lymphocytes with invasion of non-necrotic fibers
B. Perifascicular atrophy with predominantly perivascular CD4-positive T-lymphocyte infiltration
C. Rimmed vacuoles containing amyloid deposits and cytoplasmic inclusions
D. Selective type II fiber atrophy without inflammatory cell infiltration
E. Grouped fiber atrophy with fiber-type grouping due to denervation and reinnervation

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's progressive, symmetric proximal weakness, markedly elevated creatine kinase and aldolase levels, and myopathic electromyographic findings support an inflammatory myopathy. The characteristic biopsy finding in polymyositis is endomysial inflammation composed primarily of CD8-positive cytotoxic T lymphocytes that invade otherwise viable, non-necrotic fibers. Choice B describes the typical biopsy pattern of dermatomyositis, which features complement-mediated microangiopathy, perifascicular atrophy, and perivascular or perimysial CD4-positive inflammation; this patient has no characteristic cutaneous findings. Choice C is associated with inclusion body myositis, which typically causes slowly progressive, often asymmetric weakness involving the quadriceps and finger flexors in adults older than 50 years and responds poorly to immunosuppression. Choice D is characteristic of glucocorticoid-induced myopathy, which generally produces normal creatine kinase levels and lacks inflammatory infiltrates. Choice E indicates a neurogenic process, such as motor neuron disease or peripheral neuropathy; such disorders typically produce denervation findings rather than the short-duration, low-amplitude motor unit potentials seen here.

Question 286  [ID: d951874b1691425faba24e857a0d259c]
A 67-year-old man presents with sharp left-sided pain after falling against the edge of a bathtub 2 days ago. The pain worsens with inspiration, coughing, and movement. He denies fever, hemoptysis, or loss of consciousness. His temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), blood pressure is 138/82 mm Hg, pulse is 88/min, respiratory rate is 18/min, and oxygen saturation is 97% on room air. Examination shows localized tenderness and ecchymosis over the left lateral thorax without crepitus or paradoxical movement. Breath sounds are equal bilaterally. Radiographs demonstrate nondisplaced cortical discontinuities involving two adjacent lower ribs without pleural air or fluid. He is prescribed appropriate analgesia and discharged. Which of the following instructions is most important to reduce the risk of a pulmonary complication during recovery?

A. Apply a circumferential elastic wrap tightly around the thorax for 1 week
B. Perform regular incentive spirometry and deep-breathing exercises while maintaining adequate pain control
C. Remain on bed rest until pain with inspiration has completely resolved
D. Suppress coughing with scheduled antitussive medication
E. Take prophylactic oral antibiotics for 7 days

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has uncomplicated, nondisplaced rib fractures. Pain can lead to shallow ventilation, impaired coughing, secretion retention, atelectasis, and pneumonia, particularly in older adults. Adequate analgesia combined with frequent deep breathing, incentive spirometry, coughing, and early mobilization helps maintain ventilation and airway clearance and is the most important preventive counseling. Choice A is incorrect because circumferential binding restricts expansion and increases the risk of atelectasis and pneumonia. Choice C is incorrect because prolonged bed rest promotes deconditioning, secretion retention, and venous thromboembolism; activity should resume as tolerated. Choice D is incorrect because routine cough suppression impairs clearance of secretions, although the patient may support the painful area with a pillow while coughing. Choice E is incorrect because prophylactic antibiotics do not prevent pulmonary complications in uncomplicated closed rib fractures and should be reserved for a documented bacterial infection or another specific indication.

Question 287  [ID: daba8e16dac840a992deaed941fef0a3]
A 46-year-old man presents with sudden posterior ankle pain that began while he was accelerating during a recreational tennis match. He reports hearing a loud pop and feeling as though someone kicked him from behind. He is unable to continue playing or push off with the affected foot. Examination reveals swelling and a palpable defect several centimeters proximal to the calcaneus. When the calf is compressed with the patient lying prone and the foot extending beyond the examination table, the affected foot does not move. Plain radiographs show no acute osseous abnormality. Which structure has most likely lost its ability to transmit force from the gastrocnemius and soleus muscles to the calcaneus?

A. Achilles tendon
B. Anterior talofibular ligament
C. Flexor hallucis longus tendon
D. Plantar fascia
E. Posterior talofibular ligament

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The Achilles tendon is the common distal tendon of the gastrocnemius and soleus muscles and inserts on the posterior calcaneus. Disruption causes sudden posterior ankle pain, a popping sensation, impaired push-off, a palpable defect, and absence of passive plantarflexion when the calf is compressed. This abnormal calf-squeeze response reflects loss of mechanical continuity between the calf muscles and calcaneus. The anterior talofibular ligament primarily resists inversion and anterior displacement of the talus and is commonly injured during an inversion ankle sprain; it does not transmit calf-muscle force. The flexor hallucis longus tendon flexes the great toe and assists plantarflexion but does not serve as the common tendon of the gastrocnemius and soleus. The plantar fascia supports the longitudinal arch and contributes to the windlass mechanism during gait; injury typically causes plantar heel or arch pain rather than an abnormal calf-squeeze response. The posterior talofibular ligament stabilizes the lateral ankle and is usually injured only in severe inversion trauma; it does not connect the calf muscles to the calcaneus.

Question 288  [ID: db8ca1744fb549d689376b5fe0668ce5]
A 27-year-old man presents with 5 days of pain and swelling in his right knee and left ankle. Three weeks ago, he had several days of watery diarrhea after eating undercooked poultry; the gastrointestinal symptoms resolved without treatment. He also reports redness of both eyes and pain at the insertion of the right Achilles tendon. He is afebrile. Examination shows nonpurulent conjunctival injection, a moderate right knee effusion, swelling and tenderness of the left ankle, and tenderness over the right Achilles insertion. Arthrocentesis of the knee yields cloudy fluid with a leukocyte count of 18,000/mm3 and negative Gram stain and culture. Nucleic acid amplification testing for Neisseria gonorrhoeae and Chlamydia trachomatis is negative. Radiographs show soft-tissue swelling without erosions. Which of the following is the best initial pharmacologic therapy for this patient's current symptoms?

A. Naproxen
B. Doxycycline
C. Methotrexate
D. Intravenous ceftriaxone
E. Etanercept

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has reactive arthritis following a gastrointestinal infection, demonstrated by acute asymmetric lower-extremity oligoarthritis, enthesitis, and conjunctivitis occurring several weeks after diarrheal illness. First-line pharmacologic treatment for acute articular symptoms is a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as naproxen. NSAIDs reduce pain and inflammation and are appropriate after septic arthritis has been reasonably excluded by synovial fluid analysis and culture.

B is incorrect because doxycycline is used to treat active Chlamydia trachomatis infection and may be indicated for the patient and sexual partners when testing is positive. This patient has negative testing and a preceding enteric infection; antibiotics do not improve the acute joint manifestations after the gastrointestinal infection has resolved.

C is incorrect because methotrexate may be considered for persistent or chronic symptoms that fail to respond to NSAIDs and local or systemic glucocorticoids. It is not first-line treatment for an initial acute episode.

D is incorrect because intravenous ceftriaxone is appropriate for disseminated gonococcal infection or bacterial septic arthritis. The subacute postinfectious presentation, negative gonococcal testing, and sterile synovial fluid culture make these diagnoses unlikely.

E is incorrect because tumor necrosis factor inhibitors such as etanercept may be considered only in severe, chronic, treatment-refractory disease. Their immunosuppressive risks make them inappropriate as initial therapy.

Question 289  [ID: dc159ba824f14b9a9bd1d7543564fd80]
A 26-year-old man presents with 9 months of progressively worsening lower back and alternating buttock pain. His symptoms are most severe in the morning, with stiffness lasting approximately 90 minutes, and improve with exercise but not with rest. He has no history of trauma. Two months ago, he was treated for an episode of acute unilateral eye pain, photophobia, and blurred vision. Physical examination shows decreased lumbar flexion, tenderness over both sacroiliac regions, and reduced chest expansion. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein are elevated; rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies are negative. Pelvic radiographs demonstrate bilateral, symmetric erosions and subchondral sclerosis of the sacroiliac joints. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Ankylosing spondylitis
B. Lumbar degenerative disk disease
C. Psoriatic arthritis
D. Rheumatoid arthritis
E. Reactive arthritis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Ankylosing spondylitis typically affects young adults and presents with inflammatory back pain characterized by morning stiffness, improvement with exercise, worsening with rest, and alternating buttock pain from sacroiliac involvement. Reduced lumbar flexion and chest expansion reflect progressive axial involvement. Bilateral symmetric sacroiliitis on imaging is a classic finding, and acute anterior uveitis is a common extra-articular manifestation. Elevated inflammatory markers with negative rheumatoid factor and anti-CCP antibodies further support the diagnosis. Lumbar degenerative disk disease generally causes mechanical pain that worsens with activity and improves with rest; it does not cause symmetric sacroiliitis or uveitis. Psoriatic arthritis may involve the axial skeleton but is more often associated with psoriasis, nail pitting, dactylitis, and asymmetric sacroiliitis. Rheumatoid arthritis primarily causes symmetric peripheral small-joint synovitis and usually spares the sacroiliac joints. Reactive arthritis can cause asymmetric lower-extremity arthritis and sacroiliac inflammation but usually follows a gastrointestinal or genitourinary infection and may be accompanied by urethritis or conjunctivitis.

Question 290  [ID: dcb3090e784743e8bce6da54d8e80e92]
A 26-year-old woman presents with 3 months of fatigue, intermittent low-grade fever, and pain and morning stiffness involving both hands and wrists. She also reports painless sores in her mouth and a facial eruption that worsens after sun exposure. Examination shows a temperature of 37.8°C (100.0°F), a well-demarcated erythematous eruption across the cheeks and nasal bridge, two shallow oral ulcers, and tenderness and swelling of multiple metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints. Radiographs of the hands show no erosions. Laboratory studies reveal hemoglobin of 10.2 g/dL, leukocyte count of 3,100/mm3, serum creatinine of 1.4 mg/dL, and urinalysis with 2+ protein, dysmorphic erythrocytes, and erythrocyte casts. Antinuclear and anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies are present, and serum C3 and C4 levels are decreased. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's clinical and laboratory findings?

A. Deposition of circulating antigen-antibody complexes with complement activation and inflammatory tissue injury
B. IgE-mediated mast cell degranulation following exposure to an environmental antigen
C. Antibody-mediated stimulation of cell-surface receptors causing persistent target-cell activation
D. Cytotoxic T-lymphocyte destruction of host cells displaying intracellular antigens
E. Defective collagen synthesis caused by impaired hydroxylation of proline and lysine residues

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has systemic lupus erythematosus, supported by constitutional symptoms, photosensitive malar eruption, painless oral ulcers, symmetric nonerosive inflammatory arthritis, cytopenias, nephritic urinary findings, positive antinuclear and anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies, and hypocomplementemia. The principal mechanism is a type III hypersensitivity reaction in which autoantibodies bind soluble nuclear antigens, forming circulating immune complexes. These complexes deposit in tissues, activate complement, recruit inflammatory cells, and produce tissue injury. Complement consumption accounts for the decreased C3 and C4 levels, while immune-complex deposition in glomeruli explains the proteinuria, dysmorphic erythrocytes, and erythrocyte casts. A is correct because it describes immune-complex deposition with complement-mediated inflammation. B describes a type I hypersensitivity reaction, as occurs in anaphylaxis and allergic disease, and does not explain autoantibodies, hypocomplementemia, or nephritic findings. C describes stimulatory type II hypersensitivity, classically seen when antibodies activate receptors in Graves disease; this is not the dominant mechanism here. D describes a type IV, T-cell-mediated hypersensitivity reaction, which is important in conditions such as contact dermatitis and type 1 diabetes mellitus but does not explain circulating immune complexes and complement consumption. E describes impaired collagen maturation associated with vitamin C deficiency, which can cause poor wound healing and bleeding but does not account for this patient's serologic and renal findings.

Question 291  [ID: dd42f78d5a2a4ffe9ccb24a383dfb981]
A 34-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe right wrist pain after falling from a ladder onto his outstretched hand 45 minutes ago. He has no significant medical history and no known medication allergies. Examination shows marked swelling and deformity of the wrist with a 2-cm laceration directly over the injured area. Moderate soft-tissue damage is present, and the wound communicates with the underlying bone. Radial pulse and capillary refill are normal, and sensation is intact. Radiographs demonstrate cortical discontinuity through the distal radius with dorsal displacement and comminution. Analgesia, sterile wound coverage, stabilization, urgent surgical consultation, and appropriate tetanus prophylaxis are initiated. Which of the following is the most appropriate antimicrobial therapy to administer at this time?

A. Oral cephalexin
B. Intravenous cefazolin
C. Intravenous vancomycin plus piperacillin-tazobactam
D. Oral doxycycline
E. Intravenous metronidazole

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has a Gustilo type II open distal radius fracture, characterized by a wound larger than 1 cm with moderate soft-tissue injury but without extensive contamination, vascular compromise, or major soft-tissue loss. Intravenous cefazolin should be administered as soon as possible, ideally within 1 hour of presentation, to provide coverage against gram-positive skin flora such as Staphylococcus and Streptococcus species. For type I and type II open fractures, a first-generation cephalosporin is standard initial prophylaxis, generally continued for approximately 24 hours after wound closure. A is incorrect because oral cephalexin does not provide the rapid, reliable serum and tissue concentrations required for initial prophylaxis. C is incorrect because broad-spectrum therapy with vancomycin and piperacillin-tazobactam is excessive in the absence of severe contamination, extensive soft-tissue destruction, or specific risk factors for resistant organisms. D is incorrect because doxycycline is not standard prophylaxis for this injury pattern and provides insufficient streptococcal coverage. E is incorrect because metronidazole provides anaerobic coverage but lacks adequate activity against the gram-positive organisms most commonly responsible for infection in this setting; anaerobic coverage is generally reserved for injuries with fecal, farm, or soil contamination.

Question 292  [ID: dd87082e94124da8b3f79b24f78957f6]
A 54-year-old man presents after falling against the edge of a boat 6 hours ago. He reports severe left-sided pain that worsens with inspiration, coughing, and movement. He has no significant medical history and takes no medications. Temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), blood pressure is 132/78 mm Hg, pulse is 88/min, respirations are 20/min, and oxygen saturation is 97% on room air. Examination shows localized tenderness and ecchymosis over the left lateral thorax without paradoxical movement or crepitus. Breath sounds are equal bilaterally. CT demonstrates acute, nondisplaced cortical discontinuities involving the left lateral sixth through eighth costal arches without pneumothorax, hemothorax, or pulmonary contusion. Serum creatinine and hepatic transaminase levels are normal. His pain limits deep inspiration despite application of ice. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic regimen for this patient?

A. Scheduled acetaminophen and a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug, with a short-acting opioid reserved for breakthrough pain
B. A long-acting opioid as monotherapy until the pain completely resolves
C. Oral prednisone followed by a gradual taper over 2 weeks
D. Prophylactic oral antibiotics and scheduled acetaminophen
E. A benzodiazepine at bedtime and a skeletal muscle relaxant during the day

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has uncomplicated, nondisplaced rib fractures without intrathoracic injury. Adequate analgesia is essential because pain-related splinting can impair ventilation and coughing, increasing the risk of atelectasis and pneumonia. A multimodal regimen using scheduled acetaminophen and an NSAID is appropriate because he has no renal, hepatic, gastrointestinal, or bleeding contraindications. A short-acting opioid may be added briefly for breakthrough pain that continues to limit deep breathing or coughing. Incentive spirometry and pulmonary hygiene should accompany analgesic therapy. Choice B is incorrect because long-acting opioid monotherapy is unnecessary for an acute injury and increases the risk of sedation, respiratory depression, constipation, and dependence. Choice C is incorrect because systemic corticosteroids do not improve healing or routine pain control in this setting and may cause adverse effects. Choice D is incorrect because closed, uncomplicated rib fractures do not require prophylactic antibiotics. Choice E is incorrect because benzodiazepines and skeletal muscle relaxants can cause sedation and suppress effective ventilation without adequately treating the primary source of pain.

Question 293  [ID: de57c22102ef4974b1a9652bdd7748ad]
A 28-year-old woman presents with 4 months of fatigue, intermittent low-grade fever, and pain and swelling of both hands and wrists. Her symptoms are most pronounced in the morning and improve after several hours. She also reports painless oral ulcers and a facial rash that worsens after sun exposure. Examination reveals a faint erythematous rash across the cheeks, two shallow oral ulcers, and tenderness and swelling of the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints bilaterally. Hand radiographs show no erosions. Laboratory studies reveal leukopenia, a positive antinuclear antibody titer, elevated anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies, and low serum complement levels. Serum creatinine and urinalysis are normal. Treatment with hydroxychloroquine is initiated. Which of the following is the most appropriate preventive recommendation regarding this medication?

A. Obtain a baseline comprehensive ophthalmologic examination and perform periodic retinal screening during long-term treatment
B. Obtain pulmonary function testing every 3 months to screen for medication-induced pulmonary fibrosis
C. Perform monthly thyroid-stimulating hormone measurements to screen for medication-induced hypothyroidism
D. Discontinue the medication during periods of increased sunlight exposure to prevent phototoxicity
E. Schedule annual colonoscopy to screen for medication-associated colorectal malignancy

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Hydroxychloroquine can cause irreversible retinal toxicity, particularly with excessive daily dosing, prolonged use, renal impairment, concomitant tamoxifen therapy, or preexisting retinal disease. Patients should receive a baseline comprehensive ophthalmologic examination to identify existing retinal abnormalities. In patients without major risk factors, annual screening generally begins after 5 years of therapy; earlier annual screening is appropriate for higher-risk patients. Recommended screening commonly includes automated visual-field testing and spectral-domain optical coherence tomography. The daily dose should generally not exceed 5 mg/kg of actual body weight. Pulmonary fibrosis is more characteristic of medications such as methotrexate and is not monitored with pulmonary function testing every 3 months in patients taking hydroxychloroquine. Routine monthly thyroid testing is not required because hypothyroidism is not a typical toxicity. Hydroxychloroquine does not need to be discontinued during periods of sunlight exposure; sun-protective clothing and broad-spectrum sunscreen are appropriate counseling measures for the patient's photosensitive manifestations. Hydroxychloroquine is not associated with colorectal malignancy, so colonoscopy should follow standard age- and risk-based screening guidelines.

Question 294  [ID: de69f4dfe72b4cc680b9328ce426ca03]
A 22-year-old man presents with right wrist pain after falling onto an outstretched hand while skateboarding 6 hours ago. He reports increasing pain with gripping and movement of the thumb. Examination shows mild swelling and focal tenderness in the anatomic snuffbox and over the scaphoid tubercle. Axial loading of the thumb reproduces the pain. Sensation, capillary refill, and radial pulse are normal. Posteroanterior, lateral, and oblique radiographs show no acute osseous abnormality. The patient is a competitive musician and requires prompt clarification of the injury to guide management. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic study to obtain next?

A. Magnetic resonance imaging of the wrist without contrast
B. Repeat plain radiographs in 10 to 14 days without immobilization
C. Diagnostic ultrasonography of the wrist
D. Computed tomography angiography of the upper extremity
E. Electrodiagnostic testing of the median and ulnar nerves

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Magnetic resonance imaging without contrast is the best next diagnostic study when there is high clinical suspicion for an occult scaphoid fracture despite normal initial radiographs. Anatomic snuffbox tenderness, scaphoid tubercle tenderness, and pain with axial loading of the thumb are characteristic findings. MRI has high sensitivity and specificity for an acute occult fracture and can also identify associated ligamentous or soft-tissue injury. It is particularly useful when an immediate diagnosis is needed. The wrist should remain immobilized in a thumb-spica splint while evaluation proceeds. Choice B is incorrect because repeat radiographs after 10 to 14 days can reveal a previously occult fracture, but the patient should not remain without immobilization, and this approach delays diagnosis. Choice C is incorrect because ultrasonography is operator-dependent and is not the preferred definitive test for an occult scaphoid injury. Choice D is incorrect because CT angiography evaluates vascular injury; this patient has intact perfusion and no findings suggesting arterial compromise. Choice E is incorrect because electrodiagnostic studies assess peripheral nerve dysfunction and are not indicated in an acute injury with a normal neurologic examination.

Question 295  [ID: de94a241716a4d518b76bfae1874b983]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 8 months of persistent, diffuse aching involving both sides of her body above and below the waist. She also reports nonrestorative sleep, daytime fatigue, intermittent headaches, and difficulty concentrating. Her symptoms worsen during periods of emotional stress. She denies joint swelling, prolonged morning stiffness, fever, weight loss, rash, or focal weakness. Examination shows normal vital signs, full range of motion, normal strength, and no joint warmth, swelling, or deformity. Complete blood count, comprehensive metabolic panel, thyroid-stimulating hormone, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein are within reference ranges. Which additional finding on history and physical examination would most strongly support the leading diagnosis?

A. Diffuse tenderness to moderate digital pressure in multiple body regions without objective tissue inflammation
B. Symmetric swelling and warmth of the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints
C. Reproducible proximal muscle weakness with an elevated serum creatine kinase level
D. Morning stiffness lasting longer than 1 hour with restricted lumbar flexion
E. Focal pain over the lateral epicondyle that worsens with resisted wrist extension

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's chronic widespread pain, fatigue, nonrestorative sleep, cognitive symptoms, normal inflammatory markers, and absence of objective synovitis or weakness are characteristic of fibromyalgia. A history demonstrating generalized pain for at least 3 months and examination showing diffuse tenderness without swelling, warmth, deformity, or other evidence of inflammation strongly support the diagnosis. Modern diagnostic criteria emphasize the distribution of pain and associated symptom severity rather than requiring a specific number of tender points. Choice B suggests an inflammatory polyarthritis such as rheumatoid arthritis. Choice C suggests an inflammatory or metabolic myopathy, which typically produces objective proximal weakness and may elevate creatine kinase. Choice D is more consistent with an inflammatory axial disorder, especially when prolonged stiffness and reduced spinal mobility are present. Choice E describes lateral epicondylitis, a localized overuse condition that does not explain chronic generalized pain or systemic symptoms.

Question 296  [ID: df9302602522407bbf6046efd5058a05]
A 48-year-old woman presents with 6 months of pain and morning stiffness in her hands and wrists. She also reports a persistent gritty sensation in both eyes, difficulty swallowing dry foods without water, and several recent dental caries. Examination shows decreased salivary pooling, bilateral parotid enlargement, and mild symmetric tenderness and swelling of the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints. Schirmer testing demonstrates markedly reduced tear production. Laboratory studies reveal a positive antinuclear antibody, rheumatoid factor, and anti-Ro/SSA and anti-La/SSB antibodies. Radiographs of the hands show no erosions. Her joint symptoms have persisted despite intermittent use of naproxen. Which of the following is the most appropriate long-term pharmacologic therapy for her inflammatory joint symptoms?

A. Allopurinol
B. Hydroxychloroquine
C. Infliximab
D. Pilocarpine
E. Prednisone at a high daily dose indefinitely

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Hydroxychloroquine is an appropriate first-line disease-modifying medication for persistent inflammatory arthralgia or nonerosive inflammatory arthritis in this patient. It may improve joint pain, stiffness, fatigue, and some systemic manifestations when NSAIDs alone are inadequate. Baseline and periodic ophthalmologic screening are required because of the risk of retinal toxicity. Allopurinol lowers uric acid and is used for chronic gout management; the symmetric small-joint symptoms and absence of crystal-associated findings do not support its use. Infliximab is a tumor necrosis factor inhibitor used for selected inflammatory conditions but is not recommended as routine initial therapy for this presentation because efficacy is limited and the risks are greater. Pilocarpine is a muscarinic agonist that can improve oral and ocular dryness but does not treat inflammatory joint manifestations. Long-term high-dose prednisone carries substantial risks, including osteoporosis, infection, hyperglycemia, hypertension, and adrenal suppression; systemic glucocorticoids should be reserved for severe organ involvement or used briefly as bridging therapy rather than indefinitely.

Question 297  [ID: e05a70a074ee4c968e0f021f80a1c94e]
A 62-year-old man with obesity, type 2 diabetes mellitus, and chronic kidney disease presents after stumbling while descending a staircase. He reports that his right leg was planted and flexed when he attempted to prevent himself from falling. He felt a sudden pop just above the kneecap, followed by immediate pain and difficulty walking. Examination shows swelling and ecchymosis above the kneecap. The kneecap appears lower than on the contralateral side. Plain radiographs show no acute fracture. Which of the following additional physical examination findings is most likely in this patient?

A. A palpable defect superior to the kneecap with inability to perform an active straight-leg raise
B. A palpable defect inferior to the kneecap with a high-riding kneecap
C. Tenderness along the medial joint line with pain and clicking during rotational compression
D. Increased anterior translation of the tibia with the knee flexed to 20–30 degrees
E. Localized tenderness over the tibial tubercle with preserved active extension

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is most consistent with a complete quadriceps tendon rupture, an extensor mechanism injury. Risk factors include older age, obesity, diabetes mellitus, and chronic kidney disease. The typical mechanism is a forceful eccentric contraction of the quadriceps while the knee is flexed. Characteristic examination findings include a palpable suprapatellar defect, loss of active knee extension, and inability to perform a straight-leg raise. A low-riding patella may be seen because the intact patellar tendon pulls the patella distally. Choice B describes a patellar tendon rupture, which produces an infrapatellar defect and a high-riding patella. Choice C suggests a meniscal injury. Choice D is characteristic of anterior cruciate ligament insufficiency on the Lachman test. Choice E may occur with pathology at the tibial tubercle, but preserved active extension argues against complete disruption of the extensor mechanism.

Question 298  [ID: e1855cd1e141470ba6eb3e6fa6d06c6d]
A 46-year-old woman presents with a 2-day history of severe headache, blurred vision, and decreased urine output. For the past year, she has experienced episodic finger discoloration with cold exposure, progressive tightening of the skin over her hands and face, difficulty swallowing, and painful swelling of several fingers. She recently completed a course of prednisone for worsening hand discomfort. Her blood pressure is 218/124 mm Hg, compared with 118/72 mm Hg at a visit 1 month ago. Examination shows taut, shiny skin over the fingers, digital tip ulcers, reduced oral aperture, and bilateral retinal hemorrhages. Laboratory studies reveal a serum creatinine level of 3.2 mg/dL, increased from a baseline of 0.8 mg/dL; urinalysis shows mild proteinuria and hematuria. A peripheral blood smear demonstrates schistocytes, and testing for anti-RNA polymerase III antibodies is positive. Which of the following medications should be initiated immediately?

A. Amlodipine
B. Captopril
C. Cyclophosphamide
D. Losartan
E. Methylprednisolone

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: This patient has systemic sclerosis complicated by scleroderma renal crisis, characterized by abrupt severe hypertension, acute kidney injury, retinal changes, and microangiopathic hemolytic anemia. Diffuse skin involvement, anti-RNA polymerase III antibodies, and recent glucocorticoid exposure further increase the risk. An angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor, typically short-acting captopril, should be started immediately and rapidly titrated. Treatment should be continued even if serum creatinine initially rises because ACE inhibition substantially reduces mortality and improves the likelihood of renal recovery.

A is incorrect because amlodipine can reduce the frequency and severity of Raynaud episodes but does not adequately treat the renin-mediated hypertensive emergency occurring here.

B is correct because captopril is the first-line medication for scleroderma renal crisis. Its short duration of action allows rapid dose titration while blood pressure and renal function are closely monitored.

C is incorrect because cyclophosphamide may be used for severe inflammatory interstitial lung disease associated with systemic sclerosis, but it does not provide urgent treatment for this renal and hypertensive complication.

D is incorrect because angiotensin receptor blockers such as losartan are not considered equivalent to ACE inhibitors for this emergency and should not replace captopril as initial therapy.

E is incorrect because high-dose glucocorticoids can precipitate or worsen scleroderma renal crisis. The patient's recent prednisone exposure is an important risk factor, so additional glucocorticoid therapy should be avoided.

Question 299  [ID: e1bb153e46564ac39cf8ba82afe41638]
A 29-year-old man presents after falling while skiing 2 hours ago. He landed with his right hand gripping a ski pole, causing the thumb to be forced away from the palm. He reports immediate pain and difficulty grasping objects. Examination demonstrates swelling, ecchymosis, and maximal tenderness along the ulnar aspect of the first metacarpophalangeal joint. With the joint flexed to 30 degrees, valgus stress produces 38 degrees of laxity and no firm endpoint. The contralateral side has 10 degrees of laxity. Sensation and capillary refill are intact. Plain radiographs show no fracture or joint malalignment. Which of the following is the most appropriate intervention?

A. Encourage early range-of-motion exercises and return to activity as tolerated
B. Apply a thumb-spica splint and arrange prompt evaluation by a hand surgeon for operative repair
C. Apply buddy taping to the adjacent digit for 2 weeks
D. Perform closed reduction followed by immobilization in an ulnar gutter splint
E. Administer an intra-articular glucocorticoid injection and reassess in 1 week

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The mechanism of forced thumb abduction, focal ulnar-sided tenderness at the first metacarpophalangeal joint, marked valgus laxity, and absence of a firm endpoint indicate a complete disruption of the primary valgus stabilizer of the thumb. A complete injury may be displaced superficial to the adductor aponeurosis, preventing spontaneous healing. Initial management includes immobilization in a thumb-spica splint and prompt referral to a hand surgeon for consideration of operative repair. A is incorrect because early motion may worsen instability and compromise pinch strength. C is incorrect because buddy taping does not adequately immobilize the first metacarpophalangeal joint. D is incorrect because there is no dislocation or fracture requiring reduction, and an ulnar gutter splint does not provide appropriate thumb stabilization. E is incorrect because glucocorticoid injection does not restore mechanical stability and may impair tissue healing.

Question 300  [ID: e357700517a74c5bb023d18e3d74e26f]
A 72-year-old man presents for follow-up after evaluation of several months of gradually worsening deep pelvic discomfort. Examination shows mild tenderness over the left hemipelvis without erythema or swelling. Laboratory studies reveal an alkaline phosphatase level of 680 U/L, with normal calcium, phosphate, aminotransferase, and bilirubin levels. Pelvic radiographs demonstrate asymmetric cortical thickening, coarse trabeculation, and enlargement of the left hemipelvis. A radionuclide scan shows increased uptake limited to this region. He receives a single intravenous dose of zoledronic acid and reports substantial improvement. Which of the following is the most appropriate plan for monitoring his response and detecting recurrent activity?

A. Measure serum alkaline phosphatase in 3–6 months and then every 6–12 months after normalization
B. Obtain pelvic radiographs every 3 months regardless of symptoms or laboratory findings
C. Measure serum calcium weekly for the remainder of the patient's life
D. Perform a radionuclide scan monthly until all radiographic abnormalities resolve
E. No further monitoring is needed once the patient's pain improves

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Serum alkaline phosphatase is the preferred marker for monitoring treatment response when it was elevated at baseline. It should generally be reassessed approximately 3–6 months after therapy and subsequently every 6–12 months once normalized or stabilized. A rising level may indicate recurrent activity and should prompt further clinical assessment and, when appropriate, repeat imaging. B is incorrect because serial radiographs every 3 months create unnecessary cost and radiation exposure; imaging is generally reserved for new symptoms, biochemical recurrence, or concern for a complication. C is incorrect because transient hypocalcemia can occur after potent antiresorptive therapy, but lifelong weekly calcium testing is unnecessary. Patients should instead receive adequate calcium and vitamin D and undergo clinically appropriate short-term monitoring. D is incorrect because radionuclide scanning is useful for defining the initial extent of involvement but is not appropriate for monthly surveillance, and structural imaging abnormalities may persist despite successful treatment. E is incorrect because symptomatic improvement alone does not reliably establish sustained biochemical remission; periodic surveillance is necessary to identify recurrent activity and reduce the risk of complications.

Question 301  [ID: e40eeee21996424eb18640748da65692]
A 47-year-old man presents with sudden pain in the front of his right elbow after attempting to catch a falling refrigerator while moving furniture. He felt a distinct pop and subsequently developed swelling and bruising over the antecubital region. Examination reveals a visible change in the contour of the upper arm, tenderness near the elbow crease, and marked weakness with resisted forearm supination. Elbow flexion is mildly weakened. The examiner is unable to hook a finger beneath the expected cordlike structure in the antecubital fossa. Plain radiographs show no fracture or dislocation. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Distal biceps tendon rupture
B. Brachialis muscle strain
C. Lateral epicondylitis
D. Radial head fracture
E. Triceps tendon rupture

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. A sudden eccentric load applied to a flexed elbow, followed by a pop, antecubital bruising, altered upper-arm contour, and pronounced weakness of forearm supination is characteristic of a distal biceps tendon rupture. The abnormal hook test, in which the examiner cannot hook a finger beneath the distal tendon, strongly supports a complete rupture. Elbow flexion may remain partially intact because the brachialis and brachioradialis continue to function. B is incorrect because a brachialis strain may cause anterior elbow pain and pain with flexion but does not typically produce an abnormal hook test, proximal change in biceps contour, or marked supination weakness. C is incorrect because lateral epicondylitis causes gradual lateral elbow pain worsened by resisted wrist extension rather than acute antecubital bruising and deformity. D is incorrect because a radial head fracture usually follows a fall on an outstretched hand and causes lateral elbow tenderness and painful limitation of pronation and supination; radiographs would generally reveal the fracture or an effusion. E is incorrect because a triceps tendon rupture causes posterior elbow pain, swelling, and weakness of elbow extension rather than anterior bruising and supination weakness.

Question 302  [ID: e51b9f982e5f4e3e909b803b4c006a7e]
A 67-year-old woman presents for follow-up after sustaining a distal radius fracture when she tripped from standing height 6 weeks ago. She underwent menopause at age 49 and has never received hormone therapy. She has no history of nephrolithiasis, esophageal disease, venous thromboembolism, or malignancy. She does not smoke and drinks alcohol occasionally. Physical examination shows a healing right wrist without deformity and mild thoracic kyphosis. Serum calcium, phosphorus, alkaline phosphatase, creatinine, thyroid-stimulating hormone, and 25-hydroxyvitamin D levels are within normal limits. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry demonstrates T-scores of −2.8 at the femoral neck and −2.6 at the lumbar spine. She can sit upright and reliably follow medication instructions. In addition to adequate calcium and vitamin D intake and weight-bearing exercise, which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. Alendronate
B. Calcitonin
C. Calcitriol
D. Raloxifene
E. Teriparatide

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Alendronate is the best initial pharmacologic therapy. This patient has osteoporosis based on T-scores at or below −2.5 and has also sustained a low-trauma fracture, placing her at increased risk for future fractures. Oral bisphosphonates such as alendronate are first-line therapy for most postmenopausal patients because they reduce vertebral, nonvertebral, and hip fracture risk. She has normal renal function, adequate vitamin D and calcium levels, no esophageal disorder, and can remain upright after administration, so she has no evident contraindication to oral therapy. Alendronate should be taken with plain water on an empty stomach, and the patient should remain upright for at least 30 minutes. Calcitonin is not preferred for long-term fracture prevention because its efficacy is limited; it may provide short-term analgesia after an acute vertebral compression fracture. Calcitriol is primarily used for selected patients with impaired renal activation of vitamin D or hypoparathyroidism and increases the risk of hypercalcemia; it is not first-line therapy in this setting. Raloxifene reduces vertebral fracture risk but has not demonstrated reliable reduction of hip fractures and increases the risk of venous thromboembolism and vasomotor symptoms. Teriparatide is an anabolic agent generally reserved for patients at very high fracture risk, those with multiple or severe vertebral fractures, or those who cannot tolerate or do not respond to antiresorptive therapy.

Question 303  [ID: e583c511b3534178bb48a4377bb8ac9b]
A 20-year-old collegiate volleyball player presents with pain and swelling near the right lateral malleolus after landing on another player's foot with the plantar surface turned inward. She was able to bear weight immediately afterward but stopped playing because of discomfort. Examination shows localized swelling and ecchymosis anterior and inferior to the lateral malleolus. There is no tenderness over the posterior edge of either malleolus, the base of the fifth metatarsal, or the navicular bone. Distal pulses, sensation, and motor function are intact. Plain radiographs show no fracture or dislocation. After the acute symptoms resolve and full range of motion and strength are restored, which of the following recommendations is most effective for reducing the likelihood of recurrence during future athletic activity?

A. Avoid all running and jumping activities for 6 months
B. Use prophylactic oral nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory medication before every practice
C. Perform balance and proprioceptive training and use external support during high-risk activities
D. Wear rigid immobilization continuously throughout the athletic season
E. Obtain surveillance radiographs before returning to competition

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The described inversion mechanism, anteroinferior lateral malleolar tenderness, preserved weight-bearing ability, and absence of fracture on imaging are consistent with a lateral ankle sprain. After recovery, neuromuscular rehabilitation emphasizing balance and proprioception reduces recurrent episodes by improving dynamic joint stability and postural control. External support, such as taping or a semirigid ankle brace during high-risk sports, provides additional preventive benefit, particularly in athletes with a prior episode. Choice A is incorrect because prolonged avoidance of activity is unnecessary after pain-free range of motion, strength, and functional performance have returned; gradual return to activity is preferred. Choice B is incorrect because prophylactic NSAID use does not prevent recurrence and can cause gastrointestinal, renal, and cardiovascular adverse effects. Choice D is incorrect because continuous rigid immobilization can cause stiffness, weakness, and impaired proprioception; it is not appropriate for prevention after recovery. Choice E is incorrect because routine surveillance radiographs do not predict or prevent recurrence and are unnecessary in an asymptomatic athlete who has regained normal function.

Question 304  [ID: e5a47312c5a44a4e81da74a93af96671]
A 48-year-old man presents with 6 weeks of progressive calf pain, diffuse muscle aching, fatigue, and a 5-kg (11-lb) unintentional weight loss. He also reports episodic postprandial abdominal pain and burning pain with weakness in his right foot. Examination shows a temperature of 37.8°C (100.0°F), blood pressure of 168/96 mm Hg, tender calf muscles, livedo reticularis over both legs, and decreased right ankle dorsiflexion. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 92 mm/hr, elevated C-reactive protein, serum creatinine of 1.9 mg/dL, and mild leukocytosis. Urinalysis shows trace protein without hematuria or cellular casts. Antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies and hepatitis B and C serologies are negative. Mesenteric angiography demonstrates multiple small aneurysms and irregular constrictions of medium-caliber arteries. Because this patient has progressive neurologic, gastrointestinal, and renal involvement, which of the following is the most appropriate initial treatment?

A. High-dose systemic glucocorticoids plus cyclophosphamide
B. Methotrexate monotherapy
C. Colchicine and a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug
D. Plasma exchange without immunosuppressive therapy
E. Rituximab plus trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The combination of constitutional symptoms, severe myalgias, livedo reticularis, mononeuropathy, postprandial abdominal pain, hypertension, renal dysfunction without an active urinary sediment, and angiographic microaneurysms indicates polyarteritis nodosa with major-organ involvement. Severe or organ-threatening disease requires prompt induction therapy with high-dose systemic glucocorticoids and cyclophosphamide. Glucocorticoids rapidly suppress inflammation, while cyclophosphamide improves disease control and survival in severe cases involving the peripheral nerves, gastrointestinal tract, or kidneys. After remission, a less toxic agent may be used for maintenance. Methotrexate monotherapy is inadequate for organ-threatening disease and is generally reserved as a steroid-sparing option in less severe illness. Colchicine and nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs do not control the underlying arterial injury and may worsen renal dysfunction or gastrointestinal complications. Plasma exchange is not appropriate as sole therapy; it may be considered with antiviral therapy and a short glucocorticoid course in selected hepatitis B-associated cases, but this patient's viral serologies are negative. Rituximab is used for certain other systemic inflammatory vascular disorders, particularly those associated with antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies, but it is not standard first-line induction therapy for this presentation; trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole may be used for opportunistic infection prophylaxis during intensive immunosuppression but does not treat the primary condition.

Question 305  [ID: e69dc13c3be24edba9aadfdd8fcb68e0]
A 52-year-old man presents with sudden pain in the front of his right elbow after attempting to catch a falling refrigerator. He reports feeling a painful “pop” and subsequently developed swelling and bruising near the elbow. Examination shows ecchymosis over the antecubital region, an abnormal proximal contour of the upper arm, and marked weakness when turning the palm upward against resistance. Flexion at the elbow is mildly weakened. Plain radiographs show no fracture or dislocation. Which of the following physical examination maneuvers would be most useful for confirming the suspected injury?

A. Attempt to hook the examiner’s index finger beneath the cordlike structure in the antecubital fossa while the patient actively contracts the arm
B. Ask the patient to abduct the arm while the examiner applies downward pressure at 90 degrees
C. Resist elevation of the arm in the scapular plane with the thumb pointed downward
D. Apply downward pressure over the clavicle while stabilizing the scapula
E. Flex the wrist and press the dorsal surfaces of both hands together for 60 seconds

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The history of an acute eccentric load, a painful pop, antecubital ecchymosis, altered upper-arm contour, and disproportionate weakness with resisted forearm supination is characteristic of a distal biceps tendon rupture. The hook test is performed with the elbow flexed and the forearm actively supinated; the examiner attempts to hook a finger beneath the intact distal biceps tendon from the lateral side. Inability to palpate and hook the tendon supports a complete rupture. Choice B describes resisted shoulder abduction and is used to assess the deltoid and supraspinatus. Choice C describes the empty-can test, which evaluates the supraspinatus. Choice D resembles the cross-body adduction maneuver used to assess the acromioclavicular joint. Choice E describes the reverse Phalen maneuver, which may reproduce symptoms associated with median nerve compression at the wrist.

Question 306  [ID: e6ef4ed1557b41079de0ab2f7bec3d18]
A 42-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe left wrist pain after falling from a ladder onto his outstretched hand 45 minutes ago. He has no chronic medical conditions or medication allergies, and his tetanus immunization was updated 2 years ago. Examination shows swelling, deformity, and a 0.5-cm puncture wound over the distal forearm with minimal bleeding and no gross contamination. The radial pulse is 2+, capillary refill is less than 2 seconds, and sensation is intact throughout the hand. Radiographs demonstrate displaced cortical discontinuities of the distal radius and ulna; the overlying wound communicates with the injured bone. Complete blood count and basic metabolic panel are within normal limits. In addition to urgent irrigation, operative management, and immobilization, which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacologic therapy?

A. Oral cephalexin
B. Intravenous cefazolin
C. Intravenous vancomycin
D. Intravenous piperacillin-tazobactam
E. No antimicrobial therapy unless fever or leukocytosis develops

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Intravenous cefazolin should be administered as soon as possible. A small, clean wound communicating with injured bone after a low-energy mechanism is consistent with a Gustilo-Anderson type I open injury. Early systemic antibiotic prophylaxis substantially reduces infection risk, and a first-generation cephalosporin provides appropriate coverage against the gram-positive skin organisms most commonly responsible for infection in type I and II injuries. Oral cephalexin is not appropriate initial therapy because parenteral antibiotics are preferred for rapid, reliable tissue concentrations. Vancomycin is generally reserved for patients with a severe beta-lactam allergy, known methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus colonization, or specific institutional indications. Piperacillin-tazobactam provides unnecessarily broad coverage for this small, minimally contaminated wound and is more appropriate when substantial polymicrobial contamination is suspected. Waiting for fever or leukocytosis is inappropriate because antibiotics are given prophylactically and should not be delayed. Additional tetanus prophylaxis is unnecessary because the patient's immunization was updated 2 years ago.

Question 307  [ID: e870117e05be4802a1803ba2b48d7723]
A 52-year-old man presents with 6 weeks of fatigue, fever, diffuse muscle pain, and a 5-kg unintentional weight loss. He also reports episodic postprandial abdominal pain and progressive burning pain with weakness of the right foot. Examination reveals blood pressure of 168/96 mm Hg, retiform violaceous skin changes over both legs, right foot drop, and tenderness of the calf muscles. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 82 mm/hr, elevated C-reactive protein, and a serum creatinine level of 1.6 mg/dL. Urinalysis shows no red blood cell casts. Testing for hepatitis B and C is negative. Abdominal angiography demonstrates multiple small aneurysms and irregular narrowing of the renal and mesenteric arteries. The patient will begin prednisone 60 mg daily and cyclophosphamide. Which of the following preventive measures is most appropriate to initiate during this treatment regimen?

A. Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole prophylaxis
B. Live attenuated zoster vaccination after immunosuppressive therapy begins
C. Daily ibuprofen to prevent treatment-related muscle pain
D. Isoniazid therapy regardless of tuberculosis screening results
E. No antimicrobial prophylaxis unless the CD4 count falls below 200 cells/mm3

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The findings are characteristic of polyarteritis nodosa, including constitutional symptoms, myalgias, livedo-like skin changes, mononeuritis multiplex, hypertension, abdominal ischemic pain, and renal and mesenteric arterial microaneurysms without glomerulonephritis. Treatment with high-dose glucocorticoids plus cyclophosphamide causes substantial cellular immunosuppression and increases the risk of Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia. Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole prophylaxis should therefore be initiated and continued while the patient is receiving significant immunosuppression. Choice B is incorrect because live vaccines should generally be administered before immunosuppression and are contraindicated during substantial immunosuppressive therapy; a recombinant, nonlive zoster vaccine is preferred when indicated. Choice C is incorrect because routine NSAID use does not prevent treatment complications and may worsen hypertension, renal dysfunction, and gastrointestinal toxicity. Choice D is incorrect because isoniazid is indicated only when latent or active tuberculosis infection is established after appropriate screening. Choice E is incorrect because the CD4 threshold of 200 cells/mm3 is primarily used to guide prophylaxis in patients with HIV; non-HIV patients receiving high-dose glucocorticoids combined with another potent immunosuppressant may require prophylaxis regardless of CD4 count.

Question 308  [ID: e9021335355f444fad312356f982a325]
A 48-year-old woman presents with 3 months of progressive difficulty climbing stairs, rising from a seated position, and lifting objects above her head. She denies sensory changes, diplopia, dysphagia, or recent medication use. Examination shows symmetric weakness of the shoulder and hip girdles with preserved distal strength, intact sensation, and normal deep tendon reflexes. No cutaneous abnormalities are present. Laboratory studies reveal a creatine kinase level of 4,800 U/L, elevated aldolase, and positive antinuclear antibodies. Electromyography demonstrates increased spontaneous activity with short-duration, low-amplitude motor unit potentials. A biopsy of the quadriceps shows endomysial mononuclear inflammatory infiltrates surrounding and invading nonnecrotic fibers. Which of the following is the best initial pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. High-dose oral prednisone
B. Oral pyridostigmine
C. Subcutaneous methotrexate as monotherapy
D. Intravenous immunoglobulin as first-line monotherapy
E. Oral carbidopa-levodopa

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has polymyositis, characterized by subacute, symmetric proximal weakness; markedly elevated muscle enzymes; myopathic electromyographic findings; and endomysial inflammation with invasion of nonnecrotic fibers. High-dose systemic glucocorticoid therapy, typically oral prednisone, is the initial pharmacologic treatment. Strength and creatine kinase levels are monitored to assess response, followed by gradual tapering once improvement occurs. Methotrexate or azathioprine may be added as steroid-sparing therapy when glucocorticoid toxicity is a concern or the response is inadequate. Pyridostigmine is used for myasthenia gravis, which typically causes fluctuating fatigable weakness and may involve ocular or bulbar function without marked creatine kinase elevation. Methotrexate can be useful as an adjunct but is generally not preferred as initial monotherapy. Intravenous immunoglobulin is usually reserved for severe, refractory, or rapidly progressive disease, especially when dysphagia is present. Carbidopa-levodopa treats dopamine-responsive parkinsonian syndromes and would not address this inflammatory process.

Question 309  [ID: e9ff3836c9c1442788a692cebf2f5597]
A 6-week-old girl is brought to the clinic for a routine well-child examination. She was delivered by cesarean section at 39 weeks after remaining in breech presentation during the third trimester. Her mother reports that the infant feeds well and moves all extremities spontaneously. There is no history of trauma or fever. Growth parameters are appropriate for age, and the newborn metabolic screen was normal. On examination, the left knee appears lower than the right when the hips and knees are flexed. Gentle abduction of the left thigh is limited, and a palpable clunk is noted when the flexed left thigh is abducted while anterior pressure is applied. No diagnostic imaging has been obtained. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic study to evaluate this infant's abnormal examination findings?

A. Anteroposterior radiography of the pelvis
B. Dynamic ultrasonography of the hips
C. Computed tomography of the pelvis
D. Magnetic resonance imaging of the pelvis
E. Technetium-99m bone scintigraphy

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Dynamic ultrasonography is the preferred diagnostic study for an infant younger than 4 to 6 months with abnormal findings on examination or significant risk factors such as female sex and breech presentation. The palpable clunk with abduction and anterior pressure represents a positive Ortolani maneuver, indicating that a displaced femoral head can be reduced into the acetabulum. Ultrasonography can evaluate the cartilaginous femoral head, acetabular morphology, and stability during dynamic maneuvers without exposing the infant to ionizing radiation.

A is incorrect because pelvic radiography is less useful in young infants before ossification of the femoral head has occurred. Anteroposterior pelvic radiographs become more informative after approximately 4 to 6 months of age.

C is incorrect because computed tomography exposes the infant to ionizing radiation and provides inferior assessment of unossified cartilaginous structures compared with ultrasonography. It is not a first-line study for this presentation.

D is incorrect because magnetic resonance imaging can define cartilaginous anatomy but is costly, often requires sedation, and is generally reserved for complex cases or assessment after reduction and immobilization.

E is incorrect because bone scintigraphy is used to assess processes such as occult fracture, infection, or osteonecrosis and does not adequately evaluate the structural instability suggested by this infant's examination.

Question 310  [ID: ea1123231160459da46ccf71e48e4906]
A 72-year-old man presents with 8 months of progressive deep aching pain in his pelvis and lower back. He also reports that his hats no longer fit and that he has gradually worsening hearing in his right ear. Examination reveals frontal bossing and tenderness over the left hemipelvis. Serum alkaline phosphatase is 680 U/L, while calcium, phosphorus, parathyroid hormone, and hepatic aminotransferase levels are within reference ranges. Radiographs of the pelvis show cortical thickening, enlargement, and coarse trabecular markings. His estimated glomerular filtration rate is 82 mL/min/1.73 m², and his serum calcium and 25-hydroxyvitamin D levels are adequate. Which of the following is the most appropriate treatment for this patient?

A. A single intravenous infusion of zoledronic acid
B. Daily subcutaneous teriparatide
C. Long-term oral prednisone therapy
D. Oral calcium and vitamin D supplementation alone
E. Immediate surgical resection of the affected pelvic area

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has symptomatic, metabolically active Paget disease of bone, supported by older age, deep skeletal pain, increasing hat size, hearing loss, markedly elevated alkaline phosphatase with otherwise normal mineral studies, and characteristic cortical thickening with coarse trabeculae. Bisphosphonates suppress excessive osteoclast-mediated resorption and are indicated for symptomatic or active disease, particularly when the skull, spine, pelvis, or weight-bearing structures are involved. A single intravenous infusion of zoledronic acid is the preferred treatment because it produces a rapid, potent, and durable biochemical response. Adequate renal function and correction of hypocalcemia or vitamin D deficiency should be confirmed before administration, as demonstrated in this patient. Teriparatide is an anabolic medication used for selected patients with severe osteoporosis and would not suppress the accelerated remodeling present here. Prednisone is not standard therapy and exposes the patient to substantial adverse effects without addressing the underlying abnormal remodeling. Calcium and vitamin D help prevent treatment-associated hypocalcemia but are not adequate as sole therapy for active symptomatic disease. Surgery is reserved for complications such as severe deformity, pathologic fracture, nerve compression, or advanced secondary osteoarthritis and is not the initial intervention in this case.

Question 311  [ID: ea646310cbb34618b2065140e6d65a35]
A 16-year-old male competitive gymnast presents with 3 months of progressively worsening low back pain. The pain is localized to the lower lumbar region without radiation and is exacerbated by tumbling, back walkovers, and other activities involving repeated extension and rotation. He denies fever, weight loss, morning stiffness, bowel or bladder dysfunction, and lower-extremity weakness or numbness. Examination shows normal lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes; straight-leg raise testing is negative bilaterally. There is focal lower lumbar tenderness and increased lumbar lordosis. Complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein levels are within normal limits. Which of the following additional physical examination findings would most strongly support the suspected diagnosis?

A. Reproduction of radicular pain with passive straight-leg elevation
B. Reproduction of focal low back pain during single-leg lumbar hyperextension
C. Pelvic drop on the unsupported side while standing on one leg
D. Restriction of hip abduction with pain during flexion, abduction, and external rotation
E. Improvement of pain with lumbar extension and worsening with forward flexion

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The presentation is characteristic of spondylolysis, a stress injury involving the pars interarticularis that commonly occurs in adolescent athletes exposed to repetitive lumbar extension and rotation. Gymnasts, football linemen, and divers are at increased risk. Typical features include focal low back pain that worsens with extension, absence of neurologic deficits, and normal inflammatory markers. The single-leg lumbar hyperextension maneuver, also called the stork test, loads the posterior elements of the lumbar spine; reproduction of focal pain supports this diagnosis, although the maneuver is not sufficiently sensitive or specific to replace imaging when clinical suspicion remains high.

A is incorrect because radicular pain with straight-leg elevation suggests lumbosacral nerve root irritation, often from lumbar disk herniation. This patient has no radiating pain, sensory loss, weakness, or reflex abnormalities.

C is incorrect because contralateral pelvic drop during single-leg stance is a positive Trendelenburg sign, indicating weakness of the hip abductors, particularly the gluteus medius and minimus.

D is incorrect because pain or restricted motion with the FABER maneuver is more consistent with pathology involving the hip or sacroiliac joint.

E is incorrect because pain relief with extension and worsening with flexion is more typical of diskogenic pain. This patient's pain is provoked by extension, which places greater stress on the posterior vertebral elements.

Question 312  [ID: ea7aa9037af343079ff3ce81bc03d3fc]
A 26-year-old woman presents with a 4-month history of fatigue and intermittent pain and swelling involving both wrists and several metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints. The stiffness is most pronounced in the morning and lasts approximately 90 minutes. She also reports increased hair shedding and an erythematous facial eruption that worsens after sun exposure. Examination shows low-grade fever, patchy nonscarring alopecia, and symmetric tenderness and mild swelling of the affected joints. There are no fixed deformities. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 3,100/mm3, a positive antinuclear antibody titer of 1:640 with a homogeneous pattern, and a negative anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody test. Urinalysis reveals 1+ protein without evidence of infection. Radiographs of both hands show soft-tissue swelling without erosions. Which additional physical examination finding should the clinician specifically assess for because it would most strongly support a unifying diagnosis?

A. Firm, chalky nodules over the olecranon bursae
B. Painless ulceration of the hard palate
C. Bony enlargement of the distal interphalangeal joints
D. Violaceous papules over the extensor surfaces of the finger joints
E. Nail pitting with separation of the nail plate from the nail bed

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. This young woman has a systemic inflammatory illness characterized by symmetric nonerosive polyarthritis, photosensitivity, nonscarring alopecia, leukopenia, proteinuria, and a high-titer homogeneous antinuclear antibody. Painless oral or nasopharyngeal ulcers, particularly on the hard palate, are an important additional examination finding associated with systemic lupus erythematosus and would further support the unifying diagnosis. A is incorrect because firm, chalky nodules over the olecranon bursae suggest tophaceous gout, which typically produces episodic acute monoarthritis or oligoarthritis rather than this pattern of systemic findings. C is incorrect because bony enlargement of the distal interphalangeal joints represents Heberden nodes, a feature of osteoarthritis; osteoarthritis generally causes brief morning stiffness and lacks constitutional, hematologic, urinary, and photosensitive findings. D is incorrect because violaceous papules over the extensor finger joints are Gottron papules, which are characteristic of dermatomyositis and would typically accompany proximal muscle weakness and elevated muscle enzymes. E is incorrect because nail pitting and onycholysis are associated with psoriasis and may accompany psoriatic arthritis, which more often causes distal interphalangeal involvement, dactylitis, enthesitis, or asymmetric joint inflammation.

Question 313  [ID: eb2bb11df51a4b68a3d2e9d5960dffb6]
A 67-year-old man presents with pain and bruising of his dominant upper arm after attempting to lift a heavy toolbox. He felt a sudden pop near the front of his shoulder, followed by immediate pain. Examination shows ecchymosis over the upper arm and a prominent, rounded bulge in the distal portion of the arm during elbow flexion. Strength testing demonstrates greater weakness with forearm supination than with elbow flexion. Sensation and distal pulses are normal. Plain radiographs show no fracture or dislocation. MRI demonstrates an empty intertubercular groove and distal retraction of a cordlike structure. Which of the following normal anatomic attachments has most likely been disrupted?

A. Coracoid process of the scapula
B. Infraglenoid tubercle of the scapula
C. Supraglenoid tubercle of the scapula
D. Greater tubercle of the humerus
E. Lateral epicondyle of the humerus

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The sudden pop, anterior shoulder pain, ecchymosis, distal arm bulge, impaired supination, empty intertubercular groove, and distal retraction indicate disruption of the proximal long head of the biceps brachii. This structure originates from the supraglenoid tubercle and superior glenoid labrum, traverses the shoulder joint, and enters the intertubercular groove. Loss of its proximal attachment allows the muscle belly to retract distally, producing the characteristic rounded bulge. The biceps is a powerful forearm supinator, explaining why supination is affected more than elbow flexion, which is also performed by the brachialis and brachioradialis. A is incorrect because the short head of the biceps and coracobrachialis originate from the coracoid process; the intact short head often preserves substantial elbow flexion strength. B is incorrect because the long head of the triceps originates from the infraglenoid tubercle and primarily extends the elbow. D is incorrect because the supraspinatus, infraspinatus, and teres minor insert on facets of the greater tubercle. E is incorrect because the lateral epicondyle is the common origin of several wrist and finger extensor muscles.
