Question 314  [ID: eba6f4b995bd44f6944831dcbc712ad5]
A 67-year-old woman presents with gradually progressive right knee pain for 3 years. The pain worsens with prolonged walking and climbing stairs and improves with rest. She reports morning stiffness lasting approximately 10 minutes and occasional swelling after increased activity. Her medical history includes obesity and hypertension. Examination shows a mildly antalgic gait, bony enlargement of the right knee, crepitus with passive motion, and reduced flexion. There is no erythema or increased warmth. Laboratory studies reveal a normal leukocyte count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and serum uric acid level. Plain radiographs demonstrate asymmetric joint-space narrowing, marginal osteophyte formation, and subchondral sclerosis without erosions. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Osteoarthritis
B. Rheumatoid arthritis
C. Calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease
D. Septic arthritis
E. Gout

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: A is correct. Osteoarthritis typically causes chronic, gradually progressive pain that worsens with activity and improves with rest. Morning stiffness is usually brief, lasting less than 30 minutes. Bony enlargement, crepitus, reduced range of motion, and the absence of marked warmth or erythema further support the diagnosis. Characteristic radiographic findings include asymmetric joint-space narrowing, marginal osteophytes, and subchondral sclerosis. Normal inflammatory markers are also consistent with this noninflammatory degenerative process.

B is incorrect because rheumatoid arthritis usually produces symmetric inflammatory polyarthritis, prolonged morning stiffness, joint warmth and swelling, and often elevated inflammatory markers. Radiographs may show marginal erosions and periarticular osteopenia rather than osteophytes and subchondral sclerosis.

C is incorrect because calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease commonly presents with acute or episodic inflammatory monoarthritis or oligoarthritis. Radiographs often demonstrate chondrocalcinosis, which is not present in this patient.

D is incorrect because septic arthritis generally causes acute, severe monoarticular pain with marked limitation of motion, warmth, swelling, and often fever or leukocytosis. Urgent synovial fluid analysis would be required when this diagnosis is suspected.

E is incorrect because gout classically presents with abrupt episodes of severe inflammatory arthritis accompanied by erythema, warmth, and exquisite tenderness. Chronic gout may produce punched-out erosions and tophi, neither of which is described. A normal serum uric acid level does not completely exclude gout, but the chronic mechanical symptoms and characteristic radiographic findings favor osteoarthritis.

Question 315  [ID: ebde615c3efc43ce862ae61f53a41d4b]
A 68-year-old man presents with a 2-day history of rapidly worsening right knee pain, swelling, and inability to bear weight. He reports fever and chills but denies recent trauma. His medical history includes type 2 diabetes mellitus and chronic kidney disease. Temperature is 39.1°C (102.4°F), blood pressure is 132/78 mm Hg, pulse is 108/min, and respirations are 18/min. The right knee is warm, erythematous, and markedly swollen, with severe pain during passive range of motion. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 17,800/mm3 and an elevated C-reactive protein level. Arthrocentesis yields cloudy fluid with 92,000 leukocytes/mm3, 94% neutrophils, and no crystals. Gram stain shows no organisms, and cultures are pending. Blood cultures are obtained, and urgent joint drainage is planned. Which of the following is the most appropriate empiric antimicrobial therapy?

A. Intravenous vancomycin plus ceftriaxone
B. Oral cephalexin alone
C. Intravenous cefazolin alone
D. Oral doxycycline plus amoxicillin
E. Intravenous amphotericin B

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The acute monoarticular presentation with fever, severe pain with passive motion, markedly inflammatory synovial fluid, neutrophil predominance, and absence of crystals requires immediate empiric antimicrobial treatment after synovial fluid and blood cultures are obtained. A negative Gram stain does not exclude a bacterial cause. Intravenous vancomycin provides empiric coverage for methicillin-resistant and methicillin-susceptible Staphylococcus aureus, while ceftriaxone adds coverage for streptococci and susceptible gram-negative organisms. Therapy should later be narrowed according to culture and susceptibility results, and antimicrobial treatment must be combined with prompt drainage. Choice B is incorrect because oral cephalexin does not provide reliable MRSA coverage and is inadequate for this potentially destructive, invasive infection. Choice C is incorrect because cefazolin covers methicillin-susceptible staphylococci and streptococci but not MRSA; it may be appropriate as targeted therapy after cultures identify a susceptible organism. Choice D is incorrect because doxycycline plus amoxicillin does not provide appropriate broad empiric intravenous coverage for this presentation. Choice E is incorrect because amphotericin B is reserved for serious fungal infections, which are generally more indolent and are not the most likely cause of this acute presentation.

Question 316  [ID: ecaebd3860f240c48684532257a06f76]
A 13-year-old girl is brought to clinic after her parent notices that her shirts hang unevenly. She has no pain, weakness, numbness, bowel or bladder dysfunction, or history of trauma. Menarche occurred 6 months ago. On examination, the right shoulder and scapula are slightly higher than the left, and the waist creases are asymmetric. Leg lengths are equal, and the neurologic examination is normal. Which of the following physical examination maneuvers is most appropriate to further evaluate this finding?

A. Measure lumbar flexion using the modified Schober test
B. Observe the posterior trunk while the patient bends forward at the waist
C. Perform a straight-leg raise with the patient supine
D. Assess pelvic stability while the patient stands on one leg
E. Flex, abduct, and externally rotate each hip while the patient is supine

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The patient's shoulder, scapular, and waist asymmetry suggests adolescent idiopathic scoliosis. The Adam forward bend test is the appropriate examination maneuver: the patient bends forward at the waist while the examiner observes from behind for rib or lumbar prominence caused by vertebral rotation. A scoliometer can quantify the angle of trunk rotation and help determine whether radiographic evaluation is warranted. Choice A is incorrect because the modified Schober test measures lumbar flexion and is primarily used when evaluating restricted spinal mobility, such as in ankylosing spondylitis. Choice C is incorrect because the straight-leg raise evaluates lumbosacral nerve root irritation, most commonly from a herniated disc. Choice D is incorrect because the Trendelenburg test evaluates hip abductor weakness and pelvic stability. Choice E is incorrect because the FABER maneuver evaluates pathology involving the hip or sacroiliac joint.

Question 317  [ID: ee004fcab6824075934b95a796381cb2]
A 72-year-old woman presents with 6 weeks of bilateral shoulder and hip-girdle aching accompanied by morning stiffness lasting approximately 90 minutes. She has difficulty getting out of bed, rising from a chair, and lifting her arms to brush her hair. She denies headache, scalp tenderness, jaw pain with chewing, vision changes, fever, rash, or recent medication changes. Examination shows painful restriction of active shoulder and hip movement, but passive range of motion is preserved. Strength is 5/5 when limited discomfort is overcome. There is no joint swelling or temporal artery tenderness. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 78 mm/hour, elevated C-reactive protein, normal creatine kinase, and negative rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies. Which of the following is the best initial pharmacologic intervention?

A. Oral prednisone 12.5–25 mg daily
B. Oral ibuprofen as needed
C. Methotrexate monotherapy
D. Intravenous methylprednisolone 1,000 mg daily for 3 days
E. Hydroxychloroquine monotherapy

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. This older patient has a characteristic inflammatory syndrome consisting of bilateral shoulder and hip-girdle pain, prolonged morning stiffness, markedly elevated inflammatory markers, preserved true strength, and a normal creatine kinase level. Initial treatment is a low to moderate dose of an oral glucocorticoid, commonly prednisone 12.5–25 mg daily. Symptoms generally improve dramatically within several days, after which the dose is gradually tapered according to clinical response and inflammatory marker trends. Patients should also receive monitoring and preventive care for glucocorticoid complications, including assessment of bone health.

B is incorrect because nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs generally do not provide adequate control and are not preferred as primary therapy, particularly in older adults who are at increased risk for gastrointestinal, renal, and cardiovascular adverse effects.

C is incorrect because methotrexate may be added as a glucocorticoid-sparing agent in patients with recurrent disease, a high relapse risk, or substantial glucocorticoid toxicity. It is not typically used as initial monotherapy.

D is incorrect because pulse-dose intravenous glucocorticoids are unnecessarily aggressive in the absence of threatened vision or another severe ischemic complication. New headache, jaw claudication, scalp tenderness, or visual symptoms would require urgent evaluation for associated cranial arterial inflammation and treatment with substantially higher glucocorticoid doses.

E is incorrect because hydroxychloroquine is used for selected autoimmune disorders but has no established role as initial monotherapy for this presentation.

Question 318  [ID: eeef33e532724624bbaa19e4ebe2b602]
A 16-year-old male high school football lineman presents with 3 months of gradually worsening lower back pain. The pain is localized to the lower lumbar region without radiation and is exacerbated by blocking drills, weightlifting, and extension of the spine. He denies weakness, paresthesias, bowel or bladder dysfunction, fever, or recent trauma. Examination demonstrates increased lumbar lordosis, tight hamstrings, and focal tenderness over the lower lumbar region. Pain is reproduced when he stands on one leg and extends his spine. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, reflexes, and straight-leg raise testing are normal. Plain radiographs show a linear defect through the pars interarticularis of the L5 vertebra without anterior displacement of the vertebral body. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Lumbar disk herniation
B. Spondylolisthesis
C. Spondylolysis
D. Vertebral compression fracture
E. Ankylosing spondylitis

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The correct answer is C. Spondylolysis is a stress fracture or defect of the pars interarticularis, most commonly involving L5. It typically occurs in adolescent athletes who participate in activities involving repetitive lumbar extension and rotation, such as football, gymnastics, diving, or weightlifting. Patients usually have focal, activity-related lower back pain without neurologic deficits. Pain with single-leg lumbar extension, sometimes called the stork test, supports the diagnosis. Imaging demonstrating a pars interarticularis defect without vertebral displacement is characteristic. A is incorrect because lumbar disk herniation more often causes radicular pain, paresthesias, weakness, or a positive straight-leg raise test. B is incorrect because spondylolisthesis requires anterior displacement of one vertebral body relative to the vertebra below it; no displacement is present here. D is incorrect because vertebral compression fractures generally follow significant trauma in younger patients or occur in older patients with osteoporosis, and imaging would show loss of vertebral body height rather than a pars defect. E is incorrect because ankylosing spondylitis typically causes chronic inflammatory back pain with morning stiffness, improvement with exercise, and sacroiliac joint abnormalities rather than focal extension-related pain and a pars interarticularis defect.

Question 319  [ID: ef11e9372b9f44f49aa34a5a7e3eccba]
A 13-year-old boy with obesity is brought to the clinic for 3 weeks of progressive left groin and knee pain associated with a limp. He denies trauma, fever, or recent illness. His temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), and laboratory studies, including a complete blood count and C-reactive protein level, are within normal limits. On examination, the left leg rests in slight external rotation. Flexion of the left hip produces obligatory external rotation, and internal rotation is markedly limited. He can walk but has an antalgic gait. Radiographs demonstrate widening and irregularity of the proximal femoral physis with posteromedial displacement of the epiphysis relative to the femoral neck. While arranging urgent specialty evaluation, which of the following is the most important instruction to give the patient and his parents to prevent further complications?

A. Continue weight-bearing as tolerated but avoid running and contact sports
B. Begin daily stretching and strengthening exercises under parental supervision
C. Maintain strict non-weight-bearing status and use crutches or a wheelchair
D. Apply heat to the painful area and return if symptoms persist for 2 weeks
E. Use a shoe lift on the affected side to correct the altered gait

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The clinical presentation and radiographic findings are characteristic of slipped capital femoral epiphysis. The most important immediate preventive instruction is strict non-weight-bearing status, with crutches or a wheelchair, while urgent orthopedic management is arranged. Continued loading can cause additional displacement and increase the risk of complications such as avascular necrosis, chondrolysis, deformity, early degenerative joint disease, and long-term functional impairment. Definitive treatment generally requires prompt surgical stabilization.

A is incorrect because even limited weight-bearing can worsen displacement; simply avoiding strenuous activity is insufficient. B is incorrect because stretching or strengthening before stabilization may increase mechanical stress and promote further displacement. C is correct because immediate elimination of weight-bearing reduces the risk of progression while awaiting definitive treatment. D is incorrect because heat and delayed reassessment fail to address an urgent condition in which treatment delay may worsen outcomes. E is incorrect because a shoe lift does not stabilize the affected physis and may encourage continued weight-bearing. Patients and families should also be educated about adherence to follow-up, weight management, and prompt reporting of symptoms in the opposite hip because bilateral involvement can occur.

Question 320  [ID: ef20da5be59b4a5ea9b3c503fbff0c2c]
A 13-year-old boy with obesity presents with 3 weeks of progressive left groin pain that radiates to the knee. He denies trauma and is still able to walk, although he has developed a limp. Examination shows the left lower extremity resting in external rotation. Flexion of the left hip produces obligatory external rotation, and internal rotation is markedly limited. He is afebrile, and laboratory studies are unremarkable. Anteroposterior and frog-leg lateral radiographs demonstrate posterior and inferior displacement of the proximal femoral metaphysis relative to the epiphysis. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management?

A. Permit weight bearing as tolerated and begin supervised physical therapy
B. Perform forceful closed reduction followed by application of a hip spica cast
C. Make the patient non-weight-bearing and arrange urgent in situ percutaneous screw fixation
D. Prescribe oral nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory medication and repeat radiographs in 6 weeks
E. Perform image-guided aspiration and begin empiric intravenous antibiotics

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: This patient has slipped capital femoral epiphysis, suggested by his adolescent age, obesity, atraumatic groin pain referred to the knee, limp, obligatory external rotation with hip flexion, limited internal rotation, and characteristic radiographic displacement. Because he remains able to ambulate, the slip is clinically stable; however, it still requires immediate cessation of weight bearing and urgent orthopedic stabilization. In situ percutaneous screw fixation prevents further displacement while minimizing injury to the femoral head blood supply. A is incorrect because weight bearing and physical therapy may worsen displacement. B is incorrect because forceful reduction increases the risk of avascular necrosis and chondrolysis; routine reduction should not be attempted before fixation. D is incorrect because observation delays definitive treatment and permits progression of the slip. E is incorrect because the patient has no fever, inflammatory findings, or other evidence of septic arthritis requiring aspiration and antimicrobial therapy.

Question 321  [ID: ef9da664724844c48f62e92ba378190e]
A 68-year-old woman presents with left wrist pain and swelling after falling from standing height onto an outstretched hand. She underwent menopause at age 49 and has never used hormone replacement therapy. She takes no corticosteroids and has no history of kidney, liver, thyroid, or gastrointestinal disease. Examination shows tenderness and deformity of the distal left forearm and mild thoracic kyphosis. Radiographs demonstrate a distal radius fracture. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry reveals a femoral neck T-score of -2.8. Serum calcium, phosphate, alkaline phosphatase, creatinine, and 25-hydroxyvitamin D levels are within normal limits. Which of the following molecular mechanisms most likely contributed to this patient's findings?

A. Decreased estrogen signaling causing increased RANK ligand expression and reduced osteoprotegerin activity
B. Defective type I collagen synthesis causing impaired formation of the extracellular matrix
C. Failure of mineral deposition due to inadequate production of active vitamin D
D. Deposition of monosodium urate crystals with activation of the NLRP3 inflammasome
E. Autoantibody-mediated stimulation of parathyroid hormone receptors

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. Estrogen normally limits osteoclast differentiation and activity partly by decreasing expression of receptor activator of nuclear factor-kappa B ligand (RANKL) and increasing production of osteoprotegerin, a soluble decoy receptor for RANKL. After menopause, reduced estrogen signaling increases the RANKL-to-osteoprotegerin ratio. Enhanced RANK activation promotes osteoclast formation, survival, and resorptive activity, resulting in loss of trabecular connectivity and cortical mass. This produces reduced density and an increased risk of low-trauma fractures despite normal serum calcium, phosphate, alkaline phosphatase, and vitamin D levels. B is incorrect because defective type I collagen synthesis is associated with an inherited connective tissue disorder that typically presents earlier in life with recurrent fractures and may include blue sclerae, hearing loss, and dentin abnormalities. C is incorrect because defective mineral deposition from inadequate active vitamin D typically causes impaired mineralization and may be associated with low or low-normal calcium, low phosphate, elevated alkaline phosphatase, muscle weakness, and diffuse discomfort. D is incorrect because monosodium urate crystals activate the NLRP3 inflammasome and cause episodic inflammatory arthritis, not generalized loss of density with fragility fractures. E is incorrect because stimulating autoantibodies to parathyroid hormone receptors are not a recognized cause of this presentation; excess parathyroid hormone activity would also be expected to produce characteristic biochemical abnormalities, particularly hypercalcemia and hypophosphatemia.

Question 322  [ID: f0a6deb0688945918790cd027b12d942]
A 67-year-old woman presents with 10 months of gradually worsening right knee pain. The pain is worse after prolonged walking and climbing stairs and improves with rest. She reports morning stiffness lasting approximately 10 minutes. Her medical history includes hypertension and obesity. Her body mass index is 34 kg/m². Examination shows a small, cool effusion, palpable crepitus with flexion and extension, and tenderness along the medial joint line. There is no erythema or ligamentous instability. Laboratory studies show a normal leukocyte count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and serum uric acid level. Plain radiographs demonstrate asymmetric medial compartment joint-space narrowing, marginal bony outgrowths, and increased density of the bone beneath the articular surface. Which of the following is the best initial treatment plan for this patient?

A. Initiate a structured low-impact exercise and weight-loss program and use topical diclofenac for pain control
B. Begin long-term oral prednisone therapy
C. Refer for arthroscopic lavage and debridement
D. Begin scheduled opioid therapy for chronic pain control
E. Refer immediately for total knee replacement

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's chronic activity-related knee pain, brief morning stiffness, crepitus, cool effusion, and characteristic radiographic findings indicate a chronic degenerative articular process. Initial management should combine nonpharmacologic and pharmacologic interventions. Low-impact aerobic exercise, strengthening, and weight reduction improve pain and physical function while decreasing mechanical load across the knee. A topical nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as diclofenac is an appropriate initial medication for localized knee symptoms and has less systemic exposure than an oral NSAID. B is incorrect because long-term systemic glucocorticoids are not recommended and can cause osteoporosis, hyperglycemia, hypertension, infection, and weight gain. C is incorrect because arthroscopic lavage or debridement does not provide meaningful long-term benefit for uncomplicated degenerative changes and is not routine initial therapy. D is incorrect because opioids have limited long-term benefit and substantial risks, including dependence, sedation, falls, and overdose; they are not first-line treatment. E is incorrect because joint replacement is reserved for severe pain and functional limitation that persist despite adequate conservative therapy or for advanced structural disease; this patient has not yet undergone appropriate initial management.

Question 323  [ID: f1188de57cfa4ff4b66df141009eda2a]
A 72-year-old man presents with 8 months of progressively worsening deep pain in the pelvis and left thigh. He reports that his hat size has increased over several years and that he recently developed difficulty hearing conversations. Examination shows frontal bossing and warmth over the left tibia. Laboratory studies reveal an alkaline phosphatase level of 680 U/L, with normal calcium, phosphorus, parathyroid hormone, and 25-hydroxyvitamin D levels. Serum creatinine is normal. Plain radiographs demonstrate cortical thickening, coarsened trabeculae, and expansion of the pelvis and left femur. A radionuclide scan shows increased uptake at these sites. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacologic therapy for this patient?

A. A single intravenous infusion of zoledronic acid
B. Daily subcutaneous teriparatide
C. Weekly oral methotrexate with folic acid supplementation
D. Daily oral prednisone
E. Subcutaneous denosumab every 6 months

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: This patient has symptomatic, metabolically active Paget disease of bone, as indicated by focal pain, skull enlargement with hearing impairment, markedly elevated alkaline phosphatase with normal calcium and phosphorus levels, and characteristic radiographic findings of cortical thickening, coarse trabeculae, and osseous expansion. Bisphosphonates are first-line therapy because they inhibit osteoclast-mediated bone resorption and suppress abnormal remodeling. A single 5-mg intravenous infusion of zoledronic acid is preferred in patients with adequate renal function because it produces rapid, profound, and durable biochemical remission. Calcium and vitamin D sufficiency should be confirmed before treatment. Teriparatide stimulates bone formation and is used for selected patients with severe osteoporosis; it would be inappropriate in a high-turnover disorder and is contraindicated in patients with this condition because of concern for osteosarcoma risk. Methotrexate is a disease-modifying antirheumatic medication used for inflammatory conditions such as rheumatoid arthritis and does not treat abnormal remodeling in this case. Prednisone is used for inflammatory and autoimmune disorders but does not correct the underlying excessive osteoclastic activity. Denosumab inhibits RANK ligand and treats osteoporosis and certain malignancy-related conditions, but it is not standard first-line therapy for this presentation; it may be considered only in unusual circumstances when bisphosphonates cannot be used.

Question 324  [ID: f333ac79bced4076a2f1384601622492]
A 72-year-old woman presents with 6 weeks of bilateral shoulder and hip girdle aching. She reports morning stiffness lasting approximately 90 minutes and difficulty raising her arms to brush her hair or rising from a chair. She denies rash, recent infection, visual changes, jaw pain, and true loss of strength. Examination shows painful restriction of active shoulder motion, but passive motion is nearly intact. Strength is 5/5 when limited discomfort is overcome. There is no joint swelling or temporal artery tenderness. Laboratory studies show an erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 78 mm/hr, elevated C-reactive protein, normal creatine kinase, negative rheumatoid factor, and negative anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies. Ultrasonography demonstrates bilateral subdeltoid bursitis. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for this patient's clinical findings?

A. Autoantibody-mediated blockade of postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors
B. Cytokine-driven inflammation of periarticular bursae and synovial structures without primary muscle fiber destruction
C. Immune-mediated necrosis of skeletal muscle fibers associated with marked creatine kinase elevation
D. Deposition of monosodium urate crystals with activation of the NLRP3 inflammasome
E. Degeneration of articular cartilage caused by loss of proteoglycans and subchondral bone remodeling

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The presentation is characteristic of polymyalgia rheumatica: age older than 50 years, abrupt or subacute bilateral shoulder and hip girdle pain, prolonged morning stiffness, markedly elevated inflammatory markers, preserved objective strength, and a normal creatine kinase level. The disorder is driven by systemic and periarticular inflammation, with cytokines such as interleukin-6 contributing to inflammation of bursae, tendon sheaths, and synovial structures. The muscles themselves are not primarily destroyed, explaining the normal creatine kinase and preserved strength. A is incorrect because postsynaptic acetylcholine receptor blockade causes myasthenia gravis, which produces fatigable weakness rather than pain, prolonged morning stiffness, or elevated inflammatory markers. C is incorrect because inflammatory myopathies typically cause objective proximal weakness and elevated muscle enzymes due to muscle fiber injury. D is incorrect because monosodium urate deposition causes gout, which usually presents with episodic acute monoarticular or oligoarticular inflammation. E is incorrect because osteoarthritis results from cartilage degeneration and typically causes pain worsened by activity, brief morning stiffness, and normal systemic inflammatory markers.

Question 325  [ID: f348dd83a493449cab4fe1ea275b4d55]
A 36-year-old warehouse employee presents with 2 days of localized lower back pain that began after he twisted while lifting a heavy box. The pain worsens with bending and prolonged sitting but does not radiate into the legs. He denies weakness, numbness, bowel or bladder dysfunction, fever, weight loss, or prior malignancy. Examination shows paraspinal tenderness and reduced active range of motion due to discomfort. Lower-extremity strength, sensation, and reflexes are normal, and straight-leg raising does not reproduce symptoms. There is no midline vertebral tenderness. Plain radiographs obtained at an urgent care clinic show no acute osseous abnormality. Which of the following counseling recommendations is most appropriate to promote recovery and reduce the risk of recurrence?

A. Continue ordinary activities as tolerated, avoid prolonged bed rest, and begin progressive trunk-strengthening and proper lifting techniques after the acute discomfort improves
B. Remain on strict bed rest until completely pain-free, followed by an immediate return to unrestricted heavy lifting
C. Wear a rigid lumbar brace continuously during work and sleep to prevent future episodes
D. Obtain serial magnetic resonance imaging every 6 months to identify progressive structural changes
E. Avoid all bending, exercise, and lifting indefinitely because recurrent episodes usually cause permanent neurologic injury

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The presentation is consistent with an uncomplicated acute spinal sprain or strain without neurologic deficits or other red-flag findings. Patients should be encouraged to remain active and continue normal activities as tolerated because prolonged inactivity and bed rest can delay functional recovery. Once acute discomfort subsides, progressive trunk and core strengthening, flexibility exercises, ergonomic modification, and instruction in proper lifting mechanics can reduce recurrence. Option B is incorrect because strict bed rest promotes deconditioning and may prolong symptoms; return to heavy lifting should be gradual. Option C is incorrect because continuous rigid bracing has not been shown to prevent recurrence and may contribute to muscle deconditioning. Option D is incorrect because routine or serial advanced imaging is not indicated in the absence of red flags, progressive neurologic deficits, or persistent symptoms despite appropriate management. Option E is incorrect because long-term avoidance of activity is unnecessary, increases fear-avoidance behavior and deconditioning, and is not supported by the benign natural history of uncomplicated spinal sprain or strain.

Question 326  [ID: f38b40eced2f4b06891280b6c4cc9114]
A 27-year-old man presents with 10 months of progressive lower back and buttock pain. His discomfort is most severe in the early morning, improves with activity, and worsens after prolonged rest. He also reports intermittent redness and pain in his right eye. Examination shows reduced lumbar flexion, limited chest expansion, and tenderness at both Achilles tendon insertions. Laboratory studies reveal an elevated C-reactive protein level, negative rheumatoid factor, and positivity for HLA-B27. Plain radiographs demonstrate bilateral sacroiliac erosions and sclerosis, and spinal imaging shows thin vertical bony bridges between adjacent vertebral bodies. Which of the following pathophysiologic mechanisms most likely accounts for the progressive imaging findings in this patient?

A. Autoantibody-mediated synovial inflammation causing marginal erosions without new bone formation
B. Immune-mediated inflammation at ligament and tendon insertion sites followed by repair with pathologic new bone formation
C. Deposition of monosodium urate crystals causing episodic neutrophil-mediated inflammation
D. Age-related loss of proteoglycans causing disc desiccation and noninflammatory osteophyte formation
E. Defective osteoclast function causing generalized failure of bone resorption

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The patient has ankylosing spondylitis, an HLA-B27-associated inflammatory spondyloarthropathy. The characteristic process begins with immune-mediated inflammation at entheses, the sites where ligaments, tendons, and joint capsules attach to bone. Chronic inflammation produces erosions, followed by reparative new bone formation and ossification. In the spine, this process creates marginal syndesmophytes that bridge adjacent vertebral bodies and may eventually produce a bamboo-spine appearance. Sacroiliitis, inflammatory back pain, enthesitis, reduced chest expansion, and acute anterior uveitis further support this mechanism. A is incorrect because autoantibody-mediated synovitis with marginal erosions and limited new bone formation is more characteristic of rheumatoid arthritis. C is incorrect because monosodium urate deposition causes gout, which typically presents with acute episodic peripheral joint inflammation and does not produce syndesmophytes. D is incorrect because degenerative disease causes mechanical pain that worsens with activity, disc-space narrowing, and nonmarginal osteophytes rather than inflammatory sacroiliitis and enthesitis. E is incorrect because defective osteoclast function causes osteopetrosis, which produces diffusely dense but brittle bones rather than inflammatory erosions followed by ligamentous ossification.

Question 327  [ID: f39414af1de44b9eb443830b02d85dbf]
A 46-year-old right-hand-dominant carpenter presents with 6 weeks of pain along the outer aspect of his right elbow. The pain began after several consecutive days of repetitive screwdriver use and is aggravated by lifting objects with the palm facing downward. He denies direct trauma, fever, joint swelling, numbness, or weakness. Examination shows focal tenderness just distal to the lateral bony prominence of the elbow and pain with resisted wrist extension while the elbow is fully extended. Grip strength is limited by pain, but sensation, reflexes, and distal pulses are normal. Plain radiographs show no fracture, joint-space abnormality, or bony lesion. In addition to short-term symptomatic treatment, which of the following patient education strategies is most appropriate to reduce the risk of recurrence?

A. Avoid all use of the affected limb for at least 6 months
B. Use ergonomic tools, reduce repetitive forceful gripping, and gradually strengthen the forearm after pain improves
C. Perform repetitive resisted wrist-extension exercises during the painful phase
D. Wear a rigid long-arm cast whenever performing occupational tasks
E. Continue usual activities without modification because recurrence is not related to repetitive loading

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The presentation is consistent with lateral epicondylitis, an overuse injury involving the common wrist extensor origin, particularly the extensor carpi radialis brevis. Prevention of recurrence focuses on reducing repetitive forceful gripping and wrist extension, improving workplace ergonomics, using appropriately sized or powered tools, correcting technique, and gradually introducing stretching and strengthening exercises after acute pain subsides. Therefore, choice B is correct.

A is incorrect because prolonged complete avoidance causes unnecessary deconditioning and is not recommended; relative rest followed by a graded return to activity is preferred.

C is incorrect because forceful resisted exercise during the painful phase may worsen symptoms. Progressive strengthening, often including eccentric exercise, should begin when pain is controlled.

D is incorrect because routine rigid immobilization is unnecessary and may lead to stiffness and weakness. A counterforce strap or short-term wrist support may be considered, but it does not replace ergonomic modification and rehabilitation.

E is incorrect because repetitive gripping and loaded wrist extension are important contributing factors. Continuing the same activities without modification increases the likelihood of persistent or recurrent symptoms.

Question 328  [ID: f506dcac5bf84945857b42cdf58b5e05]
A 27-year-old man presents with 10 months of progressive low back and alternating buttock pain. His symptoms are most severe upon awakening, with stiffness lasting approximately 90 minutes. The pain improves with exercise but does not improve with rest. He also reports a prior episode of a painful, red eye associated with photophobia. Examination reveals tenderness over the sacroiliac regions and limited lumbar flexion. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate is elevated; rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies are negative. Pelvic radiographs demonstrate bilateral, symmetric erosions and subchondral sclerosis of the sacroiliac joints. Which of the following additional physical examination findings is most likely in this patient?

A. Lumbar excursion of less than 5 cm during a modified Schober test
B. Reproduction of radicular pain below the knee with passive straight-leg elevation
C. Pain and weakness during resisted shoulder abduction
D. Hyperextension of the knees and elbows beyond the normal range
E. Tenderness at multiple bilateral soft-tissue points without joint abnormalities

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's young age, chronic inflammatory-pattern back pain, prolonged morning stiffness, improvement with exercise, alternating buttock pain, prior acute anterior uveitis, and bilateral symmetric sacroiliac erosions are characteristic of ankylosing spondylitis. Progressive restriction of lumbar mobility is common. The modified Schober test measures lumbar flexion by marking the lower lumbar region and measuring the increase in distance during forward bending; an increase of less than 5 cm indicates reduced lumbar flexion and supports this diagnosis.

B is incorrect because radicular pain reproduced by straight-leg elevation suggests lumbosacral nerve root irritation, commonly from lumbar disk herniation, rather than an axial inflammatory condition.

C is incorrect because pain and weakness with resisted shoulder abduction suggest rotator cuff pathology, particularly involvement of the supraspinatus tendon.

D is incorrect because excessive extension of the knees and elbows is associated with generalized joint hypermobility, which is not expected in a disorder characterized by progressive axial stiffness.

E is incorrect because multiple bilateral tender points without objective joint abnormalities are associated with fibromyalgia. This would not explain the patient's sacroiliac radiographic changes, elevated inflammatory marker, or restricted lumbar motion.

Question 329  [ID: f64300f40f9e46b3a015e1cff945c155]
A 67-year-old man presents to the emergency department with severe left-sided torso pain and difficulty breathing after falling from a ladder and striking his side against a railing. He is alert but anxious. Temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), blood pressure is 146/88 mm Hg, pulse is 112/min, respirations are 28/min, and oxygen saturation is 91% on room air. Examination reveals extensive ecchymosis and marked tenderness over the left anterolateral torso. During inspiration, a segment of the injured area moves inward while the remainder expands; during expiration, the same segment moves outward. Breath sounds are diminished on the left without tracheal deviation or jugular venous distention. CT demonstrates discontinuity of the left fourth through seventh ribs at both their anterior and lateral aspects, with an underlying patchy pulmonary opacity. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Simple pneumothorax
B. Flail chest
C. Pulmonary contusion without structural instability
D. Cardiac tamponade
E. Costochondritis

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Flail chest occurs when at least three adjacent ribs are each disrupted in two or more locations, creating a free-floating segment. The characteristic finding is paradoxical movement: the unstable segment moves inward during inspiration and outward during expiration. The CT findings involving the fourth through seventh ribs at two sites confirm the structural pattern. The patchy pulmonary opacity suggests an associated pulmonary contusion, which commonly contributes to hypoxemia and respiratory distress. A simple pneumothorax may cause unilateral diminished breath sounds and dyspnea but does not produce paradoxical segmental movement, and no pleural air is described. Pulmonary contusion may explain the opacity and hypoxemia but does not by itself cause an unstable free-floating segment. Cardiac tamponade typically presents with hypotension, jugular venous distention, and muffled heart sounds rather than focal paradoxical movement. Costochondritis causes reproducible anterior pain without trauma-related structural disruption, respiratory compromise, or abnormal segmental motion.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Question 330  [ID: f7fb8c35fb5a4df1a1c757a57bc22636]
A 42-year-old woman presents with 10 months of progressive hand stiffness and difficulty opening her mouth. She reports episodic blanching followed by blue and red discoloration of her fingers when exposed to cold. She also has worsening heartburn, difficulty swallowing solid foods, and exertional dyspnea. Examination shows shiny, thickened skin involving the fingers, hands, forearms, upper arms, chest, and face. The fingertips have small ulcerations, and fine inspiratory crackles are heard at both lung bases. Muscle strength is normal. Laboratory studies show a positive antinuclear antibody with a nucleolar pattern, positive anti–topoisomerase I antibodies, and a normal creatine kinase level. Pulmonary function testing demonstrates a restrictive pattern with decreased diffusing capacity. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Dermatomyositis
B. Diffuse cutaneous systemic sclerosis
C. Limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis
D. Mixed connective tissue disease
E. Systemic lupus erythematosus

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: Diffuse cutaneous systemic sclerosis is the most likely diagnosis. The key findings include progressive skin thickening extending proximal to the elbows, cold-induced triphasic digital color changes, fingertip ulcerations, esophageal dysmotility, and interstitial lung involvement. Anti–topoisomerase I antibodies are strongly associated with the diffuse cutaneous form and an increased risk of interstitial lung disease. A nucleolar antinuclear antibody pattern also supports this diagnosis.

A is incorrect because dermatomyositis typically causes symmetric proximal muscle weakness, elevated muscle enzymes, and characteristic findings such as a heliotrope eruption and Gottron papules. This patient's normal strength and creatine kinase level argue against it.

C is incorrect because limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis generally causes skin thickening confined to areas distal to the elbows and knees, often with facial involvement. It is more strongly associated with anticentromere antibodies and late-onset pulmonary arterial hypertension rather than early interstitial lung disease.

D is incorrect because mixed connective tissue disease usually presents with overlapping clinical features and high-titer anti-U1 ribonucleoprotein antibodies. Although cold-induced digital color changes and swollen hands may occur, the extensive proximal skin thickening and anti–topoisomerase I positivity favor diffuse cutaneous systemic sclerosis.

E is incorrect because systemic lupus erythematosus may cause inflammatory joint symptoms, mucocutaneous findings, nephritis, and cytopenias, but it does not typically produce progressive widespread skin tightening. Anti–double-stranded DNA and anti-Smith antibodies would provide greater diagnostic support for that condition.

Question 331  [ID: f8e7e8c3c1a24959b3cdd609e072b188]
A 6-week-old girl is brought for evaluation after her pediatrician noted asymmetric thigh folds during a routine examination. She was delivered by cesarean section at 39 weeks after a frank breech presentation. Her parents report no fever, irritability, feeding difficulty, or history of trauma. Examination shows unequal knee heights when the legs are flexed and a palpable clunk when the left flexed thigh is abducted while anterior pressure is applied. Ultrasonography demonstrates a shallow left acetabulum with lateral displacement of the femoral head that is reducible with positioning. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial intervention?

A. Observation with repeat ultrasonography at 12 months of age
B. Application of a Pavlik harness
C. Immediate closed reduction followed by spica casting
D. Open surgical reduction
E. Acetabular osteotomy

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: A Pavlik harness is the preferred initial intervention for a reducible, unstable femoral head in an infant younger than 6 months. The harness maintains flexion and controlled abduction, promoting stable positioning of the femoral head within the acetabulum and allowing normal acetabular development. Treatment requires close clinical and ultrasonographic follow-up because excessive flexion or abduction can cause complications such as femoral nerve palsy or avascular necrosis. A is incorrect because observation alone is inappropriate when instability and displacement are demonstrated on examination and ultrasonography; delayed treatment increases the risk of persistent deformity and later functional impairment. C is incorrect because closed reduction and spica casting are generally reserved for infants approximately 6 to 18 months old or for younger infants in whom harness treatment fails. D is incorrect because open reduction is typically used when closed methods fail or in children presenting later with fixed displacement. E is incorrect because acetabular osteotomy is generally reserved for older children with persistent acetabular deformity or residual instability after reduction.

Question 332  [ID: f91fd7f07d314004825a6120d099ce78]
A 39-year-old man presents with 4 months of gradually worsening deep right groin pain that is exacerbated by weight-bearing and internal rotation. He has systemic lupus erythematosus and has taken prednisone 20 to 40 mg daily for the past 2 years. He denies trauma, fever, or intravenous drug use. Examination demonstrates an antalgic gait and decreased internal rotation of the right hip. There is no erythema, warmth, or focal tenderness. The leukocyte count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are normal. Plain radiographs show no articular surface collapse or joint-space narrowing. MRI reveals a well-demarcated, serpiginous subchondral lesion in the anterosuperior femoral head with surrounding bone marrow edema and preservation of the femoral head contour. Which of the following is the most appropriate intervention to preserve the native joint in this patient?

A. Core decompression of the femoral head
B. Immediate total hip arthroplasty
C. Open irrigation and debridement with intravenous antibiotics
D. Intra-articular glucocorticoid injection
E. Arthroscopic repair of the acetabular labrum

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Core decompression is the preferred joint-preserving intervention for symptomatic, early-stage disease in which MRI demonstrates a characteristic subchondral lesion but the femoral head has not yet collapsed. Drilling into the affected area decreases intraosseous pressure and may promote revascularization and new bone formation. This patient is at increased risk because of prolonged systemic glucocorticoid exposure. Total hip arthroplasty (B) is generally reserved for advanced disease with subchondral collapse, secondary degenerative changes, or failure of joint-preserving therapy. Open irrigation and debridement with intravenous antibiotics (C) would be appropriate for septic arthritis, which is unlikely given the chronic course, absence of fever or joint warmth, and normal inflammatory markers. An intra-articular glucocorticoid injection (D) would not correct the underlying structural process and may worsen risk related to glucocorticoid exposure. Arthroscopic labral repair (E) is used for symptomatic labral pathology, which typically causes mechanical symptoms and would not account for the characteristic MRI findings within the femoral head.

Question 333  [ID: f94bb59a40fb4a518d94d28215cdf68e]
A 27-year-old woman presents with 4 months of pain and swelling in both hands and wrists. She reports morning stiffness lasting 90 minutes, intermittent painless oral ulcers, fatigue, and a facial rash that worsens after sun exposure. Examination shows tenderness and mild swelling of the bilateral metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints without deformity. A faint erythematous eruption is present over both cheeks but spares the nasolabial folds. Laboratory studies show leukopenia, a positive antinuclear antibody titer of 1:640, elevated anti-double-stranded DNA antibody levels, and decreased C3 and C4 levels. Serum creatinine and urinalysis are normal. Radiographs of the hands show no erosions. Which of the following is the best initial long-term pharmacologic intervention for this patient?

A. Cyclophosphamide
B. Hydroxychloroquine
C. Ibuprofen alone as needed
D. Prednisone 60 mg daily
E. Rituximab

Correct Answer: B
Explanation: The correct answer is B. This patient has systemic lupus erythematosus with nonerosive joint involvement, photosensitive malar eruption, oral ulcers, leukopenia, positive antinuclear and anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies, and hypocomplementemia. Hydroxychloroquine is recommended for nearly all patients with this condition unless contraindicated. It improves joint and skin manifestations, reduces disease flares and cumulative organ damage, and may improve long-term survival. A baseline ophthalmologic examination is recommended because retinal toxicity is a rare but important adverse effect.

A is incorrect because cyclophosphamide is reserved for severe, organ-threatening manifestations such as proliferative nephritis, severe neuropsychiatric involvement, pulmonary hemorrhage, or systemic vasculitis. This patient has no evidence of major organ involvement.

C is incorrect because an NSAID may provide short-term relief of pain and stiffness but does not adequately reduce overall disease activity or prevent future flares when used alone.

D is incorrect because high-dose systemic glucocorticoid therapy is inappropriate for stable, non-organ-threatening manifestations. Prolonged high-dose therapy carries substantial risks, including infection, osteoporosis, hyperglycemia, hypertension, and avascular necrosis. A short course of low-dose glucocorticoids may occasionally be used as bridging therapy during a flare.

E is incorrect because rituximab is generally reserved for severe or refractory disease that has not responded to standard therapies. It is not appropriate as initial treatment in a patient with primarily joint and skin manifestations.

Question 334  [ID: fa3bdcd08fb84669a6ac9dce390f2570]
A 16-year-old girl presents with 4 months of gradually worsening right groin pain that is most noticeable after running and prolonged walking. She denies trauma, fever, morning stiffness, or pain in other joints. Her medical history is notable for treatment with a lower-extremity abduction brace during infancy. Examination demonstrates a mild limp, pain with passive flexion and internal rotation of the right lower extremity, and reduced abductor strength. There is no erythema, warmth, or swelling. A complete blood count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and C-reactive protein level are normal. An anteroposterior radiograph demonstrates a shallow right acetabulum, increased acetabular inclination, and a lateral center-edge angle of 15 degrees without fracture or degenerative changes. Activity modification, physical therapy, and referral to a pediatric orthopedic specialist are planned. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmaceutical therapy for symptomatic relief?

A. Alendronate orally once weekly
B. Methotrexate orally once weekly
C. Prednisone orally with a prolonged taper
D. Naproxen orally twice daily as needed
E. Intra-articular triamcinolone injection

Correct Answer: D
Explanation: The clinical history and radiographic findings are consistent with residual symptomatic developmental dysplasia of the hip. A shallow acetabulum provides inadequate femoral head coverage, increasing mechanical stress on the joint and surrounding soft tissues. In a patient without contraindications, a short course of an oral nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug such as naproxen is appropriate for initial symptomatic relief while activity modification, targeted physical therapy, and orthopedic evaluation are pursued. Medication does not correct the underlying structural abnormality, and persistent symptoms may require a joint-preserving procedure such as periacetabular osteotomy. Alendronate is used primarily for disorders involving reduced bone density and has no role in correcting acetabular undercoverage. Methotrexate is a disease-modifying antirheumatic drug used for inflammatory arthritis; this patient has no systemic symptoms, inflammatory examination findings, or laboratory evidence of inflammation. Systemic prednisone is not indicated for mechanically mediated pain and carries substantial adverse effects with prolonged use. An intra-articular glucocorticoid injection is not preferred as initial treatment in this adolescent because it does not address the structural abnormality and may expose cartilage to unnecessary risk.

Question 335  [ID: faeb2a73ec4140448fa7217f78aae82a]
A 48-year-old woman presents with 8 months of bilateral hand and wrist pain accompanied by morning stiffness lasting approximately 60 minutes. She also reports persistent gritty, burning eyes and difficulty swallowing dry foods without water. Examination shows tenderness of the wrists and metacarpophalangeal joints without deformity or significant swelling. Oral examination reveals dry mucosa and multiple dental caries. Schirmer testing demonstrates decreased tear production. Laboratory studies reveal a positive antinuclear antibody, positive anti-Ro/SSA and anti-La/SSB antibodies, an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and a normal C-reactive protein level. Hand radiographs show no erosions. Artificial tears and saliva substitutes have improved her dryness, but her pain and stiffness continue to interfere with daily activities. Which of the following is the best next pharmacologic intervention for her persistent inflammatory symptoms?

A. Hydroxychloroquine
B. Methotrexate
C. Oral prednisone as indefinite maintenance therapy
D. Rituximab
E. Pilocarpine

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Hydroxychloroquine is the best next intervention for persistent inflammatory arthralgia in this patient with characteristic sicca manifestations, positive anti-Ro/SSA and anti-La/SSB antibodies, and nonerosive hand and wrist symptoms. It is commonly used as a steroid-sparing agent for inflammatory pain and stiffness when conservative measures are insufficient. A baseline ophthalmologic examination and periodic retinal toxicity screening are required. Methotrexate may be considered when inflammatory symptoms do not respond adequately to hydroxychloroquine or when more substantial synovitis is present, but it is not the preferred initial disease-modifying medication here. Indefinite oral prednisone is inappropriate because long-term glucocorticoid therapy carries substantial risks, including osteoporosis, infection, hyperglycemia, and adrenal suppression; brief courses may be reserved for selected severe flares. Rituximab is generally reserved for severe, refractory, or systemic manifestations such as vasculitis, significant neurologic involvement, or major organ disease. Pilocarpine can stimulate salivary and lacrimal secretion but would not adequately treat the patient's persistent inflammatory pain and stiffness.

Question 336  [ID: fb0b17a734d0444f9a7e9c6753b21699]
A 24-year-old man presents with pain and swelling at the base of his right thumb after falling while skiing 2 days ago. He was holding a ski pole when the thumb was forcibly pulled away from the palm. Examination reveals ecchymosis and focal tenderness along the ulnar aspect of the first metacarpophalangeal joint. Stress testing with the joint flexed to 30 degrees demonstrates marked valgus laxity without a firm endpoint. A small, tender mass is palpable proximal to the joint. Plain radiographs show no fracture or dislocation. MRI demonstrates complete disruption of a stabilizing ligament with the torn distal end displaced superficial to the adjacent adductor aponeurosis. Which of the following is the most appropriate intervention?

A. Immediate referral for operative repair
B. Thumb spica immobilization for 4 weeks without specialist referral
C. Early unrestricted range-of-motion exercises
D. Intra-articular glucocorticoid injection
E. Closed reduction followed by buddy taping

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient has a complete rupture of the thumb ulnar collateral ligament with displacement superficial to the adductor aponeurosis, producing a Stener lesion. The palpable mass, absence of a firm endpoint during valgus stress testing, and characteristic MRI findings support this diagnosis. Because the interposed aponeurosis prevents the ligament from returning to its anatomic position, spontaneous healing is unlikely; prompt referral for operative repair is indicated. Choice B is incorrect because thumb spica immobilization alone is appropriate for stable partial tears or selected nondisplaced complete tears, not a displaced rupture with a Stener lesion. Choice C is incorrect because unrestricted motion can worsen instability and impair healing. Choice D is incorrect because glucocorticoid injection does not restore ligament continuity and may further weaken injured tissue. Choice E is incorrect because there is no dislocation requiring reduction, and buddy taping does not provide adequate stabilization of the first metacarpophalangeal joint.

Question 337  [ID: fb1533027e3a45e5b7e7b3b2d07a8c77]
A 29-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a high-speed motor vehicle collision in which his flexed left knee struck the dashboard. He reports severe left groin and buttock pain and is unable to bear weight. Examination shows that the left lower limb is shortened, adducted, and internally rotated. Dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses are intact. The patient has decreased sensation over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot, with weakness of ankle dorsiflexion. An anteroposterior pelvic radiograph demonstrates the left femoral head positioned superior and posterior to the acetabulum without an associated fracture. Based on the anatomic relationship to the displaced femoral head, which neural structure is most likely injured?

A. Femoral nerve
B. Obturator nerve
C. Sciatic nerve
D. Superior gluteal nerve
E. Lateral femoral cutaneous nerve

Correct Answer: C
Explanation: The correct answer is C. The mechanism, limb position, and radiographic findings indicate posterior displacement of the femoral head. The sciatic nerve passes posterior to the hip joint and is therefore the neural structure most vulnerable to traction or compression. Its common fibular division is affected most often, producing weakness of ankle dorsiflexion and sensory loss over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot. The femoral nerve lies anterior to the joint and would more likely cause impaired knee extension and reduced sensation over the anterior thigh and medial leg. The obturator nerve courses medially and primarily supplies thigh adduction and medial thigh sensation. The superior gluteal nerve supplies the gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae; injury causes impaired hip abduction and a Trendelenburg gait rather than foot drop. The lateral femoral cutaneous nerve is a purely sensory nerve supplying the anterolateral thigh and does not account for the motor deficits described.

Question 338  [ID: fcb40e6cb76e43f5a3f8444709d63b21]
A 46-year-old woman presents with an 18-month history of progressive tightness of the skin over her fingers and difficulty removing her rings. She reports that her fingers become white and then blue when exposed to cold, followed by painful redness during rewarming. She also has frequent heartburn and intermittent difficulty swallowing solid foods. Examination reveals shiny, thickened skin of the distal fingers, decreased oral aperture, scattered facial telangiectasias, and several healed ulcers on the fingertips. Strength is 5/5 throughout. Laboratory studies show a positive antinuclear antibody with a centromere pattern and a normal creatine kinase level. Chest radiography shows no acute abnormality. Which of the following additional physical examination findings would most strongly support the suspected unifying diagnosis?

A. Dilated nailfold capillary loops with areas of capillary dropout
B. Violaceous papules over the metacarpophalangeal and interphalangeal joints
C. Marked hyperextensibility of the skin with generalized joint hypermobility
D. Firm, painless nodules over the olecranon processes
E. Tenderness at the Achilles tendon insertions bilaterally

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The correct answer is A. The combination of cold-induced triphasic digital color changes, progressive thickening and tightening of the distal skin, reduced oral aperture, telangiectasias, fingertip ulceration, esophageal symptoms, and anticentromere antibodies is characteristic of systemic sclerosis, particularly the limited cutaneous form. Nailfold capillaroscopy commonly demonstrates enlarged or dilated capillary loops, microhemorrhages, and areas of capillary loss or dropout. Assessing the nailfolds is an important component of the physical examination because these abnormalities help distinguish secondary Raynaud phenomenon associated with connective tissue disease from primary Raynaud phenomenon.

B is incorrect because violaceous papules over the extensor surfaces of the finger joints are Gottron papules, a characteristic finding of dermatomyositis. Dermatomyositis would also commonly cause symmetric proximal muscle weakness and elevated muscle enzymes.

C is incorrect because marked skin hyperextensibility and generalized joint hypermobility suggest a heritable collagen disorder such as Ehlers-Danlos syndrome. The patient instead has tightening and reduced mobility of the skin.

D is incorrect because firm nodules over pressure points, including the olecranon processes, are associated with rheumatoid arthritis. Rheumatoid arthritis more typically presents with symmetric inflammatory polyarthritis and prolonged morning stiffness.

E is incorrect because Achilles enthesitis is associated with spondyloarthropathies, such as ankylosing spondylitis or psoriatic arthritis, and does not explain the patient's vasospastic episodes, skin tightening, telangiectasias, and esophageal symptoms.

Question 339  [ID: fcbabf09504f4b48952c9e9fbf25b2de]
A 42-year-old man presents with sudden posterior ankle pain that began while he was playing recreational basketball. He reports pushing off to sprint when he felt as though someone had kicked him from behind, followed by an audible pop and immediate difficulty walking. Examination shows swelling and ecchymosis proximal to the heel, weak active plantar flexion, and a palpable defect approximately 4 cm proximal to the calcaneal insertion. When the calf is squeezed while the patient lies prone with his feet extending beyond the examination table, the affected foot does not plantar flex. Plain radiographs show no fracture. What is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Achilles tendon rupture
B. Lateral ankle sprain
C. Gastrocnemius muscle strain
D. Peroneal tendon subluxation
E. Plantar fascia rupture

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: Achilles tendon rupture is the most likely diagnosis. It typically occurs in middle-aged recreational athletes during forceful push-off, sudden acceleration, or jumping. Patients often describe an audible pop or the sensation of being kicked in the posterior ankle. Key findings include a palpable defect several centimeters proximal to the calcaneal insertion, markedly weakened plantar flexion, and absence of passive plantar flexion when the calf is squeezed, indicating a positive Thompson test. Normal radiographs help exclude an associated fracture. A lateral ankle sprain usually follows inversion and causes lateral ligament tenderness rather than a posterior defect or abnormal Thompson test. A gastrocnemius strain can cause calf pain and swelling but generally preserves plantar flexion during calf compression and does not produce a defect near the heel. Peroneal tendon subluxation causes snapping and pain behind the lateral malleolus, often after forced dorsiflexion and eversion. Plantar fascia rupture causes acute pain and ecchymosis along the plantar arch or heel but does not impair the calf-squeeze response.

Question 340  [ID: fd51f608f7404bf6a7c9cde0116a9856]
A 29-year-old woman presents with pain and swelling at the base of her right thumb after falling while skiing 2 days ago. She was holding a ski pole when her thumb was forcibly pulled away from her palm. Examination reveals ecchymosis and focal tenderness along the ulnar aspect of the first metacarpophalangeal joint. Stress testing demonstrates increased valgus laxity without a firm endpoint compared with the contralateral side. Sensation and capillary refill are normal. Plain radiographs show no fracture. Ultrasonography demonstrates discontinuity of the stabilizing structure on the ulnar side of the joint, with its distal end displaced superficial to an adjacent tendinous expansion. Which of the following mechanisms most directly explains why this injury is unlikely to heal without operative repair?

A. Interposition of the adductor pollicis aponeurosis between the disrupted ligament and its distal attachment
B. Compression of the median nerve beneath the flexor retinaculum
C. Ischemic necrosis of the proximal pole of the scaphoid
D. Entrapment of the flexor pollicis longus tendon within the first dorsal compartment
E. Displacement of the extensor pollicis longus tendon around Lister tubercle

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The forced abduction mechanism, ulnar-sided first metacarpophalangeal joint tenderness, valgus instability without a firm endpoint, and ultrasonographic displacement indicate a complete disruption of the thumb ulnar collateral ligament with a Stener lesion. In this lesion, the torn distal ligament becomes displaced superficial to the adductor pollicis aponeurosis. The aponeurosis is interposed between the ligament and its insertion at the base of the proximal phalanx, preventing anatomic apposition and spontaneous healing; operative repair is therefore generally required. Choice B describes carpal tunnel syndrome, which causes median-distribution paresthesias and thenar weakness rather than valgus instability of the thumb. Choice C is a complication of scaphoid fracture due to its retrograde blood supply; radiographs show no fracture, and it would not explain the examination findings. Choice D is anatomically incorrect because the first dorsal compartment contains the abductor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis, not the flexor pollicis longus. Choice E concerns extensor pollicis longus anatomy and potential tendon dysfunction near the distal radius, which does not cause ulnar-sided joint laxity or prevent healing in this case.

Question 341  [ID: ff5370aa8de14180bc20461be6b0bcb4]
A 16-year-old boy presents with progressive exertional dyspnea, reduced exercise tolerance, and intermittent substernal discomfort. His parents report that an inward depression of the lower anterior thorax has become more pronounced during his recent growth spurt. Physical examination shows a symmetric concavity centered over the lower sternum without tenderness. Cardiac auscultation reveals a soft systolic ejection murmur, and breath sounds are clear bilaterally. Complete blood count and comprehensive metabolic panel are normal. Electrocardiography shows an incomplete right bundle branch block, and spirometry demonstrates a mild restrictive pattern. Which of the following diagnostic studies is most appropriate to quantify the anatomic severity of this patient's abnormality and help determine whether operative correction should be considered?

A. Noncontrast computed tomography with calculation of the transverse thoracic diameter-to-anteroposterior diameter ratio
B. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry of the lumbar spine and proximal femur
C. Magnetic resonance imaging of the thoracic spinal cord with gadolinium
D. Radionuclide bone scintigraphy using technetium-99m
E. Diagnostic thoracentesis with pleural fluid analysis

Correct Answer: A
Explanation: The patient's progressive inward lower sternal depression, exertional symptoms, mild restrictive physiology, and incomplete right bundle branch block are characteristic of pectus excavatum. Noncontrast computed tomography can define the anatomy and permit calculation of the Haller index, which is the ratio of the maximal transverse internal thoracic diameter to the narrowest anteroposterior diameter between the sternum and vertebral body. A value greater than approximately 3.25 indicates a severe deformity and may support operative correction when accompanied by symptoms or cardiopulmonary compromise. B is incorrect because dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry evaluates bone mineral density and does not quantify this deformity. C is incorrect because spinal cord MRI is used for suspected neural compression, tumors, or congenital spinal abnormalities; it does not provide the standard severity index used here. D is incorrect because bone scintigraphy evaluates metabolically active lesions such as occult fractures, osteomyelitis, or skeletal metastases. E is incorrect because thoracentesis is indicated for evaluation or treatment of a pleural effusion, which is not suggested by the examination or spirometry findings.
